## 📚 Paper List

- **[AutoVLA-D: Learn a Trajectory-Geometric Condition from Reasoning for Autonomous Driving VLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12382)**  `arXiv:2611.12382`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuguang Yang, Zhewen Tan, Canyu Chen, Linlin Yang, Cheng Chi, Chunyang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive VLA planning usually predicts continuous trajectories as the final output, but driving instructions often describe actions rather than ego states, which weakens direct conditioning on the final continuous trajectory. We address this gap with TrajCond, which introduces a discrete trajectory-geometric condition for VLA planning. Specifically, we represent TrajCond as a 1D-Brohan differential action trajectory and learn it with a two-stage training framework before final continuous trajectory prediction. With the simultaneous autoregressive generation of discrete and numeric trajectories, we name our method AutoVLA-D (Double). Experiments on NAVSIM navtest, nuScenes, and NeuroNCAP show that discrete trajectories are weaker as direct outputs but more effective as TrajCond, consistently improving final continuous trajectory prediction across closed-loop and open-loop driving benchmarks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SPARK: Simple Post-training for Adapting pRetrained Knowledge to Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12102)**  `arXiv:2611.12102`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Taekyung Kim, Jeongeun Park, Byeongho Heo, Sungjoon Choi, Sangdoo Yun, Dongyoon Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale representation learning has produced public foundation models that provide strong general-purpose visual features. However, their pretraining objectives are not designed for robot representation learning, so the resulting embeddings tend to emphasize global semantics rather than the temporally sensitive representations required for robot control. Training robot models from scratch is also undesirable due to the limited scale and high collection cost of robotics data, as well as the substantial computational burden of pre-training. In this paper, we propose SPARK (Simple Post-training for Adapting pRetrained Knowledge), a post-training method that adapts foundation models to robot control. SPARK is built on two principles: temporal abstraction, which encourages the encoder to derive compact features that preserve information essential for understanding temporal changes and action-relevant scene structure, and knowledge preservation, which aligns patch-level representations with those of the original foundation model to retain useful pretrained semantics. These objectives yield compact visual state representations that remain semantically meaningful while preserving useful prior knowledge. Experiments across multiple robotics benchmarks show that SPARK consistently improves success rates and generalization over vanilla foundation models, and further demonstrate that these gains transfer to real-world robot manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Budget-aware Correspondence-driven View Selection for Real-time 3D Perception of Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11809)**  `arXiv:2611.11809`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Vaishnav Ramesh, Jacob Carter, Shivali J. Patel, Sanjeev J. Koppal, Md Jahidul Islam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D visual perception and scene reconstruction are critical for autonomous robots and intelligent transportation, yet real-world deployments often generate long, redundant image sequences that hinder downstream processing. This report investigates BCVS (budget-aware correspondence-driven view sampling), a pose-free view selection algorithm that offers 22+ FPS on a CPU, enabling online filtering for efficient 3D perception by mobile robots. Unlike existing approaches that rely on full structure-from-motion (SfM) pipelines or camera pose estimation, BCVS employs a purely correspondence-based scoring strategy that prioritizes view novelty while maintaining sufficient overlap for geometric stability. Given a user-specified budget, BCVS iteratively selects the most distinct frames and suppresses near-duplicates, producing compact subsets that preserve connectivity and minimize reconstruction loss. Extensive experiments on benchmark show that BCVS outperforms CPU-friendly baselines at tight budgets and remains competitive as budgets scale. Field trials with large aerial and marine robotics data further validate its practicality and scalability: BCVS reduces redundancy up to 16% while sustaining robust SfM registration and high-quality 3D reconstructions.
  </details>

- **[HARP-Bench: Benchmarking Robot Manipulation Around Active Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11123)**  `arXiv:2611.11123`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Qinhong Zhou, Riley Zong, Chunru Lin, Diyi Hu, Shivraj Singh Bhatti, Hongxin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent robot manipulation benchmarks have enabled systematic evaluation of general-purpose robot policies across diverse objects, scenes, instructions, and embodiments. However, most existing benchmarks still assume that the robot is the only active agent and that task semantics are determined primarily by the instruction and object state. This assumption overlooks a common source of real-world variation: humans actively sharing the workspace. In such settings, humans may reveal an implicit goal, complete part of the task, temporarily change object availability, or constrain safe robot motion while working nearby. We introduce HARP-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating robot manipulation policies in workspaces shared with active humans. HARP-Bench treats human activity as part of the manipulation problem, rather than as background scene variation. It organizes human-present manipulation into four conditions: Cueing, Contribution, Interruption, and Co-presence. These conditions are instantiated through everyday manipulation tasks, including paired human-present variants for controlled comparison with standard manipulation. HARP-Bench also provides a demonstration-generation pipeline for dynamic human-present settings. We evaluate VLA models, imitation-learning methods, and heuristic policies on HARP-Bench. Human-present variations challenge current policies, and higher task success does not consistently improve safety. Quantitative failure analysis identifies manipulation and recovery as the dominant remaining challenges. Training with HARP-Bench demonstrations improves human-present performance in simulation. A real-world study on one task family shows improved performance under human contribution, but not interruption, providing initial evidence of the data's utility and identifying limits of transfer. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HSGNav: Hypothesis Scene Graphs for Multi-Scale Lifelong Navigation under Object Relocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10547)**  `arXiv:2611.10547`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Kaiyao Liao, Qingfeng Li, Chen Chen, Qing Sun, Ningbo Gu, Xiaoze Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In real homes, assistive robots must repeatedly locate targets from large furniture to tiny tabletop items, as people move objects between household surfaces. Uniformly instantiating detections at different scales as 3D objects can be brittle for small on-surface items; without explicit object-state validation, stale entries may persist after scene changes. Our key insight is to separate persistent 3D furniture entities from revisable surface-object hypotheses, retaining their visual evidence without requiring independent object geometry. We formalize this design as the Hypothesis Scene Graph (HSG), which preserves small-object evidence and supports local revision without rebuilding the stable entity topology. Building on HSG, HSGNav uses this separation for multi-scale lifelong navigation: retrieval selects entity or hypothesis evidence according to target scale, exploration acquires additional surface observations, and verification confirms target presence or invalidates contradicted hypothesis records. Because existing lifelong benchmarks such as GOAT-Bench rarely require mid-episode memory invalidation or multi-scale search, we introduce FAROS-Bench (Find And Re-find Objects across Scales) for mid-episode relocation of surface objects and multi-scale find goals. Experiments show that HSGNav performs competitively on GOAT-Bench while substantially outperforming prior methods on FAROS-Bench.
  </details>

- **[RCT-AD: Reliability-Aware Context and Temporal BEV Reasoning for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04689)**  `arXiv:2607.04689`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Argho Dey, Yunfei Yin, Swachha Ray, Md Minhazul Islam, Zheng Yuan, Sijing Xiong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable temporal representation learning is important for end-to-end autonomous driving, particularly when visual observations are degraded by occlusion, motion blur, illumination variation, and sensor noise. Existing Bird's-Eye-View (BEV) approaches provide a unified representation for perception and planning; however, temporal features are commonly aggregated across frames without explicitly accounting for the reliability of individual observations. Consequently, low-quality or inconsistent features may propagate through temporal representations and degrade downstream perception, prediction, and planning. To address this problem, we propose RCT-AD, a Reliability-Aware Context and Temporal BEV Reasoning framework for end-to-end autonomous driving that explicitly models feature reliability and temporal consistency within a shared BEV representation. RCT-AD introduces a Reliable Context Awareness (RCA) module that estimates frame-level reliability using spatial, semantic, temporal, and visual-quality cues, and selectively preserves trustworthy information through a reliability-guided temporal memory mechanism. When the current observation is unreliable, historical high-reliability features are aligned and integrated to construct a more stable temporal BEV representation. A Temporal Trajectory Planner models long-term temporal dependencies and multi-agent interactions, while semantic segmentation and motion-guided detection provide complementary supervision for refining the shared BEV representation. Experiments on the nuScenes benchmark demonstrate consistent improvements across perception, motion prediction, and trajectory planning. RCT-AD achieves 61.5 NDS, 52.9 mAP, and 52.3 mIoU while maintaining competitive computational efficiency. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of reliability-aware temporal BEV reasoning for unified perception, prediction, and planning.
  </details>

- **[Review the Episodes, Refine the Lessons: Simple Memory Evolution for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11509)**  `arXiv:2611.11509`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yifei Yao, Yude Zou, Xing Gao, Guanbo Jiao, Yukai Wang, Jianming Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies of embodied agents based on frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) develop harness engineering around the MLLM, such as harnesses with post-trained policies and recursive self-improvement loops. Yet whether these additions exceed the native capability of the MLLM remains unexplored. We introduce SimplEvo to explore how far an MLLM can improve under direct control when it retains its execution experience in self-written text memories. It requires no weight updates, post-trained policies, or privileged information. The MLLM writes a coordinate-free task memory once from a single demonstration. Under a strict train-test protocol, it then creates a bounded working memory on training episodes only and refines it after each episode with lessons on procedure, completion, and recovery. Both memories stay fixed at test time. On EBench, SimplEvo lifts the success rate (SR) of GPT-6-Astra under direct control from 46.7% to 81.7%, and tops the RoboDojo leaderboard with 69.63% SR and a score of 75.32. Compared with memories that also store past visual frames, SimplEvo reaches a similar SR with text memory alone while using fewer tokens. With its memories fixed, SimplEvo also generalizes to unseen layouts and randomized scene configurations. Moreover, the working memory transfers across models: written by GPT-6-Astra, it can be reused as is by weaker MLLMs and improve their performance without any adaptation of their own.
  </details>

- **[Tactile MNIST: Benchmarking Active Tactile Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06361)**  `arXiv:2506.06361`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tim Schneider, Guillaume Duret, Cristiana de Farias, Roberto Calandra, Liming Chen, Jan Peters_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tactile perception has the potential to significantly enhance dexterous robotic manipulation by providing rich local information that can complement or substitute for other sensory modalities such as vision. However, tactile sensing is an inherently local sensor modality, providing information only at the points of contact. This strict locality necessitates the use of active perception techniques to acquire useful, task-relevant information from manipulated objects or the environment in general. Hence, the agent must actively guide sensors toward regions with more informative or significant features and integrate this information over time to understand a scene or complete a task. While active perception and tactile sensing methods have received significant attention recently, both fields lack standardized benchmarks. To bridge this gap, we introduce the Tactile MNIST Benchmark Suite, an open-source, Gymnasium-compatible benchmark specifically designed for active tactile perception tasks, including classification, localization, pose estimation, and volume estimation. Our benchmark suite offers diverse simulation scenarios, from simple toy environments to complex tactile perception tasks using vision-based tactile sensors. Furthermore, we offer a comprehensive dataset comprising 13,580 synthetic 3D MNIST digit models and 153,600 real-world tactile samples collected from 600 3D-printed digits. Using this dataset, we train a CycleGAN for realistic tactile simulation rendering. By providing standardized protocols and reproducible evaluation frameworks, our benchmark suite facilitates systematic progress in tactile sensing and active perception more broadly.
  </details>

- **[A Benchmark and Layered Evaluation Protocol for Evidence-Grounded Shared Belief Updating in Connected Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11842)**  `arXiv:2611.11842`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Ranulfo Bezerra, Kimitoshi Yamazaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Connected physical systems fuse observations from robots, IoT sensors, and edge-AI modules into a shared account of the world, yet most evaluations score only the final answer or action and cannot tell whether a method attributed the evidence, updated its belief, handled contradictions, and justified its decision. We argue that shared reasoning in Connected Robotics should be evaluated as an evidence-to-belief trajectory, and introduce the Connected Robotics Evidence Graph (CREG) Benchmark to make that trajectory measurable. CREG comprises 350 deterministically generated scenarios across seven regimes (justified revision, belief preservation, relation association, contradiction onset and resolution, stale/delayed evidence, and source reliability), each exposing a normative posterior reference at every evidence prefix, validated by an independent log-odds checker and screened by a blinded expert audit. Nine representation conditions separate relation content from serialization, ordering, and metadata. Evaluating four open-weight instruction models and six transparent fusion baselines yields three findings. First, entity grounding accounts for a substantial part of the weak-model deficit. Second, deleting semantic relations costs 15.3 points of strict belief-state accuracy on relation-dependent scenarios (Holm-corrected p<0.001), but an information-matched structured list outperforms the full graph and shuffling the graph does not hurt: relation content matters where required, while graph serialization confers no consistent advantage. Third, belief accuracy is strongly non-monotonic along the evidence sequence, with mid-trajectory failures that endpoint-only evaluation cannot detect. All scenarios, references, prompts, metrics, and analysis code are released for offline, hardware-free reproduction.
  </details>

- **[UniOMA: Unified Optimal-Transport Multi-Modal Structural Alignment for Robot Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12351)**  `arXiv:2611.12351`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinrui Zu, Mark Hoogendoorn, Kevin Sebastian Luck, Shujian Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving generalizable and well-aligned multimodal representations remains a core challenge in robot learning. While recent approaches have attempted to align modalities by modeling conditional or higher-order statistical dependencies, they do not explicitly consider the structural coherence across modalities. In this work, we propose a novel multimodal alignment method that augments existing contrastive losses with a geometry-aware Gromov--Wasserstein (GW) distance-based regularization. To this end, we encode intra-modal geometry for each modality and reduce cross-modal structural discrepancies through a dynamically learned GW barycenter, thereby encouraging structural consistency in addition to instance-level contrastive alignment. We apply this optimal-transport-based alignment strategy to robot perception tasks involving underexplored modalities such as force and tactile signals, where modality data often exhibit varying sample densities. Extensive experiments on five robotic benchmarks show that our method improves structural alignment and downstream performance over state-of-the-art multimodal contrastive baselines across a wide range of downstream tasks, including robot and environment state prediction. Moreover, our GW-based regularization can be seamlessly integrated into most InfoNCE-like objectives.
  </details>

- **[Embodied First Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12177)**  `arXiv:2611.12177`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Kangrui Wang, Qineng Wang, Changhe Chen, Pingyue Zhang, Chi Gui, Feishi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early successes on agentic robotics point to a potential paradigm shift toward using coding agents to control robots. Existing robotics benchmarks are built around visuomotor policy learning, leaving it open whether coding agents can reliably control diverse robots under physical-world constraints. We therefore introduce the Embodied First Exam, a unified, comprehensive, and agent-native evaluation framework where coding agents can choose their own control approach: direct control, code to control, or train a policy to control. We hope it serves as a first exam for coding agents before they enter the physical world, with 1,156 tasks in 35 suites on 47 robots, including 9 new suites that go beyond pick-and-place to spatial puzzles, dexterous manipulation, multi-robot interaction, dynamic whole-body control, and sim-to-sim engineering. Each task can be run in up to three evaluation settings. The Standard Setting mimics the real world: the agent receives only sensor observations and, by default, cannot reset the environment. Its Realtime variant keeps the environment running while the agent reasons. The Privileged Setting gives the agent access to the full simulator state and unlimited resets, allowing it to search for an oracle solution for training purposes. Success rates in this setting serve as an approximate upper bound. On the 456 core tasks, the strongest of eight agents, GPT-6 Astra in Codex, achieves a success rate of 34% in the Standard Setting, leaving much room for improvement. To see where agents fail, we label the stage at which failed attempts go wrong and compare failures across agents, settings, and stage categories.
  </details>

- **[GeoMind: A Benchmark for Spatial Understanding in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12175)**  `arXiv:2611.12175`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinghe Wang, Xinrui Cao, Duo Wu, Chenghao Gu, Yong Zhong, Linjia Kang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation has advanced rapidly in mapping visual observations and language instructions to actions. However, reliable manipulation in open-world environments requires robust spatial understanding: the ability to identify targets in complex layouts (spatial reasoning) and adapt to scenes that continuously evolve through interaction (spatial memory). Yet existing embodied benchmarks fall short in evaluating spatial understanding: they often introduce strong visual cues that bypass spatial reasoning, and focus on static scenes that offer little insight into an agent's capacity for spatial memory. To address this gap, we introduce GeoMind, a systematic benchmark for structured spatial understanding through tabletop manipulation. GeoMind comprises 54 evaluated task variants organized around spatial reasoning and spatial memory. It also incorporates a scalable pipeline that automates the generation and annotation of task instances with verified targets and executable interactions. Extensive evaluations across diverse embodied agents reveal their consistent weaknesses in spatial understanding. Our findings highlight that these agents struggle to track identities after spatial changes and convert relational or geometric constraints into executable actions. We release the GeoMind benchmark atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Grasp-Taxonomy Conditioning as a Mode Switch for Dexterous Grasping of Climbing Holds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12017)**  `arXiv:2611.12017`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Rumi Loghmani, Abraham George, Amir Barati Farimani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous grasping in robotics relies on distinct hand configurations depending on object geometry. We introduce BetaGrasp, a 3D diffusion policy that conditions a DP3-style point-cloud policy on a four-class grasp label (crimp, sloper, pinch, and jug), running on a Franka arm with a 16-DoF LEAP hand. We also introduce a climbing-hold benchmark: a spring testbed whose ratchet gives a slip-force reading in newtons. In our analysis the policy's point-cloud encoder did not separate the four holds, so the scan alone gave it little to choose a grasp from. Without the label (a DP3-style baseline), simulated rollouts from the robot's reset pose all end in a jug grasp, whatever the hold (25% correct grasp type, versus 94% for BetaGrasp). The label works as a switch for which grasp the policy performs. On 80 paired hardware trials, BetaGrasp raises average slip force from 9.6 N to 14.3 N, and grasps slipping fully off the hold drop from 44% of trials to 10%.
  </details>

- **[Decoupled Asynchronous System for Humanoids (DASH): Latency-Agnostic Scaling of Vision-Language-Action Models for Humanoid Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11854)**  `arXiv:2611.11854`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Bingzhang Wang, Nathan Zhao, Jialeng Ni, Jiajin Zheng, Yin Zhang, Kehua Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models pair a vision-language backbone with an action head, but in closed-loop control the backbone sits on the control-critical path: while it computes, the robot keeps moving, so each executed action is conditioned on an increasingly stale observation. A larger backbone therefore directly blocks reactivity, especially in humanoid dexterous manipulation. We present DASH, a Decoupled Asynchronous System for Humanoids that removes this coupling. DASH splits the policy into a slow vision-language model (System 2) and a fast flow-matching action head (System 1) that run as concurrent processes over the LCM communication protocol, so the control rate is set by the action head alone, independent of backbone size. The action head fuses stale semantics from a frozen backbone with fresh vision from its own ResNet encoder, desynchronized training exposes it to the staleness it meets at deployment, and an adaptive temporal aggregator aligns overlapping action chunks under variable inference latency. On an asynchronous RoboTwin benchmark that charges inference latency in simulated time, DASH holds 20 Hz control rate from 0.8B to 8B backbones while the synchronous rate falls from 9.7 to 4.1 Hz, with higher success at every size. On a real humanoid with dexterous hands, DASH achieves clearly higher pick-and-place success than the synchronous pipeline.
  </details>

- **[HolidayFactory 1.0: Simulating, Generating, and Evaluating Industrial Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11789)**  `arXiv:2611.11789`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Holiday Robotics, Jeongmin Lee, Taehyeon Kim, Dohyun Jang, Minji Lee, Seonghun Oh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots are typically evaluated in episodes that begin from a reset, while industrial work consists of repeated jobs whose outcomes affect subsequent operations. Evaluating such work at scale requires simulation of task-critical physical interactions, construction of industrial environments, and measurements of performance as scene state persists. We present HOLIDAYFACTORY 1.0, a framework for simulating, generating, and evaluating industrial robot work. Given a human-specified workplace and task, the framework defines a world pairing a scenario of scene variants and process logic with an evaluation defining a job, metrics, and a rubric. HOLIDAYFACTORY 1.0 comprises three components. HOLIDAY-SIM extends Newton's SDF-based hydroelastic contact with additional constitutive laws and a semi-implicit primal-dual solver, couples stateful actuator dynamics to articulated motion, and derives tactile and joint observations from simulated states. Agentic world construction generates simulation-ready assets and scene variants using geometric and interaction checks, and proposes task-conditioned evaluations for human confirmation and executable checks. 24/7-BENCH introduces seven industrial tasks in which jobs are evaluated without resetting the scene, measuring completed, failed, and unfinished jobs, throughput, latency, and motor energy. Controlled studies characterize the proposed contact, actuator, and tactile models and examine the effectiveness of verification-guided world construction. Experiments on scene and evaluation generation further reveal the limitations of model critics and the difficulty of ensuring that generated evaluations faithfully capture task requirements. We examine job-level evaluations with scripted trajectories (e.g., code-as-policy) but have not yet compared policies over persistent runs.
  </details>

- **[REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10748)**  `arXiv:2611.10748`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhihong Cui, Hengyu Liu, Haoran Tang, Shijun Liu, Amir Taherkordi, Tor Skeie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses -- runtime scaffolds that wrap an LLM's reasoning loop with tool dispatch, verification, and persistent memory -- have become the standard substrate for deploying LLMs as autonomous agents, as demonstrated by Claude Code and Codex in software engineering. In trajectory planning, however, this paradigm has not taken hold: existing LLM-based trajectory planning methods either keep the LLM in the decision loop, exceeding the millisecond-level control budget, or offload it to offline rules or training-time supervision -- leaving the runtime planner non-evolving. Intermediate hybrids still lack the verification-and-consolidation loop that makes harnesses reliable. We ask: what runtime harness would let an LLM planner meet real-time constraints while self-evolving into a deployable one? We propose REINS, a harness built on three LLM-planner couplings: LLM outputs are (i) grounded to a learned variable-level dynamics model, (ii) verified via calibrated predicates and forward rollout, and (iii) consolidated into an indexed rule memory that serves future scenes in O(log n) without re-invoking the LLM. The harness closes a self-evolution loop: familiar scenes resolve by memory lookup; novel scenes invoke the LLM, whose verified outputs enrich the memory. Without modifying LLM weights, REINS attains 98%+ compliance, nearly 50% collision reduction, and 1.9-4.6 ms latency on simulated and real-world benchmarks at the tactical decision layer, with LLM invocation rate monotonically declining as memory matures. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Frequency-Aware Flow Matching for Continuous and Consistent Robotic Action Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20135)**  `arXiv:2606.20135`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianing Guo, Fangzheng Chen, Zihao Mao, Wong Lik Hang Kenny, Zhenhong Wu, Yu Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow matching has emerged as a standard paradigm for robotic manipulation owing to its strong expressive power for modelling complex, multimodal action distributions, alongside similar approaches like diffusion policy. However, existing methods rely on discretized action chunks, making them brittle to demonstrations collected at heterogeneous control frequencies and prone to temporally inconsistent actions that degrade control stability. In this paper, we propose Frequency-Aware Flow Matching (FAFM), which outputs continuous, temporally consistent actions. To handle heterogeneous frequency input, we transform discrete action sequences into the frequency domain with the discrete cosine transform (DCT), perform flow matching over the resulting coefficients, and reconstruct continuous actions via cosine basis expansion. To generate temporally consistent actions, we regularize the first-order temporal derivative to promote smooth actions. This corresponds to a Sobolev-type constraint that suppresses high-frequency errors and discourages abrupt action changes. Our FAFM is simple, introduces no additional network parameters and applies to standalone flow-matching policies and vision-language action models. Across synthetic toy benchmark, obstacle avoidance, LapGym, and LIBERO, FAFM improves success rates, multimodal expressivity, motion smoothness, convergence speed, robustness to mechanical bias and mixed-frequency input. These gains are consistent when deployed on a real-world Franka robot. Code available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EffiNav: Fusing Depth and Vision-Language for Efficient Object Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18634)**  `arXiv:2606.18634`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zecheng Yin, Benedict Jun Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To locate a target object while exploring the unknown environment is a fundamental capability for autonomous agents, with applications ranging from search-and-rescue to field robots. A simplified version of such task is Object Goal Navigation (ObjNav). In ObjNav, successful arrival at the target object provides a basic measure of performance; however, the efficiency of the navigation trajectory is equally important, as it indicates how intelligently the agent explores and how much time remains for subsequent tasks. In unknown environments, the key to efficient navigation lies in deciding where to explore next. While many prior works aim to address this core challenge and achieved promising performance in certain settings, recent training-based models and non-training frameworks still suffer from generalization and efficiency issues respectively, which in the worst cases can lead to excessive exploration of already-visited areas or redundant back-and-forth motion. We evaluate EffiNav on two widely used simulation benchmarks Habitat Matterport 3D (HM3D) and Open-Vocabulary Object goal Navigation (OVON), and further validate its effectiveness on physical robots in real-world settings. We conduct failure analysis on massive simulation episodes. With minimal modification, we also extend EffiNav to a memory-augmented ObjNav task on the GOAT-BENCH dataset, demonstrating its adaptability beyond standard ObjNav settings. Across two standard metrics--Success Rate (SR) and Success weighted by Path Length (SPL), EffiNav matches or outperforms recent baselines, reflecting its efficiency, robustness, and practical applicability. Recognizing the different emphases of the two datasets, the performances reveals this framework is more balanced and generalizable for efficient ObjNav.
  </details>

- **[RoboWits: Unexpected Challenges for Robotic Creative Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30326)**  `arXiv:2605.30326`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Chunru Lin, Hongxin Zhang, Fenghao Yu, Zhehuan Chen, Thomas L. Griffiths, Yejin Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The ability to reason, adapt, and creatively solve problems under unexpected challenges is essential for robots operating in real-world environments. However, current robotic benchmarks primarily emphasize skill-level execution and provide limited insight into such cognitive reasoning capabilities. We introduce RoboWits, a bi-manual robotic benchmark designed to systematically evaluate cognitive reasoning, creative tool use, and robustness to unexpected conditions. To enable scalable construction of high-quality reasoning-centric unexpected scenarios, we propose an automated task generation pipeline formulated as a multi-agent cooperative framework, comprising agents for seed task generation and verification, metric generation, scene generation, and task mutation. Using the pipeline, we curated 30 diverse seed tasks and 200 tasks with mutations and graded difficulty across geometry, material, and assembly-based reasoning. We benchmark popular robot policies, pre-trained VLAs, a zero-shot policy built on a frontier VLM, and oracle-state planners.Our results reveal a significant performance gap: while pre-trained VLAs exhibit preliminary success on seed tasks after fine-tuning, they struggle to perform on mutated tasks, implying their brittleness in manipulation tasks requiring reasoning, strategy adaptation, and robustness to deceptive or constrained environments. A zero-shot policy built on GPT-6 Astra outperforms all fine-tuned VLAs, yet still loses about a fifth of its success on mutated tasks, showing that stronger foundation models alone do not close the robustness gap. See our project pagethis https URLfor videos.
  </details>

- **[Simulating Vein Compressibility for Ultrasound-Guided Femoral Access: Speed, Accuracy, and Phantom Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12818)**  `arXiv:2611.12818`  `cs.RO`  
  _Amit Manicka, Laurent Coulon, Darwin Ang, Michael Loushin, Timothy M. Kowalewski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In ultrasound-guided femoral access, probe pressure deforms the tissue and changes the vascular anatomy a robot observes. A simulator must reproduce these mechanics fast enough for robotic interaction, yet the computational effort needed for a clinically meaningful signal like vein collapse before artery is unclear. We test the feasibility of a physics-based simulation in NVIDIA's Isaac Sim for Healthcare, with Stable Neo-Hookean tissue solved by Vertex Block Descent on one GPU. A simulation of a low-fidelity cylindrical benchtop phantom with known geometry and mechanical properties, pressed by a robot, predicted its artery within 5 percentage points at every depth. It matched the vein within 3 points through 11 mm of a 25.4 mm press and followed the collapse behavior of the low-fidelity physical phantom. Across element size, time step, and solver iterations, wall clock time per frame scaled linearly with solver work, but accuracy did not improve with more work. The chosen setting reduced solver time per frame by a factor of 976, from 29,700 ms to 30.4 ms, while remaining within 1.03 points of the finest reference setting. The physics solver alone took 30.4 ms per frame, while the full stack, including ultrasound and graphics rendering, took 56.4 ms per frame. Finally, when seeded with realistic literature-based mechanical properties, the digital leg model compressed the vein far more than the artery at every setting, reproducing the expected clinical signal of vein collapse before artery, which is used to distinguish them. In total, these results suggest that the NVIDIA Isaac Sim platform may be suitable for augmenting reinforcement learning training or online simulation for autonomous femoral access with ultrasound.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Real-World Robotic Manipulation through Stochastic Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12804)**  `arXiv:2611.12804`  `cs.RO`  
  _Elias Gläßer, Gülhan Şikaroğlu, Pavlo Kupyn, Marc Pollefeys, Zuria Bauer, Hermann Blum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A challenging bottleneck in manipulation research is reproducibility and comparability of methods in real-world environments. Since manipulation is an interactive task, it cannot be evaluated on a static dataset of observations. Benchmarking in simulation is a common remedy, but cannot cover the whole complexity of real-world tests. This paper proposes a new paradigm of stochastic challenges. Like the well-known YCB objects, it is based on reproducible real-world scenes and therefore allows system-level innovation and comparability between different platforms. Due to their stochastic nature, our challenges however, mimic the rigor and surprise of hidden test sets. To this end, we show how different robots in different labs can be compared based on their success rate over different random task samples, if these samples come from the same distribution. We propose stochastic challenges as a general paradigm for robot benchmarking and implement this paradigm in a novel benchmark for mobile manipulation, on which we compare two robots tested in two different labs.
  </details>

- **[Correctness Boundaries of Typed Plans for Natural-Language Robot Fleet Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12654)**  `arXiv:2611.12654`  `cs.RO`  
  _Artem Tuzov, Dmitrii Vizitei, Evgenii Shlomov, Senya Belykh, Anastasia Malakhova, Alexander Maloletov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We examine what plan validation and execution reports establish in natural-language robot fleet control. Commands are traced through typed plans, deterministic corrections, fleet coordination and outcome checks in a three-level Robot Operating System 2 implementation with twenty simulated robots. An offline benchmark of 44 cases checks plan structure and selected command requirements. Across five local configurations without a response schema, 70.0-87.3% of responses pass. All runs in a selected 18-run simulation series meet their pose-based task conditions. This series includes two successful repeats that replaced incomplete runs after a software change. Saved plans and logs document duplicate pickup assignments and goal adjustments within the original task zones. Separate formation traces show a structurally accepted plan with the wrong formation shape and a rejected route, and an arrival report that omitted a requested robot after replanning. Carry completion records pickup and subsequent drop-off zone presence for the same robot. The cases distinguish changes to executable actions from changes to the requirements covered by a completion report. They motivate checking the effective assignments, goals and reported outcome against the original command.
  </details>

- **[Action Representation for Spatial Generalization in Robot World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11869)**  `arXiv:2611.11869`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuhang Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial generalization in robot world models depends on whether action representations capture the physical relationships that remain valid across changing spatial configurations. Yet different coordinates can describe the same interaction, whereas similar motions can produce different outcomes when local physical conditions change. How these distinctions affect learned prediction remains insufficiently understood. This study evaluates matched action representations defined in world, object, and surface coordinate frames using a controlled robotic peeling benchmark. Global pose and contact relocation experiments reveal a cost associated with limited training data when equivalent interactions receive different spatial descriptions. Removing information about peel thickness creates an irreducible ambiguity because identical predictive inputs can correspond to different material removal outcomes. Coordinates defined relative to the surface improve consequence prediction and counterfactual action selection under accurate local observations, but learned predictors remain vulnerable to uncertainty in the coordinate frame. Joint centering of the surface and tool removes sensitivity to translation of their common origin, whereas incorrect contact geometry and actual execution deviations introduce different failure mechanisms. These results show that effective spatial generalization depends on preserving the relationships between actions and states that determine physical consequences, rather than simply minimizing coordinate variation. This perspective provides a principled basis for designing robot world models that reuse physical knowledge across spatial configurations while retaining the distinctions essential to prediction and planning.
  </details>

- **[PAIR: Proprioception-Aligned Image-Ray Representations for Viewpoint-Robust Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11798)**  `arXiv:2611.11798`  `cs.RO`  
  _Sung-Wook Lee, Xuhui Kang, Dylan Losey, Yen-Ling Kuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While imitation learning has made substantial progress toward generalizable robot manipulation through scaling, viewpoint robustness remains a major bottleneck. Visuomotor policies rely heavily on visual inputs, yet images provide an inherently ambiguous signal under viewpoint change: similar scenes may appear markedly different across viewpoints, while distinct states may share certain visual overlaps. A policy must therefore infer not only the task-relevant state, but also how it is visually projected under a particular camera configuration. This entanglement of viewpoint-dependent appearance and task-relevant state can make learning inefficient and limit generalization to unseen viewpoints. Recent work has begun to address this challenge through camera-ray conditioning and representation learning using geometric cues to induce invariance. However, providing camera information as additional context does not by itself ground visual representations in how the task scene is visually projected, while geometric alignment without camera context can introduce spurious correspondences by ignoring viewpoint-dependent projection. We propose PAIR, a contrastive learning framework that aligns joint image-ray representations using robot proprioception. PAIR encourages observations with similar robot states to remain close in representation space across viewpoints, providing a structured grounding signal for learning viewpoint-robust features. PAIR is a highly adaptable framework: it can be used to train Diffusion Policy (DP) or post-train vision-language-action (VLA) models. In simulated and real-world manipulation benchmarks with camera variation, PAIR consistently outperforms the behavior cloning baseline without ray conditioning by 12.7% and 26.7% points, respectively, demonstrating stronger generalization to unseen viewpoints. Project website:this http URL.
  </details>

- **[RoboMoCap: A Large-Scale Whole-Body Motion Capture Dataset with Egocentric Vision for Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10994)**  `arXiv:2611.10994`  `cs.RO`  
  _Mayur Deshmukh, Aaron Tung, Junyi Li, Nikash Bhardwaj, Ademi Adeniji, Vincent Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid robots must coordinate locomotion and manipulation to navigate complex environments and perform everyday tasks. Learning such behaviours from human demonstrations requires accurate whole-body motion paired with egocentric vision, particularly for lower-body movements and foot placements that are frequently invisible from a first-person perspective. However, existing egocentric datasets often lack high-fidelity optical motion capture or systematic coverage of diverse loco-manipulation behaviours. To address this gap, we introduce RoboMoCap, a large-scale multimodal dataset combining egocentric vision with marker-based optical motion capture, specifically designed for locomotion-centric humanoid learning. RoboMoCap comprises over 200 hours of recordings from 14 participants, featuring six head-mounted cameras, wrist-mounted visual and inertial sensing, and optical full-body motion capture at 120 Hz. The dataset systematically covers diverse locomotion primitives, dynamic whole-body movements, and loco-manipulation tasks, including variable-speed walking, turning, constrained navigation, terrain traversal, object transportation, and manipulation during locomotion. It provides temporally aligned multimodal observations and metric whole-body motion representations, enabling the study of human movement beyond directly observable body regions. We systematically evaluate existing methods on three benchmark tasks: egocentric full-body motion reconstruction, foot contact and placement estimation, and human-to-humanoid motion retargeting. By pairing egocentric vision with high-fidelity human motion across diverse locomotion and interaction scenarios, RoboMoCap provides a foundation for egocentric motion understanding and humanoid learning from human demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[BAT-WAM: Bridging Action and Touch in World-Action Models for Dexterous Hands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10967)**  `arXiv:2611.10967`  `cs.RO`  
  _Dong Fang, Feixuan Huang, Yuanxin Zhong, Yuxiang Gao, Qi Song, Yunlong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous manipulation with world action models (WAMs) relies on hand configuration and contact information, yet joint-state and whole-hand tactile observations may be partially or entirely missing. RGB images provide scene context and hand-object geometry but are insufficient to reliably recover these missing modalities. We further observe a correlation between hand state and tactile information. Motivated by this observation, we introduce BAT-WAM, a framework that bridges visual, joint-state, and tactile representations for WAMs. BAT-WAM learns separate codebooks and decoders for the tactile and action streams. An RGB-conditioned continuous latent bridge connects the two streams for bidirectional learning, while visual information serves as continuous context. Under the HTBench evaluation protocol, BAT-WAM reduces the root mean squared error (RMSE) of tactile reconstruction by 34.73% under fully masked conditions and 38.84% under partially masked conditions, relative to an image-conditioned HandTouch checkpoint. For future tactile prediction conditioned on target-time RGB and historical actions, it reduces mean RMSE by 85.56% relative to OpenWAM with HandTouch. Real-robot experiments further show that BAT-WAM consistently outperforms related WAM baselines in dexterous manipulation. Generalization experiments on unseen objects and scenes also demonstrate substantial improvements over baseline models. These findings support joint modeling of visual context, hand state, and touch as a representation approach for multimodal WAMs.
  </details>

- **[Burden, Bounty, or Bliss? Muscle-Aware Retargeting and Null-Space Exploration for Whole-Body Musculoskeletal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10682)**  `arXiv:2611.10682`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ruiyang Kong, Takeshi Oishi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning whole-body musculoskeletal control from human motion is important for developing human-like robots, assistive devices, and rehabilitation systems. This task presents two main challenges, namely reference trajectories that are kinematically plausible but difficult to reproduce through muscle-driven dynamics, and a highly redundant 700-dimensional action space in which unstructured exploration can easily disrupt the target motion. In this paper, we propose a motion-to-policy pipeline that exploits muscle redundancy in two complementary stages. First, muscle-aware retargeting (MAR) performs bounded adaptation of kinematic trajectories subject to hard semantic, anatomical, and contact constraints. We motivate the constraint bounds by analyzing how reference-trajectory artifacts affect muscle-driven tracking. Second, null-space exploration (NSE) estimates a one-control-frame mapping from muscle excitations to generalized forces and injects bounded, anatomically structured perturbations along its null space into the actions of a value-guided flow policy. On a 24-clip motion-capture benchmark, MAR and NSE together solve every reference, reach the full horizon with half the samples of the baseline pipeline, and lower joint-center error by 44% without late collapse. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first end-to-end pipeline that transforms monocular video into a trained whole-body imitation policy controlling 700 muscles.
  </details>

- **[Look Only Where You Plan for Real-Time End-to-End Urban Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10264)**  `arXiv:2611.10264`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haohua Que, Tianle Zhu, Qianyi Wu, Zhipeng Bao, Handong Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense bird's-eye-view (BEV) construction accounts for 60% of the parameters in a strong recent driving planner. This paper presents LOWP, a planner that looks only where it plans: two sets of sixteen learnable queries read the cameras through the known calibration, one by sampling image features at projected 3D anchors and one by attending to features labeled with their viewing rays, so no dense BEV is built and no object or map is supervised. Evaluations on open-loop nuScenes, pseudo-simulated NAVSIM and closed-loop Bench2Drive show competitive planning with a compact representation. On nuScenes, average L2 changes from 0.302 to 0.296 m with 53% of the dense counterpart's parameters and 1.7 times its speed on the same GPU; the anchor-based reader performs better on turns than the global reader. On NAVSIM, scoring the planner's own trajectories alongside a vocabulary gives 85.9 PDMS without retraining. The closed-loop control configuration scores 65.5 on 218 routes, with a brake-induced standstill failure whose release rule has an inconclusive aggregate benefit. We distinguish measured latency from hardware-transferred estimates, which do not establish a common-hardware ranking.
  </details>

- **[MemTransfer: Benchmarking Memory Beyond Matched Experience in Embodied Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32313)**  `arXiv:2609.32313`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haiming Tang, Xianjie Dai, Gujie Shao, Zuyi Guo, Jingguang Li, Kailang Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory lets an embodied agent reuse past experience, yet retaining useful information does not ensure that the agent can apply it when conditions change. We present MemTransfer, a benchmark comparing six memory representations, a working-memory baseline and five representations of past experience, under a shared frozen vision-language-model policy. It comprises 100 navigation cases across ten task types in a simulated warehouse, with expert demonstrations supplying the history. Three comparisons vary the starting pose, route availability, and amount and task relevance of history. With one demonstration per task, Full-context and Episodic memory reach 95.3% and 100.0% success at the original demonstration start, but lose 48-49 percentage points at a new test start. Summary changes little between these two test starts, yet with four demonstrations per task it retains a smaller fraction of its unchanged-route success after blocking (39.3%) than Working memory (44.8%) or the two trajectory memories (56-58%). At the new test start, increasing from one to four relevant demonstrations raises Episodic success by 14.3 percentage points, while the other evaluated representations gain no more than 1.3 percentage points. Replacing half of the relevant histories with other-task experience lowers success for both trajectory memories. These results show that robustness to one kind of mismatch does not imply robustness to another, motivating evaluation of both stored information and its use at decision time.
  </details>

- **[MineXplore: An Open-Source Reinforcement Learning Exploration Benchmark for GNSS-Denied Underground Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04569)**  `arXiv:2606.04569`  `cs.RO`  
  _Abhishek Seetharamu, Badrikanath Praharaj, Sreeram MV_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Underground mines present extreme conditions for autonomous robot navigation: GPS is denied, lighting is degraded, and tunnel topology is loop-rich and non-convex. Simulation benchmarks grounded in real production-mine geometry and compatible with GPU-accelerated learning pipelines do not yet exist in the open-source ecosystem. We present MineXplore, an open-source MuJoCo-based navigation benchmark derived from the Leung et al. 2017 Chilean underground copper mine dataset. The environment reconstructs a 104,423 sq.m tunnel network through an six-stage contour-to-MJCF pipeline incorporating octagonal wall cross-sections, LiDAR-sourced jagged wall geometry, three terrain friction zones, a global 5 degree incline, and periodic spot lighting. Geometric fidelity is validated at an Intersection over Union (IoU) of 0.9538 against the source survey map, and surface texture similarity scores 79.4% across six structural dimensions. A single-agent PPO baseline trained via RLlib across five independent random seeds achieves a best rolling coverage of 88.89% (3 of 5 seeds reaching the 90% coverage target), confirming that MineXplore supports stable and reproducible policy learning under realistic underground sensing and topology.
  </details>

- **[RSLCPP -- Deterministic Simulations Using ROS 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07052)**  `arXiv:2601.07052`  `cs.RO`  
  _Simon Sagmeister, Marcel Weinmann, Phillip Pitschi, Markus Lienkamp_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation is crucial in real-world robotics, offering safe, scalable, and efficient environments for developing a variety of robotic applications. While the Robot Operating System (ROS) has been widely adopted as the backbone of these robotic applications in both academia and industry, its asynchronous, multi-process design complicates reproducibility, especially across varying hardware platforms. Deterministic callback execution cannot be guaranteed when computation times and communication delays vary. This lack of reproducibility complicates scientific benchmarking and continuous integration, where consistent results are essential. To address this, we present a methodology to create deterministic simulations using ROS 2 nodes. Our ROS Simulation Library for C++ (RSLCPP) implements this approach, enabling existing nodes to be combined into a simulation routine that yields reproducible results usually without requiring any source code changes. We demonstrate that our approach produces identical results across various CPUs and architectures when testing both a synthetic benchmark and a real-world robotics system. RSLCPP is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FUSE-PnPL: Flexible, Unified, Statistically Consistent, and Asymptotically Efficient Camera Pose Estimation from Points and Lines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.16151)**  `arXiv:2407.16151`  `cs.RO`  
  _Guangyang Zeng, Yuan Shen, Yiguo Peng, Guodong Shi, Biqiang Mu, Junfeng Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Camera pose estimation from 3D-2D correspondences is fundamental to robotics. Three practical considerations motivate this work. First, point and line features provide complementary geometric information, motivating a unified estimator. Second, the number and composition of available features vary across scenarios, requiring support for minimal and arbitrary non-minimal configurations. Third, modern visual front ends extract abundant features from individual images, enabling highly accurate pose estimation. We propose FUSE-PnPL, a flexible and unified camera pose estimator that is statistically consistent and asymptotically efficient. Specifically, we represent each line using two distinct points and adopt a unified image-space noise model for point and line measurements. By employing Plücker coordinates for line parameterization, we explicitly characterize the propagation of image endpoint noise into line residuals and formulate a joint maximum likelihood (ML) estimation problem for combined point and line measurements. To efficiently solve this problem, FUSE-PnPL employs a two-stage estimation scheme. First, noise variance estimation and bias elimination yield a consistent initial pose estimate. A single Gauss-Newton iteration then refines this estimate, achieving asymptotic efficiency as its mean squared error asymptotically attains the Cramér-Rao lower bound. Moreover, the proposed estimator has linear computational complexity with respect to the number of correspondences, making it suitable for high-precision and real-time robot pose estimation. Extensive experiments on synthetic data and real-world benchmarks validate the theoretical properties of FUSE-PnPL and demonstrate its advantages in both static global localization and dynamic SLAM systems.
  </details>

- **[Deterministic Narrative Gravity: Bridging Theory of Mind and Historical Simulation through ABM-LLM Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11212)**  `arXiv:2611.11212`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Alicia Colmenero-Fernández, Lidia Ortega Alvarado_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historical social simulation faces a fundamental reproducibility challenge: the stochastic nature of large language models (LLMs) introduces a digital butterfly effect that prevents exact replication across multi-turn runs. Furthermore, the inability to control low-level server parameters in commercial LLM hosting environments severely limits reproducibility. To overcome this opacity, we present a deterministic multi-agent framework coupling LLMs to an Agent-Based Modelling (ABM) pipeline grounded in Theory of Mind (ToM) and Cognitive Archaeology, and validate exact artifact identity within local, pinned execution environments using SHA-256 hashing. Run locally at temperature zero with clean per-call model reloads, the system injects differentiated cognitive states per turn through dissonance-driven behavioural modes and gated internal voices. Evaluating seven model configurations spanning 4B to 31B parameters against an ablated baseline reveals clear operational boundaries. Small models ($\leq$8B) suffer from context capacity overload, producing repetitive, specular dialogue loops. However, at $\geq$12B parameters, the ABM scaffolding breaks the monotonous response patterns seen in ablation. Crucially, these scaffolded models exhibit spontaneous diegetic expansion: they coherently expand scenario lore through tactically motivated, anachronism-free details rather than escalating into aggression. By resisting the structural pull toward premature scene closure, scaffolded sessions sustain an open terminal Narrative Vector ($V_N = \mathrm{Open}$ in 12/21 overall; 9/9 in official $\geq$12B configurations) whereas unscaffolded ablation never does (0/21). This framework provides a transparent, auditable benchmark architecture for virtual archaeology, with the full pipeline released as open-source software to enable independent replication.
  </details>

- **[MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01728)**  `arXiv:2611.01728`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.
  </details>

- **[LAMaS: A Modular Platform for Learning Low-Latency Multi-Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10560)**  `arXiv:2601.10560`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xi Shi, Mengxin Zheng, Qian Lou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In a multi-agent system (MAS), removing an expensive agent call can save tokens without making the answer arrive sooner: latency depends on the longest chain of dependent calls. We present Latency-Aware Multi-agent System (LAMaS), a modular platform for learning low-latency orchestration. A framework can send a query to LAMaS and receive an answer and execution trace, or keep its own graph builder and use the platform's latency-optimized orchestration layer. During training, the layer gives operators on the critical path the greatest latency penalty while targeting a floor on expected accuracy. During inference, a lightweight controller uses partial results to decide whether to skip work that has yet to start. Across four benchmarks, LAMaS reduces end-to-end latency by over 50% relative to the evaluated learning-based MAS baselines while maintaining competitive or better accuracy. GDesigner and AgentDropout retain their graph builders and train the controller on their own traces; both achieve lower latency across the same benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[AIM: Adaptive Interaction in Multi-Agent Debate for Multimodal LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08950)**  `arXiv:2611.08950`  `cs.MA`  
  _Wei Fan, JinYi Yoon, Bo Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Agent Debate (MAD) improves reasoning on multimodal tasks, but its accuracy and token efficiency depend on how agents interact during debate. A largely overlooked design choice is which interaction modalities agents use to exchange evidence. Existing methods rely on fixed text- or graph-based interaction, which fails to convey fine-grained multimodal evidence and thus degrades accuracy while inflating token cost due to verbose descriptions. To address this, we propose Adaptive Interaction in Multi-Agent Debate (AIM), an adaptive routing framework that dynamically selects the optimal modality combination for inter-agent interaction in multimodal MAD. Beyond text and graph, AIM introduces task-dependent modality interaction, where agents exchange targeted regions of interest from the task-specific multimodal input, such as image regions, audio segments, or spatio-temporal video clips. To determine the best combination for each instance, AIM first generates a structured baseline response and extracts interpretable routing features that capture which evidence the instance requires, how confident the baseline agent is, and how much new evidence each modality would add beyond the baseline response. A lightweight router then uses these routing features to select a modality combination that avoids both under-fusion (i.e., agents miss decisive evidence) and over-fusion (i.e., redundant modalities inflate token cost) risks. Across eight benchmarks spanning three multimodal task families, AIM achieves the highest accuracy. Combining modalities improves accuracy by up to 6.4% over the best single-modality baseline, and adapting the combination per instance adds up to 4.2% over the most accurate fixed combination of each benchmark with up to 53.4% fewer tokens.
  </details>

- **[FlowMAS: Learning Multi-Agent Workflow Topology via Information-guided Generative Flow Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37151)**  `arXiv:2609.37151`  `cs.MA`  
  _Haitao Wang, Chenjing Liang, Haipeng Zhang, Jiawei Hu, Sicheng Wang, Songzhu Mei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated multi-agent systems offer clear advantages over manually designed ones in scalability and adaptability, but existing workflow topology methods still face important limitations. Search-based methods are often computationally expensive, textual-gradient-based methods rely on coarse-grained feedback, and existing generation-based methods are not well suited to discrete workflow topologies with complex dependencies. To address these limitations, we propose FlowMAS, a multi-agent workflow topology method based on Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets). FlowMAS models workflow generation as reward-guided flow over the topology space and introduces three components: a GFlowNet-based topology generation backbone, a curiosity-driven module for structure-aware exploration, and an information-guided optimization module for evaluating intermediate topologies. Concretely, the curiosity-driven module encourages exploration of structurally novel workflows, while the information-guided module measures both the information contribution and the communication efficiency of different operators to favor more informative and effective collaboration patterns. Experiments on six benchmark datasets with three LLM backbones show that FlowMAS consistently outperforms multiple baselines.
  </details>

- **[Simulation-aided Reinforcement Learning with Control Variates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12643)**  `arXiv:2611.12643`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Omer Hemo, Ido Sefi, Mirco Mutti, Aviv Tamar, Kfir Y. Levy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms often rely on data collected from a target environment, which can be expensive and noisy. Although simulators can generate large amounts of additional data, standard simulation mixing approaches, which directly combine simulated and real experience, can degrade performance due to simulator inaccuracies, especially when they bias the solution away from the optimal policy. We propose a conservative simulation-aided learning method based on control variates, where simulated data is used solely to reduce variance in the learning process rather than directly shaping the learning target. We develop our approach for Policy-Evaluation, which is a fundamental building block of RL algorithms. Then we combine it within the actor-critic framework towards policy improvement, as well as extend it towards offline RL scenarios. Experiments on multiple MuJoCo benchmark tasks demonstrate improvements over standard simulation mixing approaches, highlighting control variates as an effective tool for simulation-aided reinforcement learning.
  </details>

- **[Learning When to Think: Adaptive Internal Computation for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10822)**  `arXiv:2611.10822`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Rohit Kumar Salla, Simon Stepputtis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Iterative neural policies usually spend the same computation on every decision, although the benefit of additional processing varies across states. We introduce Adaptive Internal Computation (AIC), an iterative policy that learns when to stop refining its latent representation and act. In AIC, we train action selection and stopping jointly, an expected-depth penalty values computation and intermediate value predictions provide auxiliary supervision. We show that when only some states benefit from further refinement, an ideal state-dependent allocator outperforms every state-independent stopping schedule at the same expected depth. With at most eight refinement steps per decision, AIC executes 3.3 steps on average across twelve benchmark tasks and lowers per-decision latency by 31--44% relative to a fixed eight-step policy on four control and robotics tasks. Its mean reward exceeds the best fixed-depth policy on seven tasks and trails it on four. Ablating adaptive stopping and value supervision separately shows that stopping accounts for most of the reward gain on PickCube and AntMaze, where it also executes more refinement steps and that supervision accounts for most of it on RareEvent.
  </details>

- **[Implicit Credit Learning for Sparse-Reward Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21828)**  `arXiv:2509.21828`  `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Viet Bui, Tien Mai, Hong Thanh Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a cooperative team receives feedback only at the end of an episode, a single outcome must guide many agents' earlier actions. Learning a dense team reward helps, but its decomposition does not automatically give each agent a useful policy update. We introduce IMAP, an online method that fits a shared Q/V reward model by comparing completed episodes according to their observed terminal outcomes. Local Q/V residuals provide agent-specific rewards; the same normalized weights combine them for a centralized critic and scale local advantages for decentralized PPO actors. We characterize when these local updates follow the learned team objective. With full returns and frozen reward heads, a sharp bound relates their gradient error to variation in future Q/V compatibility; an exact identity identifies additional continuation terms for general GAE. In experiments with terminal-only feedback, IMAP's local-actor configuration attains the highest reported mean among the compared methods on all nine SMACv2 scenarios and four MAMuJoCo tasks, and on two of three SMACLite scenarios. Controlled relay interventions confirm the predicted changes in actor credit while holding the learned team reward fixed. The gradient result concerns the fitted objective; benchmark returns measure performance on the original task outcomes.
  </details>

- **[How Many Candidates Does a One-Step World Model Need? A Random-Codebook Account of Nearest-of-m Training on Frozen DINO Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11887)**  `arXiv:2611.11887`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yong Hwi Song, Keon Myung Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One-step stochastic world models avoid the mode averaging of deterministic feature predictors without the tens of network evaluations per step that diffusion and flow matching require. The simplest such objective is nearest-of-m matching (conditional implicit maximum likelihood estimation, IMLE): draw m candidate futures and pull only the one closest to the observed outcome. Recent work conjectures that a small m suffices for feature-space world models and that a strictly proper energy score should replace the hard minimum. We test both claims with one-step world models on frozen DINOv2/v3 patch tokens, using benchmarks whose futures can be enumerated. Under mode-like uncertainty, nearest-of-m with m=16 covers the enumerated futures better than 50-step flow matching (at about 1/30 of its latency), one-step flow generators and an energy-score generator at matched parameter counts, and its rollouts stay on the token manifold where flow rollouts drift. One quantity governs the choice of m: the nearest-of-m objective is the expected distortion of a random codebook of m draws from the generator. This view yields three predictions, recorded before the corresponding runs: (i) memorization of the realized outcome in contexts that never repeat, whose strength is governed by the codebook's miss distortion I(m) rather than its miss probability, and which an energy-score generator does not escape by raising m; (ii) a flattening of mode probabilities with m, matching a known optimum within 0.01; and (iii) under-dispersion on continuous high-dimensional outcomes, which bounds the method on real robot video. Per-step matching on self-rollouts cuts long-horizon error by a third on a stochastic Push-T variant; an ablation separates its manifold and coverage benefits. Planning success does not improve despite accurate risk estimates, a negative result we report with its diagnosis.
  </details>

- **[Towards Multi-Source Domain Generalization for Sleep Staging with Noisy Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10009)**  `arXiv:2604.10009`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Kening Wang, Di Wen, Yufan Chen, Ruiping Liu, Junwei Zheng, Jiale Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic sleep staging is a multimodal learning problem involving heterogeneous physiological signals such as EEG and EOG, which often suffer from domain shifts across institutions, devices, and populations. In practice, these data are also affected by noisy annotations, yet label-noise-robust multi-source domain generalization remains underexplored. We present the first benchmark for Noisy Labels in Multi-Source Domain-Generalized Sleep Staging (NL-DGSS) and show that existing noisy-label learning methods degrade substantially when domain shifts and label noise coexist. To address this challenge, we propose FF-TRUST, a domain-invariant multimodal sleep staging framework with Joint Time-Frequency Early Learning Regularization (JTF-ELR). By jointly exploiting temporal and spectral consistency together with confidence-diversity regularization, FF-TRUST improves robustness under noisy supervision. Experiments on five public datasets demonstrate consistent state-of-the-art performance under diverse symmetric and asymmetric noise settings.
  </details>

- **[How Far Can Untrained Neural Networks Go in Anomaly Detection? From Tables to Pixels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08136)**  `arXiv:2611.08136`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Hyojun Yi, Geunyoung Kim, Hogeon Seo, Seunghyoung Ryu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomaly detection (AD) is often framed as a representation-learning problem, where strong performance is expected to require learned features or AD-specific optimization. However, this view can overlook the intrinsic separability of anomalies from normal references, even in the absence of learned representations. We therefore investigate how far untrained neural networks can go in anomaly detection. To this end, we propose PRISM, a framework combining fixed random nonlinear features with normal-reference Mahalanobis scoring for tabular detection and dense visual localization. Across 47 tabular datasets in ADBench, PRISM-T achieves the highest mean AUROC (86.4%) and the second-highest mean AUPRC (65.9%) among the compared methods. On MVTec AD and VisA, covering 27 visual categories, PRISM-V achieves mean image-level and pixel-level AUROCs of 90.4% and 96.4%, respectively. Notably, both models achieve these results without neural weight optimization or pretrained representations. A theoretical analysis further characterizes how nonlinear transformations affect the mean and covariance contributions to the Mahalanobis score gap, while empirical analyses examine the resulting score separability. These findings establish PRISM as both a competitive anomaly detector and a prior-to-learning baseline for assessing the added value of learned representations in anomaly detection.
  </details>

- **[Can the Confidence and Decisions of System 1 Models Be Trusted? An Explainability and Perturbation Audit of Jev-Style Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12521)**  `arXiv:2611.12521`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Julien Delaunay_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  "System 1" decision models (pioneered by TypeSafe AI's Jev) replace autoregressive text generation with a single forward pass returning typed probability distributions over declared schemas (Choice, Score, Noul). While deployed for low-latency routing and automated triage, their statistical calibration and post-hoc explainability remain uncharacterized. We introduce an open, deterministic audit benchmark evaluating System 1 decision models across calibration under label-preserving perturbations, cross-question interference, and post-hoc attribution faithfulness. Evaluating five open-weight models (0.8B to 27B) alongside generative System 2 baselines -- both single-token logprob scoring and structured JSON/CoT decoding -- across NLI (e-SNLI), adversarial syntactic shortcuts (HANS), synthetic clinical triage (N=900), prompt injection defense (Tensor Trust, N=250), and an 86-case logic-gate trigger suite yields five findings: (1) provider confidence is an exact closed-form dispersion transform (1 - H(p)/ln K on sub-10B models; normalized margin on Clef-27B) rather than empirical accuracy; (2) bundling multiple questions in one request induces directional cross-question interference (11.7% to 22.4% binary flips on Tev1-0.8B, vs. 0.1% to 0.2% on Tev1-4B); (3) System 1 models with >= 4B parameters are faster and better calibrated under syntactic shifts (ECE_w = 0.030 to 0.087) than logprob (0.144) and JSON (0.378) baselines; (4) for prompt-injection defense (N=250), >= 4B models achieve 0.988 to 0.998 AUROC with up to 95.2% recall and 0.0% false alarms at 48 to 60 ms; and (5) single-pass inference makes 512-coalition XAI interactive (1.3 to 3.2 s/input), while KernelSHAP with a logit link (S1-SHAP) mitigates softmax saturation on disjunctive OR gates (89.3% to 100.0% gate recovery vs. 57.1% to 82.1% in prob space; p_adj = 0.004).
  </details>

- **[Coverage-Verified Sparse Attention: Closed-Loop Quality Control for Long-Context LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10634)**  `arXiv:2611.10634`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yan-Cheng Li, Chun-Yi Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention accelerates long-context decoding by reading a subset of the KV cache, but existing methods are open-loop: the kernel commits to its selection with no signal indicating whether enough attention mass was preserved and no recovery path when it was not. The needed signal already exists inside the kernel. The fraction of softmax mass on the selected tokens, which we call coverage, satisfies an exact algebraic equality with per-step output error and serves a dual role as the acceptance criterion and the interpolation weight for recovery. Coverage-Verified Sparse Attention (CVSA) turns coverage into a training-free, closed-loop verify-then-correct decode loop that accepts high-coverage drafts and recovers only the heads that fall short, with no sampling, no draft model, and no speculative-decoding infrastructure. Quality varies by only $0.14$ points across the threshold sweep; budget and scheduling ablations characterize the deployed operating point.
