On LongBench at 7B and 70B, CVSA comes within $1.13$ points of dense attention, statistically matching it at 70B, and it loses $0.8$ points on RULER; measured median decode-step speedup reaches $6.21\times$ at 128K context.
  </details>

- **[Two-Sided Conformal Evidence Sets for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10335)**  `arXiv:2611.10335`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ben Jenkins, Mihaela Cardei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can surface passages that support a claim, contradict it, or fail to address it, yet existing calibrated grounding methods are typically one-sided and model only supporting evidence. We introduce Conformal Evidence Sets (CES), a two-sided framework that returns separate support and refutation sets with finite-sample inclusion coverage of at least $1-\alpha$ on each side under exchangeability; a Bonferroni union yields joint coverage of at least $1-2\alpha$. On labeled candidate pools from the contrastive VitaminC benchmark, CES closely tracks nominal coverage across $\alpha\!\in\!\{0.05,0.10,0.15,0.20,0.30\}$, with maximum deviations of $3.0$ percentage points for support and $5.4$ for refutation, and reaches essentially the same empirical coverage--size frontier as a test-tuned fixed-threshold heuristic without any test exposure. Stress tests mark the boundary of that guarantee: under FEVER distribution shift both sides under-cover, by up to $12.7$ percentage points, and support-side group-stratified recalibration substantially reduces the support gap. Scorer swaps, hard-negative retrieval, cross-dataset transfer, and a live end-to-end pilot expose a clear separation: when exchangeability holds and relevant evidence is retrieved, evidence inclusion remains calibrated even with an imperfect scorer, whereas the support/refute verdict tracks scorer quality directly. Calibrated evidence coverage and reliable verdicts are therefore distinct properties and should be reported separately.
  </details>

- **[How Much Is Left Matters: False Positives and False Negatives in Partial-Prompt Contamination Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10307)**  `arXiv:2611.10307`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Abhishek Anand_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partial-prompt completion, where a model receives the first part of a benchmark problem and is checked for whether it reproduces the rest, is a widely used test for benchmark contamination. We audit this test on seven open-weight models using MATH-500 and recent AIME 2025/2026 problems, which serve as true negatives. Memorization flags depend strongly on how much text remains: for every model, problems with at most 8 leftover words are flagged several times more often than problems with at least 25 (31.5% vs. 5.5% for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), and half to three-quarters of all ROUGE-based flags come from short leftovers. On true negatives, a ROUGE-L >= 0.75 threshold flags up to 6.7% of problems at a 60% prefix, and at an 80% prefix even exact match flags 8.6% (pooled over models; 6.2% on AIME 2026, which postdates every model), against 0% at 40-60%. The test also misses contamination upstream: models distilled from Qwen2.5-Math, whose base is heavily flagged in prior work, show almost no model-specific recall. Calibrating each model against models from other families removes most flags while isolating a consistent Qwen2.5-specific signal at every prefix length. We recommend length-stratified reporting, prefixes of at most 60%, style-matched post-cutoff controls, and cross-family calibration.
  </details>

- **[LLMs as Adaptive Meta-Solvers: Strategy-Diverse RL for Industrial-Scale Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34427)**  `arXiv:2609.34427`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Shihao Zhang, Weiting Liu, Siyu Shao, Yitian Chen, Jianfeng Feng, Dongdong Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling LLM-based optimization from textbook-scale instances to real-world, industrial tasks remains a critical open challenge. Existing approaches are predominantly evaluated on small, self-contained textual problems and often commit to a solver-integrated paradigm, limiting their ability to handle the scale and structural diversity of practical optimization workloads. In this work, we propose a practical framework for training open-source LLMs to tackle real-world, industrial-scale optimization. We first show empirically that solver-integrated reasoning, exact combinatorial algorithm, and heuristic search exhibit complementary strengths across different problem structures and scales. Motivated by this, we introduce Strategy-Diverse Reinforcement Learning (SDRL), which trains LLMs as adaptive optimization meta-solvers. SDRL leverages this complementarity through a correctness-gated hierarchical diversity reward that promotes robust exploration across varying strategies and within each strategy, effectively preventing premature strategy collapse. We further introduce a mixed-format training scheme that jointly supports both self-contained textual problems and file-grounded instances. Across comprehensive evaluations, our framework outperforms existing fine-tuned methods and frontier models including DeepSeek-V4-Pro and GPT-5.5, both on average across benchmarks and on industrial-scale optimization tasks. We release our code and training data to facilitate future researchthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Knowing When to Ask: Segment-Level Credit Assignment for LLM Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27788)**  `arXiv:2605.27788`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Abhijit Kumar, Zoey Wu, Mohit Suley_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans know when to reach for help e.g. $347 \times 28$ warrants a calculator while $2+2$ does not. Language models do not. Prompt-based approaches can instruct a model when to invoke tools, but this scaffolding does not teach it to recognize the boundary of its own knowledge. RL approaches that assign a single outcome reward to the whole trajectory fare no better: trajectory-level credit cannot isolate which tool call in a successful episode actually helped, nor penalize unnecessary calls. We propose CARL (Competence-Aware Reinforcement Learning), which trains a critic on the model's own rollouts to learn where parametric knowledge suffices and where it needs external help. By decomposing each rollout at natural tool-use boundaries (e.g., code fence delimiters and context block transitions), CARL assigns independent credit to each segment from a single binary outcome, without external judges or step-level annotations. As a result, erroneous tool calls, incorrect extractions, and unnecessary calls each receive appropriately signed advantages. The trained critic captures the model's domain competence: it separates parametrically solvable from tool-dependent questions with AUC 0.93 at 7B. On five benchmarks spanning arithmetic, multi-hop factual QA, and numerical reasoning over financial tables, CARL improves average exact-match accuracy on the three search benchmarks by 6.7 points at 7B and 9.6 points at 3B over the best RL baseline, with the largest gain (+8.3 EM at 7B, +12.5 EM at 3B) on MuSiQue. At 7B, CARL has a 53% lower tool-use rate than Search-R1 PPO on parametrically answerable questions while remaining 9.8 EM points more accurate on them. Gains are largest at small scale: the 3B improvement is $1.4\times$ the 7B improvement, suggesting that knowing when to ask disproportionately benefits models with smaller parametric memory.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing Policy Optimization via Logits Convexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00963)**  `arXiv:2603.00963`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Hongzhan Chen, Tao Yang, Yuhua Zhu, Shiping Gao, Xiaojun Quan, Ting Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While reinforcement learning (RL) has been central to the recent success of large language models (LLMs), RL optimization is notoriously unstable, especially when compared to supervised fine-tuning (SFT). In this work, we investigate the stability gap between SFT and RL from a gradient-based perspective, and show that the convexity of the SFT loss with respect to model logits plays a key role in enabling stable training. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that this property induces favorable gradient directionality during optimization. In contrast, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), a widely adopted policy gradient algorithm utilizing a clipped surrogate objective, lacks this stabilizing property. Motivated by this observation, we propose Logits Convex Optimization (LCO), a simple yet effective policy optimization framework that aligns the learned policy with an optimal target derived from the original RL objective, thereby emulating the stabilizing effects of logits-level convexity. Extensive experiments across multiple model families show that our LCO framework consistently improves training stability and outperforms conventional RL methods on a broad range of benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[ChemBART: Pre-training a Reaction Language Model for Comprehensive Synthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02915)**  `arXiv:2601.02915`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Kenan Li, Yijian Zhang, Jin Wang, Haipeng Gan, Zeying Sun, Xiaoguang Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated transformative potential across diverse fields. However, in computer-aided synthesis planning (CASP), current LLM methodologies are often restricted to single-molecule representations, limiting their application to isolated, single-task scenarios such as precursor prediction. Addressing this gap, we present ChemBART, a BART-based language model that adopts a reaction-centric pre-training strategy. By leveraging a mask-filling pre-training task on holistic reaction expressions (Reactant > Reagent > Product), ChemBART effectively solves a variety of chemical problems, including precursor/reagent generation, temperature-yield regression, and molecular property classification. The reaction-centric pre-training further enables the integration of ChemBART with Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) for multi-step synthesis route design, where the model serves to optimize the policy and value functions within a reinforcement learning framework. Beyond single-molecule pre-trained LLMs that are constrained to specific applications, ChemBART unifies diverse chemical tasks to facilitate synthesis planning with comprehensive implementation information. Most significantly, the proposed multi-step synthesis routes and reaction conditions were validated in the wet lab, confirming the discovery of shorter synthetic pathways with a >20% yield improvement over established literature benchmarks. Our work highlights the potential of reaction-focused pre-training and showcases the broad utility of ChemBART in closing the loop between in silico prediction and wet-lab synthesis.
  </details>

- **[$Σ$-LeWM: Learning World Models via S-Information for Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12732)**  `arXiv:2611.12732`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Xinrui Zu, Michael Przystupa, Zhao Yang, Shujian Yu, Kevin Sebastian Luck_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models have increasingly been applied as a model-based control method for robotics tasks such as manipulation and locomotion. While early world model architectures relied on decoders to prevent mode collapse and learn a predictable latent space, recent works on world models, such as Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture or LeWorldModel, increasingly aim to learn embeddings without a grounding decoder, utilising only a latent forward dynamics predictor and a stabilising entropy term. In this paper, we set out to demonstrate that the further introduction of two additional latent losses to LeWorldModel, a backward dynamics predictor and an inverse dynamics action predictor, enforces further structure on the latent embedding, leading to further gains in both performance and planning efficiency. These are critical properties for improving the applicability of such methods to real-time model-based control of real-world robots. We demonstrate these gains on standard world model benchmarks and two simulated robot manipulation tasks.
  </details>

- **[How Many Exposures Does a World Model Need? Learning Unseen Game Mechanics at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12324)**  `arXiv:2611.12324`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Ku Onoda, Haruto Kawano, Shota Takashiro, Kohei Hayashi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models enable agents to plan and learn from imagined experience, but in an unfamiliar environment they must learn its mechanics from limited interaction. A mechanic is a rule for how an action or an interaction changes part of the environment. Each occurrence of a mechanic that the model observes is one exposure. We ask how many exposures a world model needs to predict the effects of a new environment's mechanics. We address this question on ARC-AGI-3, a benchmark of interactive games defined by their programs. We introduce MechEval, which labels 69 recurring mechanics across 20 games from these programs. It scores each prediction on the cells the mechanic changes at each exposure. As a test bed, we build WARC (World model for ARC-AGI-3), a 35M-parameter vision transformer that is pretrained on other games and updates all of its parameters after each transition. On held-out games, WARC learns to predict four in five mechanics with at least 80% accuracy on the cells they change and keeps that accuracy to the end of the recordings. Half of them reach that accuracy within about 80 exposures, in a single play of the game, and without updates only one in seven does at all. In closed-loop play on the public games, WARC-Agent, which pairs WARC with a fixed graph explorer, triples the explorer's official efficiency score from 0.15 to 0.46, mostly through test-time training. On the hidden games of the Kaggle leaderboard, our entry scores 0.20.
  </details>

- **[Co-RL: Unsupervised Reasoning Emerges from Diverse Cohort in Multi-agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17253)**  `arXiv:2608.17253`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yunhao Yang, Yuexin Bian, Yunjie Tian, Di Fu, Tianjin Huang, Yuanyuan Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning (RL) has emerged as a powerful approach for improving reasoning in language and vision-language models, yet its strongest successes still depend heavily on ground-truth supervision (e.g., verifiable reward). Such annotations are costly to obtain and become increasingly scarce as reasoning capabilities advance beyond what humans can reliably evaluate. Self-rewarding RL reduces this dependence by enabling models to derive reward signals from their own completions. However, training solely on self-generated feedback can reinforce existing biases, reduce response diversity, and ultimately lead to homogenized responses and training collapse. In this work, we show that unsupervised reasoning can emerge through cooperative multi-agent training. We introduce Co-RL, a framework in which multiple decoupled models, sharing no parameters, are simultaneously optimized through RL using rewards derived from the other agent. We further show that increasing cohort diversity, through heterogeneous model families, sizes, and rephrased training samples, reduces the correlated errors that drive self-reinforcing feedback loops. This diversity consistently improves reasoning performance and mitigates training collapse. Without any ground-truth labels, Co-RL improves the base models by 3.0-7.9% on average across seven benchmarks and by 1.6-4.4% across four multimodal benchmarks. It also outperforms the prior state-of-the-art multi-agent RL method CoMAS by 4.0% and the self-rewarding vision-language RL method MM-UPT by 2.0%. Our code is available atthis https URLand website atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Do Collusion Probes Read? Three Pitfalls in Evaluating Multi-Agent Collusion Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10620)**  `arXiv:2611.10620`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Mainul Hasan Mohsin, Mohammad Shahidur Rahman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation probes look like near-perfect monitors for secret collusion among large language model (LLM) agents. We show that they mostly detect that an agent was told to collude, not that it decided to. Our benchmark (2,500 committee and 1,476 code-review episodes) adds agents who receive the collusion instruction and refuse it, separating instruction from decision. Published detectors barely tell colluders from these refusers, and our best probe separates them mostly by reading the instruction: without it, agent AUROC falls from 1.00 to 0.61. Two further pitfalls inflate reported results: scored after the vote, a rule that only reads the votes reaches AUROC 0.990 but finds nothing before it; and in 57% of attempts no colluder argues for the target in public. The decision is still there: a probe trained on agents' own choices reads it (AUROC 0.73 without the instruction), and probes find colluders whose goal was fine-tuned into the weights (AUROC 0.995; pre-registered). We turn the controls into a protocol for testing any collusion monitor, and adapt scan statistics into an online monitor for groups of any size.
  </details>

- **[Speculative Self-Distillation enables Efficient Knowledge Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12016)**  `arXiv:2611.12016`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shayan Talaei, Agam Bhatia, Arshia Soltani Moakhar, Jonas Hübotter, Amin Saberi, Azalia Mirhoseini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many real-world applications require language models to internalize knowledge that was absent from pretraining, to incorporate continually updated facts, or to learn new domain skills. Self-distillation has emerged as a natural approach for this task. A model conditioned on the source material acts as the teacher, and an unconditioned student is trained to reproduce the teacher's behavior closed-book. We propose Speculative Self-Distillation (SSD), an efficient per-token mixed-policy method for self-distillation. The student generates each rollout by default, while the teacher intervenes only at positions where its next-token distribution substantially diverges from the student's. Unlike off-policy distillation with teacher-generated prefixes, this design keeps training rollouts close to the student's test-time behavior, while avoiding the low-signal states that fully on-policy rollouts drift into. In effect, SSD lets the teacher steer the rollout exactly where the privileged context changes the prediction. On three closed-book QA benchmarks measuring fact recall, knowledge updates, and skill acquisition, SSD nearly matches on-policy distillation while using 38-69% fewer supervised tokens and consistently outperforms off-policy distillation on the held-out test set. On a forgetting stress test, SSD retains the most general capability while also achieving the best in-domain accuracy and using less supervision than the on-policy baseline. Overall, SSD establishes a new accuracy-efficiency-forgetting Pareto frontier for self-distillation.
  </details>

- **[Generating Diverse and Verified Synthetic Clinical Notes with Labels by Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10789)**  `arXiv:2611.10789`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Matthias Lehmann, Madeleine Werthebach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Under strict privacy regulations in the clinical domain, de-identification is necessary to enable the use of clinical data such as for natural language processing. However, we currently lack sophisticated ways to tell which de-identification models actually are suitable. Specifically, we need benchmarks that are challenging, diverse, and clinically plausible, all while ensuring label correctness. To this end, we present SPARCLING, an LLM-based pipeline to generate synthetic discharge letters with character-exact PHI span annotations. Our pipeline combines structured attribute sampling, template generation, and red teaming to obtain diverse PHI-labeled discharge letters. In our experiments, we demonstrate this superior content diversity over existing public datasets. Further, we show SPARCLING's downstream utility as a benchmark to uncover failure modes of de-identifiers in the German clinical domain. Finally, our experiments show that training on our synthetic data can yield more capable de-identifiers than training on the scarce public data alone. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Typed Edge-Tensor Non-Reciprocal Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation for Counterfactual Open-World Deductive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37351)**  `arXiv:2609.37351`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zeyu Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling test-time computation in continuous latent spaces offers an attractive alternative to verbose token-by-token generation for multi-step deductive reasoning. However, unregularized recurrent latent updates suffer from a deliberation drift cliff when rolled out beyond training horizons, while strictly energy-conserving or symmetric message-passing dynamics cannot break parent-to-child causal symmetry or suppress cyclic modus-ponens amplification on cyclic distraction graphs. We introduce Typed Edge-Tensor Non-Reciprocal Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation (TE-BPHD), a neuro-symbolic continuous-time latent reasoning architecture that couples skew-symmetric port-Hamiltonian conservation with relation-typed non-reciprocal directional transport and monotone dissipative damping on a semantic entity-state manifold. We prove (i) unconditional input-to-state stability and exponential contraction of autonomous rollouts across arbitrary inference depths without external norm projection, (ii) strict directional DAG reachability separation over symmetric message passing, and (iii) exact cycle-suppression margins under finite cyclic horn perturbations. Across counterfactual open-world deductive benchmarks (PrOntoQA-OOD up to depth 15, ProofWriter-OWA Cyclic at depths 5-10, FOLIO-Symbolic, and Cluttr-Relational), TE-BPHD eliminates the deep-horizon drift cliff, improves depth-10 cyclic reasoning accuracy to 90.0% (+/- 0.8%) over symmetric port-Hamiltonian and standard Transformer baselines, and supports zero-retraining adaptive Lyapunov halting that reduces average solver evaluations by 44% with negligible accuracy loss.
  </details>

- **[Program-as-Weights: A Programming Paradigm for Fuzzy Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02512)**  `arXiv:2607.02512`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Wentao Zhang, Liliana Hotsko, Woojeong Kim, Pengyu Nie, Stuart Shieber, Yuntian Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many everyday programming tasks resist clean rule-based implementation, such as alerting on important log lines, repairing malformed JSON, or ranking search results by intent, and are increasingly outsourced to large language model APIs at the cost of locality, reproducibility, and price. We propose fuzzy-function programming: compiling such a function from a natural-language specification into a compact, locally executable neural artifact. We instantiate this paradigm with Program-as-Weights (PAW), in which a 4B compiler trained on FuzzyBench, a 10M-example dataset we release, emits parameter-efficient adapters for a frozen, lightweight interpreter. A 0.6B Qwen3 interpreter executing PAW programs matches the performance of direct prompting of Qwen3-32B, while using roughly one fiftieth of the inference memory and running at 30 tokens/s on a MacBook M3. PAW reframes the foundation model from a per-input problem solver into a tool builder: invoked once per function definition, it produces a small reusable artifact whose subsequent calls per function application are cheap and offline.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Reward Hacking in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback via Advantage Sign Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02986)**  `arXiv:2604.02986`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shinnosuke Ono, Johannes Ackermann, Soichiro Nishimori, Takashi Ishida, Masashi Sugiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models (RMs) used in reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) are vulnerable to reward hacking: as the policy maximizes a learned proxy reward, true quality plateaus or degrades. We make the assumption that reward hacking is often caused by flipped advantage signs: instead of reducing the likelihood of a bad response, a flipped sign causes the update to increase it. By considering an adversarial perturbation in the RM parameter space, we can derive a certified sign-preservation radius, which is the smallest perturbation that can flip the advantage sign during policy optimization. Based on this formulation, we propose Sign-Certified Policy Optimization (SignCert-PO), down-weighting non-robust completions in the policy gradient update. Unlike prior approaches that require multiple RMs or access to the RM training data, SignCert-PO is lightweight and operates purely at the policy optimization stage using only the RM parameters and on-policy completions. On TL;DR summarization and AlpacaFarm benchmarks, SignCert-PO consistently achieves a better win rate than baselines and reduces reward hacking.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion LLMs with Entropy-Guided Step Selection and Stepwise Advantages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12554)**  `arXiv:2603.12554`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Vishnu Teja Kunde, Fatemeh Doudi, Mahdi Farahbakhsh, Dileep Kalathil, Krishna Narayanan, Jean-Francois Chamberland_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has been effective for post-training autoregressive (AR) language models, but extending these methods to diffusion language models (DLMs) is challenging due to intractable sequence-level likelihoods. Existing approaches therefore rely on surrogate likelihoods or heuristic approximations, which can introduce bias and obscure the sequential structure of denoising. We formulate diffusion-based sequence generation as a finite-horizon Markov decision process over the denoising trajectory and derive an exact, unbiased policy gradient that decomposes over denoising steps and is expressed in terms of intermediate advantages, without requiring explicit evaluation of the sequence likelihood. To obtain a practical and compute-efficient estimator, we (i) select denoising steps for policy updates via an entropy-guided approximation bound, and (ii) estimate intermediate advantages using a one-step denoising reward naturally provided by the diffusion model, avoiding costly multi-step rollouts. Experiments on coding and logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art results, with strong competitive performance on mathematical reasoning, outperforming existing RL post-training approaches for DLMs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TabDLM: Free-Form Tabular Data Generation via Joint Numerical-Language Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22586)**  `arXiv:2602.22586`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Donghong Cai, Jiarui Feng, Yanbo Wang, Da Zheng, Yixin Chen, Muhan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic tabular data generation has attracted growing attention due to its importance for data augmentation, foundation models, and privacy. However, real-world tabular datasets increasingly contain free-form text fields (e.g., reviews or clinical notes) alongside structured numerical and categorical attributes. Generating such heterogeneous tables with joint modeling of different modalities remains challenging. Existing approaches broadly fall into two categories: diffusion-based methods and LLM-based methods. Diffusion models can capture complex dependencies over numerical and categorical features in continuous or discrete spaces, but extending them to open-ended text is nontrivial, since they model each column as a continuous value or one of a closed set of categories, whereas text lives in an exponentially large sequence space. In contrast, LLM-based generators naturally produce fluent text, yet their discrete tokenization can distort precise or wide-range numerical values, hindering accurate modeling of both numbers and language. In this work, we propose TabDLM, a unified framework for free-form tabular data generation via a joint numerical-language diffusion model built on masked diffusion language models (MDLMs). TabDLM models textual and categorical features through masked diffusion, while modeling numerical features with a continuous diffusion process through learned specialized numeric tokens embedding; bidirectional attention then captures cross-modality interactions within a single model. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of TabDLM compared to strong diffusion- and LLM-based baselines. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Linear Model Merging Unlocks Simple and Scalable Multimodal Data Mixture Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04937)**  `arXiv:2602.04937`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Davide Berasi, Matteo Farina, Massimiliano Mancini, Elisa Ricci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting the best data mixture is critical for successful Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) of Multimodal Large Language Models. However, determining the optimal mixture weights across multiple domain-specific datasets remains a significant bottleneck due to the combinatorial search space and the high cost associated with even a single training run. This is the so-called Data Mixture Optimization (DMO) problem. On the other hand, model merging unifies domain-specific experts through parameter interpolation. This strategy is efficient, as it requires only a single training run per domain, yet it often leads to suboptimal models. In this work, we take the best of both worlds, studying model merging as an efficient strategy for estimating the performance of different data mixtures. We train domain-specific multimodal experts and evaluate their weighted parameter-space combinations to estimate the efficacy of corresponding data mixtures. We conduct extensive experiments on 14 multimodal benchmarks, and empirically demonstrate that the merged proxy models consistently select near-optimal mixtures. This decouples the search for optimal mixtures from the resource-intensive training process, thereby providing a scalable and efficient strategy for navigating the complex landscape of mixture weights. Code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799)**  `arXiv:2611.12799`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tingcheng Bian, Kangye Gong, Jinchang Luo, MingQuan Cheng, Feiran Liu, Ni Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.
  </details>

- **[NearFormer: Linear-Size Sparse Attention via Randomized Near Neighbors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12186)**  `arXiv:2611.12186`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Parviz, Alex Cloninger, Gal Mishne_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph transformers can capture long-range interactions, but their quadratic attention cost limits their use on large graphs. We introduce \emph{NearFormer}, a graph transformer whose global attention is restricted to a randomized near-neighbor (NN) graph. NearFormer uses an efficient randomized construction to connect each node to a small number of near neighbors from a broader neighborhood defined by a label-independent feature or spectral geometry. For a fixed retained budget \(k\), the resulting attention support has linear size \(\mathcal{O}(|E|+nk)\). The NN graph is built once by within-bin search and then held fixed. We use these edges as sparse global-attention supports, with optional learned attention biases, while preserving the original graph for local message passing. Experiments on long-range graph benchmarks and synthetic structural tasks show that NearFormer provides a favorable accuracy--scalability trade-off relative to local message passing, dense and sparse attention.
  </details>

- **[Visible Tails: Characterizing Adaptation to Unknown Moments in Heavy-Tailed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12140)**  `arXiv:2611.12140`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tuan Ngoc Do, Hai Duc Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Heavy-tailed bandit learners without moment parameters face an obstruction: the rewards that carry an arm's mean can stay unseen. We address the remaining part of a COLT open problem by characterizing the optimal-arm laws that permit adaptation to unknown moments at constant-factor cost. The condition, variance-core visibility, is one-sided and translation-invariant: the mean hidden in any rare event is at most a constant multiple of the standard error of the rewards that remain. A quantile-trimmed UCB index that knows the visibility constants attains the central-moment benchmark on this class, and no single strategy adapts across all visibility levels. The same index achieves logarithmic expected regret on every instance with a visible optimal arm and finite-mean rewards. On an explicit family with no higher moment, gap $\Delta$ and constant $A$, its logarithmic coefficient and the information lower bound are of order $\Delta^{-1}e^{A/\Delta}$, exponential in the inverse gap within one visibility class.
  </details>

- **[CARE-PO: Controllable Tool-Call Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12125)**  `arXiv:2611.12125`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyang Ye, Xiao Yao, Hairui Zhu, Yiying Yang, Bei Gan, Jinliang Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. In a step-level diagnosis of Qwen3.5 trajectories at two model sizes on GAIA, a long-horizon tool-use benchmark, we find that most contain removable calls, at a similar rate at both sizes. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.
  </details>

- **[FireGate: Bounding Behaviour Change Before Training in Continual RL Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11979)**  `arXiv:2611.11979`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Md Anwar Hossen, Ali Jannesari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning a deployed language model on one task after another can change behaviour that should stay fixed, such as earlier skills, general chat or refusals. Today, such forgetting is found only after training, on the benchmarks someone chose to run. This work bounds, before training, how often its outputs change on chosen prompt distributions. FireGate places each task's update, such as a new LoRA, behind a gate, which is a classifier on the model's frozen lower layers that decides for each prompt whether the update is used. Where no gate fires, the model computes exactly what the old one computes. As a result, on any prompt distribution, every bounded behaviour score changes by at most the gates' firing rate there over a whole task stream. Calibrating each gate on unlabeled prompts of what must not change fixes that rate before training, with a PAC guarantee, whatever weights training produces. Forgetting is thus bounded by the gate's false-alarm rate, and its price is the gate's power: no update, gated or not, that changes as few protected outputs can change new-task outputs more often than the best test of that level tells new-task prompts from protected ones. On reinforcement-learning streams of up to five tasks, gated training keeps the earlier tasks and changes 3.3% of held-out chat responses, whereas ungated training changes 91.8%. Across seven models, the gain kept behind the gate follows its firing rate on new-task prompts, measured before training. The certificate covers the gated model as deployed, on the prompt distributions sampled for calibration, not against an adversary who searches for prompts that fire the gate.
  </details>

- **[MixRoute: Rethinking Single-Distribution Training for Generalizable Neural Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11977)**  `arXiv:2611.11977`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hang Yi, Ziwei Huang, Yining Ma, Zhiguang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO) is a promising paradigm for solving routing problems, yet its generalization across diverse data distributions remains a critical bottleneck. Existing methods typically tackle cross-distribution generalization through complex multi-distribution training or intricate meta-learning schemes, implicitly presuming that single-distribution training yields a weak zero-shot baseline. In this paper, we demonstrate that the potential of single-distribution training has been substantially underestimated and can be unlocked by a simple architectural inductive bias: Mix Normalization. To approach this, we first construct a comprehensive benchmark spanning three representative routing problems and 179 fine-grained datasets, capturing diverse shifts in node coordinates, customer demands, capacity and time windows. Building on this benchmark, we propose MixRoute, a neural framework that adaptively combines normalization statistics at different granularities to mitigate varying distribution shifts. Trained exclusively on uniform distributions, MixRoute generalizes effectively to a wide range of unseen distributions without any distribution-specific adaptation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MixRoute consistently achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot generalization. Our work revisits normalization as a parsimonious alternative for zero-shot generalization against complex training-adaptation schemes.
  </details>

- **[An Evaluation of Tabular Foundation Models as Training-Free World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11921)**  `arXiv:2611.11921`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xian Yeow Lee, Ryoichi Takase, Ahmed Farahat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models are widely considered to be a potential key component of model-based reinforcement learning and robot planning, but typically require task-specific training on substantial interaction data. We investigate whether a pretrained tabular foundation model (TabFM) can serve as a training-free dynamics model for world modeling by framing next-state prediction as in-context regression over transition pairs, and where such a model helps and where it breaks. Across synthetic dynamical systems and robot locomotion benchmarks, TabFM is more accurate than LSTM and MLP models trained from scratch on the same limited transitions in almost all settings, and among regressors fitted to the same data it is the most accurate on locomotion, while a Gaussian process is more accurate on smooth low-dimensional systems. However, models trained on the full dataset remain substantially better on most systems. When rolled out autoregressively over multiple steps, TabFM's accuracy depends strongly on how much context it is given. With a short context, its rollouts quickly degrade to the level of a trivial baseline, whereas with a long context they improve substantially and, on one locomotion task, exceed a trained LSTM. Inference is also about three orders of magnitude slower than an LSTM. Overall, in their current state, we find that training-free priors are useful as predictors when data are scarce and enough context is available, but they are not yet a substitute for trained world models in planning.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Counterfactual Explanations for Time Series via Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11704)**  `arXiv:2611.11704`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Peiyu Li, Pouya Hosseinzadeh, Soukaina Filali Boubrahimi, Shah Muhammad Hamdi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating counterfactual (CF) explanations for time series is challenging due to complex temporal dependencies and the computational inefficiency of traditional instance-specific optimization. We propose a policy learning framework for counterfactual explanation in time series, trained as a one-step contextual-bandit policy-learning problem. By training an actor-critic agent in the latent space of a pre-trained autoencoder, our method learns a transferable policy that captures global structural regularities. Unlike existing methods that rely on iterative optimization for each new input, the learned policy enables direct generation of valid and plausible counterfactuals through a single forward pass, avoiding per-instance optimization at inference time. Experiments on 13 UCR benchmark datasets show that our framework achieves high validity and strong plausibility across multiple anomaly detection metrics. Overall, by decoupling counterfactual generation from per-instance optimization, the proposed method provides an efficient and scalable alternative to existing approaches for time series counterfactual explanations.
  </details>

- **[Direct GPU Energy Measurements of Batch-Level Sharing in Mixture-of-Experts and Recurrent Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11696)**  `arXiv:2611.11696`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Rafael Pina, Varuna De Silva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Energy claims for efficient neural networks are often inferred from operation counts. We report direct GPU energy measurements on two workloads that batch very differently, recurrent policies in GPU-vectorised multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) and mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models, and ask one question of both: can the rows of a batch be made to share computation, across the batch and over time, at a measured saving? On recurrent policies the answer so far is no. Shared activity patterns exist but rarely last one step, masking a frozen policy fails, and trained-in masks succeed only because a fixed mask is equivalent to a narrow GRU, at 11x the energy of a small dense policy in our implementation. Test-time steering adds nothing beyond re-picking on either workload. On MoE models the answer is yes, and granularity decides how much: on Qwen3-30B-A3B, batch-aware routing reads 25-31% fewer expert weights within KL 0.05, with benchmark changes within noise at 500 items, and uses 12-18% less energy per token on a fused forward. We also introduce persistence-aware OEA, a one-parameter rule that keeps the batch-aware cut with about 40% fewer new experts per step than native routing at batch 32-64, though its energy has not yet been measured. These are pilot measurements on one GPU type, and we state their limits.
  </details>

- **[Data-informed Adaptation of Foundation Models for Causal Effect Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11504)**  `arXiv:2611.11504`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chunwei Ma, Russell D Wolfinger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Due to the fundamental problem of causal inference -- the impossibility of observing counterfactual outcomes -- there is always a gap between the training goal (outcome, propensity) and the inference target (CATE, ATE). Traditional causal effect estimation revolves around setting assumptions, selecting identification strategies, and choosing causal estimators, while more recently, Causal Foundation Models (CFMs), built on Prior-Data-Fitted Networks (PFNs), ease this process by pretraining on massive synthetic prior datasets. Despite their success, such amortization makes causal inference less transparent, obscuring which assumptions the model implicitly relies on for a given dataset. The high diversity of real-world data-generating processes (DGPs) and Structural Causal Models (SCMs) makes it hard to know, a priori, which causal inference strategy is compatible with a given dataset. In this work, we focus on this compatibility between an observational dataset and method-specific assumptions, and find that geometric characteristics of data embeddings -- specifically, the variogram -- provide a useful signal of whether a given causal estimation strategy is appropriate. We develop MoE-Learner, a mixture-of-causal-experts framework that embeds a dataset under multiple assumption-specific views and uses the variogram as a counterfactual-free router to assess each expert's validity. Rather than eliminating causal assumptions, this shifts their validation from a fixed, a priori choice to a criterion that can be checked directly from data geometry. MoE-Learner achieves the best average rank on benchmark datasets (IHDP, ACIC, Lalonde). Being orthogonal to (causal) foundation models, our method builds a bridge from data characteristics to causal estimation strategy rather than proposing another CFM.
  </details>

- **[JoMoHa: Joint Model--Harness Optimization with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11499)**  `arXiv:2611.11499`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xudong Wu, Guangchen Lan, Mingqi Yuan, Jiayu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The performance of language agents depends jointly on model weights and the harness. Optimizing either alone can be constrained by the other component, limiting overall performance gains. Our analysis of naive alternating training reveals progressive incompatibility between the model and harness, which increasingly interferes with task execution and may also undermine model training, leading to performance degradation. To coordinate the evolution of models and harnesses, we introduce JoMoHa, to our knowledge the first framework to jointly train an executable harness generation policy and a task execution policy through reinforcement learning. JoMoHa outperforms the evaluated weight-only and harness-only baselines and achieves the highest success rates among the evaluated methods on ALFWorld, WebShop, and DBBench, averaging 70.3% and reaching 97.0% on ALFWorld. By learning both task execution and runtime improvement from its own generated trajectories, JoMoHa performs recursive self-improvement (RSI).
  </details>

- **[D3Tree: Learning to Prune Block-Diffusion Draft Trees for Efficient Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11371)**  `arXiv:2611.11371`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hong Nie, Zhangchi Gao, Jun Yu, Bo Wang, Jianqing Sun, Shengping Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block-diffusion drafters propose a whole block of tokens in one forward pass, and draft trees built from their position-wise marginals increase the expected accepted length of speculative decoding. Every tree node occupies a query row in target verification, so under concurrent serving low-yield branches compete with other requests for verification capacity. Existing tree methods select verified nodes by draft probability under a fixed or cost-selected per-request budget, and thus always retain a prefix of the drafter's ranking. We present D3Tree, which learns which nodes of a block-diffusion draft tree are worth verifying. A tree-size head with 2H+2 parameters predicts each candidate's conditional survival from the draft state, the candidate embedding, and its draft log-probability. Thresholding the product of these scores along parent chains yields an ancestor-closed subtree whose size adapts to each request and decoding round. The head is trained on a frozen drafter with conditional labels on the full candidate tree; an optional joint mode also steers the drafter toward the retained reference path. At serving time, D3Tree verifies whole trees under light load and, under heavy load, allocates one batch-wide node budget by predicted survival, sized to the target's matrix-multiplication tiles. Retained trees share one ancestor-masked target pass, while construction, pruning, and scheduling run on the GPU and overlap with the host. Across six vision-language verifiers and 17 text and multimodal benchmarks, D3Tree is fastest in 70 of 72 load settings, with 3.2-4.0x speedup over autoregressive decoding for one request and 1.3-1.8x at 64 concurrent requests, where fixed-budget trees fall below autoregressive speed. Under a time-varying arrival trace, it has the lowest mean end-to-end latency in every arrival phase.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Node Attributes: A Controlled Evaluation and Ablation of Local Interaction Statistics for Ethereum Phishing Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11263)**  `arXiv:2611.11263`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Thanh Dat Tran, Duy Loi Duong, Duy Luu Hoang, Minh Huy Tran, Vo Minh Thu Le, Nhat Tung Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Methods for detecting phishing accounts on Ethereum are usually evaluated as complete pipelines that differ in features, architecture, and training protocol, which makes the contribution of interaction information hard to isolate. This paper evaluates the Unlabeled Feature Extension (UFE), a block of 12 local interaction statistics computed from each account's incoming and outgoing call and transfer edges without reading class labels. UFE is appended to the 14 source features of HEIG-Phish and tested with six model families: Logistic Regression, Random Forest, LightGBM, Graph Attention Network, GraphSAGE, and Meta-IFD. Within each pair of Baseline and Plus variants, the data partition, model configuration, random seed, decision threshold, and sampled neighborhoods are identical; only the input feature block changes. Across 20 predetermined stratified partitions of the same 2,763 labeled externally owned accounts, UFE increased test Macro-F1 by 1.0 to 9.1 percentage points across the six models. Every pair improved on all 20 partitions, all Nadeau-Bengio corrected 95% confidence intervals lie above zero, and the false-positive rate decreased for all six models. An exact-edge Random Forest ablation over 10 predetermined partitions associates most of the gain with the incoming transfer-edge sum attribute; once this feature is present, an incremental contribution of the remaining 11 UFE columns was not established. The results support reporting feature-augmented baselines alongside graph models, but they show stability under re-partitioning of one static, transductive benchmark rather than temporal or cross-dataset generalization.
  </details>

- **[Jointly Optimizing Memory, Computing and Offloading for Energy-Efficient DNN Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11106)**  `arXiv:2611.11106`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yunchu Han, Zhaojun Nan, Sheng Zhou, Zhisheng Niu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In edge intelligence systems, computation offloading and dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) are widely used to improve energy efficiency. However, as applications like deep neural network (DNN) inference evolve in complexity, their demands for memory resources have progressively increased, establishing memory as a critical bottleneck that impacts both latency and energy consumption. In this paper, we propose a joint optimization scheme integrating memory, computing and offloading for energy-efficient DNN inference. We formulate the energy minimization problem under deadline constraints as a mixed-integer nonlinear programming (MINLP) problem, where the main challenge arises from the coupling between offloading and resource optimization. To address this issue, we decompose the original problem into two subproblems under a fixed offloading policy. The local subproblem is transformed into an equivalent geometric programming (GP) problem to derive the optimal memory frequency and computing frequency, while the edge subproblem is solved via convex optimization. An efficient coordinate descent (CD) algorithm is further developed to jointly optimize all variables. Simulation results demonstrate that our proposed algorithm achieves near-optimal performance with the gap from the optimal policy being at most 9.3%, and reduces the energy consumption of devices by up to 16.1% compared with benchmarks without memory frequency optimization.
  </details>

- **[Phase-Conditioned Reward Shaping for Token-Efficient LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11024)**  `arXiv:2611.11024`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhe Chen, Xiaolin Zhou, Xiyang Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Extended reasoning can help language models solve complex problems, but long responses often contain both useful computations and redundant checks. Conventional length penalties encourage shorter responses without distinguishing the functions of intermediate steps. We propose Phase-Conditioned Reward Shaping (PCRS), a framework that combines phase identification with training-time reward allocation. We investigate two classification routes: PCRS-H with a hidden-state classifier and PCRS-T with a frozen text classifier. Our main text classifier implementation assigns overlapping reasoning, execution, and revision labels to native chunks, then uses these labels and exact repetition to adjust existing token penalties. We compare the completed configurations on six mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks and examine four reward-weight settings with the text classifier. Compared with a baseline using token-level length penalties, the default text classifier configuration reduces mean response length by 3.13%; sampled Pass@1 is approximately 62.07% for both methods, while oracle Pass@16 rises from 86.08% to 87.67%. These results show how phase-informed penalty allocation can reduce output volume and improve multi-attempt coverage without inference-time classification.
  </details>

- **[PIA: Agentic Physics-Informed Runtime Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11009)**  `arXiv:2611.11009`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Abbas Yazdinejad, Hadis Karimipour_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Runtime anomaly detectors for cyber-physical systems are either learned as black boxes from normal data, which miss stealthy manipulations that keep every signal inside its normal range, or derived by hand from physical models, which requires expert effort for every plant. We introduce physicsinformed agents (PIA), in which a large language model agent writes the detector itself as a set of physical residuals, such as pump characteristics, tank mass balances, and controller logic, and refines every candidate against normal operating data through an evaluation workbench before deployment. The result is a compact, human-readable detector that runs in milliseconds and names the component behind each alarm. On the BATADAL water distribution attack benchmark, PIA with Claude Opus 5.5 reaches an F1 of 0.878 on the test attacks against 0.504 for the strongest of six statistical and deep baselines, keeps detecting attacks concealed by replayed and offset sensor readings, and attributes every detected test attack to the attacked component. Ablations show that testing hypotheses against data is decisive: agents that write residuals from the plant description alone lose up to 0.28 F1, whereas agents that see only anonymized signals recover most of the physical structure.
  </details>

- **[Horizon-Scalable Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10987)**  `arXiv:2611.10987`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Xu, Pragya Srivastava, Xinjie Liu, Siddhant Agarwal, Amy Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning accurate off-policy state-action value functions (Q-functions) from offline datasets becomes harder as the task horizon grows. One-step temporal-difference (TD) learning bootstraps after every transition, so small per-step errors accumulate along the horizon, and early states can be badly misvalued even when the TD loss is small. Multi-step TD shortens this chain by following the dataset trajectory for several steps; however, since the behavior policy is often suboptimal, every followed action adds a bias, and no fixed number of steps suits a whole trajectory. In this paper, we propose Horizon-Scalable Q-Learning (HQL), a simple method that enables accurate long-horizon value learning by automatically balancing between one-step and multi-step returns. HQL extends a target through a dataset action only when the critic values it at least as highly as the best-of-N actions sampled from the BC policy, and otherwise falls back to a one-step target. We show that this rule is a principled off-policy correction that needs no action densities, which makes HQL compatible with expressive flow and diffusion policies. Experiments show that HQL avoids the failures of both one-step and multi-step TD on a combination-lock task across horizons, and outperforms prior horizon-reduction methods on long-horizon OGBench and OCBench tasks.
  </details>

- **[CoupledFlow: One-Step Neural Operators for Coupled Multi-Physics PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10945)**  `arXiv:2611.10945`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Trong Khiem Tran, Minh Long Bui, Phi Le Nguyen, Mrinal Sen, Sanjay Srinivasan, Trong Nghia Hoang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces COUPLED FLOW, a new neural operator learning framework for multi-physics PDE systems that captures the co-evolution of multiple physical processes by modeling their cross-process interactions. Unlike existing neural operator methods that primarily target single-physics settings, COUPLED FLOW employs a novel structure-constrained flow matching algorithm to learn process- specific mean velocity fields conditioned on the full multi-physics state. This enables fast and accurate one-step prediction of coupled PDE systems that would otherwise require expensive numerical simulations. A key challenge in training COUPLED FLOW however lies in sampling structure-constrained conditional flow interpolants that capture cross-process correlations and serve as training examples for flow matching. To address this challenge, we derive a multi-physics mean-flow consistency identity that characterizes cross-process interactions and defines the structure-constrained mean-flow matching loss. To provide informative training samples for flow matching with this loss, we develop a principled method for sampling correlated conditional interpolants based on posterior multi-output Gaus- sian processes, which capture richer cross-process correlations than the commonly used endpoint-conditioned linear interpolants. We also establish a theoretical re- sult showing that the Wasserstein-2 distance between the predicted and oracle PDE solution distributions is upper-bounded in terms of the structure-constrained flow matching loss and the smoothness of the learned flow. Experiments across diverse coupled PDE benchmarks demonstrate that COUPLED FLOW consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art neural operators and generative PDE solvers.
  </details>

- **[Structurally Separated DAG Learning with Multi-Scale Normalized Closure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10860)**  `arXiv:2611.10860`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuai Feng, Hao Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous DAG learning commonly enforces acyclicity through scalar functions of a weighted adjacency that aggregate evidence from weighted closed walks. We add a complementary layer: matrix-valued closure logits---explicit multi-hop structural relation scores for each ordered pair---supervised alongside data fit, sparsity, and a DAGMA-style barrier on the weighted adjacency. The distinction is not scalarization of the overall objective but how path evidence is represented and exposed before aggregation. SMNC (scale-normalized multi-scale closure) builds these scores on a structural gate by repeated squaring in log space and divides each scale by its path-length budget, counteracting product-style attenuation along long cycles; diagonal entries support node-wise cycle supervision and off-diagonals optional pairwise reachability cues. In the linear model, a bounded structure--magnitude factorization ties strong effective edges to strong gates; a nonlinear induced gate offers another construction with the same intent. For sufficiently strong cycles, normalized logits admit a length-independent lower bound; under a unique-active-cycle assumption, the hard final-scale readout yields a polynomial log-gate gradient factor rather than exponential product-style decay. On cue-free, unstandardized Gaussian linear and nonlinear ER benchmarks, these models improve over DAGMA, run with default hyperparameters at a reduced budget, on denser graphs and are close to it on sparse ones.
  </details>

- **[Proximal Preference Optimization: A Unified Framework for Stable and Reward-Free Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10785)**  `arXiv:2611.10785`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qisai Liu, Zhanhong Jiang, Zahid Hasan, Soumik Sarkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a proximal preference optimization framework that unifies the implicit reward formulation of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) with the trust-region stability of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Our framework yields two complementary algorithms. P$^2$O (Proximal Preference Optimization) introduces a clipped importance ratio directly into the DPO preference margin, producing a reward-model-free algorithm that supports safe dataset reuse, bounded policy divergence, and monotonic preference improvement guarantees. PKTO (Proximal Kahneman-Tversky Optimization) extends P$^2$O by replacing the DPO margin with KTO's prospect-theoretic utility margins, adding a self-regulating adaptive baseline that tightens the effective trust region as the policy drifts, and an asymmetric weighting that handles imbalanced or asymmetric preference datasets. We provide a unified theoretical analysis, including Kullback Leibler (KL) trust-region bounds, a policy-improvement lower bound, and convergence guarantees under multi-epoch proximal updates. Empirically, P$^2$O and PKTO match or exceed DPO and KTO in alignment quality while maintaining better stability under repeated dataset reuse across multiple LLMs on three preference benchmarks. The code base is available here.
  </details>

- **[Causal Heteroscedastic Structure Learning From Incomplete Temporal data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10668)**  `arXiv:2611.10668`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Abdellah Rahmani, Pascal Frossard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discovering causal structures in multivariate time series (MTS) is critical in domains such as finance and neuroscience, yet remains challenging in practice due to three distinct obstacles. First, MTS are routinely corrupted by missing values, where naive impute-then-discover pipelines distort the underlying data distribution and yield spurious causal graphs. Second, identifying instantaneous effects is difficult even on complete data, and missingness further entangles imputation with graph recovery. Third, real-world data frequently exhibits heteroscedastic noise with variance driven by both instantaneous and lagged causes, violating the core assumptions of standard discovery methods. Existing approaches fail to address these challenges concurrently, typically presuming complete data, homoscedastic noise, or strictly time-lagged relationships. Crucially, we show through ablation studies that resolving these issues sequentially, rather than jointly, leads to a substantial degradation in performance. To overcome this, we introduce Cheesefill, an optimal transport framework for causal structure learning under missingness and heteroscedastic noise. Cheesefill parameterizes causal mechanisms using conditional normalizing flows and jointly learns a stochastic correction map that refines naive imputations into trajectories consistent with the inferred causal mechanisms. Theoretically, we prove that optimizing over stochastic correction maps is equivalent to a conditional kernel reformulation of the Kantorovich problem, from which our neural parameterization yields a tractable objective. Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that Cheesefill consistently outperforms existing baselines across diverse missingness regimes.
  </details>

- **[RAD-TFM: Robust and Domain-Adapted Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10230)**  `arXiv:2611.10230`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Matthew Peroni, Franck Le, Vadim Sheinin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The development of tabular foundation models (TFMs) has accelerated in recent years, outperforming traditional ML methods on many tabular benchmarks. A key finding is that TFMs can be pretrained entirely on synthetic datasets, opening opportunities to design data generators that encourage desirable model properties. Prior work has mainly focused on crafting high-quality priors over generators to improve overall pretraining performance. Our key insight is that parameterizing the generator distribution enables an adversarial, distributional robustness perspective: during training, we can adapt the generator to emphasize datasets that are particularly challenging for the model, which we formalize by introducing an optimality gap measure. Further, we develop a method to align the synthetic data generation with real-world datasets from a given domain, constraining the adversary to generate realistic data. Together, these algorithms comprise the Robust and Domain-Adapted Tabular Foundation Models (RAD-TFM) pipeline, a model-agnostic adversarial training framework. Applied to the TabPFN V2 classifier, RAD-TFM improves performance across 6 diverse tabular benchmarks, with up to a 10.8 percentage-point improvement in mean normalized AUC over the strongest baseline, using fewer than 100k additional synthetic training datasets, less than 0.1% of the original pretraining data. Applying the same pipeline to TabICL V2 also improves performance across three major benchmarks, demonstrating the generality of the framework. These results highlight a promising new direction for targeted adversarial continued training of TFMs using synthetic data alone.
  </details>

- **[Topological Deep Learning Challenge 2026: Bridging the Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10046)**  `arXiv:2611.10046`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Guillermo Bernárdez, Lev Telyatnikov, Mathilde Papillon, Marco Montagna, Louisa Cornelis, Louis Van Langendonck, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper describes the 2026 edition of the Topological Deep Learning Challenge: Bridging the Gap, hosted at the second Topology, Algebra, and Geometry in Data Science (TAG-DS) Conference. Where previous editions grew the modelling and data ecosystem of Topological Deep Learning (TDL), this edition set out to place Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and Topological Neural Networks (TNNs) side by side under a single evaluation pipeline. Participants implemented recent state-of-the-art architectures in TopoBench, with Track 1 covering GNNs and Track 2 covering TNNs. They evaluated these implementations on shared synthetic benchmarks generated with GraphUniverse, which independently controls homophily, average degree and degree-distribution heaviness. The challenge yielded 68 qualifying submissions from 34 teams, spanning the graph, simplicial, cellular, hypergraph and combinatorial domains. For the first time, submissions were also tracked on a public leaderboard, which we summarize and analyze here. Our analysis finds that the differences between the submitted tracks are strongly task-dependent: the mean accuracy gap is modest on community detection, while on triangle counting the Track 1 mean error is 1.85 times the Track 2 mean. Several structurally informed GNNs also outperform the Track 2 mean on triangle counting.
  </details>

- **[Causal Discovery Bench: Can LLMs Autonomously Conduct Effective Experiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09934)**  `arXiv:2611.09934`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuoyang Liu, Ning Peng, Ming Shen, Zijun Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language model (LLM) agents get actively adopted for autonomous research, they must not only reason over existing evidence, but also design informative experiments, allocate resources, analyze sequential observations, and decide when to stop and make conclusions. Yet these capabilities remain poorly measured. We introduce Causal-Discovery-Bench (this https URL), a verifiable sandbox with configurable structural equation models and resource constraints to evaluate sequential experimental design and causal discovery without domain-semantic cues. Across 3,802 episodes with 13 models from 5 vendors, we analyze agent behavior along 40 metrics in seven families. Agents' performance exhibits a four-level difficulty hierarchy spanning single-experiment design, sequential exploration of causal relationships, resource-aware experimentation balancing cost and statistical power, and autonomous resolution of conflicting evidence. We identify a substantial performance gap between causal inference from correctly curated evidence and navigating causal discovery through autonomous experimentation: Analyzing agents' action trajectories attributes 94.5% of their mistakes to experimental design rather than to inference, which is further consolidated with ablation studies. Relaxing resource constraints alone does not reliably resolve failures in sequential discovery. In contrast, basic statistical tools substantially improve performance across multiple model families, while advanced tools provide little additional benefit when models lack prerequisite lower-level capabilities.
  </details>

- **[The Geometry of Refusal: Why Post-Hoc Refusal Is Fragile and Pretraining-Time Refusal Persists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06934)**  `arXiv:2609.06934`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Srikanth Malla, Chiho Choi, Joon Hee Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-hoc safety training (RLHF, DPO) is the dominant way to align large language models, yet jailbreaks (Zou et al., 2023), fine-tuning attacks (Qi et al., 2024), and activation-space edits (Arditi et al., 2024) keep recovering the behaviors it was meant to remove. We give this fragility one geometric explanation and follow it into pretraining. We measure the safety update $\Delta = W_{safe} - W_{base}$ against the curvature of the model's capabilities (the empirical Fisher of a capability loss). Across five model families, post-hoc safety lands in a suppression regime: $\Delta$ is nearly orthogonal to the capability directions, and its small in-subspace part concentrates on a few high-curvature ones. The update is thin but sharp, a refusal gate laid over intact capabilities rather than erasure of them. A kernel-immobility lemma explains why such an update can only mask a capability, not remove it, and a little benign fine-tuning switches the gate off: 100 benign examples cut the AdvBench refusal of Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3-8B-Instruct by 35 to 38 pp.Following the account into pretraining, a pretraining-checkpoint sweep of OLMo-2-1B (Team OLMo et al., 2024) shows the features that refusal attaches to emerging in a sharp transition between 1B and 63B pretraining tokens. We then use the account constructively: models trained from scratch with safety co-training spread continuously across pretraining reach 87 to 98% AdvBench refusal that the same attack erodes by only 2 to 14 pp at every scale from 410M to 6.9B, at a small cost on short-answer capability probes; a windowed schedule of equal total safety weight installs no refusal. Persistence, not timing, decides whether refusal survives. It does not keep output safer: by a harmful-output classifier, the attack raises harmful completions by 8 to 12 pp after post-hoc safety and by 11 to 27 pp after co-training.
  </details>

- **[TraceML: What Auto-Research Agents Miss in Long-Horizon ML Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26086)**  `arXiv:2608.26086`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiarui Yan, Weiwei Sun, Sijie Li, Wenhan Li, Yiming Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auto-research agents now run machine-learning development unattended for hours, revising data pipelines, models, and validation from their own feedback, yet on most competitions they still finish below strong human competitors. Outcome-based benchmarks record this gap but not its cause, because they grade the final submission and discard the development process behind it. We introduce TraceML, which pairs human and agent work on the same competitions under one version-level schema: 4,465 human Kaggle trajectories across 134 competitions, seven of which are also worked by two agent scaffolds, giving 430 paired human and 207 agent trajectories. Every code version carries its score, its timestamp, and labels for the action taken, its intent, the edit size, and the score effect. Read this way, the gap becomes concrete. Experts alternate data work, validation, model changes, and ensembling, and return to approaches they had set aside. Each agent scaffold instead collapses into a narrow loop: Codex spends its steps re-weighting ensembles and tuning submissions, MLEvolve mutates its model in place, and neither pivots at the human rate nor reopens abandoned work. A short planning prompt distilled from human practice moves the behaviors it names toward the human profile and lifts scores, but the effort profile stays agent-shaped: instruction closes only the part of the gap that reduces to instructions. We release the corpus, the schema, and the labeling models, together with an open-source toolkit that turns a run from any command-line agent into a TraceML trajectory and reads it against the human cohorts.
  </details>

- **[EvoST: Adapting Traffic Forecasters to Evolving Sensor Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25875)**  `arXiv:2607.25875`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Du Yin, Xiachong Lin, Yue Tan, Ruiyi Yang, Jinliang Deng, Estrid He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web-based navigation and traffic information services depend on accurate forecasts as sensor networks evolve. On checkpoints reused years later, updating only shared weights or adding a low-rank node prompt leaves substantial degradation, whereas, in the evaluated architectures, refreshing node identities recovers most of it while the shared spatio-temporal weights are reused. We propose EvoST, a framework that maintains traffic forecasters by refreshing node identities while keeping shared weights fixed. EvoST updates existing identities using recently released labels and initializes new sensors with identities borrowed from behaviorally similar established sensors. Once enough delayed labels arrive, it jointly refits all identities, and a per-sensor recursive least-squares calibrator corrects forecasts as labels become available. Across twelve PeMS region-year settings, identity refresh reduces the relative one-hour MAE gap between five-year-old STAEFormer checkpoints and target-year models from 17.7% to 2.5%, with all forecasters calibrated, and its MAE is 0.7% above that of full-parameter fine-tuning on the same data. In Graph WaveNet, identity refresh recovers 88% of the five-year MAE gap to calibrated target-year models. Similar recovery holds for STAEFormer checkpoints up to fifteen years old, while refreshing STID recovers 67% of its five-year gap. On an annual benchmark covering nine EvoXXLTraffic districts and Transport for NSW, EvoST has the lowest average MAE, RMSE, and MAPE among the reported non-foundation methods on all ten networks, and calibration accounts for most of its Avg-MAE reduction from the matched frozen checkpoints. Code and the extended version:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Epistemic Uncertainty: Beyond OOD Detection and Active Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14817)**  `arXiv:2607.14817`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jakub Paplhám, Willem Waegeman, Eyke Hüllermeier, Vojtěch Franc_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current evaluation of epistemic uncertainty relies on tasks such as out-of-distribution detection, active learning and selective prediction. However, common epistemic uncertainty scores, such as mutual information or the variance of the predictions, estimate the regret, the excess error of a predictor over the Bayes-optimal predictor. Building on the epistemic reject-option framework, we evaluate epistemic uncertainty using its ability to identify regret, and compare this evaluation with the other tasks. Benchmarking standard methods on datasets with repeated human labels reveals that the tasks rank the methods differently, including pairwise rank inversions between methods that are top-ranked on one criterion and bottom-ranked on another.
