The disagreement is present already at the optimum, where each task calls for a different score. Formulating selective prediction as a constrained optimization over coverage, expected risk, and regret, we prove that the optimal score is a convex combination of the ground-truth aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties, so that selective prediction and the regret task are the two endpoints of one family of tasks. Out-of-distribution detection, active learning and the regret task thus disagree, theoretically as well as empirically, on which method is a good estimator of epistemic uncertainty, while selective prediction departs from the regret task where the true risk and the regret order the inputs differently. Researchers must therefore state the downstream decision that an epistemic uncertainty estimate is meant to support, instead of relying on a general notion of model ignorance.
  </details>

- **[A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Framework for Distributional Shifts in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16933)**  `arXiv:2606.16933`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ardianto Wibowo, Paulo E Santos, Amer Baghdadi, Matthew Stephenson, Karl Sammut, Jean-Philippe Diguet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distributional shift in reinforcement learning (RL) is defined inconsistently across the literature, and it is studied alongside out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization and non-stationarity without a clear account of how the three terms are related. Methods are also often described as robust after evaluation under only a few particular changes, leaving the scope of such claims unclear. We develop a unified taxonomy that brings the three terms under one definition of distributional shift. We factorize the joint distribution of a one-step interaction into five components, each produced by its own generating function: initial-state distribution, observation mechanism, policy implementation (distinct from the policy being optimized), transition dynamics, and reward mechanism. A shift is specified by what changes (the changed components) and when it changes (the interaction regime: OOD, non-stationary, or combined), while changes in interaction data and performance are treated as observed effects. We complement the taxonomy with an evaluation framework that measures immediate generalization and subsequent adaptation, summarizing adaptation in a shift adaptation score (SAS). Published benchmark results, controlled Gridworld experiments with DQN, A2C, and PPO, and a mapping of selected studies show that method responses and rankings vary with the shifted component, and that existing evaluations cover only some components. Robustness claims should therefore be bounded by the components and regimes evaluated, since strong performance under one shift does not establish robustness under others. Identifying an unknown component-level source from observed interaction data remains a direction for future research.
  </details>

- **[Generative OOD-regularized Model-based Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24405)**  `arXiv:2605.24405`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Aysin Tumay, Jiahe Huang, Elise Jortberg, Rose Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study sequential decision-making with offline reinforcement learning (RL). Traditional offline RL policies may result in out-of-distribution (OOD) actions when training relies only on sparse offline representations. To ensure safe offline policies in a sparse state-action space, we explore how density estimation models can be integrated into model-based RL methods to avoid OOD regions. Generative models are capable of explicitly modeling the density in sparse state-action spaces. Building on this, we introduce Generative OOD-regularized Model-based Policy Optimization (GORMPO), a density-regularized offline RL algorithm that uses generative density modeling to restrict policy updates to high-density areas of the dataset. Furthermore, we examine whether better OOD detection corresponds to better model-based offline policies. We compare (1) the OOD detection capabilities of various density estimators and (2) their performance within the GORMPO framework on a real-world medical dataset and sparse offline RL datasets. We provide theoretical guarantees bounding GORMPO's performance gap in terms of density estimation error, ensuring near-optimal policies under imperfect density estimates. Empirically, GORMPO remains competitive in reward and improves clinical metrics compared to state-of-the-art clinical RL models, and outperforms state-of-the-art offline RL models in offline RL benchmarks. Our empirical findings show that better OOD detection generally results in improved policies in environments with stable dynamics, while conservative penalties with poor density estimation are favored when dynamics are uncertain. Code, checkpoints, sparse, and OOD D4RL datasets are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Teacher-Guided Policy Optimization for On-Policy Reasoning Distillation under Large Policy Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13230)**  `arXiv:2605.13230`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Liu, Kechen Jiao, Chunyang Xiao, Runsong Zhao, Junhao Ruan, Bei Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) has become a promising paradigm for reasoning-oriented post-training of large language models (LLMs), especially when combined with reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR). Existing OPD methods rely on reverse KL (RKL)-based teacher supervision over trajectories sampled from the student policy. However, we identify a critical limitation: under large teacher--student policy divergence, RL-driven exploration often produces trajectories outside the teacher distribution, resulting in uninformative negative feedback. To address this, we propose Teacher-Guided Policy Optimization (TGPO), an on-policy reasoning distillation method that remains effective under large policy divergence settings. Rather than relying solely on evaluative supervision, TGPO uses teacher to directly guide token level generation conditioning on student-generated contexts; together with RLVR-style trajectory level rewards, TGPO steers exploration toward improved continuations. Experiments on reasoning benchmarks show that TGPO consistently outperforms existing RKL-based OPD methods and remains robust across different teacher models.
  </details>

- **[Active Learning for Gaussian Process Regression Under Self-Induced Boltzmann Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10654)**  `arXiv:2605.10654`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jixiang Qing, Henry Moss, Matthias Sachs_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We consider the active learning problem where the goal is to learn an unknown function with low prediction error under an unknown Boltzmann distribution induced by the function itself. This self-induced weighting arises naturally in problems such as potential energy surface (PES) modeling in computational chemistry,yet poses unique challenges as the target distribution is unknown and its partition function is intractable. We propose \texttt{AB-SID-iVAR}, a Gaussian Process-based acquisition function that approximates the intractable Bayesian target distribution in closed form while avoiding partition function estimation, and is applicable to both discrete and continuous input domains. We also analyze a Thompson sampling alternative (\texttt{TS-SID-iVAR}) as a higher variance Monte Carlo variant. Despite the unknown target, under mild conditions, we establish that the terminal prediction error vanishes with high probability, and provide a tighter average-case guarantee. We demonstrate consistent improvements over existing approaches in this setting on synthetic benchmarks and real-world PES modeling and drug discovery tasks. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Global Feature Collapse in Graph ODEs via Hysteretic Topology Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24293)**  `arXiv:2604.24293`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qinhan Hou, Jing Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph neural ordinary differential equations provide continuous-time propagation on graphs, but their long-time behavior is largely determined by the asymptotic structure of the mixing operator. We show that diffusion-style Graph ODEs with positive irreducible mixing face a \emph{monostability trap}, where node features converge to a rank-one consensus subspace. This reveals a structural limitation of positive diffusion-style continuous graph propagation: within this consensus class, exact long-time avoidance requires changing the effective support; otherwise, smooth suppression should be interpreted as finite-horizon or metastable mitigation. We address this by introducing a \emph{hysteretic mechanism} for topology evolution, where candidate edges carry latent bistable potentials in a double-well landscape. A node-feature-conditioned force field evolves these potentials, and a smooth sigmoid gate converts them into differentiable propagation weights. We prove global consensus under the stated mixing assumptions and block-wise consensus under idealised support separation. For consensus-form dynamics, we further establish a finite-horizon perturbation bound under small cross-block leakage, and empirically evaluate the resulting nonlinear HGODE on node- and graph-level benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Conformal PM2.5 Mapping Under Spatial Covariate Shift: Satellite-Reanalysis Fusion for Africa's Green Industrial Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22787)**  `arXiv:2604.22787`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yaw Osei Adjei, Davis Opoku, Ephraim Abotsi, Kwadwo Owusu Amanqua, Oliver Kornyo, Elisha Soglo-Ahianyo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Africa's green industrialization imperative demands reliable infrastructure for monitoring air quality. We present a satellite-reanalysis PM2.5 fusion system trained on 2,068,901 records from 404 monitoring locations in 29 African countries (OpenAQ, 2017-2022), combining LightGBM with leakage-resistant spatial cross-validation and conformal prediction to quantify predictions and their geographic applicability limits. Under 5-fold location-grouped spatial cross-validation, LightGBM achieves RMSE = 30.83 +/- 5.07 ug/m3, MAE = 14.54 +/- 1.66 ug/m3, R2 = 0.134 +/- 0.023, and macro F1 = 0.336 +/- 0.018. This R2 is substantially below random-split benchmarks (>0.90) but reflects true geographic generalisation difficulty rather than model failure. Split conformal prediction targeting 90% marginal coverage reveals severe East Africa degradation (actual PICP = 65.3% vs. nominal 90%), consistent with medium-strength covariate shift (humidity KS = 0.2237, sat_pblh KS = 0.2558). We operationalise these findings through regional reliability flags (High/Medium/Low/Unreliable) and a monitor prioritisation score directing infrastructure expansion toward highest-burden unmonitored populations, directly supporting Africa's green industrial transition and SDGs 3.9, 7.1.2, 9, 11.6.2, and 13.
  </details>

- **[Target-Aligned Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.29501)**  `arXiv:2603.29501`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Leonard S. Pleiss, James Harrison, Maximilian Schiffer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many value-based deep reinforcement learning algorithms rely on target networks - lagged copies of the online network - to stabilize training. While effective, this mechanism introduces a fundamental stability-recency trade-off: slower target updates improve stability but reduce the recency of learning signals, hindering convergence speed. We propose Target-Aligned Reinforcement Learning (TARL), a simple drop-in refinement for existing algorithms that emphasizes transitions for which the target and online network estimates are highly aligned. By focusing updates on well-aligned targets, TARL mitigates the adverse effects of stale target estimates while retaining the stabilizing benefits of target networks. We empirically demonstrate consistent improvements within discrete and continuous control algorithms across various benchmark environments without any hyperparameter tuning, including a 38.18% median peak score gain on Atari-10, while incurring less than a 4% increase in wall-clock time.
  </details>

- **[Understanding the Challenges in Iterative Generative Optimization with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23994)**  `arXiv:2603.23994`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Allen Nie, Xavier Daull, Zhiyi Kuang, Abhinav Akkiraju, Anish Chaudhuri, Max Piasevoli, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative optimization uses large language models (LLMs) to iteratively improve artifacts (such as code, workflows or prompts) using execution feedback. It is a promising approach to building self-improving agents, yet in practice remains brittle: despite active research, only 9% of surveyed agents used any automated optimization. We argue that this brittleness arises because, to set up a learning loop, an engineer must make "hidden" design choices: What can the optimizer edit and what is the ``right'' learning evidence to provide at each update? We investigate three factors that affect most applications: the starting artifact, the credit horizon for execution traces, and batching trials and errors into learning evidence. Through case studies in MLAgentBench, Atari, and BigBench Extra Hard, we find that these design decisions can determine whether generative optimization succeeds, yet they are rarely made explicit in prior work. Different starting artifacts determine which solutions are reachable in MLAgentBench, truncated traces can still improve Atari agents, and larger minibatches do not monotonically improve generalization on BBEH. We then study these factors jointly in harness optimization on TBLite, evaluated on Terminal-Bench 2.0. We also propose a batch-size and credit-horizon rule that, calibrated on TBLite, transfers to DeepSWE after adjusting to its token costs and comes within 1% of the best configuration tested at each context budget.
  </details>

- **[Soft Contamination Means Benchmarks Test Shallow Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12413)**  `arXiv:2602.12413`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ari Spiesberger, Juan J. Vazquez, Nicky Pochinkov, Tomáš Gavenčiak, Peli Grietzer, Gavin Leech, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training on benchmark test items is a classic way to overestimate generalization to unseen problems. Standard decontamination methods miss semantic duplicates (SemDs): training items equivalent in content to a test item, but distant in string space. We call training on such items soft contamination; when the resulting gains extend to held-out items of the same benchmark (but not to related benchmarks), we call the result shallow generalization. We first embed Olmo 3's entire post-training dataset and search it for SemDs of benchmark items; we then finetune Olmo 3 and Qwen models on natural and synthetic semantic duplicates. We find (1) that soft contamination is widespread: the Olmo corpus contains SemDs of 76% of the CodeForces test set and 100% of MBPP's; (2) that finetuning on SemDs of a benchmark raises accuracy on its held-out items by up to 22pp in controlled experiments and 10-11pp at realistic contamination rates, though the effect varies across benchmarks; (3) that the gains from training on SemDs mostly do not transfer to conceptually similar benchmarks, i.e. we find shallow generalization; (4) that finetuning on items with similar embeddings that are not SemDs produces no such gain, indicating that the mechanism is not mere topical similarity; and (5) that at realistic contamination rates, soft contamination can raise held-out performance about as much as exact duplicates (or more). Benchmark gains here are therefore confounded - in a way that standard decontamination does not address.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Grid: Distance-Guided Reinforcement Learning in Large Discrete Action Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08616)**  `arXiv:2602.08616`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Heiko Hoppe, Fabian Akkerman, Wouter van Heeswijk, Maximilian Schiffer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning (RL) is increasingly applied to large-scale decision-making problems like logistics, scheduling, and recommender systems, but existing algorithms struggle with the curse of dimensionality in such large discrete action spaces. We propose Distance-Guided Reinforcement Learning (DGRL), combining Sampled Dynamic Neighborhoods and Distance-Based Updates to enable efficient RL in problems with up to $10^{36}$ actions. Unlike prior methods, DGRL performs stochastic volumetric exploration and transforms policy optimization into a stable regression task, decoupling gradient variance from action space cardinality. On structured tasks, DGRL provably guarantees local value improvement. DGRL naturally handles action spaces without regular semantic structure and hybrid continuous-discrete action spaces. We demonstrate performance improvements of up to 66% against state-of-the-art benchmarks across regularly and irregularly structured environments, while simultaneously improving convergence speed and computational complexity.
  </details>

- **[Advancing Model Refinement: Muon-Optimized Distillation and Quantization for LLM Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09865)**  `arXiv:2601.09865`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jacob Sander, Brian Jalaian, Venkat R. Dasari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Correction notice (v2). Two implementation issues affect these experiments. All LoRA training ran under DeepSpeed, which replaced the optimizer passed to the Trainer with AdamW at a constant learning rate, so the Muon and Adam arms used the same optimizer. The cross-entropy term was computed without the next-token shift, so the preference for alpha = 1 reflects that objective rather than the value of the teacher. Each comparison also uses a single GPTQ quantization, and repeated quantizations differ by up to several points. The findings on Muon's quantization robustness, the distillation weight and accuracy relative to GPTQ alone should not be relied on. See the notice on page 1.Large Language Models (LLMs) enable advanced natural language processing but face deployment challenges on resource-constrained edge devices due to high computational, memory, and energy demands. Optimizing these models requires addressing three key challenges: acquiring task-specific data, fine-tuning for performance, and compressing models to accelerate inference while reducing resource demands. We propose an integrated framework combining GPTQ-based quantization, low-rank adaptation (LoRA), and a specialized data distillation process to significantly reduce model size and complexity while preserving or enhancing task-specific performance. By leveraging data distillation, knowledge distillation via Kullback-Leibler divergence, Bayesian hyperparameter optimization, and the Muon optimizer, our pipeline achieves up to 2x memory compression (e.g., reducing a 6GB model to 3GB) and enables efficient inference for specialized tasks. Empirical results demonstrate superior performance on standard LLM benchmarks compared to GPTQ quantization alone, with the Muon optimizer notably enhancing fine-tuned models' resistance to accuracy decay during quantization.
  </details>

- **[Equivariant Spherical Transformer for Efficient Molecular Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23086)**  `arXiv:2505.23086`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Junyi An, Xinyu Lu, Yunfei Shi, Peijia Lin, Licheng Xu, Qianwei Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Equivariant Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have significantly advanced the modeling of 3D molecular structure by leveraging group representations. However, message passing built only from exactly equivariant operations can have restricted practical expressiveness at a fixed representation degree. We introduce the Equivariant Spherical Transformer (EST), a plug-and-play module that applies Transformer attention to spherical spatial representations obtained from group representations. EST explicitly mixes information across sampled orientations, unlike prior orientation-wise spherical operations, while retaining a strong approximate equivariant inductive bias through near-uniform spherical sampling. As demonstrated by our experiments on challenging benchmarks like OC20, MPtrj and QM9, EST-based models achieve state-of-the-art performance. For the complex molecular systems within OC20, small models empowered by EST can outperform some larger models and those using additional data. We characterize the ideal closed-domain condition under which the continuous operation is exactly equivariant and empirically quantify the finite-sampling accuracy--equivariance--memory trade-off used in practice.
  </details>

- **[Language in the Flow of Time: Time-Series-Paired Texts Weaved into a Unified Temporal Narrative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08942)**  `arXiv:2502.08942`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihao Li, Xiao Lin, Zhining Liu, Jiaru Zou, Ziwei Wu, Lecheng Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While many advances in time series models focus exclusively on numerical data, research on multimodal time series, particularly those involving contextual textual information, remains in its infancy. With recent progress in large language models and time series learning, we revisit the integration of paired texts with time series through the Platonic Representation Hypothesis, which posits that representations of different modalities converge to shared spaces. In this context, we identify that time-series-paired texts may naturally exhibit periodic properties that closely mirror those of the original time series. Building on this insight, we propose a novel framework, Texts as Time Series (TaTS), which considers the time-series-paired texts to be auxiliary variables of the time series. TaTS can be plugged into any existing numerical-only time series models and effectively enable them to handle time series data with paired texts. Through extensive experiments on both multimodal time series forecasting and imputation tasks across benchmark datasets with various existing time series models, we demonstrate that TaTS can enhance multimodal predictive performance without modifying model architectures. Our Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Generalized Influence Functions for Better Model Change Estimates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.05586)**  `arXiv:2312.05586`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hyeonsu Lyu, Jonggyu Jang, Sehyun Ryu, Hyun Jong Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Influence functions (IFs) approximate the effect of removing or reweighting training data on a trained model, but their linear approximation often lacks the accuracy required for reliable post hoc model editing. We identify two culprits and propose generalized influence functions (GIFs) and pseudo-LiSSA (p-LiSSA) to address each in turn. First, classical IFs update all parameters indiscriminately, leading to unnecessary updates to data-irrelevant parameters. GIFs instead restrict edits to a data-relevant subspace and leave the remaining parameters fixed. We theoretically show that GIFs achieve a tighter error bound than classical IFs when the parameter subset is properly selected, and that effective subspace selection requires avoiding low-curvature directions while retaining a sufficiently large component of the target gradient. Second, LiSSA iterations are destabilized by negative Hessian eigenvalues, which existing methods handle via $\ell_2$ damping, at the cost of a biased inverse-Hessian-vector product (iHVP) estimate. p-LiSSA eliminates this bias by solving the restricted inverse-Hessian problem without such damping. We further show that the presence of low-curvature directions slows p-LiSSA convergence, and that the data-relevant subspace selected by GIFs mitigates this. Across five image and text unlearning benchmarks, GIFs update only 10% of the parameters yet preserve accuracy up to 2.12 percentage points better than IF-based baselines, while improving unlearning performance by up to 2.73 percentage points. Moreover, p-LiSSA improves restricted iHVP estimation by reducing the residual by 9.6$\times$ over existing iHVP baselines, while using 19.8% less memory with comparable computation time. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Vendi Anomaly Scores for Efficient and Accurate Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12871)**  `arXiv:2611.12871`  `cs.LG`  
  _Amey Pasarkar, Adji Bousso Dieng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomaly detection (AD) is a critical for trustworthy machine learning and scientific discovery. Existing methods typically define anomalies through local density, isolation heuristics, or distance from a fitted distribution, making them sensitive to neighborhood scale, partitioning choices, or restrictive distributional assumptions. In this work, we introduce a new paradigm by formulating anomaly detection from a diversity perspective. We propose the $\textit{Vendi Anomaly Score}$ (VAS), which detects anomalies by quantifying how much a sample changes the diversity of the dataset, as measured by the Vendi score, when removed. VAS captures both local redundancy and global data structure without relying on density estimation or partitioning heuristics. Moreover, VAS requires no hyperparameter tuning, instead adapting automatically to the spectral structure of the data. VAS is non-parametric and scales linearly with dataset size. Across 148 benchmark embedding datasets derived from 10 source datasets and 4 embedding architectures, VAS achieves state-of-the-art AD performance. We further validate VAS on large-scale ImageNet experiments, where it remains robust as both contamination rate and dataset scale increase.
  </details>

- **[Inversion-Free High-Dimensional Counterfactual Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12815)**  `arXiv:2611.12815`  `cs.LG`  
  _Saketh Vishnubhatla, Pingchuan Ma, Shu Wan, Alimohammad Beigi, Andre Harrison, Adrienne Raglin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual inference is the task of answering hypothetical "what-if" queries about a unit, given an observational dataset, modeling assumptions, and a causal graph. This enables a modeler to reason about hypothetical scenarios that cannot otherwise be answered without assumptions or a causal graph. The simpler case involves a causal graph with scalar 1-D variables that denote different attributes in the data-generation process. While the literature is clear in the 1-D case, extending this to settings with high-dimensional nodes (e.g., images) remains challenging. Conventionally, in the literature, methods recover the latent state from the factual image (inversion), generate counterfactual attributes from the graph, and render the counterfactual image from the latent state using the updated attributes. In our work, we investigate the necessity of the inversion step in computing high-dimensional counterfactuals. We first discuss moving from the scalar 1-D case to the higher-dimensional case. Secondly, we discuss the relevance of the inversion step and evaluate an inversion-free counterpart repurposed from the image-editing literature. Finally, on image datasets such as CelebA and ADNI, we benchmark our approach using two measures: namely, effectiveness and identity preservation. While effectiveness is a measure of how faithfully the counterfactual attribute is rendered, identity preservation measures whether the same unit (image) is being rendered, both of which are crucial for high-dimensional counterfactual inference. We present the tradeoffs of using inversion-based and inversion-free approaches in our work.
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Adaptive Trust-Region Bayesian Optimization for Open-Ended Molecular Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12591)**  `arXiv:2611.12591`  `cs.LG`  
  _Madhav R. Muthyala, Mahsa Askari, Farshud Sorourifar, Evan Zhou, Joe Gauthier, Joel A. Paulson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing molecules under small evaluation budgets requires deciding both where in chemical space to search and which candidate within that region to evaluate. We introduce Trust Region Adaptive Chemical Exploration (TRACE), a framework for open-ended molecular Bayesian optimization (BO), where candidates are generated on demand rather than selected from a fixed library. TRACE combines an objective-free graph-perturbation mechanism with a fixed, chemistry-aware Gaussian process (GP) surrogate using fragprints, a representation combining molecular fingerprints and fragment descriptors. Rather than learning the search geometry itself, TRACE makes the scale of chemical exploration explicit through a Tanimoto-similarity threshold controlling chemical extent and a cardinality budget controlling candidate-search breadth. A simple success/failure controller expands or contracts both quantities during optimization, while the GP and acquisition function select which admissible molecules to evaluate. Across six challenging molecular optimization benchmarks under matched 1,000-evaluation budgets, TRACE achieves the strongest overall sample efficiency as measured by the best molecule found as evaluations accumulate. Crossed ablations on two objectives further show that adaptive region control contributes beyond the proposal mechanism, while its benefit depends on the proposal distribution. We further apply TRACE to the stability of iron phthalocyanine (FePc), a non-precious molecular electrocatalyst whose degradation can involve loss of the Fe center. Using a first-principles density functional theory (DFT) workflow designed for accurate energy evaluation, TRACE increases the computed thermodynamic resistance to Fe dissolution from 0.42 to 2.59 eV over 80 designed evaluations.
  </details>

- **[UCB-min: Combining Bandits Under Variance Heterogeneity (Extended Version)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12427)**  `arXiv:2611.12427`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lilia Izri, Benoit Groz, Silviu Maniu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-armed bandits are a natural framework for many real-world sequential decision problems, from recommender systems and clinical trials to educational test recommendation and online advertising. The performance of these algorithms depends on how they explore different arms, and exploration strategies range from variance-independent (e.g., UCB1) to variance-aware (e.g., UCB-V). In practice the reward variance of each arm is rarely known and varies widely across arms, and the reward support is often underspecified. In these cases no single rule is uniformly best: variance-independent methods are robust but loose when variances are small, while variance-aware methods are tight only when their variance estimates and the assumed support bound are reliable. Such heterogeneous, unknown variances arise in many settings, for instance when multiple objectives are scalarized into a single reward. We propose UCB-min, a per-arm adaptive meta-algorithm that selects, for each arm, the tighter of a variance-independent and a variance-aware exploration bonus, without prior knowledge of the variance regime and without reward normalization. We prove that, with UCB-V as variance-aware base, its regret is bounded by the sum of the two base bounds at the standard exploration constant, and by their per-arm minimum, up to a constant, for a larger one. We validate it on synthetic data, an educational tutoring task, and two group-recommendation benchmarks (MovieLens, Steam), showing that UCB-min consistently tracks the best base algorithm across diverse variance regimes and datasets.
  </details>

- **[Learning Evidence-Grounded Reasoning for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12285)**  `arXiv:2611.12285`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xingpeng Gao, Mingyue Cheng, Daoyu Wang, Huajian Zhang, Xiaoyu Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven time series forecasting has become increasingly promising by leveraging contextual features to generate future values directly or refine forecast priors. However, incorporating contextual features alone does not ensure that models can identify which features matter for forecasting and understand what they suggest about future dynamics. We argue that the fundamental challenge is how to extract predictive evidence from contextual features and when and how to leverage it to guide forecasting decisions. In this paper, we propose CastReasoner, an evidence-grounded reasoning model for context-aware time series forecasting. Specifically, CastReasoner uses a structured chain of thought (CoT) to extract predictive evidence from contextual features, infer the future dynamics suggested by this evidence, and assess these dynamics against the forecast prior to guide adaptive forecasting decisions. To enable this capability, we design a reasoning-centric two-stage post-training framework including supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). First, we synthesize reasoning trajectories through controllable multi-teacher guidance and select high-quality ones using a novel student-aware difficulty metric. Second, we design fine-grained multi-objective rewards to further improve reasoning and forecasting. To address heterogeneous learning needs across domains in both stages, we introduce a convergence-guided data balancing strategy. Through this two-stage training, CastReasoner improves both reasoning quality and predictive accuracy. Extensive experiments across multi-domain forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that CastReasoner achieves superior forecasting performance.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Flat Walks: Compositional Abstraction for Autoregressive Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12160)**  `arXiv:2611.12160`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiwen Bian, Andrew H. Yang, Yusu Wang, Ali Parviz, Gal Mishne_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive graph generators typically linearize graphs through flat node-edge walks, which can obscure higher-order structure in large and complex graphs. We introduce a hierarchical graph abstraction and coarse-to-fine generation pipeline that separates coarsening, tokenization, and decoding. This exposes three design axes: community coarsening, hierarchical tokenization, and order randomization. Across these axes, traversal-based hierarchy consistently improves distributional and structural fidelity, while its V.U.N.\ cost depends strongly on the coarsening and randomization policy. In particular, the fidelity--V.U.N.\ trade-off observed under within-community randomization is substantially reduced when traversal order is randomized across the full hierarchy. With a fixed backbone, training protocol, and matched training time across five molecular and five non-molecular benchmarks, hierarchical serialization yields consistent distributional and structural gains on all molecular datasets, while the non-molecular results further show that these gains depend on how well the supplied hierarchy aligns with structure in the target distribution. Together, these results characterize when and how hierarchical serialization benefits autoregressive graph generation.
  </details>

- **[Do Time-Series Foundation Models Forecast Index-Futures Returns? A Preregistered Evaluation of Models, Metrics, and Input Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12141)**  `arXiv:2611.12141`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rishabh Upadhyay_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We ask whether the apparent forecasting skill of frozen time-series foundation models (TSFMs) on intraday index-futures returns is incremental skill. In a pre-registered evaluation on E-mini Nasdaq-100 (NQ) and E-mini S&P 500 (ES) futures, a common panel of five zero-shot models (Chronos-Bolt, Chronos-2, TimesFM 2.5, Moirai 2.0 and TiRex), plus separate experiments with Kronos, is compared with a no-change forecast at 5-120 minutes, using native prices, log returns, volatility-standardised returns and a volume clock as inputs. On a blind set opened once, 0 of 160 model-representation-horizon-market cells beat no-change, and a Hansen SPA test gives p = 1.000 on both markets; a later retrospective hold-out, scored as a descriptive check, gives 0 of 120. Input representations change forecast magnitude but not accuracy, and a transformation-aware ridge regression does as well. Several standard measures nevertheless suggest skill. Price-level correlation exceeds 0.9996, but no-change scores higher. Within-day RankIC across forecast origins rewards a trivial revert-to-mean rule (+0.35 at 30 minutes); we derive the expected within-day covariance under a random walk in closed form, and a sign-randomised null reproduces about 95% of the observed value. One model beats HAR for 5-minute volatility, but not the EWMA its input already contains. On strongly planted synthetic signals the models recover part of the signal at 5 minutes, and recovery deteriorates at 30 minutes. Benchmark-level success of TSFMs should therefore not be assumed to carry over to low-signal domains such as intraday returns.
  </details>

- **[TSFFormer: A Frequency-Domain Spike Transformer for Green Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12090)**  `arXiv:2611.12090`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiale Deng, Shuiying Xiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearable Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) enable pervasive Human Activity Recognition (HAR), but traditional Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) suffer from an "arithmetic-latency deadlock" due to dense floating-point MAC operations. While Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) offer ultra-low power, existing ones face spatial blind spots, rigid neurons, and gradient degradation. To address these, we propose TSFFormer, a purely spiking Fourier Transformer for Green HAR. It introduces two key innovations: (1) a MultiHeadFourier attention that performs frequency-domain global mixing, reducing complexity from \(O(V^2)\) to \(O(V\log V)\), eliminating spatial blind spots, and saving \(\sim\)50% GPU memory; and (2) a dual-compartment Temporal Spiking LIF (TSLIFNode) that utilizes dynamic thresholds to adapt to non-stationary rhythms and mitigate gradient degradation. Extensive experiments on 8 public IMU benchmarks demonstrate that TSFFormer surpasses state-of-the-art SNNs and even the classic DeepConvLSTM, achieving a \(\sim\)3\(\times\) faster training speed with an energy footprint of 0.1 pJ. Ablation studies confirm the synergistic effect of our modules, providing a highly accurate, ultra-low-power event-driven paradigm for wearable sensing.
  </details>

- **[Correct First, Fast Second: What a Local 7B Code Model Can and Cannot Do for GPU Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12079)**  `arXiv:2611.12079`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jui-Chien Tsou, Po-Yi Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how much the interaction strategy around a fixed, locally hosted 7B-parameter code model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) affects its ability to replace PyTorch operators with custom CUDA kernels. On 30 KernelBench tasks (levels 1-3, first 10 each, evaluated on a V100), we compare four regimes of increasing structure: zero-shot prompting, profiling-guided prompting, iterative refinement on execution feedback, and a multi-agent loop (analysis, retrieval, generation, evaluation, feedback triage) in which the same model fills four roles around a non-LLM evaluator. Three findings stand out. (1) Correctness, not speed, is the binding constraint: even correct generated kernels average 0.97-0.99x the speed of eager PyTorch, because a 7B model cannot out-write cuBLAS/cuDNN on dense GEMM and convolution; the only real wins are fused memory-bound epilogues. (2) The best strategy depends on difficulty: profiling-guided prompting is strongest overall (43% correct, 27% faster than baseline), but the agent loop is the only method that solves any level-2 fusion problem (3/10 vs. 0/10 for all others). (3) Naive agentic scaffolding is actively harmful: our first agent scored 3% correct, below zero-shot's 30%, and the decisive repairs were matching the agent's ambition to the model's competence and spending compute on best-of-n sampling under a real execution oracle (3% to 33% correct from n=1 to n=8), not on more reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[Operation-aware Multimodal Clinical Decision Support System for ICU Discharge Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11960)**  `arXiv:2611.11960`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guoqing Zhang, Rafik Hadfi, Takayuki Ito_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unplanned 30-day intensive care unit (ICU) readmission is a critical benchmark for evaluating healthcare quality. Most predictive models assume readmission risk depends solely on a patient's individual pathophysiological state, ignoring hospital operational context and resource capacity. In practice, discharge decisions are dynamic trade-offs between patient stability and bed turnover pressures. To address this gap, we develop an Operation-aware Multimodal Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) for ICU discharge risk assessment. The system combines multi-agent collaborative extraction with self-evolving retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to eliminate template noise from progress notes, while constructing departmental workload indicators alongside admission flow features, continuous vital signs, and sparse medical codes (ICD/DRG). Addressing severe class imbalance (about 10.5% readmissions) and alarm fatigue, we implement data normalization, class reweighting, and decision-threshold calibration. In 20 independent runs across 20,000 ICU stays from MIMIC-III, empirical findings demonstrate that: vital signs alone have limited predictive capacity under real-world class imbalance (PR-AUC of 0.1387 +/- 0.0086); directly concatenating raw text embeddings degrades decision tree performance (XGBoost and LightGBM PR-AUC drop to 0.3893 and 0.4030); whereas an end-to-end multimodal fine-tuning architecture (Transformer E2E FineTune) utilizing layer-wise parameter freezing and differential learning rates reverses text negative transfer, achieving the highest AUROC of 0.8288 +/- 0.0110, PR-AUC of 0.4373 +/- 0.0310, and F1-Score of 0.4757 +/- 0.0196. Operational indicators supply independent risk signals, laying an engineering foundation for high-utility clinical decision support tools.
  </details>

- **[Decision Aware Origin-Destination Demand Estimation with Structurally Constrained Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11946)**  `arXiv:2611.11946`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xianzhe Wang, Yueyue Fan, Johannes Royset_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Origin-destination (OD) demand plays an important role in transportation system design and management applications. Conventional OD estimation methods typically judge estimation quality based on how estimated demand aligns with observed traffic data and historical demand patterns. We argue that data alignment alone is insufficient; how errors in OD estimates influence downstream decision quality should also be considered. This paper develops a decision aware OD demand estimation framework that leverages structurally constrained traffic data to characterize an admissible demand region, within which we identify OD estimates that would lead to least regret in the downstream decision. Therefore, our OD estimates are both data-consistent and decision-relevant. Methodologically, we approximate the admissible demand region induced by physical network constraints through a geometric representation, which regularizes the search over feasible OD estimates. Within this regularized region, we use a convex surrogate of downstream regret to obtain a tractable decision aware optimization problem. Numerical experiments show that the proposed framework consistently reduces decision regret relative to conventional estimation benchmarks, demonstrating the value of aligning OD demand estimation with downstream decision quality.
  </details>

- **[Can Hindsight Critics Guide Delayed Actors?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11863)**  `arXiv:2611.11863`  `cs.LG`  
  _Francisco M. López, Francisco Cruz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sensory delays create temporal misalignments between the state of the environment and the observations available to an agent, turning Markovian control problems into partially observable ones. In this work, we inquire whether such misalignments can be mitigated during training by using hindsight to recover aligned state-action transitions. We introduce Hindsight Critics, an asymmetric extension of the Soft Actor-Critic for reinforcement learning under partial observability. The critics are trained on hindsight-aligned transitions whereas the actor learns from the resulting privileged value estimates despite only having access to delayed observations. Comparisons against delay-aware and unaware baselines on the DelayBench suite show that Hindsight Critics can partially compensate for observation delays without requiring modifications to the agent's observations during deployment.
  </details>

- **[MOOD: Benchmarking Post-Hoc OOD Detection for Materials Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11839)**  `arXiv:2611.11839`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shirley X. Yu, Amey P. Pasarkar, Adji Bousso Dieng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is essential for deploying machine learning models in high stakes scientific applications, yet methods that flag OOD inputs without retraining the model have been validated almost exclusively on image classification. We introduce MOOD (Materials Out-of-Distribution Detection), a benchmark for evaluating whether these post-hoc OOD detectors transfer to graph neural networks (GNNs) on materials property prediction. MOOD evaluates 15 post-hoc detectors and 2 supervised diagnostic probes across 4 GNN architectures, 5 MatBench tasks, and 15 physically motivated compositional and structural shifts, yielding 264 evaluation settings and over 13,000 AUROC measurements across three encoder training seeds. Our evaluation reveals three key findings. First, current post-hoc detectors perform near chance; all 15 detectors achieve an AUROC around 0.5. Second, detector failures decompose into two regimes: method bottlenecks and encoder bottlenecks. Method bottlenecks arise when supervised probes can separate in-distribution (ID) from OOD samples but unsupervised detectors fail, meaning that the embeddings contain OOD signal that current detectors do not extract. Encoder bottlenecks arise when even supervised probes fail to separate ID from OOD samples, indicating that the relevant shift is not accessible in the frozen GNN representation. Third, when OOD prediction error exceeds ID error, the AUROC of distance-based and density-based detectors correlates with the size of that gap. Collectively, these findings indicate that reliable OOD detection in scientific domains requires representation learning strategies that explicitly preserve OOD relevant signal, particularly for tasks where current GNN architectures encode no extractable signal at all.
  </details>

- **[Variational Approach to Optimal IPS Estimator for Multi-logger Off-Policy Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11712)**  `arXiv:2611.11712`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joon Suk Huh, Junghoon Seo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study off-policy evaluation (OPE) in contextual bandits with data collected from multiple logging policies. Inverse propensity scoring (IPS) is a standard approach to OPE and extends naturally to the multi-logger setting. However, as highlighted by Agarwal et al. [2017], neither balanced nor weighted IPS consistently outperforms the other in this setting. We resolve this dilemma by deriving an optimal IPS estimator with sample-dependent weights that minimize variance subject to unbiasedness. Using a variational calculus approach, we obtain closed-form optimal weights, yielding an estimator that is unbiased and achieves asymptotically optimal variance within a generalized weighted-IPS estimator class. Instead of a reward model, the estimator needs only a $K\times K$ matrix and a $K$-vector of moments, where $K$ is the number of logging policies, and we quantify how much efficiency this costs relative to the semiparametric oracle. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that our estimator generally improves on existing multi-logger IPS methods. We also extend our estimator to a doubly robust form by incorporating a conditional reward estimator. We compare the resulting DR extension with the semiparametrically efficient DR estimator of Kallus et al. [2021], both theoretically and empirically. We show that the two estimators achieve the same asymptotic variance when the conditional reward estimator is consistent, while our estimator can retain semiparametric efficiency under certain forms of reward misspecification. Empirically, the proposed DR estimator often achieves lower estimation error than the existing DR baseline.
  </details>

- **[UTOPI: Efficient Egocentric Long-Video Understanding in AR via User-Guided Token Pre-Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11562)**  `arXiv:2611.11562`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziqi Wang, Su Chen, Qiance Tang, Jieyu Lin, Ziyun Li, Barbara De Salvo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As long-video understanding gains increasing attention, augmented reality (AR) devices are emerging as a compelling deployment platform. However, egocentric videos exhibit significant temporal redundancy, imposing substantial memory and computational burdens on resource-constrained AR hardware. To address this challenge, we introduce UTOPI, a plug-and-play pre-compression module designed specifically for egocentric long videos on AR platforms. First, we leverage user motion signals to estimate inter-frame overlap and propose a viewpoint-shift-invariant pruning algorithm. Second, we exploit eye-tracking sensors to identify user fixations, enabling the separation of attended foreground content from background and its preferential retention via a fine-tuned neural network-based adjustment mechanism. Across multiple benchmarks and model backbones, UTOPI achieves up to 95% compression while preserving performance. Code and project page are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ConvLUT: Rethinking Convolutional Architectures for Ultra-Low-Latency LUT-Based Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11507)**  `arXiv:2611.11507`  `cs.LG`  
  _Marta Andronic, Olivia Weng, Danial Zuberi, Ryan Kastner, George A. Constantinides_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lookup-table (LUT)-based neural networks (NNs) offer a compelling path to ultra-low-latency inference on FPGAs. However, existing LUT-based neural networks have largely been restricted to MLP-style topologies, leaving structured vision and audio tasks underexplored. We present ConvLUT, a family of fully unrolled LUT-mappable convolutional architectures targeting the MLPerf Tiny benchmarks. It is comprised of a standard convolutional model built around a hierarchical tree of LUTs and a depthwise separable model following the MobileNet design philosophy. These architectures are enabled by four key contributions: a unified LUT-based convolutional framework supporting standard, depthwise, and pointwise convolutions under explicit fan-in constraints; a LUT-tree that addresses the $k^2c$ receptive field dimensionality bottleneck; a channel-partitioned LUT pooling layer that replaces conventional spatial pooling; and a hardware-aware iterative pruning strategy for stable convergence at scale. Evaluated on CIFAR-10, Visual Wake Words, and Keyword Spotting, ConvLUT achieves competitive accuracy with fully unrolled inference latencies below $\sim 50$ ns. On CIFAR-10 it reaches at least a $8844\times$ latency reduction over non-logic-based prior implementations, and outperforms existing MLP-style LUT-based models on the same task by at least $20$ pp in test accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Spatial Harness: Grounding Spatial Reasoning in Executable World States with Physics Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11344)**  `arXiv:2611.11344`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chentao Cao, Zhanke Zhou, Sangni Duan, Bo Han, Hang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning requires understanding how objects and observers are arranged in a scene and how their relations change with viewpoint or movement. Vision-language models (VLMs) typically perform both steps through model-internal reasoning, forming an implicit scene interpretation from partial views and imagining the consequences of spatial transformations. A misread scene can lead to a wrong answer, while even a correct interpretation does not ensure that imagined transformations satisfy spatial constraints. We introduce Spatial Harness, which represents scenes as explicit and executable world states to ground spatial reasoning in physics-engine feedback. A Perceiver integrates scene constraints extracted from the question with specialized visual estimates to place objects and cameras in a shared coordinate frame. The resulting state is instantiated in a physics engine, where a Reasoner executes viewpoint changes and uses computed measurements and rendered views to guide reasoning. We evaluate on MindCube, SAT, MMSI-Bench, and SpatialRGPT-Bench with open- and closed-weight VLMs. On MindCube, Spatial Harness improves Qwen3.5-27B from 52.70% to 71.14%, surpassing pySpatial by 7.52 percentage points. Given identical annotated object and camera poses, it reaches 98.25% versus 71.81% for model-internal reasoning. Code is publicly available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[When Credit Turns Against Tools: Transition-Aware Credit Assignment in Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11330)**  `arXiv:2611.11330`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiangyu Lu, Zhanke Zhou, Jiazhe Ning, Chentao Cao, Jiangchao Yao, Bo Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards trains language models through multi-turn tool interactions, yet their reasoning performance and tool-calling ability can decline during training. We trace this degradation to three coupled mechanisms: (1) tool-token imbalance produces negative overall tool credit when incorrect trajectories contain more tool tokens per turn than correct ones; (2) aggregation amplification can further strengthen this negative signal as token-mean aggregation gives longer incorrect trajectories greater weight in policy updates; and (3) failure-driven retries prolong trajectories as models tend to call tools again after execution errors, extending potential negative credit to recovery attempts. To counter this suppression, we propose Transition-Aware Credit Assignment (TACA), which targets the transition from reasoning to tool use through supervision of both call initiation and generation. Specifically, TACA uses Advantage Projection to enforce a nonnegative mean advantage on tool-call tokens within each prompt group through a minimal uniform shift. Additionally, TACA guides tool-use decisions with Decision Contrast, comparing rewards for tool-assisted and direct answers to the same prompt. Through Supervised Replay, it reinforces tool-call generation using calls from previously sampled correct trajectories. Across models and tasks, TACA improves performance and sustains more reliable tool interaction during training, raising mean accuracy over MT-GRPO on six reasoning benchmarks by 4.61 percentage points for Qwen2.5-1.5B and 3.61 for Qwen2.5-3B. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ALETHEIA: A Multi-Frequency Eddy Current Pulsed Thermography Dataset for Neural Operator Learning in Nondestructive Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11252)**  `arXiv:2611.11252`  `cs.LG`  
  _Changbin Sun, Jie Xiao, Xiaotian Chen, Yuankai Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning neural solvers for spatiotemporal partial differential equations (PDEs) under real-world constraints remains a key challenge in scientific machine learning, especially for inverse tasks with sparse and noisy boundary observations. We present the Aletheia dataset, the first 3D benchmark for learning data-driven solvers in the context of nondestructive testing (NDT). The dataset simulates eddy-current-induced heating in conductive solids and models the resulting transient heat propagation governed by the heat equation. Aletheia contains over 4,700 high-resolution samples across 10 excitation frequencies (1-100,kHz), each providing volumetric heat source and temperature fields over time. It supports both forward prediction of temperature evolution and inverse reconstruction of internal heat sources or defects from surface infrared measurements. Real infrared thermography data from cracked rail specimens are included for calibration and comparison. We define four canonical tasks on both regular and irregular grids and benchmark them using various neural operators. Aletheia establishes a unified platform for evaluating neural PDE solvers under realistic NDT conditions, enabling progress in reliable, data-driven inverse modeling. The dataset and code are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Discrete Diffusion Playground: A 2D Benchmark for Discrete Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11068)**  `arXiv:2611.11068`  `cs.LG`  
  _Justin Shao, Sihyun Park, David Ryan Koes, Maria Chikina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete diffusion is a promising framework for categorical generative modeling, but current evaluation protocols often fail to reveal whether models have actually learned the target distribution. We introduce the Discrete Diffusion Playground, a diagnostic benchmark that converts known 2D continuous distributions into binary token sequences through an exact discretized bijection. This creates a rare setting in which models are trained on discrete sequences, but generated samples can be decoded back into 2D and compared directly against a known ground-truth distribution. The benchmark spans smooth Gaussian mixtures and tilted checkerboards with sharp boundaries, disconnected support, and coordinate-dependent structure, allowing it to expose failures that are invisible in standard proxy metrics. It supports exact grid divergences, Wasserstein distances, density visualizations, and nonparametric two-sample tests, providing both quantitative evaluation and interpretable failure diagnosis. Using this testbed, we show that masked diffusion models can learn meaningful adaptive reveal orders but remain less stable than autoregressive baselines on smooth targets and largely fail on tilted checkerboards, where autoregressive models preserve the target geometry. Control experiments with reversed and interleaved token orderings show that autoregressive performance is not explained by favorable bit order alone. The Playground provides a simple but stringent benchmark for stress-testing discrete generative models and for developing evaluation metrics that detect real distributional mismatch rather than proxy performance. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Hydro-Dataset: A Multi-Modal Open-Source Pipeline for Nordic Hydrological Research and Foundation Model Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10992)**  `arXiv:2611.10992`  `cs.LG`  
  _Getnet Demil, Md Moinul Islam, Mourad Oussalah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite world-class snow monitoring by the Swedish Meteorological and Hydrological Institute (SMHI) and Finnish Meteorological Institute (FMI), no station-anchored, multi-parameter snow benchmark dataset exists for the Nordic boreal domain. We present Hydro-Dataset v1.1, a reproducible open-source pipeline that harmonizes 582 SMHI meteorological observation stations across nine hydrological winters (2015/16-2023/24) into a CF-1.9 NetCDF product with 1.8~million annotated station-days. Six derived snow parameters, such as Snow Water Equivalent (SWE), snow phenology, melt rates, degree-day melt factor, rain-on-snow flags, and freeze-thaw cycles, supply benchmark-ready labels for hydrological foundation model training. ERA5-Land cross-validation (Pearson R=0.78, bias=-262mm) confirms observation-anchored SWE provides a more reliable benchmark than reanalysis for machine learning training.
  </details>

- **[Robust and Efficient Continual Model Merging via Global Singular Subspace Separation and Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10881)**  `arXiv:2611.10881`  `cs.LG`  
  _Juncheol Shin, Byeongho Yu, Semin Kim, Eunhyeok Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual model merging incrementally integrates task-specific models without revisiting past models or data. However, repeated merging entangles past task components and distorts their singular geometry, making balanced task-wise allocation increasingly difficult. We propose Global Singular Subspace Separation and Restoration (GS3R), which augments the merged update with a compact restoration state. At each merge, GS3R restores the retained singular geometry, separates task-wise subspaces, and globally reallocates the available rank across tasks fairly. To reduce merging-time memory overhead, we further introduce Pre-merge Restoration and Flushing (PRF). Experiments on vision, NLP, and multimodal benchmarks show that GS3R provides a strong accuracy-efficiency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[BRAT: Bounded Residual-Aligned Training for Stable Multi-Pass LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10873)**  `arXiv:2611.10873`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xunlan Zhou, Yuzhe Zhao, Xuhong Huang, Feng Chen, Xuanlin Chen, Lang Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for large language model post-training often reuses each batch of sampled rollouts for several gradient passes to amortize the cost of generation. Under such multi-pass reuse, existing objectives fail in different ways: unclipped updates collapse as the policy drifts away from the sampling policy, adding a negative baseline avoids collapse but reinforces every sampled response, and PPO-style ratio clipping cuts off gradients abruptly. We first ask why reuse is fragile. Measuring how well an update estimated from a small rollout group aligns with the KL-regularized target on held-out rollouts of the same prompts, we find that this alignment drops by nearly an order of magnitude from in-sample to held-out rollouts across three base models, suggesting that repeated passes largely overfit the sampled group. Motivated by this finding, we propose Bounded Residual-Aligned Training (BRAT), motivated by a proximal update toward a bounded reweighting of the sampling policy. BRAT assigns each token a gradient weight that decreases linearly with its probability ratio and vanishes at a trust-region boundary, and normalizes group-centered rewards by their maximum magnitude, keeping advantages zero-mean and bounded without extra hyperparameters. With four reuse passes, BRAT avoids training collapse, limits policy drift, and achieves higher average accuracy with lower seed variance on most benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Port-Hamiltonian Gaussian Processes for Learning from Noisy Dynamics on Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10864)**  `arXiv:2611.10864`  `cs.LG`  
  _Thribhuvan Rapolu, Luke McLennan, Chandrajit Bajaj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning predictive models for dynamics is difficult because observations are often sparse and noisy, while the underlying dynamics evolve on nonlinear configuration manifolds and are affected by environmental dissipation and stochastic disturbances. Standard neural dynamics models can fit short-term trajectories but often violate physical structure, accumulate error over long horizons, or rely on parameterizations that introduce singularities such as gimbal lock. We introduce LieSPHGP, a stochastic Gaussian-process framework for learning rigid body dynamics with port-Hamiltonian structure and stability-aware robustness. LieSPHGP represents motion on Lie groups and Lie algebras, enabling coordinate-consistent optimization without singular parameterizations. Learned mass, potential energy, dissipation, and control components retain physical roles and a separate diffusion term represents stochastic forcing. Each component is modeled by Matérn kernel Gaussian processes which capture uncertainty induced by noisy observations. To improve physical validity, Lie group exponential map updates preserve rotational constraints, while positive-definite damping ensures nonnegative deterministic energy dissipation. The implicit-explicit integrator additionally provides unconditional stability for the frozen linear damping subsystem. The resulting model learns predictive dynamics that are robust to noise, respect rigid body geometry, and capture both intrinsic motion and environmental dissipation. Experiments on various noisy rigid body benchmarks show improved prediction accuracy, stronger long-horizon stability, and better satisfaction of manifold constraints compared with neural dynamics baselines. Code, data and interactive results are available on the project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EEGFaceSem: An EEG Benchmark with Paired Generative Latents for Semantic Visual Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10781)**  `arXiv:2611.10781`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jun Ma, Michiel M. Spapé, Keith M. Davis III, Tuukka Ruotsalo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) is widely used to measure brain activity for brain-computer interface applications for its non-invasive nature and high temporal resolution. However, progress in modeling the neural responses to visual semantics has been limited by a reliance on fixed image collections that lack the fine-grained control needed to isolate fine-grained changes in visual features and their relation to perception. In this work, we present EEGFaceSem, a large-scale dataset pairing high-quality EEG with photorealistic face images synthesized from a generative model. We provide the generative latent vector for each stimulus, enabling research into the continuous mapping between a visual latent space and neural representations. Each participant's brain responses were recorded while they attended to specific semantic features (e.g., smiling, hair color) in a rapid serial visual presentation paradigm. The dataset contains EEG signals from 30 participants, totaling 67,200 time-locked EEG recordings. We present benchmarks for single-trial semantic classification under single-subject, cross-subject, and subject-adapted settings, as well as for cross-subject voting that aggregates per-stimulus predictions across participants. We further demonstrate the dataset's unique utility with two complementary applications: deriving semantic directions in the latent space for brain-supervised image editing, and conditioning a generative model on brain-decoded labels for brain-supervised image generation. Our dataset paves the way for advancing brain-computer interfaces (BCI) for semantic-level visual saliency detection and brain-supervised generative modeling. Our dataset and code are openly released at OSF (this https URL) and at HuggingFacethis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AbductionBench: Benchmarking Abductive Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10705)**  `arXiv:2611.10705`  `cs.LG`  
  _Moein Salimi, Amir Qeysarbeigi, Babak Hosseini Mohtasham, Faraz Doagoye Tehrani, Mahdi Naieni, Hanieh Sartipi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Abductive reasoning is the inference of plausible explanations for observed evidence. Evaluating this capability requires more than a single task: a system may need to generate an explanation, discriminate among competing hypotheses, revise its hypotheses as evidence arrives, or decide what evidence to seek next. Yet these capabilities are typically evaluated through isolated benchmarks with different task formulations and scoring procedures, so claims that a model is ``good at abduction'' may reflect only a narrow aspect of this capability. We introduce AbductionBench, a unified framework whose taxonomy characterizes abductive tasks. We instantiate this framework on 27 datasets spanning ten domains and evaluate eight LLMs from four model families evaluated on single-choice selection tasks. Beyond harmonizing heterogeneous tasks, AbductionBench separates final-answer evaluation from analysis of the observable reasoning traces and interaction histories produced alongside those answers. We analyze written traces as observable model outputs rather than faithful representations of internal computation, characterizing evidence use, hypothesis comparison, uncertainty, contradiction handling, and answer commitment. Jev matches or exceeds small LLMs when selecting among explanations over compact evidence but falls substantially behind on longer cases, suggesting that candidate discrimination and extended evidence integration place different demands on a system. In interactive tasks, the proportion of actions judged relevant declines at later steps, and a relevant first question is associated with a correct final answer; in the passive task, models rarely revise their initial hypothesis as new evidence arrives. Together, these findings highlight the value of evaluating abductive reasoning through both final answers and observable evidence use, hypothesis revision, and information gathering.
  </details>

- **[Safe Offline Reinforcement Learning using Behavior Regularisation and Latent Feasibility Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10677)**  `arXiv:2611.10677`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mahesh Keswani, Parth Bhardwaj, Ankur Kumar, Raunak P. Bhattacharyya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe offline reinforcement learning (RL) seeks to learn policies that maximize return while satisfying safety constraints, using a static dataset. To characterize state-wise safety, reachability analysis identifies the feasible region, defined as the set of states from which constraints can be satisfied at every future step. Policies are commonly extracted from learned value functions with advantage-weighted regression (AWR). However, AWR relies on exponentiated weights that amplify value estimation errors, whereas behavior-regularized policy gradient methods directly utilize the action gradient of the value function and often make more effective use of learned critics. We propose Feasibility-Optimized Conditional Actor Learning (FOCAL), a method that incorporates behavior-regularized policy gradients into a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) actor. FOCAL optimizes this policy using a first-order objective partitioned by feasibility, updating the actor to increase return on transitions estimated to be feasible and to reduce constraint violations on transitions estimated to be infeasible. During inference, FOCAL dynamically adjusts the scale of the latent prior according to the estimated feasibility of the state to adjust the conservatism of action sampling. Theoretically, we show that for AWR, the policy update variance depends exponentially on the advantage error, whereas for FOCAL it scales linearly with the variance of the value estimation error. Across the DSRL benchmark, FOCAL satisfies the constraints on all evaluated tasks while achieving competitive or higher returns compared to baselines.
  </details>

- **[Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation: Evaluating Hierarchical Policies from Non-Hierarchical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10543)**  `arXiv:2611.10543`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunfu Deng, Josiah P. Hanna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates the expected return of a target policy from previously collected data without additional environment interaction. While OPE methods for flat Markovian policies are well studied, little work has addressed evaluating hierarchical policies in which a high-level policy selects temporally extended options that generate primitive actions until a termination condition is met. The primary existing approach applies per-decision importance sampling at the option level, but requires option-annotated trajectories and exhibits variance that still grows with the horizon. For non-hierarchical policies, fitted Q-evaluation (FQE) typically achieves lower error than importance sampling by leveraging the classic Bellman equation for policy evaluation; however, a naive extension of FQE to hierarchical policies results in a biased policy value estimate. In this paper, we introduce Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation (IO-FQE), which estimates a value function in the augmented state-option space; this approach enables OPE of hierarchical policies even when we lack annotations of what option was run in the data. We develop two continuous-action instantiations of IO-FQE and show on long-horizon hierarchical continuous control tasks (AntMaze navigation and OGBench Puzzle manipulation) that IO-FQE eliminates the bias incurred by a naive application of FQE to hierarchical policies and substantially lowers mean squared-error compared to option-level importance sampling.
  </details>

- **[CALFA-KV: Joint Calibration and Fisher-Guided Anchors for 1-Bit KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10511)**  `arXiv:2611.10511`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiancai Tian, Leichao Zhang, Shenhui Zhang, Yi Shan, Hailong Li, Feng Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  1-bit key-value (KV) cache compression can combine quantizer calibration with selective high-precision storage to reduce reconstruction error. We propose Calibration and Fisher-guided Anchors for KV cache compression (CALFA-KV). Offline, we jointly fit a key center, radial normalization parameters, and K/V vector codebooks to match the frozen model's projected attention output. We also estimate channel-wise Fisher-type sensitivities from squared gradients of a reference next-token cross-entropy loss. Online, we use these sensitivities to weight the squared reconstruction errors of each KV state. Here, a KV state is one token's key-value pair within one layer and KV head. We retain the highest-scoring states at high precision under a per-layer, per-KV-head budget. Under exact restoration, we bound the excess risk of score-based anchor selection relative to the optimum at the same anchor budget, where risk is the conditional mean square of the first-order loss change. With a budget of 56 additional FP16 anchors per layer and KV head, CALFA-KV (d8b8) outperforms all evaluated nominal 1-bit baselines on the mean score across seven benchmarks for each of the three models. At the same anchor budget, CALFA-KV (d8b10) uses a nominal index rate of 1.25 bits per channel and narrows the gap to BF16 to 0.90--2.01 points on the same metric.
  </details>

- **[From Task Affinities to Optimal Allocations: Probabilistic Graphical Modelling for Supervised Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10389)**  `arXiv:2611.10389`  `cs.LG`  
  _Prateek Chanda, Saral Sureka, Krishnateja Killamsetty, Ganesh Ramakrishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning over a large pool of heterogeneous tasks is often computationally expensive, leading practitioners to train language models on a fixed training budget of carefully chosen SFT tasks. Thus, achieving strong downstream performance hinges on selecting an effective subset of tasks, or equivalently, identifying an optimal task mixture under a fixed budget constraint. In practice, supervised finetuning mixtures are often determined using simple heuristics (e.g., uniform or size-proportional sampling) that ignore task interactions, which can hurt transfer and waste budget on redundant task sources. We introduce TASKMIXPGM, a framework for learning continuous task mixtures through energy-based modelling over SFT tasks. Each task forms a node in a Markov random field, with unary potentials capturing task utility and pairwise potentials modelling inter-task relationships via behavioral divergences between predictive distributions of single-task fine-tuned models. Optimizing this objective yields task mixtures that balance utility, coverage, and redundancy. We further show that the induced set function defined over the set of such tasks is weakly submodular under budget constraints, enabling approximation guarantees for discrete task selection. Empirically, we provide a fixed, benchmark-independent mixture that improves over heuristic mixing across multiple model families and different training budgets. TASKMIXPGM also uncovers interpretable structure in task interactions, revealing complementarities and redundancies that inform effective SFT mixtures.
  </details>

- **[CHARM: Certified Homology-Aware Reverse-scale Mapping via Diffusion-Guided Quotient Towers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10375)**  `arXiv:2611.10375`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhe Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Widely used dimensionality reduction methods prioritize neighborhood or distance 1 preservation, yet planar coordinates alone cannot certify the homological type or 2 sample-level support of higher-order structures. Obtaining this evidence directly 3 is costly because simplicial complexes grow rapidly with dimension. We propose 4 Certified Homology-Aware Reverse-scale Mapping (CHARM), which combines 5 two-dimensional coordinates with certified homology records and sample-level 6 localization. Diffusion coordinates guide cover proposals; safe contractions and a 7 Cover Quotient Tower reduce the domain of structural computation; exact algebra 8 certifies and tracks homology representatives; and reverse-scale layout places their 9 supports in shared coordinates. Safe contraction preserves the homotopy type of 10 the input flag complex. The quotient tower is multiplicatively 3-interleaved with 11 the coarse-graph hop-distance Rips filtration. Verifiable distance-distortion bounds 12 give an additive guarantee between the original Euclidean and coarse-graph Rips 13 filtrations, and composite multiplicative guarantees for the tower on positive scales. 14 Across 22 datasets and 41,637 samples under a fixed configuration, CHARM 15 ranks among the top two on all 15 sample-weighted metrics for nonsynthetic data, 16 including the smallest values on all four Euclidean persistence-diagram distances. 17 It also provides 957 traceable support records across the full benchmark. On a 18 spherical shell, one certified H2 record identifies 1,578 original samples in the 19 two-dimensional view. CHARM thus combines geometric exploration with explicit 20 evidence of homological type and location.
  </details>

- **[TripletAD: Weakly Supervised Anomaly Detection in Embedding Space with Rare Anomalies and Scarce Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10352)**  `arXiv:2611.10352`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sumit Wadhwa, Souvik Nath_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many practical anomaly detection problems come with only a handful of confirmed anomalies, and the unreviewed remainder of the data cannot be trusted to be normal. We study this setting under a curated-label protocol, in which unconfirmed anomalies are removed from training rather than relabelled as normal, and propose TripletAD, a weakly supervised detector that learns a triplet-margin embedding and calibrates a linear scoring head with two soft penalties: a partial cross-entropy on labelled anomalies and a relative-contrast hinge that separates the mean scores of anomalous and normal samples.
