Because every mined triplet contains a labelled anomaly, the few labels are reused across the entire training set instead of being diluted by it. On 24 ADBench datasets whose inputs are pre-trained embeddings (text, image and speech vectors), TripletAD outperforms established state-of-the-art weakly supervised detectors (DevNet, PReNet and DeepSAD) and gradient-boosted trees when anomalies are rare. At a 1% anomaly rate it has the highest mean AUCPR of six methods, 20% above DevNet; on the naturally rare Speech dataset it doubles the best baseline (0.394 vs. 0.196 AUCPR); and on MVTec-AD it is best when only a quarter or half of the anomalies are labelled. Pooled over 70 rare- or sparse-label settings, it has the best average rank (2.21 of 6) and outperforms each baseline under Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm correction. When anomalies are more frequent it performs on par with these methods. The method needs no per-dataset tuning and is insensitive to its loss weights and training length; on raw tabular features, gradient-boosted trees remain the stronger choice.
  </details>

- **[A Clinical Evaluation of Conformal Prediction for Chest Radiograph Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10288)**  `arXiv:2611.10288`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nikolaos Kontogeorgis, Pascal Krezer, Klaus-Rudolf Kladny, Arne Estler, Christian F. Baumgartner, Lisa Koch, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have improved automated radiology report generation, they remain prone to hallucinations, necessitating clinically meaningful risk-control methods. Conformal prediction (CP) provides statistical guarantees, yet existing CP approaches rely on uncalibrated automated extractors and fail to account for the clinical relevance of errors. We introduce clinical admissibility criteria (CLARITY), a framework for evaluating radiology reports that explicitly distinguishes clinically consequential errors from minor deviations. To eliminate reliance on automatic judges, we collected radiologist annotations of MLLM-generated reports using CLARITY. By benchmarking CP strategies and automatic evaluation metrics against these expert annotations, we demonstrate that commonly used admissibility proxies systematically fail to detect clinically relevant errors. Our findings reveal that automated admissibility judgments are largely decoupled from true clinical admissibility.
  </details>

- **[Can Time Series Foundation Models Serve as Surrogates for Power Systems Dynamic Simulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10247)**  `arXiv:2611.10247`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sandy Miguel, Bruno Paes Leao, Cheng Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-fidelity dynamic simulation is essential for scientific and engineering applications, but its computational cost limits large-scale scenario screening. We investigate the use of pretrained time-series foundation models (TSFMs) as computationally efficient surrogates to address this limitation. In-context learning and post-training strategies, including retrieval-augmented reference sample selection and physics-informed regularization, are considered in the investigation. Results are benchmarked against a traditional AI model representative of the state-of-the-art in the domain. Experiments using an open-source TSFM and data from a simulated 9-bus power system indicate promising results. Retrieval augmentation improves in-context learning performance, and fine-tuning leads to superior results compared to the traditional AI model, especially when including physics-informed regularization. The fine-tuned TSFMs achieve lower errors and improved computational costs compared to the traditional AI model, and a 563$\times$ speedup compared to the original physics-based simulation.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking How to Leverage Cheap Physics for Data-Efficient Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10217)**  `arXiv:2611.10217`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shuo Zhang, Senwei Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators learn mappings from PDE inputs, such as initial conditions and coefficient fields, to the corresponding solutions. Once trained, they can rapidly predict solutions for new inputs. However, obtaining input-solution pairs for supervised training often requires expensive numerical simulations, whereas PDE inputs and governing equations provide inexpensive physical information. This raises a question: how can cheap physical information reduce the need for labeled solutions? A natural approach is to use physics to select inputs for labeling, but our acquisition experiments show no consistent advantage over random sampling. This observation motivates exploring an alternative: using cheap physics to improve neural operator initialization before supervised training. We propose Physics-Informed Initialization (PI-Init), a simple yet effective method that pretrains neural operators on unlabeled inputs using physics-informed losses and retains the learned parameters for supervised fine-tuning on the labeled dataset. A linearized analysis shows that pretraining can improve downstream prediction accuracy by reducing initialization errors that limited labeled data cannot effectively correct. Experiments across diverse PDE benchmarks and neural operator architectures demonstrate improvements in predictive accuracy, out-of-distribution generalization, and label efficiency. Representative results show error reductions of up to 62.7% at the same label budget and comparable or better accuracy with 90% fewer labels, relative to supervised-only training.
  </details>

- **[BO-Arena: An Evolving Benchmark for High-Dimensional Bayesian Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10198)**  `arXiv:2611.10198`  `cs.LG`  
  _Colin Doumont, Thomas Christie, Henry Moss, Philipp Hennig_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-dimensional Bayesian optimisation (HDBO) is an increasingly crowded field with new methods proposed at every major conference, making it ever harder to answer a deceptively simple question: "Which method is currently state-of-the-art?" In this paper, we question the efficacy of benchmarking practices in the field, and find them to be systematically ineffective: multiple published works fail to benchmark against the strongest available baselines, or even include incorrect implementations of key methods. Without a shared reference implementation, such errors are difficult to detect during peer review. We therefore introduce BO-Arena, an actively maintained and extensible package providing canonical implementations of state-of-the-art algorithms, which allows researchers to develop new methods and benchmark them rigorously. Using BO-Arena, we find that what currently drives performance in HDBO is even simpler than previously thought. We use these insights to propose a new algorithm which is competitive with the state of the art on problems with many observations, without resorting to the complex GP approximations used in recent methods. Finally, we question whether there is currently sufficient evidence that any more complex method outperforms a simple Gaussian process baseline for HDBO.
  </details>

- **[Coordinated Mixed-Precision KV Cache Quantization for Efficient LLM-based Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10096)**  `arXiv:2611.10096`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Jinchao Zhang, Xiawu Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multi-agent systems solve complex tasks through collaboration, but their growing, simultaneously resident key-value (KV) caches create a memory bottleneck. Applying single-model quantization policies independently ignores differences in agents' precision needs, potentially degrading collective reasoning. We introduce Coordinated Mixed-Precision KV Cache Quantization (CoMixKV), which combines online sensitivity estimation with joint agent-layer bit-width allocation under a shared memory budget. To our knowledge, CoMixKV is the first mixed-precision KV-cache quantization framework to jointly allocate precision across simultaneously resident agent caches under such a budget. The estimator derives sensitivity from a local attention-error bound using recent queries, keys, and values, without calibration data, trial quantization, or additional model passes. A shared controller combines these scores with remaining quantizable capacity and byte-level storage costs to minimize total estimated error. Allocations are refreshed as agents start or finish and new observations become available. Selected widths apply only to newly quantized blocks; recent states retain native precision and previously quantized blocks remain unchanged. Across four benchmarks and three LLM backbones at average 2-bit, 3-bit, and 4-bit settings, CoMixKV achieves strong accuracy against the evaluated quantization baselines. At the average 3-bit setting on Gemma-4-31B-it, it achieves 68.35% average accuracy, exceeding the strongest quantized baseline by 0.59%. On Qwen3-32B, it reduces peak KV-cache memory by 32.08% and achieves a 1.38x inference speedup over FullKV.
  </details>

- **[fNIRSAtlas: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Functional Near-Infrared Spectroscopy Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10082)**  `arXiv:2611.10082`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kalle Mäkelä, Tuukka Ruotsalo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) is a promising modality for brain-computer interfaces and cognitive state decoding, but progress in machine learning for fNIRS has been limited by small, isolated datasets and inconsistent evaluation protocols. We introduce fNIRSAtlas, the largest open benchmark for fNIRS classification to date, comprising 23 classification tasks from 19 public data collections, spanning diverse experimental paradigms, including motor execution, motor imagery, mental arithmetic, working memory, and emotion recognition. fNIRSAtlas provides a fully automated pipeline from raw data to evaluation and defines standardized benchmark tasks for both cross-subject and within-subject classification, ensuring reproducible comparison of methods. We evaluate a variety of baseline models from fNIRS and related neuroimaging and time-series machine learning literature on the benchmark. Our results demonstrate that performance varies substantially across tasks and that methods validated on limited data -- especially deep learning approaches -- often fail to generalize beyond their original evaluation. fNIRSAtlas serves as an extensible, open, and reproducible benchmark to support more robust and cumulative progress in machine learning for fNIRS data. Our code and data can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[On the limitations of molecular representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08420)**  `arXiv:2611.08420`  `cs.LG`  
  _Acer Blake, Charlotte M. Deane, Fergus Imrie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular representations such as fingerprints and learned embeddings play a central role in cheminformatics and small-molecule machine learning. They serve as the primary input for a diverse array of tasks, including clustering, dataset splitting, and virtual screening, yet their evaluation is often limited to downstream model performance on narrow benchmark tasks. We carried out a systematic investigation of the quality of molecular representations, finding that none of the state-of-the-art molecular embedding models capture graph structure, 3D shape, or ADMET endpoint similarity better than fingerprints or physicochemical descriptors. However, classical fingerprints also revealed their own material limitations. For example, we show that depending on the representation, between 225M and $\sim$4B molecules have non-unique representations in the 10.8B Enamine REAL database, with implications for virtual screening. Our findings provide an insight into the current limitations of molecular representations, outlining future research directions for the development of more robust encodings of small molecules.
  </details>

- **[SecureGARD: Dynamic Ontology-Guided Governance for Secure Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06682)**  `arXiv:2611.06682`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guoqin Xiong, Shengtao Wen, Xiang Chen, Xinyi Zeng, Zhaoxin Fan, Wei Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents translate model outputs into consequential actions, turning indirect prompt injection into an execution-security problem. Existing defenses constrain tool calls, trajectories, or information flows, but often leave action semantics, state dependencies, and evidence relations implicit, limiting generalization across heterogeneous tools and complicating safe updates. We present SecureGARD, a dynamic ontology-guided governance framework. SecureGARD grounds heterogeneous calls in a shared semantic representation and combines an executable ontology with episode-level task state to derive deterministic ready, waiting, and forbidden decisions. A waiting decision identifies an unmet but recoverable obligation, enabling bounded replanning without expanding the agent's authorization boundary. SecureGARD evolves its ontology offline from verified training executions. An offline LLM proposes instance-independent action-state axioms, which are accepted only if they address ontology errors exposed by training failures, preserve logical consistency and protected entailments, and pass held-out validation before the ontology is frozen. We evaluate SecureGARD on AgentDojo, ASB, and AgentDyn under benign and indirect prompt-injection settings. SecureGARD achieves a 0% attack success rate (ASR) across all three benchmarks while improving task utility under attack compared with existing baselines. These results show that explicit semantic governance can strengthen execution security without indiscriminate blocking.
  </details>

- **[Ground-Wing: A Parametric 3D Wing-in-Ground-Effect Dataset for Benchmarking Neural Surrogate Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04571)**  `arXiv:2611.04571`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrea Cenzato, Lukas Karch, Andre Luckow, Yoran Pichon, Francesco Pasqua, Abouzar Ghasemi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a CFD dataset of a highly parametrized 3D wing operating in ground effect, designed for benchmarking neural surrogate models for aerodynamic prediction, using a wing shape and flow conditions typically encountered in motorsport applications. The dataset comprises 770 RANS simulations of 194 unique wing configurations, sampled across 27 airfoil profile variants and 4 distances from the ground, spanning variations in wing sweep, chord, taper, angle of attack, and tip twist. Six neural network architectures representing distinct paradigms --- convolutional (DoMINO), graph-based (X-MeshGraphNet), transformer-based (AB-UPT, GeoTransolver, GAOT), and JEPA-based (AeroJEPA) --- are evaluated on their ability to predict surface pressure coefficient and wall shear stress distributions, from which integral drag and lift coefficients are recovered. The dataset is intended to be used as a challenging benchmark for surrogate CFD models, due to the uniqueness of the ground effect being absent from other wing and airfoil datasets. The dataset is available for download atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[ReFrame: Silence-Aware Compute Reclamation for Full-Duplex Speech Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01835)**  `arXiv:2611.01835`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zheng Zhou, Guanyi Chen, Jing Wei, Haoyuan Liang, Jian Zhang, Haohuan Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-duplex speech models execute dense model inference for every audio frame throughout a live interaction. This uniform framewise schedule creates sustained GPU load across dialogue states whose computation has strong, reusable temporal structure. ReFrame formulates full-duplex inference as a silence-aware computation problem. Mutual Silence Speculation (MSS) operates when both participants are silent: it evaluates multi-frame Temporal continuations, verifies text and hidden-state stability, and applies batched Mimi audio-token correction. Listening Frame Folding (LFF) operates while the user speaks and the agent listens: it processes already-arrived user frames through parallel Temporal execution and frame-batched audio-token generation while accepting only the verified prefix. Frame-Budget Reinvestment (FBR) assigns the released frame intervals to resumable external retrieval and language-model workers, then injects timely references through Moshi's conditioning interface. We instantiate ReFrame on five full-duplex models spanning Moshi-family, phase-structured, and flattened autoregressive generation. Across the complete 1,225-conversation FDBench, ReFrame reduces measured GPU execution time by 18.80-31.01% and retains 96.92-100% non-PAD text agreement on five different models. On HaluEval tests, FBR increases the number of correct generated spoken answers from 86 to 205 under the same auxiliary-service budget. ReFrame turns recurring dialogue structure into lower inference cost and higher end-to-end service capability.
  </details>

- **[OCELOT: Planetary-Scale Species Distribution Modeling with Satellite Foundation Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00284)**  `arXiv:2611.00284`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bart van Merriënboer, Lauren Harrell, Keith Anderson, Burcu Karagol Ayan, Elise Kleeman, Tom Denton, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale repositories of species observations now contain billions of occurrence records from around the world, but these sightings cluster heavily in human-accessible areas. Existing models use only a small fraction of these records and are not evaluated on extrapolating to remote, unvisited regions--where species distribution models can be used as geographic priors to help classify data from camera traps and acoustic recorders. We scale relative occurrence species distribution models by training a multimodal implicit neural representation network called OCELOT that fuses spatial coordinates with satellite foundation embeddings on two orders of magnitude more observations than previous work: 2 billion GBIF records across 100,000 taxa. Scaling to this regime requires co-designing the data pipeline, taxonomic representation, loss formulation, and model architecture: spatiotemporal thinning and sub-linear cell sampling correct for spatial redundancy and observer bias, while an additive taxonomic readout shares representations across species, genera, and families to improve predictions for rare taxa. To evaluate performance in remote areas, we benchmark geographic extrapolation using the NDCG metric on spatial block holdouts up to 600 km. Satellite foundation embeddings improve extrapolation by 51% over coordinate-only models and outperform classical bioclimatic rasters. The resulting model also matches or outperforms existing baselines on established interpolation benchmarks, demonstrating that optimizing for geographic extrapolation does not compromise in-distribution accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Exhaustive Canonical Skeletons Generation: A Formal Dataset for Decomposable Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20108)**  `arXiv:2610.20108`  `cs.LG`  
  _Giorgio Morales, Anikó Ekárt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural symbolic regression (SR) models require diverse datasets of mathematical expressions for robust training. However, existing datasets rely predominantly on stochastic sampling methods, which suffer from high structural redundancy and incomplete functional coverage. To address this, we introduce an exhaustive dataset of univariate canonical skeletons generated via a deterministic constrained context-free grammar. A canonical skeleton acts as a generic structural representation that subsumes more specific functional forms, eliminating redundant expressions and avoiding repeated computational efforts. Although our dataset consists of univariate skeletons, it is designed to support the decomposable SR paradigm to solve multivariate SR problems without assuming linear separability. Furthermore, we pair this dataset with a sampling strategy that generates clean numerical data without triggering numerical instabilities or relying on artificial protected operators. Canonicalizing two existing neural SR datasets reveals that our dataset is substantially more compact, eliminates structural repetitions, and achieves a uniform operator distribution free of simplicity bias. Finally, we establish a benchmark of 150 SR problems across three tiers, on which state-of-the-art SR methods are shown to struggle significantly.
  </details>

- **[Distilling Graph Geometry: Knowledge Gap from GNNs to MLPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10520)**  `arXiv:2610.10520`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhewei Chen, Hao Zhu, Jiaojiao Jiang, Ahad N. Zehmakan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GNN-to-MLP distillation aims to retain the predictive accuracy of a message-passing teacher while deploying a graph-free MLP at inference. Existing methods mainly transfer node-wise predictions or use confidence-based reweighting, but they do not specify where the student should preserve the teacher's graph-induced geometry. We show that this omission leads to two spectral failure modes in the student's representation space. On sparse graphs, the student suffers from spectral underfit, missing high-energy teacher directions induced by aggregation. On dense graphs, it suffers from spectral overfit, retaining spurious directions that the teacher has collapsed through aggregation. Motivated by an energy-weighted teacher-student alignment objective, we propose Graph Geometry-aware MLP (G^2MLP), a training-time distillation framework guided by Ollivier-Ricci curvature. Curvature acts as a local proxy for allocating supervision between prediction-level and representation-level alignment. The deployed model is a standard MLP with no graph access at inference. Across node-classification benchmarks, G^2MLP improves over graph-free distillation baselines in most settings, reduces the teacher-student rank gap in both regimes, and transfers to Graph Transformer teachers and link prediction.
  </details>

- **[When the Agent Edits Its Own Limits: Evaluating a Write-Time Floor Against Prompt Prohibitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06274)**  `arXiv:2610.06274`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sajib Hossain, Moeen Uddin Mahmud_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many agent runtimes give the agent a tool for editing its own configuration. Some settings grant abilities, such as enabling a tool. Others set the agent's limits: where it may write, who may message it, which address it listens on, how callers authenticate, and the gate that blocks risky writes. We measure how often models widen those limits while handling ordinary requests, and how well two kinds of defence hold: a prohibition in the system prompt, in three wordings, and a check at the point of writing that we call a containment floor. Every case records whether a protected write was attempted and whether it was saved, read from the tool's arguments and from the file rather than from the model's reply. With no defence, a frontier model saved a protected value on 25 of 72 requests, often when the request never named the field. A prompt that listed the protected field names stopped every request that used those names and did not reduce writes on requests that only described the goal; a prompt that described the forbidden effects did the reverse. With the floor, 0 of 167 protected writes were saved while 65 were attempted. A single-pass pilot on two further models shows the same pattern. We draw four lessons for evaluating agent safeguards: count attempts beside outcomes, test a prompt defence with requests written in a different vocabulary from the prompt, check that a zero means the safeguard fired, and log what the harness sends to the model, because a request-ordering defect in our own runtime was invisible in the transcripts and was found again, independently, by a later benchmark.
  </details>

- **[MLoRA:Continual Low-Rank Adaptation via Relative-Response Subspace Selection and Momentum Orthogonalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02283)**  `arXiv:2610.02283`  `cs.LG`  
  _Junkang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) provides a parameter-efficient approach to continual learning, but its nominal rank can conceal a loss of effective adaptation capacity. We identify \emph{spectral plasticity collapse}: during sequential adaptation, update energy becomes concentrated in a small subset of singular modes, leaving much of the available low-rank space underutilized. This exposes a limitation of interference avoidance alone: protecting historical representations does not ensure that the remaining adaptation capacity is responsive to new tasks or effectively utilized. To address this problem, we propose \texttt{MLoRA}, which jointly controls capacity allocation and utilization. First, historical whitening identifies input directions with strong current-task response relative to accumulated historical response, yielding a task-adaptive basis that remains fixed during training. Second, approximate polar orthogonalization of momentum updates reduces spectral concentration within theselected space. An orthonormal basis connects these mechanisms by transferring the factor-update spectrum exactly tothe induced weight update. We establish a max--min characterization of exact subspace selection and derive cumulative spectral bounds under controlled cross-step anisotropy. Across five class-incremental benchmarks and eight incremental settings, \texttt{MuLoRA} achieves the highest mean accuracy in 15 of 16 reported metrics.
  </details>

- **[Quantum-Inspired Score-Based Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39164)**  `arXiv:2609.39164`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuchen Cong, Zerui Tao, Chao Li, Zhe Sun, Qibin Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Score-based variational inference (SBVI) provides an alternative to classical Kullback--Leibler (KL)-based variational inference by learning a distribution using only score queries of the target. EigenVI~\cite{cai2024eigenvi} formulates SBVI as an eigenvalue problem, but faces two limitations: the number of variational parameters grows exponentially with the data dimension, and the single-eigenvector formulation cannot represent the full minimum-eigenvalue eigenspace. We propose QuanVI, a quantum-inspired SBVI algorithm that addresses both limitations. Rather than selecting a single eigenvector, QuanVI captures a learned subspace through a density-operator formulation. To enable scalable computation, \model{} employs a matrix-product-operator (MPO) structure parameterized by a quantum tensor network (QTN) with unitary-constrained local tensors and auxiliary ancilla wires. Numerical experiments demonstrate the scalability of QuanVI beyond EigenVI and competitive performance on synthetic distributions and Bayesian posterior-approximation benchmarks, including challenging non-Gaussian targets. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Progressive Memory Transformer: Memory-Aware Attention for Time-Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31351)**  `arXiv:2609.31351`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tord Sture Stangeland, Andreas Köhler, Steffen Mæland, Adín Ramírez Rivera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series carry structure simultaneously at multiple scales (fine-grained variation, mid-range motifs, and global properties) and downstream tasks operate at correspondingly different scales. Most existing self-supervised learning approaches supervise representations globally via instance-level contrastive losses and limited temporal neighborhood supervision, but do not explicitly exploit the structural hierarchy. We propose a learning framework that explicitly enforces a structural hierarchy across three scales independently: a local objective for token alignment, a mid-range objective for window-level motifs, and a global objective for sequence-level agreement. Realizing this framework requires the backbone to expose a representation at each scale; we introduce \textbf{Progressive Memory Transformer} (PMT), which augments a transformer with writable, window-aligned memory that exposes the mid-range scale alongside the token and sequence-level representations conventional transformers already provide. Across eight UCR/UEA/UCI classification benchmarks, a cue-retention probe, and forecasting benchmarks, PMT learns representations that probe well at the global, mid-range, and local scales---strong low-label classification (1--5\% labels), competitive forecasting performance across multiple horizons, and quantitative and qualitative evidence that memory states capture mid-range motifs.
  </details>

- **[OraclePhys: A Systematic Framework for LLM Fine-Tuning on Structural Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17162)**  `arXiv:2608.17162`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mingyu Li, Guorui Song, Jing Lin, Haoqian Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What fine-tuning teaches a model is usually diagnosed after the fact. We make it an experimental variable. OraclePhys has three parts. OraclePhys-Bench is a structural-mechanics benchmark whose finite-element oracle grades every answer and counterfactual edit. OraclePhys-30K holds seven answer forms over byte-identical structure descriptions. A controlled training study compares the forms and verifier roles. Two findings follow. First, within this task family the answer form of the label determines what fine-tuning teaches. Ranking supervision lifts held-out localization of the most-deforming story on taller structures from 0.25 to 0.86, a peak-value label yields at best a partial predictor, and a pass/fail label none. This ranking-versus-peak gap replicates on a second domain, a second model family and a paraphrased surface. Second, the gap between our standard SFT and GRPO runs is mostly a recipe effect. With learning rate and step budget matched, the two reach held-out accuracies within 0.03 of each other. At higher rates, GRPO falls below its start while SFT does not. Both also teach the model to apply edits given as instructions. The trained 8B matches a specialist and exceeds a frontier LLM. Both domains are linear, steady-state systems with synthetic text inputs.
  </details>

- **[SinAE: A Single-Architecture Flow-Matching Autoencoder for Cross-Domain Atomic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12380)**  `arXiv:2607.12380`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Ren, Fan Yang, Jianhua Yao, Yatao Bian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small molecules, crystals, and proteins all reduce to atoms in 3D space, yet their generative pipelines remain fragmented across domains, each with its Small molecules, crystals, and proteins all reduce to atoms in 3D space, yet their generative pipelines remain fragmented across domains, each with its own graph, equivariant, or frame-based architecture. Cross-domain training would mitigate per-domain data scarcity, but direct generation in 3D coordinate space cannot easily handle the heterogeneous structural priors of all three domains, and no prior latent autoencoder is simultaneously lossless and architecturally general across all three. We introduce SinAE, a single-architecture flow-matching autoencoder for molecules, crystals, and proteins, with vanilla Transformer encoder and decoder and no equivariant, graph, or domain-specific operators. Rather than requiring the encoder to capture fine-grained geometry, SinAE shifts the reconstruction burden into an iterative flow-matching decoder, achieving near-lossless reconstruction across domains and reducing reconstruction errors by orders of magnitude relative to prior latent baselines. The same per-token latent supports a standard Diffusion Transformer prior that reaches strong performance on molecular, crystal, and protein generation benchmarks. Joint molecule--crystal training strictly improves both domains, providing direct evidence of cross-domain transfer through a shared atomic latent. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Seahorse: A Unified Benchmarking Framework for Spatiotemporal Event Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01022)**  `arXiv:2607.01022`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yahya Aalaila, Sebastian Vollmer, Gerrit Großmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatiotemporal point processes (STPPs) model event data in continuous time and space, with applications in mobility, epidemiology, and public safety. Recent neural STPPs span expressive intensities, density models, continuous-time latent dynamics, normalizing-flow spatial decoders, and score-based generative mechanisms. Yet comparison remains fragile because implementations differ in preprocessing, coordinate normalization, splits, likelihood conventions, and evaluation protocols. We present Seahorse, a unified framework for reproducible training and evaluation of STPPs. Seahorse provides an encode-evolve-decode interface and an executable benchmarking contract that standardizes dataset handling, tuning, raw-coordinate likelihood reporting, and run artifacts across classical baselines, neural likelihood models, continuous-time Neural STPP variants, and sample-based generative models. On 13 real datasets and 19 presets, no model dominates: likelihood, calibration, and next-event prediction favor different models, and the strongest models take from minutes to hours to train. Using HawkesNest as a controlled synthetic stress-test suite, we show how diagnostic benchmarking can probe neural STPP behavior under increasing spatiotemporal entanglement. Seahorse provides reusable infrastructure for comparing, diagnosing, and extending STPP models beyond implementation-specific leaderboards. The implementation, including preset configurations and reproduction scripts, is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Demystifying Numerical Instability in LLM Inference: Achieving Reproducible Inference for Mission-Critical Tasks with HEAL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21023)**  `arXiv:2606.21023`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenting Zhu, Lucas Thai, Shan Yu, Yicheng Liu, Yifan Qiao, Chenxi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As Large Language Models (LLMs) deploy into mission-critical domains (e.g., finance, medicine, and law), output reproducibility has become a strict system requirement. While practitioners use greedy decoding to eliminate algorithmic stochasticity, empirical deployments with 16-bit precisions still exhibit catastrophic output divergence across heterogeneous GPUs. Through SASS-level profiling, we reveal that this inconsistency is fundamentally driven by truncation errors introduced during downcasting at kernel boundaries. However, achieving reproducibility via a global FP32 pipeline incurs prohibitive system penalties: bypassing 16-bit hardware accelerators hurts compute efficiency, while upcasting the KV cache doubles memory overhead. To bridge this gap, we propose Hybrid Error ALleviation (HEAL), a targeted intervention that approximates FP32 precision while resolving hardware constraints through two targeted mechanisms. First, recognizing that floating-point formats underutilize their bit-width for Q, K, V tensors, HEAL applies INT16 quantization that preserves numerical stability without expanding the KV cache footprint. Second, HEAL synthesizes high-precision matrix multiplications via an algebraic error compensation strategy, executing entirely on high-throughput 16-bit Tensor Cores. To evaluate our approach practically, we introduce MCR-Bench, a benchmark targeting reproducibility in mission-critical tasks. HEAL achieves the same level of reproducibility on downstream tasks as the FP32 baseline while reducing the performance overhead by up to 6.0$\times$.
  </details>

- **[Utility-Constrained Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14029)**  `arXiv:2606.14029`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mehrdad Moghimi, Bernardo Avila Pires_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Constrained MDPs (CMDPs) are a widely adopted framework for incorporating safety into RL agents; however, the framework does not support risk-sensitive constraints. This can be problematic: For example, CMDPs allow for optimal solutions that, in order to satisfy the risk-neutral constraints, mix infrequent catastrophic behaviors and frequent, overly conservative ones. Moreover, prior empirical results suggest that enforcing stricter, risk-sensitive constraints can improve performance even under risk-neutral evaluation. The natural framework to incorporate risk-sensitive constraints is utility-constrained MDPs (UCMDPs), but no practical solutions for this problem existed. In this work, we introduce a simple yet powerful methodology for UCMDPs and constrained RL. Besides allowing for risk-sensitive constraints, our framework does not require us to fix constraint limits in advance of training the agent, provided that a sensible range is known. This increases policy flexibility and, in practice, allows for adjustments to these limits at no extra training cost. Besides benefiting from the generality of the framework, our agent shows strong performance in practice, consistently matching or outperforming existing baselines in several Safety Gymnasium benchmark tasks.
  </details>

- **[HARENA: A Living Benchmark for Sensor-Based Human Activity Recognition on Resource-Constrained Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13194)**  `arXiv:2606.13194`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maximilian Burzer, Tobias King, Till Riedel, Michael Beigl, Tobias Röddiger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sensor-based human activity recognition runs on edge devices, trading performance against latency, memory, and energy. Yet new architectures are usually compared with few, often outdated baselines on few datasets with study-specific data preparation and hardware, leaving open which perform best at what cost. We present HARENA, a living benchmark with a public leaderboard, to which new architectures and datasets can be added under one protocol. Its initial suite evaluates 16 neural architectures and three classical baselines on 29 datasets, measuring performance on unseen subjects and deployment cost on a smartphone, smartwatch, and microcontroller. CNN-HAR, the suite's oldest deep architecture, leads overall, but 8 architectures lie within 2.5 points of its mean test macro-F1, while their cost differs by orders of magnitude. On the microcontroller, Random Forest leads on latency and peak RAM, LITEWAY-Light on flash, and TinyHAR is most accurate within the real-time budget. Rankings shift with dataset and channel count, so general claims require many datasets, which a growing benchmark can provide. We find no sign of benchmark overfitting, but performance drops by a median of 15.0 points on unseen subjects, about three times the typical spread between architectures, making generalization and adaptation the most promising future directions.
  </details>

- **[From Residuals to Reasons: LLM-Guided Mechanism Inference from Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22897)**  `arXiv:2605.22897`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad R. Rezaei, Rahul G. Krishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When software fails, we read the error, form a hypothesis, write a patch, and test it. LLMs now do this for neural networks by editing weights; when a tabular model fails, we usually retune it. Most interpretability methods report which features matter rather than how they combine. Rather than asking an LLM to predict the target over the whole output space, we anchor the prediction with a base model and ask the LLM to debug it. We introduce Multi-Agent Residual In-Context Learning (MARICL), in which LLM agents read high-residual examples, hypothesize the missing structure, and write executable correction terms that are then refined by multi-turn textual gradient optimization. The resulting corrections are readable, executable hypotheses about what the base model misses, rather than validated mechanisms. Across nine benchmarks covering scientific, biomedical, socioeconomic, and synthetic data, MARICL reliably repairs its base model's structured residuals, improving it on every dataset, and on synthetic data absent from pretraining it recovers the hidden structure (two seeds). Against the strongest competing baseline it leads on five of the eight real benchmarks and trails TabPFN on the other three, while adding an inspectable correction that black-box baselines do not provide.Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FISolver: Data-Driven Discovery of First Integrals via Backward Generation and Guided Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21160)**  `arXiv:2605.21160`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jingfeng Zhong, Zhengxiang Liu, Zhijie Wang, Shuai Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The discovery of first integrals is of profound scientific significance for understanding physical conservation laws in dynamical systems. However, existing symbolic computation systems and Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with this task due to the scarcity of high-quality training data and the reliance on mathematical intuition for finding solutions. This paper introduces FISolver, an LLM-based solver designed to bridge this gap. First, we propose a novel "Backward Generation" algorithm that systematically constructs large-scale datasets of (differential equations, first integrals) pairs by deriving equations from sampled integrals, effectively overcoming the data scarcity bottleneck. Second, we perform supervised fine-tuning on a compact mathematical model and further enhance its capabilities using reinforcement learning with a Levenshtein Distance-based shaped reward function. Additionally, we introduce data synthesis and blending techniques that enable effective adaptation to specific hard instances with sparse examples. Experiments demonstrate that FISolver, with significantly lower computational cost, substantially outperforms larger mathematical LLMs and commercial solvers like Mathematica on complex benchmarks, establishing a new data-driven paradigm for automated mathematical discovery.
  </details>

- **[PRIM: Meta-Learned Bayesian Root Cause Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08786)**  `arXiv:2605.08786`  `cs.LG`  
  _Christopher Lohse, Anish Dhir, Amadou Ba, Bradley Eck, Marco Ruffini, Jonas Wahl_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Root Cause Analysis (RCA) aims to identify the main drivers of anomalies in complex multivariate systems. The task is challenging due to the need for structural causal knowledge for some methods, and the computational cost of inference at test time. This paper introduces PRIM (Prior-fitted Root cause Identification with Meta-learning), a causal meta-learning approach that frames RCA as a Bayesian inference task over a prior of causal models. By marginalising out structural uncertainty, PRIM detects changes in the data-generating mechanism between normal and anomalous periods, infers distributional differences without explicit statistical testing, and implicitly learns causal structure without model fitting at test time. Following the simulation-based meta-learning paradigm of prior-fitted networks (PFNs), PRIM uses a transformer neural process (TNP) that jointly attends over normal and anomalous samples and the structural dependencies of nodes, enabling fast zero-shot inference in systems with up to 100 variables. Across synthetic benchmarks and two realistic benchmark datasets, PetShop and CausRCA, PRIM is competitive with other graph-free methods.
  </details>

- **[AnomaMind: Coarse-to-Fine Agentic Diagnosis via Hybrid Inference for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13807)**  `arXiv:2602.13807`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Tao, Yuchong Wu, Mingyue Cheng, Ze Guo, Min Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) supports the analysis of web service key performance indicators (KPIs) by identifying anomalous intervals. Recent large language model (LLM)-based agentic approaches have achieved promising results through reasoning and tool use. Despite their effectiveness, precise anomaly localization remains challenging, as diverse anomaly patterns demand targeted numerical evidence and iterative refinement to accurately determine anomaly boundaries. In this work, we propose AnomaMind, a coarse-to-fine agentic diagnosis framework for TSAD. AnomaMind first identifies suspicious regions from time-series visualizations, then adaptively acquires targeted numerical evidence through multi-turn tool interactions, and finally refines anomaly decisions through self-reflection. This process is supported by a tool engine that integrates knowledge memory and numerical analysis. To coordinate this diagnostic process, we design a hybrid inference strategy that employs frozen general-purpose LLMs for initial localization, tool orchestration, and evaluation, alongside a specialized LLM detector for evidence-grounded interval prediction. We optimize only the specialized detector through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on diagnostic trajectories and reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable detection rewards. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning web traffic, service KPIs, and system-monitoring signals, under both in-domain and multi-domain training settings, demonstrate competitive performance and support the effectiveness of coarse-to-fine adaptive diagnosis through targeted evidence acquisition and self-reflectivethis http URLcode is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Self-Rewarding Sequential Monte Carlo for Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01849)**  `arXiv:2602.01849`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwei Luo, Ziqi Jin, Lei Wang, Lidong Bing, Thomas B. Schön_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work presents self-rewarding sequential Monte Carlo (SMC), an inference-time scaling algorithm enabling effective sampling of masked diffusion language models (MDLMs). Our algorithm stems from the observation that most existing MDLMs rely on a confidence-based sampling strategy, where only tokens with the highest prediction confidence are preserved at each step. This restricts the generation to a noise-sensitive, greedy decoding paradigm, resulting in an inevitable collapse in the diversity of possible paths. We address this problem by launching multiple interacting diffusion processes in parallel, referred to as particles, for trajectory exploration. Importantly, we introduce the trajectory-level confidence as a self-rewarding signal for assigning particle importance weights. During sampling, particles are iteratively weighted and resampled to systematically steer generation towards globally confident, high-quality samples. Our self-rewarding SMC is verified on various masked diffusion language models and benchmarks, achieving significant improvement without extra training or reward guidance, while effectively converting parallel inference capacity into improved sampling quality. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[COMET: Codebook-based Online-adaptive Multi-scale Embedding for Time-series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01635)**  `arXiv:2602.01635`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jinwoo Park, Hyeongwon Kang, Seung Hun Han, Pilsung Kang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time-series anomaly detection aims to identify abnormal temporal patterns in complex real-world systems. However, robust unsupervised time-series anomaly detection remains challenging because anomalies can manifest over different temporal ranges and emerge from interactions among variables, while distribution shifts can cause normal patterns to drift over time. To address these challenges, we propose Codebook-based Online-adaptive Multi-scale Embedding for Time-series anomaly detection (COMET), a unified framework with three tightly coupled components. First, Multi-scale Patch Encoder learns correlation-aware patch embeddings by combining variable-specific temporal dynamics with shared inter-variable structure across multiple patch scales. Second, Vector-Quantized Coreset quantizes these embeddings into representative normal prototypes, which are used to detect anomalies through a dual score combining quantization error and density-aware memory distance. Third, Online Codebook Adaptation leverages the same codebook structure to identify reliable normal samples from training-activated codebook entries, and then updates the prototypes through contrastive learning at inference time. Experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that COMET achieves the best performance on 39 out of 45 evaluation metrics compared to seven baselines, showing robust performance across diverse evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[JURIST: Training Small Data-Reweighted Reasoning Judges for Test-Time Scaling in Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22230)**  `arXiv:2601.22230`  `cs.LG`  
  _Peijia Qin, Ruiyi Zhang, Qi Cao, Pengtao Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Best-of-N selection for code generation is only as good as the judge that picks among the candidates, and the judges accurate enough for this role are costly frontier LLMs. A small judge can be trained on abundant earlier data labeled by unit tests, but it is deployed on newer problems: the earlier problems are easier and teach it little, their tasks differ, and their candidates come from weaker policies, while data close to deployment are scarce. We present JURIST, a 1.7B reasoning judge that uses a small meta set close to deployment to learn how much each earlier training sample should count. In our experiments, we find that training on the meta set alone, or adding it to the training data, does not help, while using it to weight the earlier data through a bi-level objective adds 3.9 Hard points over adding it to the training data. On LiveCodeBench, JURIST reaches 84.7% Overall and matches Claude Sonnet 4.6 on Hard problems (73.8%) at 463x lower cost; on BigCodeBench, it surpasses all test-time scaling baselines (35.9%). Abundant earlier data, guided by a small deployment-aligned meta set, can thus train a compact judge that selects accurately at low cost.
  </details>

- **[LeMat-GenBench: A Unified Evaluation Framework for Crystal Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04562)**  `arXiv:2512.04562`  `cs.LG`  
  _Siddharth Betala, Samuel P. Gleason, Ali Ramlaoui, Andy Xu, Georgia Channing, Daniel Levy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative machine learning (ML) models hold great promise for accelerating materials discovery through the inverse design of inorganic crystals, enabling an unprecedented exploration of chemical space. Yet, the lack of standardized evaluation frameworks makes it challenging to evaluate, compare, and further develop these ML models meaningfully. In this work, we introduce LeMat-GenBench, a unified benchmark for generative models of crystalline materials, supported by a set of evaluation metrics designed to better inform model development and downstream applications. We release both an open-source evaluation suite and a public leaderboard on Hugging Face, and benchmark 12 recent generative models. Results reveal that an increase in stability is accompanied by lower novelty and diversity on average, with no model excelling across all dimensions. Altogether, LeMat-GenBench establishes a reproducible and extensible foundation for fair model comparison and aims to guide the development of more reliable, discovery-oriented generative models for crystalline materials.
  </details>

- **[WirelessMathBench-XL: An Auditable Benchmark for Wireless Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23219)**  `arXiv:2509.23219`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xin Li, Mengbing Liu, Yiyang Zhu, Wenhe Zhang, Li Wei, Jiancheng An, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks built from arXiv papers can overlap the arXiv text used in LLM pretraining, yet technical-domain releases rarely ship the per-item evidence needed to check this. We introduce a reverse-probe n-gram audit that indexes the benchmark side and streams the corpus past it, so memory scales with the benchmark rather than the corpus and flags on the distributed problems can be inspected and recomputed. We instantiate it in WirelessMathBench-XL, a 4,027-problem benchmark for wireless mathematical reasoning built from 836 arXiv papers across 20 subfields, with multiple-choice, progressive fill-in, and full-equation-completion formats. Streaming 12.9 B RedPajama-arXiv 13-grams yields a strict zero-hit view $\mathcal{S}_0$ covering 95.7% of the problems, and shows that the Dolma and ProofPile-2 arXiv slices repackage the same RedPajama dump. Filtering to $\mathcal{S}_0$ moves every evaluated model by less than 1 pp. With 30/800 flagged test items, this comparison bounds detected-overlap inflation at 0.31-0.51 pp for frontier models rather than testing for contamination, and it does not cover paraphrase, target-answer, alternate-version, post-training, or closed-corpus exposure. Frontier models reach 86.5-91.3%, and GRPO on the released training split lifts a 7B model 25.1 pp above a size-matched general-math control. We release source-paper identifiers, per-problem audit and threshold metadata, license-aware data files, audit and bootstrap scripts, Croissant metadata, and a Datasheet.
  </details>

- **[Staying in the Sweet Spot: Boost Reasoning Evolution via Responsive Rollout Difficulty Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06923)**  `arXiv:2509.06923`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziheng Li, Zexu Sun, Jinman Zhao, Erxue Min, Yongcheng Zeng, Hui Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has advanced the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, existing RLVR methods often suffer from exploration inefficiency due to mismatches between problem difficulty and model capability: overly difficult problems hinder reasoning path discovery, while overly simple problems offer little learning signal. To address this, we first formalize the effect of problem difficulty by quantifying the relationship between loss descent magnitude and rollout accuracy. Building on this analysis, we propose SEELE, a supervision-aided RLVR framework that dynamically adjusts problem difficulty to lie within the high-performance region. SEELE augments each training sample by appending a hint (part of a full solution) for difficulty reduction. Unlike previous hint-based approaches, SEELE deliberately computes the hint length for each individual problem to achieve an optimal difficulty. The optimal hint length is determined via multi-round rollout sampling, where an item response theory model fits accuracy-hint pairs from previous rounds to predict the next-round hint. This instance-level, real-time difficulty adjustment aligns problem difficulty with the evolving model capability, thereby improving exploration efficiency. Experiments show that SEELE outperforms Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Supervised Fine-tuning (SFT) by +10.0 and +8.4 points, respectively, and exceeds the best prior supervision-aided approach by +3.8 points on average across six math reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SurgTAP-3D: A Large-Scale Cross-Surgery Video Dataset with Contact-Aware 3D Motion Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11697)**  `arXiv:2611.11697`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Jiaxin Guo, Wenzhen Dong, Taoyu Wu, Haomin Kuang, Mengya Xu, Kejian Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding 3D scene dynamics in minimally invasive surgery (MIS) is a fundamental step toward physically intelligent and autonomous surgical systems. However, existing public datasets offer limited scalability and diversity in real-world clinical scenarios. Moreover, annotations typically focus on either tissue or instruments alone, overlooking tool-tissue interactions. This gap limits applications such as augmented reality guidance and autonomous surgery driven by vision-language-action (VLA) models or world models, which demand large-scale, high-quality 3D supervision. In this work, we construct SurgTAP-3D, a large-scale in-vivo pseudo-3D surgical motion tracking dataset and benchmark, comprising 520K frames, 4,549 action clips, and 1.4M 2D/3D point trajectories from diverse robotic and manual procedures under unified action categories. To make annotation scalable, we design a unified framework for surgical 3D point tracking that converts uncalibrated monocular surgical videos into structured 4D annotations through contact-region prompting, active-tool segmentation, dynamic bundle adjustment, and global 4D refinement. This pipeline adapts foundation priors to surgical videos and produces temporally consistent trajectories, pseudo-scale geometry, camera poses, and instrument masks. The dataset captures realistic challenges inherent to surgical environments, including occlusions, smoke, bleeding, and rapid instrument motion. We establish a comprehensive benchmark on SurgPose, SurgT, and STIR, covering both instrument and tissue tracking. We demonstrate instrument displacement estimation, deformable surface reconstruction, and foundation-model fine-tuning. Extensive experiments and analysis demonstrate the effectiveness of our dataset and annotation framework for 3D surgical point tracking, reconstruction, and surgical action understanding. Homepage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Locating and Repairing Domain Shift in VLM Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10720)**  `arXiv:2611.10720`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhihong Cui, Hengyu Liu, Michael Alexander Riegler, Guandong Xu, Amir Taherkordi, Tor Skeie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are increasingly adopted as end-to-end driving planners, yet they suffer from cross-city domain shift. Existing methods treat this shift as a single quantity addressed by uniform alignment or single-site editing. We challenge this view on two counts: (i) domain shift in VLM trajectory planners is inherently structured across modality and layer, and (ii) a locate-then-repair recipe matched to this structure outperforms every uniform-objective baseline. We analyse domain shift via activation patching and uncover two regularities. (F1) Modality axis. Image and text tokens exhibit distinct shift patterns in each VLM. (F2) Layer axis. The layer-wise shift partition is architecture-dependent and dataset-stable. These findings recast domain shift as a two-dimensional tensor indexed by modality (image vs. text tokens) and layer, motivating a simple principle: locate where shift occurs, and repair only there. We instantiate this principle in MoLaRx (Modality-Layer Repair), a locate-then-repair framework. The Causal Importance Score (CIS) estimates the domain shift as a modality-layer tensor and partitions the layers into functional zones. CIS-Guided Selective Repair (CISR) then applies a matched operator (Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) / low-rank adaptation (LoRA) / freezing) to repair each zone, with the composition adapting automatically to each architecture. We evaluate MoLaRx on three architecturally distinct VLMs (LFM2-VL-1.6B, Qwen2-VL-2B, and PaliGemma2-3B) across two cross-city benchmarks (nuScenes and Argoverse 2). MoLaRx attains the lowest cross-city trajectory-error gap among adaptation methods in all six main-table settings, while using 50-60% fewer trainable parameters and 8.4-10.9 ms lower inference latency. In contrast, uniform MMD-only adaptation collapses in all six settings, highlighting the importance of structure-guided repair.
  </details>

- **[Dynamics-Informed Action Learning for Goal-Conditioned Latent Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10660)**  `arXiv:2611.10660`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yinuo Jiang, Guangzhi Wang, Yiran Wang, Junfu Pu, Cheng Cheng, Honghao Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models enable visual control by planning action sequences in compact representation spaces, but a gap remains between how latent dynamics are learned and how they are used for planning. First, teacher-forced training does not expose the dynamics model to the recursively predicted latent states encountered during planning. Second, sampling-based planners typically initialize action search from goal-agnostic proposals, without exploiting the action-conditioned dynamics learned by the world model. We introduce Dynamics-Informed Action Learning (DIAL) to bridge these two gaps. During training, rollout alignment exposes the forward model to its own autoregressive latent predictions, while multi-step inverse supervision trains a lightweight inverse dynamics head to infer actions from latent-state transitions. We further apply the inverse head to model-generated transitions with its parameters held fixed, encouraging predicted rollouts to retain action-relevant information. At test time, DIAL autoregressively couples the inverse and forward dynamics to construct a dynamics-informed action sequence toward the goal, which initializes the mean of the Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) proposal distribution. The subsequent CEM search then refines this initialization using the standard sampling and updating procedure. Experiments across four visual control benchmarks demonstrate that DIAL outperforms existing latent world models and planning baselines in both standard and long-horizon scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Controllable Road Marking Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05771)**  `arXiv:2610.05771`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhiyu Joey Cai, Yufan Zhang, Ruichen Tan, Zengxiang Lei, Satish Ukkusuri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lane and road markings provide critical guidance for vehicle navigation and multi-agent coordination, yet authoring them at scale remains a manual workflow that limits quantitative analysis and scenario testing. We introduce Controllable Road Marking Generation, which synthesizes a missing center-region marking layout from a drivable-area mask, optional outer-ring markings, and a textual description. Our benchmark uses deterministic, metadata-derived prompts and three output channels: lane dividers, road dividers, and pedestrian crossings. We develop a conditional bird's-eye-view (BEV) pipeline that combines (i) a text-conditioned latent rectified-flow DiT trained with a topology-aware auxiliary loss, (ii) Gaussian-blurred training targets that provide smoother supervision for thin, sparse markings, and (iii) Structured Gaussian Render (SGR), a training-free post-process that recovers crisp divider geometry by extracting polylines, fitting cubic Bézier curves, and re-rendering them as anisotropic super-Gaussian primitives. On 4,597 Argoverse~2 test tiles, our system achieves Buffered F1 of 76.8 and clDice of 50.2, compared with 38.8 and 24.6 for an adapted mask-refinement baseline. When trained independently on the Waymo Open Dataset, it yields 88.0 Buffered F1 and 66.2 clDice. Component ablations show complementary connectivity gains from topology-aware supervision and SGR. Text-editing experiments reveal that stronger guidance improves edit success but also increases changes to non-target structures. We see this framework as a step toward simulation-ready road-marking variation, automated map completion, and early-stage infrastructure design exploration.
  </details>

- **[The Road Ahead in Autonomous Driving: The KITScenes Multimodal Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02956)**  `arXiv:2606.02956`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Richard Schwarzkopf, Fabian Immel, Alexander Blumberg, Jonas Merkert, Nils Rack, Kaiwen Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing autonomous driving datasets have enabled major progress, but fall short in sensor fidelity, map completeness, or geographic diversity. We present KITScenes Multimodal, a European dataset built around high-fidelity sensors and maps. Our fully synchronized sensor suite combines high-resolution global-shutter cameras, long-range lidar beyond 400 m, 4D imaging radar, and redundant GNSS/INS localization. Our HD maps are, to our knowledge, the most complete of any sensor dataset, validated through autonomous driving trials on open-source software. For the first time in a public dataset, all driving-relevant traffic elements, such as traffic lights, are mapped in 3D to a reprojection-accurate level with full topological connectivity. Recorded in cities with irregular street layouts and mixed traffic modes, our dataset complements existing datasets by broadening the available geographic diversity. We also introduce four benchmarks, each advancing spatial learning for embodied AI: online HD map construction, long-range depth estimation, novel view synthesis, and end-to-end driving. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Watching Itself Watch: Self-Auditing Visual Reliance for Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11949)**  `arXiv:2611.11949`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Rohan Dugad, Mi Luo, Kristen Grauman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video reasoning hinges on a small number of visually decisive tokens embedded in otherwise largely language-driven chains of thought. Yet prevailing post-training methods such as GRPO overlook this structure, assigning coarse trajectory-level rewards while requiring multiple rollouts and often costly external judges. We introduce Visually-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation (VIOS), a post-training framework that identifies where a model under-utilizes visual evidence and concentrates learning at those positions. Our key insight is that a model can audit its own visual reliance: the divergence between its next-token distributions with and without the video reveals how strongly each prediction depends on the visual input. Measuring this reliance for both a student and an answer-privileged teacher exposes a per-token reliance gap, identifying positions where the teacher exploits visual evidence that the student overlooks. VIOS translates this signal into dense, targeted supervision through three complementary components: an exponential moving average (EMA) teacher that co-evolves with the student, a contrastive objective that encourages predictions to depend on the video, and a reliance-gap gate that focuses learning on visually under-utilized tokens. Crucially, VIOS requires only a single on-policy rollout from a single model, without external rewards, judges, or larger teachers. Across 12 benchmarks spanning general, temporal, spatial, knowledge-intensive, and long-video understanding, VIOS consistently improves three model backbones and outperforms contemporary video-reasoning systems, while training in over 9x fewer steps, 60x fewer FLOPs, and 111x fewer generated tokens. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Semantically Aligned KV Cache Compression for Efficient Agentic Autoregressive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11452)**  `arXiv:2611.11452`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Huaijia Kou, Tianyu Xie, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic autoregressive video generation combines semantic planning, sequential generation, and visual verification for controllable synthesis. Growing visual histories enlarge the key-value (KV) cache, increasing memory use and attention costs across candidate rollouts. Existing compression methods primarily optimize individual generators without the planner's semantic constraints, risking low priority for states supporting requested attributes or actions. We introduce SemanticKV, a training-free method for semantically aligned KV cache compression. To our knowledge, SemanticKV is the first KV cache compression method for agentic autoregressive video generation that explicitly uses planner-extracted semantic constraints to guide retention. Text cross-attention identifies constraint-related video positions, whose self-attention queries are collected into separate groups. Attention weights are averaged within each group and maximized across groups, giving each cached entry a high score when strongly used by any represented constraint. A single top-k selection retains high-scoring entries under the memory budget, leaving their keys, values, and positions unchanged. For fixed query groups and equal entry costs, this selection minimizes an additive upper bound on worst-group discarded attention. The method operates independently within each candidate without retraining or additional video generations. We evaluate four AR video backbones on three benchmarks for 5-, 30-, and 60-second generation. On Causal Forcing at a compression ratio of 0.7, SemanticKV obtains a VBench Total Score of 84.10, compared with 84.05 for the strongest compressed baseline and 83.99 for Full KV. It achieves a 1.22x speedup over Full KV and reduces KV-cache memory from 6.04 GB to 1.81 GB, a 3.35x reduction.
  </details>

- **[PhysDNet: Physics-Driven Gradient Amplification for Real-Time Image Dehazing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10376)**  `arXiv:2611.10376`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Rayen Dhahri, Lei Sun, Victor Javier Kartsch Morinigo, Alice Pagnoux, Luca Benini, Danda Pani Paudel, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern dehazing networks are often computationally expensive and overfit to synthetic haze, limiting real-time deployment. This underscores an urgent demand for efficient, high-fidelity restoration methods that can operate on resource-constrained edge devices in practical, dynamic environments. We introduce PhysDNet, a lightweight multimodal architecture that fuses NIR imagery with sparse LiDAR depth and is trained with a physics-constrained dual-head objective. Differentiating through a fixed Koschmieder inversion induces density-adaptive gradient amplification, acting as an implicit resource allocation mechanism that concentrates learning on heavily degraded regions without increasing inference cost. PhysDNet delivers superior restoration with minimal capacity and enhanced training efficiency, facilitating real-time deployment on edge devices without increasing inference overhead. On the Seeing Through Fog benchmark, PhysDNet outperforms competitors by 1.67 dB at 5.2 times lower compute while recovering 54.9% of fog-induced detection loss. Ablations and cross-domain evaluations show that physics-guided training improves generalization, including under non-homogeneous haze that violates the Koschmieder assumption. PhysDNet further demonstrates compatibility with fixed-function NPUs, running at 29/45/99 FPS at <4 W across L/M/S variants. Code, deployment scripts and pre-trained checkpoints are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reliability-Aware Benchmarking of Self-Supervised Learning in Histopathology Under 1-10% Label Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10003)**  `arXiv:2611.10003`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Alicem Koyun, Pranjali Changadeo Pingale, Sachin Mote, Gianluca Pizzuti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert annotation in digital pathology is expensive, and low-label regimes remain common in realistic histopathology workflows. Self-supervised learning (SSL) offers a practical way to learn transferable representations from unlabeled pathology images, but performance-only evaluation can hide unreliable confidence behavior under domain shift. We present a reliability-aware benchmark of SimCLR, BYOL, and an MAE-inspired masked-reconstruction baseline on PatchCamelyon (PCam; 96x96) and PANDA (prostate cancer) under 1%, 5%, and 10% labeled regimes with frozen-encoder linear probing. To address common single-run concerns, ResNet-18 results are repeated over 3 splits x 3 seeds (n=9) and ViT-B/16 over 1 split x 3 seeds (n=3) under practical compute constraints, with mean+/-std reporting and per-run logs. Beyond AUROC, accuracy, and F1-score, we report Expected Calibration Error and Brier score, OOD confidence diagnostics for cross-dataset PCam<->PANDA scoring, federated-vs-centralized SimCLR comparisons on PCam, and cross-dataset transfer to colorectal histology tiles. SSL improves label efficiency on the more controlled PCam task, while PANDA's noisier patch-level proxy can remain near coin-flip with higher variance. The study highlights that histopathology SSL should be assessed not only by ranking performance but also by calibration, robustness, and transparent reproducibility diagnostics before clinical interpretation.
  </details>

- **[AAMBERS-UAV: Acquisition-Aware Multimodal Backbone Evaluation and Ranking for UAV Weedy Rice Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05762)**  `arXiv:2609.05762`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Tarek Rahman, Nazim-E-Alam, Md Kishor Morol, Jannatun Noor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  UAV image collections contain spatially and temporally related frames, yet semantic-segmentation benchmarks commonly split them at image level. Such splitting can place samples from one acquisition in both model development and testing, obscuring transfer to a genuinely new survey. Using the 734-sample WeedyRice-RGBMS-DB, we fix a 124-image target-acquisition test set and compare two protocols with identical train, validation, and test counts: target-held-out, which excludes the target acquisition from development, and target-exposed, which admits its remaining images. SegFormer-B0 is evaluated with RGB, four-band multispectral (MS), and seven-channel RGB+MS input over two fixed-split seeds. RGB is strongest under complete acquisition holdout ($0.7317\pm0.0201$ IoU), whereas RGB+MS becomes strongest after target exposure ($0.7822\pm0.0269$). A fixed-split U-Net/ResNet18 replication confirms positive exposure gains for all three inputs, but retains RGB as the best modality under both protocols. Acquisition exposure therefore increases measured performance across both evaluated backbones, while its effect on modality ranking is architecture-dependent. A supplied-split audit reveals strong near-sequential dependence, and corruption tests show that early fusion is substantially more sensitive to RGB--MS displacement than to moderate radiometric scaling. These results support acquisition-aware same-test evaluation as a necessary complement to ordinary image-level splitting in multimodal UAV benchmarks. The code and supporting the findings of this study will be publicly released upon acceptance of the paper.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19882)**  `arXiv:2606.19882`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Tongqing Shi, Ge Yan, Tuomas Oikarinen, Tsui-Wei Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) enhance the interpretability of deep neural networks by aligning the features extracted from images with human-interpretable concepts. However, conventional CBMs typically use a linear classifier that maps concept activations to a fixed set of predefined classes, limiting their flexibility in open-vocabulary settings. In this paper, we propose Multimodal Concept Bottleneck Model (MM-CBM) to address these issues and extend CBMs into CLIP structure. MM-CBM utilizes dual Concept Bottleneck Layers (CBLs) to align both the image and text embeddings into interpretable features. This allows us to perform new vision tasks like zero-shot classification or image retrieval in an interpretable way. Compared to existing methods, MM-CBM achieves 23.28% accuracy improvement on average across four standard benchmarks. Our method maintains competitive performance compared with black-box CLIP model while offering interpretability. Under classification tasks, MM-CBM's accuracy is on average 3.94% lower than CLIP in the target-dataset adaptation setting and 9.84% in the zero-shot transfer setting. Under image-text retrieval tasks, it achieves an average gap of 2.48% for image retrieval and 9.22% for text retrieval.
  </details>

- **[Root-Selecting Fixed-Point Inversion for Rectified Flows via Trajectory Straightness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17584)**  `arXiv:2606.17584`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Semin Kim, Jihwan Yoon, Seunghoon Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Finding the initial noise that generates a given data sample, known as inversion, is a key component for downstream applications such as training-free image editing. Existing fixed-point inversion methods improve inversion accuracy by formulating each inversion step as a fixed-point problem, but they lack a principled mechanism for selecting among multiple fixed-point solutions that can arise in practice. We observe that different selections induce different inversion trajectories, leading to substantial variation in reconstruction and editing quality. For rectified flows, we further find that this variation is closely associated with trajectory straightness, motivating straightness as a principled selection criterion. We propose SelFix, a fixed-point inversion method that selects fixed-point solutions inducing straighter inverse trajectories while retaining convergence to an exact inverse root under standard local assumptions. Experiments on FLUX.1-dev and PIE-Bench show that SelFix improves fixed-point inversion, achieving stronger real-image reconstruction and better source-preserving prompt-based editing than prior inversion baselines. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Drives Test-Time Adaptation for CLIP? A Controlled Empirical Study from an Update Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14299)**  `arXiv:2606.14299`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiazhen Huang, Xiao Chen, Zhiming Liu, Yaru Sun, Jingyan Jiang, Zhi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) such as CLIP have become a standard backbone for open-vocabulary recognition, yet their zero-shot predictions remain vulnerable to distribution shifts encountered at deployment. Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) has recently been extended to CLIP as a lightweight solution, leading to a rapidly growing body of TTA4CLIP methods. However, empirical progress in this area has largely outpaced our understanding of what truly drives adaptation, where their gains originate, and under which shifts they remain reliable. In this paper, we take a step back from the pursuit of state-of-the-art accuracy and conduct a systematic controlled study of TTA4CLIP. We first organize existing methods into three unified paradigms according to what is updated at test time. We then introduce TTABC, an open-source TTA Benchmark for CLIP, which standardizes evaluation protocols and integrates more than 20 representative methods. Our controlled empirical analysis focuses on three key areas. First, we determine the driving factors in parameter-based methods, revealing that adaptation gains are primarily driven by test-time evidence and reliable proxies rather than heavy optimization. Second, we explore evidence utilization beyond heavy parameter tuning, showing that competitive and efficient performance can be achieved through cross- or current-sample evidence and lightweight prototype updates. Finally, we demonstrate that there is no silver bullet for TTA: no single adaptation paradigm is universally optimal, and the preferred paradigm depends on the nature of shift. We hope our benchmark and study provide a clearer understanding of the current TTA4CLIP landscape and establish a foundation for further research. The project page is available atthis https URL
  </details>
