  Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) offer complementary routes to post-training. For visual agents, however, tool use presents two related challenges. Models may overlook useful tool-returned observations, while poorly grounded actions can limit the evidence available for subsequent distillation. We introduce Reframe, a post-training framework that treats the observation state, rather than only the output distribution, as an object of alignment. First, Ground-Drop-GRPO (GD-GRPO) trains spatial and temporal specialists with evidence-aware rewards and stochastic removal of the original visual input, encouraging them to acquire grounded evidence and use the returned observations. Second, Observation-Gated-MOPD (OG-MOPD) replaces deficient tool actions with canonical ones mid-rollout and resumes distillation from the repaired observation. The two stages complement each other, as learning to rely on tool-returned observations can help the policy benefit from observation repair. Neither stage requires a task-specific SFT cold start. Extensive experiments over 14 image and video benchmarks demonstrate that Reframe outperforms both Mix-RL and vanilla MOPD. Ablation results show that GD-GRPO increases observation dependence and tool gain compared with outcome-based GRPO.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Modality Preference: Controlling Multimodal Arbitration under Modality Conflict in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12883)**  `arXiv:2611.12883`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaoying Pan, Shenyu Lu, Jun Hui Wong, Junyi Chai, Xiaoqian Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) integrate information across modalities, but become unreliable when those modalities disagree. Under conflicting inputs, MLLMs may favor modalities that are not the most reliable for the task, thus motivating inference-time control of modality arbitration. We identify a small set of attention heads, termed arbitration heads, that causally influence whether predictions follow visual or textual evidence. Building on this, we introduce a lightweight, training-free intervention that biases these heads toward a designated trusted modality at inference time. The identified heads are stable across calibration set sizes and generalize across settings, and steering them enables effective bidirectional control of modality reliance across diverse benchmarks. Our findings identify an effective control mechanism to align MLLM predictions with task-specified modality priorities. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Click3R: Interactive Stereo 3D Reconstruction with Sparse Correspondence Clicks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12875)**  `arXiv:2611.12875`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Gu, Jin Yao, Zezhou Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite impressive progress, existing feed-forward large 3D reconstruction models produce a single, irrevocable prediction. When these models fail, there is no mechanism to correct their mistakes. To address this limitation, we introduce Click3R, a framework for interactive geometric correction of feed-forward models using sparse human correspondence clicks. Given an image pair and a small set of cross-view point correspondences provided by a user, Click3R injects geometric constraints into the 3D reconstruction networks through a lightweight point correspondence adapter, enabling the model to resolve ambiguities in challenging scenarios such as repetitive textures, symmetric structures, large viewpoint changes, or textureless regions. To evaluate interactive reconstruction, we curate a challenging benchmark specifically designed to expose failure modes of existing feed-forward 3D reconstruction methods. Experiments show that Click3R reduces reconstruction error with as few as a single correspondence click, improving performance on both standard and our curated benchmarks. Our results highlight that sparse human interaction provides an effective and practical mechanism for correcting geometric reconstruction errors.
  </details>

- **[What Breaks Scribble-Supervised Segmentation? An Audit-Informed Benchmark for Scribble Annotation Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12674)**  `arXiv:2611.12674`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minheng Wu, Di Wen, Ruiping Liu, Junwei Zheng, Yufan Chen, Kailun Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scribble-supervised segmentation is typically evaluated with a fixed annotation set, leaving robustness to distinct semantic and geometric imperfections poorly understood. Motivated by an audit of 12,031 manual PASCAL Visual Object Classes (VOC) 2012 scribbles and prior work, we introduce six controlled perturbation operators. Class corruption, spatial offset, and directional perturbation directly operationalize audit signals. Stroke-length reduction is motivated by prior scribble studies, while random dropout and fragmentation serve as diagnostic controls. Each operator reports requested and achieved changes. We evaluate manual and automatic scribbles across two image domains, two principal backbone settings, and multiple training pipelines, with an additional MiT-B1 PFA anchor evaluation. In the five-seed Tree Energy Loss (TEL)-ResNet101 VOC study, mean intersection over union (mIoU) drops from 70.08 to 1.46 under class corruption, compared with 35.57 for spatial offset, 52.20 for directional perturbation, and 63.24 for stroke-length reduction at their strongest settings. At the evaluated endpoints, semantic-anchor corruption produces the largest degradation, while geometric and support responses depend on the pipeline and dataset. Our benchmark provides a reproducible protocol for diagnosing annotation robustness beyond clean-set accuracy. The operator code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EditJudge-Bench: Auditing VLM Image-Edit Judges with Synthetic Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12524)**  `arXiv:2611.12524`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jens Parslov, Marco Schouten, Dim P. Papadopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as automated judges for image-editing benchmarks, yet their reliability is poorly understood. Auditing these judges is difficult because real edited images lack controlled ground truth and entangle the requested changes with model artefacts, unintended side effects, and ambiguous degrees of success. In this paper, we introduce EditJudge-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,382 synthetic before/after image pairs rendered in Blender, with edits parameterised across nine categories derived from real user requests and exact ground truth from known 3D scene parameters. Because every edit is generated by a known intervention on a controlled scene, EditJudge-Bench evaluates VLM judges as binary verifiers of whether an instruction was correctly executed, isolating judge perception from editor quality. Across 25 judge configurations, we find substantial reliability gaps: (a) the widely adopted VIEScore template reaches only 69.0% AUROC with GPT-4o; (b) fine-tuned judges score 51.7-66.3%; spatial edits such as rotation and shape remain challenging; (c) prompt-formatting choices like image-text ordering shift performance by up to 14.1 points. Despite using only synthetic data, judge rankings on EditJudge-Bench correlate strongly with two real-image human-preference benchmarks ($\rho$=0.95 and $\rho$=0.71), indicating that controlled audits surface failure modes relevant to real evaluation.
  </details>

- **[LongSCOP: Semantically Consistent Long Video Outpainting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12445)**  `arXiv:2611.12445`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu Tang, Zhiyuan You, Zhixiao Wang, Fanghua Yu, Qingyu Zhang, Xiang Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video out-painting is an important problem, yet existing research focuses primarily on short clips, leaving long video out-painting largely underexplored. Extending video out-painting to long videos poses substantial challenges. A natural solution is iterative out-painting, i.e., generating videos chunk by chunk, but this introduces two major issues: generation instability or even failure caused by inter-chunk inconsistencies and error accumulation, and semantic drift over long temporal horizons. In this paper, we propose LongSCOP, short for Semantically Consistent long Video Out-Painting. First, we introduce a training recipe with three components for smooth and stable long-horizon generation: self-forcing training, latent calibration, and future references. Beyond generation stability, we recognize that the rich temporal context of long videos provides valuable references for maintaining semantic consistency. We therefore develop a VLM-based agent that automatically selects the most informative reference frames from the entire video to maintain consistent subjects, scenes, and backgrounds throughout long-horizon generation. We also construct a high-quality training dataset of 62K video clips. To facilitate research on this underexplored problem, we introduce LVO-Bench, a benchmark curated by human experts to assess long-horizon stability and semantic consistency. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms existing approaches in both generation stability and semantic coherence.
  </details>

- **[MoSTE: Mixture of Spatio-Temporal Experts for Deepfake Detection in Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12443)**  `arXiv:2611.12443`  `cs.CV`  
  _Charbel Yahchouchi, Nabyl Quignon, Noemi Roggero, Laurent Saroul, Antitza Dantcheva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalizable deepfake video detection remains challenging because forgery artifacts vary substantially across manipulation types and often combine diverse spatial and temporal inconsistencies. Existing parameter-efficient adaptation methods typically rely on a single shared representation, rendering them prone to manipulation-specific bias and limiting transfer to unseen forgeries. To address this challenge, we propose Mixture Of Spatio-Temporal Experts (MoSTE), an adaptation framework that enables conditional specialization of forensic representations. Instead of forcing one adapter to model all forgery patterns, MoSTE decomposes adaptation into parallel spatial and temporal expert pools and dynamically routes tokens to the most relevant experts through an unsupervised token-level routing mechanism. This allows experts to naturally specialize in complementary forgery cues without requiring explicit manipulation labels or multi-stage training, while effectively transferring representations from CLIP to the video domain. Extensive experiments across cross-dataset and cross-manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that MoSTE sets a new state of the art in average generalization while preserving strong performance across diverse manipulation families. Additional analysis of expert routing and attention behavior reveals the emergence of expert diversity, suggesting that MoSTE learns complementary spatio-temporal forensic features. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[TRIAD: Multimodal Retrieval across Abstraction, Constraints, and State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12364)**  `arXiv:2611.12364`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangyang Wu, Chen Ju, Xu Chen, Likang Peng, Jinsong Lan, Haihong Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal retrieval increasingly supports agentic systems, where user requests can involve cross-level relations, combine multiple constraints, and evolve across dialogue turns. In these settings, a highly similar result may still violate the user's requirements, while retrieved content may directly inform downstream actions without human screening. We introduce TRIAD, a multimodal retrieval benchmark for evaluating how well retrieval systems satisfy these complex, evolving requests. TRIAD comprises three tracks: abstraction, testing bidirectional retrieval between visual instances and concepts at different hierarchical levels; constraints, testing the joint satisfaction of positive and negative conditions; and state, testing the integration of requirements across dialogue turns and the updating of earlier conditions when users revise their requests. The benchmark contains six subsets, each with 1,000 queries and a fixed pool of 1,000 candidates per query, evaluated using a common multi-positive mean average precision (mAP) protocol. Simpler reference tasks and hard negatives grouped by the requirements they violate help characterize specific retrieval difficulties. We evaluate 20 configurations of 17 systems and find that no system leads across all six subsets. Performance declines with hierarchical distance, and negative-only queries generally underperform positive-only queries with the same number of constraints. Across paired tasks expressing the same final intent, multi-turn retrieval yields lower mAP in 39 of 40 configuration-domain comparisons. The benefits of explicit reasoning also vary across models and tasks. These findings highlight the gap between retrieving similar content and satisfying a complete request.
  </details>

- **[ReVMap: Vectorized Global Mapping via Connectivity-Aware Local Map Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12346)**  `arXiv:2611.12346`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongjae Shin, Jungho Kim, Donghyuk Kwak, Myeongjun Kim, Seunghoon Yu, Jiyong Oh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale annotation of vectorized high-definition (HD) maps is labor-intensive and costly, motivating local-to-global auto-labeling approaches that incrementally construct global maps from local vectorized predictions. However, existing approaches often overlook prediction uncertainty and rely on heuristic map integration, potentially propagating errors and introducing topological inconsistencies. To address these limitations, we propose ReVMap, a reliable local-to-global framework that iteratively refines local map predictions and global map construction through uncertainty-guided prior utilization and connectivity-guided global map revision. Specifically, ReVMap leverages point-wise localization uncertainty to incorporate global map priors into local map prediction, improving robustness against inaccurate prior information. It then predicts local-to-global connectivity and selectively updates the global map through uncertainty-aware fusion based on the reliability of local predictions. By iteratively coupling local prediction and global revision, ReVMap progressively improves both local map accuracy and global map consistency. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes and Argoverse 2 benchmarks demonstrate that ReVMap achieves state-of-the-art performance in both local map prediction and global map construction.
  </details>

- **[TMT: Adapting CLIP to Infrared Imagery via Text-Derived Modality Knowledge Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12345)**  `arXiv:2611.12345`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruofan Wang, Puzuo Wang, Xin Chen, Zibo Yu, Jungang Yang, Yi Hou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models such as CLIP achieve strong performance across computer vision tasks, yet their image-text alignment is less reliable for infrared (IR) imagery because large-scale pre-training is dominated by visible-spectrum data. Due to the high cost of the infrared-text labeling, we investigate adapting CLIP to IR through only paired infrared-visible images, which requires considering modality-specific knowledge while preserving scene content and CLIP's image-text alignment. To achieve this, we propose TMT (Text-Derived Modality Knowledge Transfer), which adapts CLIP's image encoder through a text-defined infrared-to-visible direction and supervision from paired IR-visible images. First, we found that modality discrepancy can be described by corresponding text embeddings, thus using the direction of text embedding differences to guide the knowledge transfer between visible and infrared modalities without erasing infrared-specific information. To enhance the representation of semantics and details of infrared images, we introduce Generative-Perceptual Co-Supervision that combines semantic alignment from CLIP visible image encoder with diffusion noise-prediction feedbacks. On four source-disjoint IR classification benchmarks, TMT achieves the highest mean zero-shot accuracy among the compared methods (48.92%). It also achieves the best IR-text retrieval results among the compared methods without infrared-text supervision during adaptation and the highest open-vocabulary segmentation mIoU on both CART and SemanticRT among the CLIP-like methods.
  </details>

- **[DisasterVL: Evidence-Based Visual Reasoning Dataset and Model for Disaster Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12144)**  `arXiv:2611.12144`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ali Diba, Aisha Al-Mohannadi, Yin Yang, Muhammad Imran, Ferda Ofli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce DisasterVL, a dataset and benchmark for evidence-grounded reasoning about disaster imagery. It contains approximately 1.2 million images from 142 countries, paired with 25 million structured annotations covering scene captions, incident and location assessments, spatial relationships, and visual question answering (VQA). The VQA annotations link answers to visible evidence and include questions about both present and absent cues, multiple answer formats, and difficulty labels. Region-level annotations link caption phrases to bounding boxes and spatial relationships to the regions they connect. The test set contains 38,254 images spanning 75 countries and 28 hazard types. Gemini 3.1 Pro audited the test annotations, and human reviewers validated a stratified subset. On this benchmark, the 9B DisasterVLM model outperforms the evaluated general-purpose baselines on captioning, open-ended VQA, incident reasoning, and region-level grounding. Gains in text overlap are larger than gains in evidence scores. We plan to publicly release the dataset, trained models, and code.
  </details>

- **[SAFE-Hair: Scalp-Anchored Fields for Exportable Single-View Hair Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12037)**  `arXiv:2611.12037`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yucheng Wang, Zedong Wang, Yuetong Wu, Yue Ma, Dan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-view 3D hair reconstruction has progressed rapidly, but many methods optimize for visual plausibility rather than producing strand assets that can be directly used in grooming, editing, or simulation pipelines. We propose SAFE-Hair, a framework that reconstructs explicit strand grooms from a single portrait by decoding hair from persistent follicles in a canonical scalp-UV domain. Given a fitted template head, SAFE-Hair projects self-supervised image features onto the scalp, uses a conditional Rectified Flow model to generate latent scalp fields, and decodes occupancy, segment directions, length allocation, and total length at a fixed set of scalp follicles. This representation enforces scalp-rooted strand identities by construction and enables direct export with follicle IDs and guide-family metadata. We further introduce attachment, collision, and bending priors to improve geometric validity without per-subject test-time optimization. To evaluate both reconstruction fidelity and asset usability, we introduce HairBench-3D, a benchmark built from public 3D hair assets with standardized alignment, rendering, resampling, and metrics for geometry, direction consistency, invisible-region completion, silhouette agreement, and collision. SAFE-Hair improves Chamfer distance from 3.74 mm to 3.28 mm, F-score from 77.9 to 81.7, invisible-region F-score from 66.1 to 69.8, and penetration ratio from 4.8% to 3.9% over the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[TIRAD: Text-guided Image Residual Learning for Generalist Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12023)**  `arXiv:2611.12023`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seongsu Lee, Seunghyun Oh, Seungjae Moon, Youngmin Ro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent anomaly detection studies have shown that residual features, which model the discrepancy between normal and abnormal patterns, provide strong generalization to anomalies unseen during training. However, existing residual-based approaches mainly rely on visual discrepancies derived from image representations, while semantic discrepancies that can be explicitly expressed through language remain largely underexplored. To address this problem, we propose TIRAD, Text-guided Image Residual Learning for Generalist Anomaly Detection, a multimodal residual learning framework that defines the discrepancy between textual representations of normal and abnormal states as a Text Residual Feature and uses it to guide the Image Residual Feature. Specifically, a Text Fusion Module converts patch-level text residuals into a gate that modulates the image residual of each patch. The gate retains the visual discrepancies that the text residual identifies as defect-related and attenuates the others, leading to more discriminative anomaly representations. Extensive experiments on industrial benchmarks and industrial-to-medical transfer demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing image-centric residual learning methods.
  </details>

- **[Where Does Proposal Selection Fail? Diagnosing Frozen Vision-Language Queries over Detector Proposals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11873)**  `arXiv:2611.11873`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bo Ma, Weiqi Yan, Jinsong Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In modular grounding a frozen detector proposes regions and a vision-language model (VLM) selects one, at lower cost than letting the VLM decode a box, but it is unclear where such systems fail. We hold perception fixed---every trained selector chooses among the same frozen proposals, which contain the referent for at least $99.8\%$ of RefCOCO/+/g queries---and vary the selection interface one factor at a time for two VLMs of one family (Qwen2.5-VL-7B, Qwen3-VL-8B). On RefCOCOg, what the selector reads matters more than how it is built: the VLM's image-conditioned query encodes the referent's location and beats a text-only query by $0.15$--$0.18$ with every head tested (by less for InternVL3-8B, whose vision encoder differs; one head tested). The remaining errors are rankings among a few top candidates; a cross-attention selector supervised with every valid box reaches the VLM's coordinate decoding on RefCOCOg, and with Qwen2.5-VL on RefCOCO/+ when also trained on their data. Beyond that distribution it does not: on Ref-L4's Objects365 images, mostly of categories outside training, every trained head falls to $0.19$--$0.28$ while Qwen decoding keeps about $0.8$ (InternVL3's drops too), neither a wider detector prompt nor a class-agnostic proposer closes the gap, and on public reasoning benchmarks Ref-Adv-s and RefBench-PRO full-budget heads trail decoding by $0.19$--$0.37$ although proposals cover most referents. A trained verifier reduces to an existence probe on the VLM's query state and does not beat the VLM's yes/no answer on public no-target benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Aggregation-Induced Calibration Failure in Sum-Based Label Expansion for Multi-Label Facial Expression Recognition: A Diagnostic Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11843)**  `arXiv:2611.11843`  `cs.CV`  
  _Weihang Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Label-space expansion - predicting a larger joint space and summing back to the original labels - is a natural design for multi-label facial expression recognition, but its failure modes have not been characterised. We evaluate Condition-Aware Label Expansion (CALE) on a self-built dataset (30,240 training images, 3,460 multi-label test images), on seven backbones, and on the public RAF-DB and RAF-ML benchmarks. The central finding is that the aggregation rule, not the representation, decides whether the method works. With a fixed threshold of 0.5, the aggregate score spans 28x across five backbones at 10% of the training data (0.24 to 6.79) and collapses to a 1.9x range at full data, while threshold-free mAP stays within 1.28x over the same backbones, which localises the fault to the summation step. The mechanism is the uncalibrated aggregation rule, which adds the main-branch score to half the sum of the three non-main branches; we verify it by measuring main- and non-main-branch activations. Two plain seven-dimensional sigmoid baselines (BCE, ASL) reach mAP 0.5055 and 0.5175; ASL exceeds three of the five CALE configurations and falls within 0.03 of the best. On single-label FER the gain over a seven-dimensional baseline is nominally significant (paired t-test, p = 0.0436) but does not survive Benjamini-Hochberg correction (p_BH = 0.2055) or a Wilcoxon test (p = 0.1250). We conclude that sum-based label expansion requires threshold calibration at the deployment data scale, or a learned aggregation, before it can support multi-label decisions.
  </details>

- **[MDA framework: multi-architecture domain adaptation for unsupervised person re-identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11836)**  `arXiv:2611.11836`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wei Zhang, Peijun Ye, Dihu Chen, Tao Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unsupervised person re-identification not only faces the challenge of domain gaps, but existing methods also rarely support multiple network architectures. To tackle both issues, this paper proposes a multi-architecture auto-adaptive domain adaptation method: Mamba augmented multi-architecture domain adaptation framework. Specifically, this paper constructs the multi-architecture domain adaptation capability at two levels. At the first level, we propose a composite operator(MDA operator) that encapsulates Mamba as a component for enhancing domain adaptation. At the second level, based on this operator, we propose a Mamba-enhanced multi-architecture Domain Adaptation framework(MDA framework). Comprehensive experiments on person re-identification benchmarks validate the consistency and flexibility of the proposed framework. The MDA framework supports versatile deployment strategies without being confined to a single integration point. Under a single deployment configuration, it surpasses several SOTA methods. Moreover, it demonstrates substantial performance improvements across both CNN and Transformer architectures. When embedded into a CNN backbone, the framework achieves a 10.2% mAP improvement on the adaptation task from CUHK03 to Market-1501. Integrated with a Transformer backbone, it yields a 19.8% mAP improvement on the same task.
  </details>

- **[When to Trust Adaptation: Contextual Fusion for Transferable Vision-Language Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11824)**  `arXiv:2611.11824`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenyuan Chen, Wenshuo Tao, Zhuo Huang, Shuo Chen, Mingming Cheng, Xiang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a vision-language model encounters a new class or domain, how much should it trust its learned prompts? Because source adaptation can correct pretrained errors on some target images while disturbing valid predictions on others, we introduce Contextual Adaptive Fusion (CAF), which retains four prediction branches by pairing frozen and prompted visual and textual representations. To arbitrate when branches disagree on an image, CAF combines each branch's predictive confidence with how frequently the branches favor its proposed class across the unlabeled target collection, placing greater trust in predictions that are locally confident yet less dominated by collection-wide frequency bias. The fusion coefficients are learned once on source data and remain fixed at inference. Trained on ImageNet, CAF achieves 69.96% average accuracy across ten cross-dataset benchmarks (1.92 percentage points above ManiPT) and remains highly competitive across four ImageNet variants, setting a new best of 52.21% on ImageNet-S. In base-to-novel transfer across eleven datasets, CAF further reaches 79.77% novel-class accuracy and an 82.81% harmonic mean. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking CNNs, Vision Transformers, and Foundation Models for Explainable Brain Tumor MRI Classification: Calibration, Decision Analysis, and External Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11793)**  `arXiv:2611.11793`  `cs.CV`  
  _Akwasi Asare_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for brain tumor MRI classification are usually compared by accuracy alone, on public datasets whose independence is rarely checked. We benchmarked six architectures: two convolutional networks (EfficientNetV2-M and ConvNeXt-V2-Tiny), two vision transformers (Swin-V2-S and ViT-L/16), and two foundation models (DINOv2-L and MedSigLIP-448), for four-class classification of 7,216 public MRI images. We assessed accuracy, calibration, explainability, ensembles, and decision curves, with every fitted quantity estimated on validation data only. Pixel-verified duplicate screening showed that 36% of the test images had a near-duplicate in the training data. Test accuracy ranged from 94.5% to 95.6%, with no pairwise difference after Holm correction, and fell to 90.3% to 91.7% on the 773 test images free of duplicates and shared patients. Ensembles did not outperform the best single model. Attribution maps passed a model-randomization test but localized expert-annotated tumors poorly. On 1,675 external BDNeuro-MRI images that did not duplicate the training data, accuracy ranged from 77.6% (MedSigLIP-448) to 95.0% (ViT-L/16), and 12 of 15 pairwise differences were significant. Internal accuracy on this public collection therefore did not distinguish the architectures, whereas the duplicate-filtered external test did.
  </details>

- **[SpinSCI: Mask-Conditioned Spatial-Temporal Spin Transformer for Video Snapshot Compressive Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11753)**  `arXiv:2611.11753`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seungki Hong, Zhishuai Zhang, Taekwang Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video snapshot compressive imaging (SCI) recovers multiple frames from one spatiotemporally coded measurement. This paper proposes SpinSCI, a mask-conditioned Spatial-Temporal Spin Transformer whose Mask-Conditioned Ising Refinement Block (MC-IRB) combines attention logits and sensing information to form signed spin couplings. Finite mean-field updates gate residual feature refinement. Physics-guided initialization and final data consistency incorporate the measurement model. Compared with HiSViT$_9$, SpinSCI achieves average PSNR gains of 0.51 dB, 0.23 dB, and 0.04 dB on small-scale gray, mid-scale color, and large-scale color benchmarks, respectively, with 16.9% fewer parameters, 21.0-21.6% fewer FLOPs, 17.1-49.9% lower latency, and 62.3-63.3% lower peak memory. Relative to DU-9stg, average PSNR differs by at most 0.13 dB across these benchmarks, with 28.2-28.7% fewer FLOPs, 64.6-71.7% lower inference latency, and 30.1-35.0% lower peak memory. Qualitative comparisons on the real benchmark further show recovery of fine details and textures. Code and reference checkpoints are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Geometric Depth in Monocular 3D Object Detection: A Projection-Consistent Reformulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11607)**  `arXiv:2611.11607`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhihao Zhang, Abhinav Kumar, Huaizhi Qu, Tianlong Chen, Xiaoming Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular 3D detection (Mono3D) hinges on recovering depth from a single image, for which the geometric formulation $z \approx f \cdot H / h_{2D}$ is widely adopted due to its simplicity and direct grounding in projective geometry. We show that this formulation harbors a systematic, projection-induced bias: substituting the 2D bounding-box height $h_{2D}$ recovers the depth of an object's visible front surface rather than its center, causing size-dependent underestimation that persists even under ground-truth inputs. We address this front-surface bias with MonoPCA, a unified framework built on a projection-consistent reformulation. At its core, PCDepth retains the measurable $h_{2D}$ and closes the gap to center depth through an orientation- and size-aware correction derived from perspective geometry, eliminating the bias without introducing ill-posed regression targets. Because PCDepth couples depth to predicted object size and orientation, attribute perception becomes essential. We therefore introduce GeoAlign, a teacher-student framework that transfers geometric representations from Vision Foundation Models into a lightweight student, strengthening attribute perception at no inference cost. Across four challenging benchmarks, MonoPCA achieves SoTA performance, including a +1.44% AP$_{3D}$ gain on the KITTI leaderboard and +2.80% on Omni3D$_{OUT}$. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Martine: Benchmarking Multi-View 3D Surface Reconstruction Across Viewpoint Coverage, Resolution, and Lighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11602)**  `arXiv:2611.11602`  `cs.CV`  
  _Robin Bruneau, Baptiste Brument, Frederic Giraud, Bastian Sigrist, Sascha Jecklin, Philipp Fürnstahl, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Controlled acquisition setups are widely used for high-accuracy multi-view 3D surface reconstruction, as they allow sensor configuration, image resolution, viewpoint distribution, and illumination to be specified. Yet benchmarking remains difficult, since reconstruction accuracy depends jointly on the reconstruction paradigm, object properties, and acquisition regime. Existing benchmarks often restrict viewpoint coverage, resolution, or illumination, which can bias comparisons towards methods suited to the chosen setting. Others lack accurate 3D ground truth or quantified image-to-geometry alignment, limiting their use for metric surface evaluation. We introduce Martine, a benchmark for controlled 3D object surface reconstruction. Martine comprises 28 objects, including transparent materials and fine structures, captured at 9568 x 6376 pixels from 84 views over a spherical cap under both single-light and multi-light regimes. It provides object-independent camera calibration, accurate 3D ground-truth scans, calibration and image-to-geometry accuracy quantified on test images, and 3D property masks for analysing errors by material properties, visibility, curvature and fine structures. Experiments across photogrammetry, classical multi-view stereo, neural implicit surfaces, Gaussian-splatting-based reconstruction, and multi-view photometric stereo show that Martine enables regime-aware evaluation with explicit control over reference-data accuracy. The benchmark resolves sub-millimetric reconstruction differences, with a best observed Chamfer distance of 0.058 mm over the evaluated visible surface.
  </details>

- **[NightSteps: A Night-Time Dataset and Benchmark for Pedestrian Crossing Intention Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11583)**  `arXiv:2611.11583`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sjoerd Takken, Shaodi You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous vehicles use pedestrian crossing intention prediction (PCIP) to participate safely in traffic. Existing models are largely trained under favourable daytime conditions with sufficient daylight. Little is known about the robustness of these models under night-time and low-light conditions, due to the lack of datasets containing night-time footage. This paper investigates how a dataset, named NightSteps, can be constructed and evaluated as a baseline benchmark. The dataset is based on nighttime dashcam footage collected from six different cities around the world, capturing a wide variety of road conditions. The dataset consists of dashcam-recorded point-of-view footage, raw data of pedestrians' locations with corresponding 'track ids' and ground-truth labels containing accurate information about pedestrian crossing behaviour, resulting in a diverse dataset. Gemini, ChatGPT, and human assessment are used to demonstrate the usability of the dataset by predicting crossings in clips from the dataset and comparing the results with the initial ground-truth labels. These results show that human input achieves an accuracy of 88.17% and an F1-score of 80.21%. Predictions from Gemini and ChatGPT perform lower, with accuracies of 60% and 79.13% respectively, and F1-scores of 42.50% and 63.64%. The evaluation demonstrates that the NightSteps dataset can be used for future model evaluation to test the performance of PCIP, with the aim of improving road safety at night.
  </details>

- **[Reaching Is Not Finding: Map-Anchored Visual Prompting for the Last Mile of Aerial Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11510)**  `arXiv:2611.11510`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ningbo Li, Kebin Liu, Jingcan Chen, Weichen Zhang, Rong Ding, Yunhao Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) asks a drone to find an object described in natural language. The description usually locates the object relative to named landmarks, such as streets and buildings. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. It roughly doubles the success rate of the two strongest trained navigators, raising HETT from 25.9% to 54.6% and FlightGPT from 24.2% to 48.9%, whereas CityNav's official detector-based last step adds less than two points to HETT.
  </details>

- **[Let the Gallery Learn Itself: Training-Free Iterative Side-View Face Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11391)**  `arXiv:2611.11391`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zijun Huang, Suncheng Xiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What if a face recognition system could learn from every encounter without a single gradient update? We present Gallery Self-Evolution (GSE), a training-free framework where the gallery itself adapts at test time. The core mechanism---dedup-FIFO curation---archives novel appearance samples after successful recognition while permanently locking the enrollment template, bounded at 30 embeddings per identity. On CFP-FP and IJB-C benchmarks, GSE closes the side-view gap by +23 points at 45° yaw and boosts TAR@FAR=0.001 by +9.4 points, all at CPU-level cost. No retraining. No GPU. Just a gallery that grows smarter every time it meets you.
  </details>

- **[Not All Layers Are Equal in Image-to-Video Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11147)**  `arXiv:2611.11147`  `cs.CV`  
  _Thinesh Thiyakesan Ponbagavathi, Constantin Seibold, Alina Roitberg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient image-to-video transfer adapts frozen image foundation models to video via lightweight trainable adapters. Existing methods insert the same adapter at every transformer layer, implicitly assuming all layers require equal temporal modeling. A layer-wise sensitivity analysis across four video-native model families and two pretraining paradigms reveals that this assumption is wrong: temporal sensitivity increases with depth, and the pretraining paradigm determines its growth profile. Vision-only models exhibit a gradual rise, while vision-language models show early suppression followed by late recovery.This finding motivates STRAP, a training-free adapter placement rule that derives a spatial-to-temporal transition boundary from a paradigm-matched video model. Spatial adapters are assigned before this boundary and temporal adapters after it, without modifying the adapter architecture itself. On four fine-grained action recognition benchmarks, STRAP improves accuracy by up to 11.8% over uniform placement, reduces trainable parameters by 29 to 48%, and lowers inference time by up to 10%. On standard benchmarks, it matches accuracy with fewer parameters. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SurgWorld: Learning Surgical Representations Beyond Observation via Latent Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11140)**  `arXiv:2611.11140`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingkun Yue, Pengxiang Wang, Siqi Zhang, Zinan Jia, Yiyao Zhang, Huihuan Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical procedures are inherently partially observable, requiring surgeons to continuously infer evolving anatomical dynamics and future surgical states beyond the current observation. However, existing self-supervised learning methods primarily learn from observed surgical videos through masked reconstruction or predicting representations within the camera's current field of view, lacking the capability to explicitly model the out-of-sight surgical dynamics. Inspired by latent predictive world models, we introduce SurgWorld, a video-native, self-supervised framework for surgical representation learning, which treats a specified spatial or temporal shift as a pseudo-action and learns to predict the target latent representation from a partial observation. Specifically, we propose Out-of-Sight Dynamics Modeling (OSDM) as the pretraining objective for predicting representations conditioned on relative spatio-temporal shifts. We further regularize the learned latent dynamics from OSDM with a Geometric Visual Similarity (GVS) objective by aligning patch-level relational structures between predicted and target representations, preserving spatial geometry and local visual coherence for dense perception. These two objectives are integrated through an agglomerative training framework that consolidates complementary supervisory signals from multiple teachers to learn a unified latent representation of surgical dynamics and visual geometry. Extensive transfer learning experiments on 17 surgical benchmarks show that SurgWorld consistently outperforms existing vision foundation models across surgical workflow recognition, fine-grained action understanding, and dense visual perception. We further conduct tailored qualitative and quantitative analyses, demonstrating that SurgWorld effectively captures surgical dynamics beyond the directly observed context through latent prediction.
  </details>

- **[From Game Generation to Game Playing: Fully Automated Ability Training for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11136)**  `arXiv:2611.11136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Changran Liu, Qi Wu, Wenchao Ma, Sharon X. Huang, Haomiao Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training vision-language-action (VLA) models to follow gameplay instructions across diverse games is limited by the cost of collecting demonstrations. We introduce a framework that automatically generates game environments and expert demonstrations. An LLM-powered agent uses game-building tools to construct executable environments and tasks targeting specified skills. Programmatic experts then use privileged simulator state to generate aligned instructions, rendered observations, and keyboard and mouse actions. We train our game policy, Script2Play, by post-training a 4B vision-language model with behavior cloning on 150.3 hours of synthetic Minecraft and Roblox gameplay, without human gameplay demonstrations or reinforcement learning. Evaluation on multiple public game benchmarks demonstrates that Script2Play follows gameplay instructions effectively and generalizes across game engines, highlighting the effectiveness of targeted synthetic supervision.
  </details>

- **[What You Show Is Not What You Get: Rethinking Demonstration Following in Visual In-Context Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11122)**  `arXiv:2611.11122`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiao Hu, Ilia Sucholutsky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual in-context segmentation uses visual demonstrations to guide predictions for a new image. In SegGPT, we identify background color mapping deviation, where non-target regions sharing a background label receive different output colors, and part-to-parent expansion, where predictions extend beyond the requested part into its parent object. These deviations can occur even when the relevant regions remain distinguishable in internal features. Controlled changes to demonstration colors and requested targets support an account in which demonstrations guide role and color assignments over familiar semantic regions, influenced by learned semantic and color preferences. Building on these findings, we introduce Semantic Contrast Prompt Selection (SCPS). Its semantic contrast encoding assigns separate colors to the requested part and the rest of its object while keeping the remaining scene black. SCPS combines existing retrieval scores with quality estimates from labeled development images to select demonstrations and their binary or semantic contrast encodings. Averaged across seven retrieval methods on three benchmarks, SCPS improves target IoU by 0.054 and reduces leakage into the rest of the object by 22.7% relative to the original binary selectors. Our findings offer a new perspective on understanding and improving demonstration following in visual in-context segmentation.
  </details>

- **[MySign: A High-Fidelity Motion-Capture Dataset for 3D Sign Generation in Bahasa Isyarat Malaysia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11115)**  `arXiv:2611.11115`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiayu Shen, Kalin Stefanov, Lay-Ki Soon, Vee Yee Chong, Alpha Agape Gopalai, KokSheik Wong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in 3D sign generation is constrained by the scarcity of high-fidelity 3D sign language datasets, a limitation that is particularly severe for under-resourced languages. We introduce MySign, the first motion-capture dataset for isolated signs in Bahasa Isyarat Malaysia (BIM). Its 5,000 samples cover 1,000 lexical items (glosses) and were produced by five Deaf native signers. Prior datasets that rely on SMPL-X parameters regressed from video provide pseudo-labels, which are often affected by depth ambiguity, inaccurate finger poses, and temporal jitter. MySign instead provides ground-truth SMPL-X sequences retargeted from recordings made with a motion-capture setup tailored for sign language; these sequences offer higher-fidelity motion, particularly in fine-grained hand and finger articulation. A controlled comparison on the same glosses and signers indicates that training on these sequences improves generation over training on such pseudo-labels. We establish MySign as a challenging benchmark for gloss-conditioned 3D sign generation: state-of-the-art models produce visually plausible outputs but fall short of the ground truth, most notably in fine-grained articulation, revealing persistent gaps in modeling articulated sign motion. In contrast, recognition baselines consistently achieve high lexical accuracy, indicating that the dataset reliably supports supervised learning. The dataset is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[NanoNet: Ultra-Lightweight Network for MRI Brain Tumor Classification toward Edge AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11071)**  `arXiv:2611.11071`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tran Nguyen-Le, Huu-Thuan Huynh, Minh-Triet Tran, Trung-Nghia Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated brain tumor classification from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) can support accessible medical image analysis, but conventional deep learning models often exceed the resource budgets of highly constrained edge-AI platforms. We present NanoNet, an ultra-lightweight convolutional neural network designed for resourceconstrained MRI classification and motivated by future edge-AI applications. Inspired by MobileNetV2, NanoNet substantially reduces network depth, channel capacity, expansion ratios, and final feature dimensionality, resulting in only five inverted residual blocks and 8,132 parameters. We further investigate dependency-aware structured pruning, knowledge distillation, and INT8 quantization-aware training for additional compression. Experiments on the BRISC 2025 benchmark show that NanoNet achieves 92.80% accuracy and 92.97% macro F1 with 8,132 parameters and 10.498 MFLOPs. Across three compression seeds, it exceeds the mean accuracy of 70%-pruned MobileNetV2 by 5.20 percentage points while using 60.2% fewer parameters and 9.8% less computation. Converted INT8 NanoNet achieves 89.70% accuracy and 90.22% macro F1 across three QAT seeds. Earlier TensorFlow Lite measurements indicate 27.7 KB of baseline model storage, motivating future edge-AI deployment.
  </details>

- **[What Does Multiple Choice Actually Measure? Disentangling Video Understanding from Option Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11062)**  `arXiv:2611.11062`  `cs.CV`  
  _Elliott Piercy, Justin Chan, Sepand Dyanatkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video understanding evaluations are intended to measure a model's proficiency in video perception and task solving. However, current VideoQA benchmarks struggle to isolate these capabilities due to their reliance on multiple-choice (MC) answer guidance, language priors, and fragile scoring pipelines. Disentangling these confounding factors reveals how much of a model's score is supplied by the evaluation context rather than earned through faithful perception. Across six VideoQA benchmarks of varying length, we find that accuracy is significantly above chance without any visual input. This is caused by shortcutting and option-prior reliance. We express these effects in the language of faithfulness: option-exploitation and answer-conditioned reasoning traces are unfaithful even when they are correct. Together, our results show that a VideoQA score is jointly determined by language priors, option timing, and how outputs and labels are converted into correctness. We produce a novel Option-Delayed Replay (ODR) mechanism that discloses options at controlled stages of an open-ended (OE) trajectory, to obtain objective MC scores from faithful option-free inference. Our work demonstrates that agentic methods with ODR and faithful inference can match option-exploiting performance. Finally, we formalize our diagnostic collection and ODR as a reproducible evaluation protocol, and we release a human-relabelled edition of LVBench (correcting $20.5\%$ of keys) to remove annotation noise as a confounding variable.
  </details>

- **[Remembering to Reason: Structured Memory for Scene and Workflow Understanding in Robot-Assisted Surgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10917)**  `arXiv:2611.10917`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiran Zhang, Hu Wang, Yichen Wu, Yutong Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical video understanding is an important foundation for intelligent assistance in robot-assisted surgery. Similar surgical views can reflect different actions or procedural stages. Interpreting these views requires access to earlier observations, yet frame-centric prompting leaves this history unavailable. We introduce MemSurg, a framework that remembers surgical observations before reasoning over them. Its structured memory graph stores timestamped entity categories, locations, motion cues, and interaction summaries. Chronological links preserve recent context, while entity-associated links connect recurring observations across the procedure. Task-adaptive retrieval then supplies evidence-grounded prompts for scene and workflow reasoning, supporting both frozen inference and memory-conditioned supervised fine-tuning. Evaluation on robot-assisted prostatectomy and two complementary surgical benchmarks shows a 10.43-percentage-point gain over SurgRAW with GPT-4o. Memory conditioning adds 6.52 points under matched Qwen3-VL-8B fine-tuning, and an inference-time plug-in adds 7.04 points to EndoChat. These results support structured memory as a reusable source of procedural evidence for surgical video understanding. Our code will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Real or Fake: A Dual-Channel Authenticity and Reasoning Protocol for Photographic Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10908)**  `arXiv:2611.10908`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoxiao Li, Ruinan Jin, Lili Meng, Miaosen Wang, Athula Balachandran, Pei Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forensic verification has converged on a binary "real vs. fake" label that confates fully synthetic, tampered, and AI-retouched images despite their very different consequences. We argue that GenAI manipulations decompose along two complementary channels: a camera channel of sensor fingerprints (destroyed by neural processing), and a semantic channel of scene-level coherence (disrupted by content edits). Each manipulation type leaves a distinctive two-channel signature. We instantiate this taxonomy in 2CAP (2-Channel Authenticity Protocol), pairing a contrastively-trained camera encoder with a frozen semantic encoder to serve two applications on a shared backbone: (i) four-class authenticity classification via cross-attention fusion with learnable reliability weights; and (ii) evidence generation, where an authentic image serves as a reference for a query and the two encoders produce per-channel alignment scores together with a patch-level saliency map. These signals are supplied as privileged context to a frozen Vision-Language Model (VLM) through an Observe-Generate-Refine loop, yielding explanations with localization without forensic fine-tuning. On a multi-source benchmark, 2CAP attains the best classification performance, including strong retouching detection where existing methods fail, and improves explanation quality.
  </details>

- **[STAR-VF: Spatio-Temporal Aware Representation Condensation for Data-Efficient Video Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10811)**  `arXiv:2611.10811`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Xian, Yunqiu Xu, Guosheng Lin, Yang He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video fusion aims to integrate complementary cues from heterogeneous sources, producing visually informative videos with coherent spatio-temporal structures. Jointly learning cross-modal complementarity and temporal dependencies typically relies on large-scale, high-quality video datasets, whose storage and repeated use during optimization incur substantial costs, making data-efficient learning highly desirable. To address this challenge, we rethink video fusion from the perspective of dataset condensation and propose STAR-VF, the first framework that condenses spatio-temporal representations for data-efficient video fusion. STAR-VF condenses the original training set into a compact set of learnable spatio-temporal representations, which are decoded into synthetic video clips and optimized using feature-level supervision provided by a frozen proxy model. The resulting condensed data preserves fusion-relevant spatial and temporal cues without requiring ground-truth fused videos as supervision. To adapt fusion learning to condensed representations, we equip the final fusion model with flow-free inter-frame temporal modeling and query-driven intra-frame aggregation, enabling direct temporal interaction and adaptive selection of fusion-relevant information in representation space. Extensive experiments on infrared-visible, multi-exposure, multi-focus, and medical video fusion benchmarks demonstrate that STAR-VF, trained with only approximately 1/16 of the original temporal training set, achieves fusion quality comparable to methods trained on the full data. Further analyses validate the contributions of learned condensation and flow-free temporal modeling, and demonstrate the robustness of the condensed supervision across different proxy encoders and downstream fusion models. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MGE: A Membership Guided Data Extraction Attack against Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10678)**  `arXiv:2611.10678`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchang Mei, Longqiang Wang, Haoyang Jiang, Mingyang Yi, Shaolei Zhang, Keman Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, diffusion models have achieved remarkable performance in image generation. However, their inherent memory properties introduce a potential safety risk of training data leakage, i.e., data extraction attack. To illustrate such risk, we propose a membership guided extraction (MGE) framework to effectively extract training data from a pre-trained diffusion model. Our core idea is to formulate the data extraction as a conditional generation, where the condition signal is from a membership inference classifier. To improve the success rate and efficiency of MGE, we propose adversarial gradient and parallel branch decoding. Our experiments on image generation benchmarks demonstrate that our MGE can extract hundreds of images, which shows that more attention should be paid to privacy-preserving of diffusion model.
  </details>

- **[SHIN: Scalable Hierarchy for INcremental 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10652)**  `arXiv:2611.10652`  `cs.CV`  
  _Reagan Koo, Yeong-Gyu Kim, Eun-Seok Ryu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) provides high-quality real-time novel-view synthesis, but deployment budgets vary across devices and network conditions. Most compact methods target one operating point, so another budget requires a separate representation. SHIN is a scalable 3DGS hierarchy with a reusable Base and additive enhancement layers, so each higher-quality operating point extends rather than replaces the decoded representation. Constructing this hierarchy requires deciding which Gaussians each stage adds and how much spherical-harmonic (SH) appearance each retains, because these choices remain fixed in later prefixes. SHIN derives one shared Gaussian order from multi-scale position-scale sensitivity and divides each stage budget between adding Gaussians and retaining SH bands using calibrated gradient-based scores per byte. Lightweight opacity updates adjust compositing while transmitted geometry and SH coefficients remain unchanged. Across three benchmarks, SHIN progressively improves one cumulative representation and achieves the best PSNR-size efficiency in every overlapping comparison with the multi-rate and progressive baselines.
  </details>

- **[ROLECUE: Diagnosing Cross-Lingual Role-Binding Failures in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10575)**  `arXiv:2611.10575`  `cs.CV`  
  _Niaz Mohaiman Abtahi, Evan Maksud_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can recognize events in images and parse sentences, but it remains unclear when they bind visual Agent-Patient structure to linguistic semantic roles across languages whose role cues depend on morphology, word order or construction. We introduce ROLECUE, a controlled diagnostic that pairs 192 validated two-person action images with German, Turkish, Japanese, English, Korean and Bangla sentences in canonical and patient-first orders. The evaluation separates image-only role recognition, text-only role parsing, direct image-sentence matching, response-compliance behavior and an external modular composition of the model's own component predictions. Across eight recent open-weight VLMs, ROLECUE reveals a structured spectrum: some models fail visual or linguistic component tests, some largely solve the diagnostic, some collapse to constant semantic assent and several show component-joint gaps in which reliable image-only and text-only role predictions do not survive direct joint inference. The largest non-degenerate gaps reach 44.9 percentage points, with final bootstrap intervals confirming that the effect is not a point-estimate artifact. These results reveal multilingual component-joint performance gaps and acceptance biases hidden by aggregate VLM benchmarks and show why component diagnostics are necessary before interpreting joint multimodal errors.
  </details>

- **[CompositionEvidence: Controlling Geometric Evidence for Cinematic Composition Reasoning with Frozen Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10559)**  `arXiv:2611.10559`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yichen He, Xin Huang, Yuxi Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cinematic composition questions depend on in-frame geometry that vision-language models (VLMs) use inconsistently. External perception tools can expose this geometry, but naive prompt injection can overwrite useful visual predictions. On ShotBench composition, pre-question geometry reduces Qwen2.5-VL-7B from 49.5% to 46.3%; corrupted measurements lower it to 33.6%. CompositionEvidence derives nine reproducible features from frozen detection, depth, and line experts and supplies their shared 24-dimensional representation through two interfaces. A training-free channel appends gated neutral evidence after the question, reaching 56.2% (+6.7, exact McNemar p=0.0018) and 48.4% under corrupted evidence. A minimal-training channel maps the same representation to four soft tokens with a 7.37M-parameter projector while leaving the VLM frozen; it reaches 61.0% (+11.5). Across nine VLMs, including five commercial models queried through official provider APIs, zero-shot accuracy ranges from 43.8% to 63.0%, and the text channel changes it by +6.7 to -6.9 points; the highest baseline is among the regressions. The effect of explicit geometry depends on measurement reliability and on how the receiving model integrates it.
  </details>

- **[PrivMark: Pixel-Space Steering against Privacy Leakage in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10528)**  `arXiv:2611.10528`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruofan Xing, Arman Akbari, Weikai Lin, Yuhao Zhu, Xuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) routinely disclose soft-biometric attributes such as age, gender, and race in their text outputs, exposing demographic information from uploaded images to any downstream API user. Existing privacy defenses do not fit this setting: face de-identification leaves distributed soft-biometric cues intact, classifier-targeted adversarial perturbations do not transfer to open-ended generation, and stronger VLM-specific methods require retraining or runtime activation access unavailable to downstream APIs. We propose PrivMark, a framework that suppresses biometric leakage at the image level. By analyzing VLM activations, we identify two weakly aligned steering vector directions in hidden-state space, each tied to a distinct leakage behavior --- one corresponding to incidental attribute disclosure under open-ended descriptions, the other to compliance with direct biometric probes instead of refusal --- and encode both directions into a single bounded pixel perturbation, so the protected image alone enforces privacy with no invasive access to model internals at deployment. To support evaluation, we assemble BioleakBench, $1{,}600$ VQA pairs covering biometric privacy attributes under both free-form description and direct-probe protocols. Across three white-box source models, PrivMark achieves up to $85.9\%$ biometric suppression while retaining $75\%$ of clean OK-VQA utility, up to $1.4\times$ the privacy-utility tradeoff of the strongest pixel-only baseline. The same one-shot perturbations transfer consistently to seven unseen VLMs including closed-source APIs.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Optimization-Oriented Hypernetworks for Implicit Neural Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10509)**  `arXiv:2611.10509`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lulu Cai, Hanxiang Ren, Siyan Dong, Li Sun, Jiefeng Wu, Youyi Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a hypernetwork that generates the parameters of an implicit neural representation (INR) for a given signal in a single forward pass, without test-time optimization. Existing hypernetworks for this problem either ignore the chain-rule structure of the test-time optimization that produces an INR or, when they preserve it, instantiate the structure with MLPs whose parameter count grows quartically in target-network width. Our central contribution is to show that this parameter-count cost is structural to the MLP instantiation, not to the optimization-oriented principle of preserving chain-rule structure, and to resolve it by replacing the MLP body with attention. The resulting hypernetwork combines three design choices: an output-neuron-centric tokenization that aligns the token axis with each layer's output-neuron axis; an information-flow consistency rule that fixes the role of every cross-attention module from the representation its output should occupy; and a token-space iterative rollout that realizes the five quantities one optimization step on the target INR computes as five cross-attention modules with parameter count independent of target-network width. Across five 2D and 3D INR generation benchmarks, our method outperforms the state-of-the-art single-pass and optimization-oriented baselines; scales to target-network widths at which the MLP-based optimization-oriented paradigm exhausts GPU memory; and produces the highest result on NeRF generation without ground-truth INR weights supervision.
  </details>

- **[SRA: Spatial Reasoning Adapter via Evolving Social Interaction Graphs for Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10506)**  `arXiv:2611.10506`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jaewoo Jeong, Seonkyu Song, Hyeonwoo Park, Jegyeong Cho, Yujin Bae, Giwon Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting where multiple agents will move next is fundamental to autonomous driving, robotics, and any system that must share space with people safely. Modern stochastic predictors read off inter-agent interactions once from the observed past and keep that view fixed throughout the generative rollout, so the reciprocal geometric constraints that emerge as the joint future sharpens are not re-examined. We address this gap with SRA (Spatial Reasoning Adapter), a modular graph denoiser that is integrated into existing diffusion and flow-matching predictors without redesigning their generative cores. At every reverse step, SRA reads the host's current future estimate, builds a sparse Euclidean interaction graph on the predicted trajectories, and returns a residual correction to the host's decoding path. Our adapter consists of three core components: a sparse neighborhood retrieved with a learned query-key score and a geometric bias, a future-conditioned relational feature recomputed on the evolving prediction, and uncertainty-weighted message passing where confident agents act as anchors for uncertain ones. We instantiate SRA on three structurally different hosts (LED, MID, and MoFlow) across four multi-agent benchmarks: SDD, NBA, Soccer, and Football. SRA improves ADE for every host on every dataset, with average gains of 5.8% in ADE and 7.0% in FDE. A cumulative ablation shows that the three components act as one mechanism: only together do they improve every host on every sports dataset, indicating that spatial reasoning over the model's own evolving future, weighted by how far that future can be trusted, is broadly useful across denoising architectures. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Channel Semantics in Vision Transformers via Token Decorrelation and Composition-Aware Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10497)**  `arXiv:2611.10497`  `cs.CV`  
  _Daeun Kim, Hyesong Choi, Hyejin Park, Dongbo Min_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-channel images encode heterogeneous channel semantics aligned over a shared spatial structure, which requires models to jointly capture globally shared structure and channel-specific variations. Recent multi-channel Vision Transformers attempt to address this challenge by augmenting the [CLS] token with memory tokens to organize multi-channel representations under varying channel compositions. However, we find that these context tokens collapse onto a few dominant channels under high inter-channel redundancy, producing redundant representations and a biased global summary that fails to reliably support composition-aware channel interpretation. We propose MuCa-ViT (Multi-Channel Composition-Aware Vision Transformer), a unified framework that addresses these limitations through two coupled mechanisms. Factorial Token Learning (FTL) enforces token-level decorrelation among context tokens, encouraging the [CLS] token to capture globally shared semantics while memory tokens preserve complementary channel-specific cues. Channel Composition-Aware Modulation (CAM) uses the FTL-refined [CLS] as a semantic anchor to generate sample-wise modulation signals, which enable composition-aware interpretation of channels within a unified backbone rather than relying on static channel embeddings. Experiments on JUMP-CP, CHAMMI, and So2Sat show that MuCa-ViT outperforms prior multi-channel ViTs across all three benchmarks, and the magnitude of improvement aligns with the inter-channel redundancy of each dataset ($\rho \in [0.19, 0.77]$), validating the diagnostic analysis that motivates our design. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[They Can See It but Not Say It: Iterative Self Knowledge Re-expression in Visual Reasoning Relative Pose Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10483)**  `arXiv:2611.10483`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mengyu Wang, Ken Deng, Yftah Ziser, Shay B. Cohen, Tiejun Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The task of visual reasoning relative pose identification (VRRPI) has exposed critical weaknesses in the multi-view 3D spatial reasoning capabilities of modern vision-language models (VLMs). We present a systematic analysis of VLMs' behavior in VRRPI, uncovering stable output biases where VLMs disproportionately predict specific motion patterns regardless of visual evidence. To mitigate these inherent biases, we propose self knowledge re-expression with iterative debiasing (SKR-ID), an annotation-free pipeline that adapts VLMs for VRRPI using only randomly extracted unannotated image pairs. SKR-ID transitions VLMs' output mechanism from generic next-token prediction to task-optimized classification heads. The pipeline proceeds in two stages: (1) a cold-start initialization using debiasing object-centric prompts to anchor motion inference in 2D visual evidence; and (2) a recursive logit-ranking mechanism that enforces statistical parity by using the median logit value as a dynamic threshold. Experimental evaluation across frontier VLMs on standard benchmarks demonstrates that SKR-ID significantly mitigates systematic biases. Our method improves open-source VLMs' Macro-F1 scores from 35.0-45.4% to 53.7-58.0%, without using any annotated data. These results show that SKR-ID can effectively improve 3D reasoning performance from a severely collapsed baseline. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WordEval: Evaluating Word-Native Operation Fidelity in Document Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10475)**  `arXiv:2611.10475`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jinyoung Park, Yupan Huang, Shimin Zhang, Jiayu Ding, Yilin Jia, Yuzhong Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Microsoft Word is a central environment for office automation, and the faithful execution of its editing operations is an important capability for document agents. Unlike plain-text editing, editing in Word modifies a structured object model, where the requested effect is realized in object properties. Existing benchmarks, however, evaluate office workflows or desktop automation, treating word-processor documents as one task domain among many and verifying outcomes with keyword matching, per-task property checks, or screenshot comparison. We introduce WordEval, an operation-fidelity benchmark that instead centers evaluation on Word editing operations, testing whether each requested edit is realized as intended changes to Word objects and properties. It contains 642 cases across 15 Word feature areas, ranging from text and lists to charts, shape geometry, and SmartArt. 546 atomic cases cover 231 commands, 96 compositional cases combine multiple edits in a single document, and a Word-native oracle checks task-specific object properties and preservation conditions. Cases are constructed from editing-unit specifications, yielding controlled input documents, reference documents, and aligned oracle checks. Each compositional case is linked to corresponding atomic cases, supporting fine-grained diagnosis of editing failures. We evaluate eight agent systems and find that many struggle on the Atomic track, with performance varying across feature areas. Moreover, every system scores lower on Compositional than on Atomic, with gaps that vary substantially by system. Exact atomic-intent links and recorded failure diagnoses provide complementary views of this variation.
  </details>

- **[ESTRA: Enabling Extreme Attention Sparsity for Training-Free Video Diffusion Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10448)**  `arXiv:2611.10448`  `cs.CV`  
  _Enhuai Liu, Yunke Wang, Yutong Wang, Changming Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video diffusion transformers generate high-quality videos, but attention over long spatiotemporal sequences makes inference computationally expensive. Sparse attention reduces this cost by computing only a subset of query-key interactions exactly, and recent training-free methods further approximate the remaining interactions. These methods, however, degrade quickly at high sparsity, which limits the achievable speedup. We present ESTRA (Extreme Sparsity Through Refined Attention), a training-free acceleration method that preserves generation quality even at extreme sparsity. ESTRA concentrates exact attention computation on a small set of important key-value tokens and approximates the remaining contributions with cluster representatives. To identify these tokens efficiently, it groups similar queries and uses their representatives to score individual keys. A variance-based correction further refines the attention weights of the cluster representatives, improving the accuracy of the approximation. Experiments on Wan2.1-14B and HunyuanVideo-13B show that ESTRA establishes a new quality-efficiency frontier for training-free sparse attention, achieving $3.26\times$ and $4.71\times$ end-to-end speedups at 5% attention density while surpassing Sol-Attn and SVG-EAR in fidelity to dense generation and on most VBench dimensions. Even at 1% density, ESTRA remains more faithful to dense generation than Sol-Attn at 12.5%, while reaching up to $5.39\times$ speedup.
  </details>

- **[S2HAND: Accuracy-Preservation Trade-Offs for Hand Specialization in Whole-Body Pose Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10416)**  `arXiv:2611.10416`  `cs.CV`  
  _Singyu Muk, Xuanren Song, Kalin Stefanov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified whole-body pose estimators jointly predict body, face, and hand landmarks, but often degrade on small, blurred, or occluded hands. Attaching a standalone hand specialist introduces extra detection overhead, coordinate alignment, and wrist-stitching artifacts. We introduce S2HAND, a hand-specialized Sapiens2-5B framework that preserves the native 308-keypoint output interface. Specifically, S2HAND-MT adapts a lightweight 0.87M-parameter pose branch to route 40 finger heatmaps while strictly preserving the shared Transformer, auxiliary task heads, and the other 268 body, foot, and face heatmaps. S2HAND-Pose further fine-tunes three Fisher-selected Transformer blocks (267.13M parameters), trading exact preservation for stronger hand recovery: it lifts macro AUC from 57.83% to 65.73% across HInt TEST, COCO WholeBody VAL, and FreiHAND EVAL, and surpasses HInt-adapted Hand5/HRNet and stitched Hand5 systems on all five Native Pipeline benchmarks. On COCO, S2HAND-Pose improves hand AP by 12.89 points and body-plus-foot AP by 0.19 points. Across a 512-image multi-task audit, it maintains 99.60% foreground segmentation agreement with the public checkpoint, with quantified normal and pointmap drift. Its 2D coordinates also lower 3D mesh error in frozen Pose2Mesh transfer, while our optional TailGuard re-cropping module cuts S2HAND-MT's severe failure rate on NewDays from 13.45% to 10.43%. S2HAND thus resolves the whole-body hand bottleneck with either exact non-hand preservation (MT) or empirically audited trade-offs (Pose).
  </details>

- **[Tiresias: Towards Language-Conditioned World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10392)**  `arXiv:2611.10392`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fengyi Wu, Yifei Dong, Yetong Sha, Lingdong Kong, Qiyu Hu, Bohan Xiong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models enable planning through internal simulation, while natural language provides intuitive task specification. Yet, language-conditioned world modeling remains fragmented across domains with disparate action spaces, datasets, and evaluation protocols. We introduce LCWM, a shared task that maps an initial agent-view observation and a language instruction to an action sequence that fulfills the instruction. Its accompanying benchmark, LCWM-Bench, integrates 15 datasets across navigation, robotics, 3D gaming, and driving under a shared action interface and evaluation protocol, pairing 142,987 episodes with 428,961 instructions in three styles. We further propose Tiresias, an agent that plans via selective imagination: a language-conditioned diffusion policy generates candidate trajectories, a trajectory ranker shortlists them, and a video-pretrained world model imagines their visual consequences. A visual ranker evaluates these rollouts, complementing trajectory scores to select the final plan. Tiresias achieves competitive planning and imagination performance on LCWM-Bench. Ablations demonstrate the complementary benefits of trajectory ranking and visual verification, while experiments with alternative proposal methods show that the trained rankers remain effective beyond our diffusion policy. Together, LCWM-Bench and Tiresias provide a unified evaluation setting and an effective starting point for studying language-guided planning through world models.
  </details>

- **[RefineSplat: Reconstruction-Guided Object-Centric Gaussian Splatting for Open-Vocabulary Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10379)**  `arXiv:2611.10379`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiren Lu, Disheng Liu, Sreya Srinidhi, Yunlai Zhou, Yiran Qiao, Chaoda Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary 3D scene understanding aims to segment and retrieve arbitrary objects in 3D scenes using natural language queries. Most existing methods typically follow a splat-then-segment paradigm, where 3D Gaussian Splatting is first reconstructed and then augmented with labels or language features for subsequent segmentation. However, this pipeline often suffers from view inconsistency, ambiguous object boundaries, and weak object-level correspondence, especially in complex static and dynamic scenes.
