  _Pasquale De Marinis, Uzay Kaymak, Rogier Brussee, Gennaro Vessio, Giovanna Castellano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-Shot Semantic Segmentation (FSS) models achieve strong performance in segmenting novel classes with minimal labeled examples, yet their decision-making processes remain largely opaque. Explainable AI has matured on standard computer vision tasks, but not on FSS, where an explanation would tell a practitioner which support pixels the prediction rested on and which examples to provide next. We argue that matching-based FSS models are interpretable by design: their query-support similarity scores, once normalized and processed, attribute the prediction to individual support pixels. Our approach, Affinity Explainer (AffEx), makes this intrinsic interpretability explicit by extracting attribution maps directly from matching scores at multiple feature levels, without requiring gradients or external perturbations. We extend the standard causal metrics for evaluating explanations to the FSS domain and propose additional metrics to better capture the practical utility of explanations in few-shot scenarios. Experiments on two FSS benchmarks, covering seven architectures that range from classical matching-based models to recent pipelines built on vision foundation models (DINOv2/v3, SAM, SAM2) and a prototype-based head, show that AffEx outperforms adapted standard attribution methods at nearly every evaluation point, and that the gain comes from the matching mechanism itself. On pipelines whose segmentation stage transforms the matching output, the same evaluation doubles as a diagnostic of where the decision is made, and on the prototype-based head AffEx subsumes the prototype-style read-out as a special case. Qualitatively, the attribution maps expose how each architecture resolves the support set, and we show how they guide model diagnosis. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[D2D: Detector-to-Differentiable Critic for Improved Numeracy in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19278)**  `arXiv:2510.19278`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nobline Yoo, Olga Russakovsky, Ye Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models have achieved strong performance in semantic alignment, yet they still struggle with generating the correct number of objects specified in prompts. Existing approaches typically incorporate auxiliary counting networks as external critics to enhance numeracy. However, since these critics must provide gradient guidance during generation, they are restricted to regression-based models that are inherently differentiable, thus excluding detector-based models with superior counting ability, whose count-via-enumeration nature is non-differentiable. To overcome this, we propose Detector-to-Differentiable (D2D), a novel framework that transforms non-differentiable detection models into differentiable critics, leveraging their superior counting ability to guide numeracy generation. Specifically, we design custom activation functions to convert detector logits into soft binary indicators, which are then used to optimize the noise prior at inference time with pre-trained T2I models. Our extensive experiments on SDXL-Turbo, SD-Turbo, and Pixart-DMD across four benchmarks of varying complexity (low-density, high-density, and multi-object scenarios) demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements in object counting accuracy (e.g., boosting up to 13.7% on D2D-Small, a 400-prompt, low-density benchmark), with minimal degradation in overall image quality and computational overhead. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[IKOD: Image-Attention-Guided Key-Value Merging against Object Hallucination in the Decoding of Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.03469)**  `arXiv:2508.03469`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiabing Yang, Chenhang Cui, Yixiang Chen, Tao Yu, Yan Huang, Liang Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) exhibit strong visual understanding and language generation capabilities yet remain prone to object hallucination, producing plausible descriptions unsupported by the input image. Analyzing generation dynamics across eight diverse LVLMs, we observe that cross-modal visual attention steadily decays as generation unfolds because appended text tokens continuously expand the softmax denominator; consequently, hallucinated objects consistently appear later and under lower image attention than grounded mentions. Unlike existing training-free remedies that rely on input perturbations or external models, we propose IKOD (Image-attention-guided Key-value merging against Object hallucination in Decoding), a collaborative decoder that directly compresses the competing text context within the native key-value (KV) cache. At each step, IKOD keeps visual tokens intact and merges low-attention text entries into a compact auxiliary cache, fusing the resulting image-focused distribution with the base branch to correct ungrounded predictions while preserving linguistic fluency. An adaptive variant (IKOD-A) further schedules collaboration strength online from the measured attention decay under a single universal configuration. Extensive experiments across hallucination and general multimodal benchmarks on eight diverse LVLMs demonstrate that IKOD and IKOD-A consistently mitigate object hallucination while preserving object recall and general multimodal capabilities at minimal inference cost. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RefRef: A Dataset and Benchmark for Reconstructing Refractive and Reflective Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05848)**  `arXiv:2505.05848`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yue Yin, Enze Tao, Weijian Deng, Dylan Campbell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern 3D reconstruction and novel view synthesis approaches have demonstrated strong performance on scenes with opaque, non-refractive objects. However, most assume straight light paths and therefore cannot properly handle refractive and reflective materials. The lack of datasets specialized for these effects has impeded efforts to fairly and thoroughly evaluate performance and thereby make progress in this domain. Most existing datasets focus on opaque scenes, while those targeting refractive objects are often limited to thin glass with negligible ray bending, untinted single-material objects, or backgrounds treated as infinitely-distant, leaving key failure modes under strong refraction untested. To address this gap, we introduce the RefRef dataset. It contains 150 synthetic and 60 real scenes spanning varying levels of geometric complexity and diverse background types that expose the limitations of existing methods. For the purpose of benchmarking, we also provide an oracle method that, given the object geometry and refractive indices, calculates accurate light paths for neural rendering, and a simple two-stage baseline that relaxes these assumptions. We evaluate these against state-of-the-art methods and show that the task is far from solved.
  </details>

- **[MultiTurnRoute: A Platform for Building and Evaluating Multi-Turn LLM Routers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10848)**  `arXiv:2611.10848`  `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Jiaqi Xue, Mengxin Zheng, Heng Huang, Qian Lou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In a multi-turn conversation, the choice of language model at one turn can affect which model should answer the next. The selected model's reply shapes the context for the next query, and its cached prompt prefix can reduce the cost of using that model again. We introduce MultiTurnRoute, a platform for building and evaluating multi-turn LLM routers. A shared interface gives built-in and user-defined routers the current query, dialogue history, and an estimate of each model's reusable prefix length. Our routing algorithm, Chain-of-Route (CoR), uses a learned gate over recent and recurrent dialogue history to predict response quality and estimates cost from each model's reusable prefix. MultiTurnRoute Arena ranks routers on recorded conversations and compares session quality and cost in interactive runs where a simulated user responds to each reply. On a benchmark drawn from WildChat and LMSYS-Chat-1M with per-turn quality annotations for five LLMs, CoR reduces the normalized cost required to match the strongest model's quality by up to 20.3% relative to the strongest routing baseline, while matching or improving peak response quality.
  </details>

- **[A Channel-Boosted Multi-Agent System with Iterative Consultation for Document Sensitivity Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22212)**  `arXiv:2609.22212`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Aleesha Zainab, Asifullah Khan, Muhammad Ahmed Khalid, Faheem Ullah Khan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Organizations in critical national infrastructure sectors must assess heterogeneous documents for sensitivity before routing or storage. Manual assessment is slow, inconsistent, and unscalable. Extending our prior leakage-controlled benchmark, BERT established the top single-encoder baseline (89.14% accuracy, 89.33% F1-score under 5-fold cross-validation on the Strategic 16K corpus). However, transformer baselines suffer from a structural limitation: fixed input length truncation discards evidence beyond the retained window-precisely where sensitive cables tend to be longest. We present Channel-Boosted MAS (CB-MAS) and instantiate it as IC-MAS (Iterative Consultation Multi-Agent System) to solve this without long-context computational costs. A Channel Critic Agent learns document-adaptive trust weights governing Gated Channel Boosting between two first-window encoders, while paired Consultation Agents iteratively exchange belief states to reconcile evidence from the beginning and end of long documents. IC-MAS holds computation constant regardless of document length by reconciling fixed windows in a compact representation space. Ablation studies show critic-controlled Channel Boosting provides the bulk of accuracy gains, while consultation recovers recall without precision collapse. Critic-Controlled Gated Channel Boosting with Max-Pool fusion and Blackboard Adaptive Consultation achieves 90.72% accuracy, 91.23% F1-score, 92.01% sensitive recall, and 90.46% sensitive precision, using about 54% less average computation than a fixed-round baseline. Gains over the single-encoder baseline are statistically significant (McNemar's test, p less than 0.000001; paired t-test). We include LIME/SHAP explainability, multi-agent evaluation, and an honest accounting of limitations.
  </details>

- **[Prune, Don't Merge: Expert Compression of Mixture-of-Experts LLMs on Multilingual Production Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12240)**  `arXiv:2611.12240`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Tomas Rasymas, Povilas Kvedaras_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts language models are cheap to run per token but expensive to hold in GPU memory, which drives the cost of serving them in production. One-shot expert compression shrinks them after a single calibration pass. Its two current state-of-the-art methods are REAP for expert pruning, which deletes the least salient experts, and REAM for expert merging, which merges them into the most salient ones. Both have been evaluated only on academic benchmarks. We compare the two on five multilingual production tasks of a web-hosting company, from classification to the generation of marketing HTML email templates, with two 30B-parameter models (Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507 and the hybrid Mamba-attention Nemotron-3.5-Lightning) at 25% and 50% of experts removed and the same calibration data. Pruning wins: on the same rows, REAP is significantly better in 16 of 20 comparisons and REAM in 2. At 25%, REAP keeps 93.6% of Qwen3-30B's generative quality and REAM 62.5%, although on benchmarks the two are within a point for the same model. The damage concentrates in the output contract rather than the content: 89.8-95.2% of each significant loss on email templates comes from hard-gate failures such as invalid JSON, broken mobile layouts and runaway generation, and most failed non-English domain descriptions copy the prompt's English opening phrase. Cross-perplexity and logit divergence, two diagnostics from the REAP paper, also track production quality, as does the rate of repetition loops; n-gram diversity does not. We close with practical guidance for compressing MoE models for production.
  </details>

- **[ChemInsight: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating and Diagnosing Chemistry Understanding and Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11794)**  `arXiv:2611.11794`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Changyu Hu, Yiming Zou, Xin Tang, Yiru Pan, Zicheng Huang, Deng Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce ChemInsight, a benchmark designed to evaluate large language models (LLMs) on chemistry tasks that require multi-step reasoning and structured chemical understanding. We propose a two-dimensional scoring system (scope and depth) to assess both task coverage and reasoning completeness. ChemInsight consists of 203 challenging chemistry questions organized into 50 bundles, which support the two-dimensional scoring evaluation scheme.We evaluate ten representative LLMs using ChemInsight and observe consistently low performance across models, with scope scores below 30% and depth scores below 11%, substantially lower than human expert performance. We then proceed to ask (1) What factor limits the advancement of current LLMs in chemistry? (2) To what extent are statistically driven language-model training methods effective at modeling chemistry symbolic language such as SMILES? And (3) Can LLM performance in chemistry be improved through intensive training on large, domain-specific corpora?
  </details>

- **[SWEET: Mitigating Spurious Feature Reliance in LLM Reasoning via Weight Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10974)**  `arXiv:2611.10974`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhizhang Fu, Lucas Zhao, Michalis Korakakis, Mengru Ding, Lukasz Szpruch, Yue Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning in large language models can be disrupted by simple input perturbations (e.g. adding irrelevant distractors), suggesting reliance on spurious correlations. Identifying and suppressing the spurious features involved in long chain-of-thought reasoning remains challenging. We introduce Spurious-feature WEight EdiTing (SWEET), a training-free method that combines counterfactual interventions with mechanistic interpretability to identify and suppress spurious-features. SWEET extracts token- and sequence-level behavioral directions from activation contrasts between original problems and their counterfactual variants - capturing local and global patterns of spurious reasoning. It then localizes the responsible MLP neurons and attention heads and suppresses their contributions along these directions through weight edits. Using only 20 original-counterfactual pairs for direction extraction, SWEET improves accuracy by an average of 11.8 percentage points on perturbed inputs and 2 points on original inputs across five open-weight reasoning models and five reasoning benchmarks. It also reduces the relative gap between original and perturbed accuracies from 17.6% to 2.5%. Analyses identify two recurring forms of spurious reasoning, i.e. over-integrating irrelevant conditions and overthinking, and provide mechanistic evidence that SWEET improves reasoning robustness by suppressing both behavioral directions.
  </details>

- **[On the Geometric Bottlenecks of Cross-Lingual Knowledge Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10646)**  `arXiv:2611.10646`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Aastha A K Verma, Anwoy Chatterjee, Sparsh Jain, Tanmoy Chakraborty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer-based autoregressive multilingual language models (LMs) are often assumed to store factual knowledge in an interlingua: a language-agnostic latent space. If this assumption holds, a parameter update applied in a source language during knowledge editing should inherently propagate to its semantic equivalents in target languages. However, empirical benchmarks show that cross-lingual propagation of edits often degrades substantially, a degradation that prior work treats largely as an algorithmic symptom rather than a structural bottleneck. In this work, we investigate whether representational anisotropy creates a structural bottleneck for source-only associative-memory editing, and perform a geometric dissection of the cross-lingual propagation gap. Using associative memory-based "locate-then-edit" methods, including ROME, MEMIT, and AlphaEdit, as analytical frameworks, we derive a closed-form decomposition of this gap into key-space propagation, parallel residual mismatch, and orthogonal residual mismatch. Our empirical findings show that cross-lingual propagation is primarily bottlenecked by a large orthogonal residual component, indicating that source-language edits often fail to span the target-language update directions required for successful transfer. Furthermore, we show that post-hoc correction using only source-language residual statistics encounters a large residual-prediction barrier. Finally, we perform a structural realignment via a targeted contrastive alignment objective and find that it substantially improves key-space propagation and partially improves cross-lingual edit transfer, while leaving value-space mismatch as a remaining bottleneck.
  </details>

- **[ASCENT: Scale-Complementary External State for Frozen Long-Context Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10175)**  `arXiv:2611.10175`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xingjian Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External state should become more valuable as a reader becomes more capable, yet long-context evaluations measure memory utility at a single model scale. This paper formulates scale complementarity: the absolute gain from external state increases when reader capacity and a compact state budget are jointly scaled. ASCENT realizes this principle with a target-blind causal writer, nested typed state, and a bounded query-conditioned readout; every language model remains frozen. Under log loss, the value of a nested refinement equals its target-relevant conditional information; a full factorial makes the reader-by-state interaction identifiable. Official 16K BABILong experiments show Qwen2.5 accuracy gains of 10.50, 78.88, and 82.63 percentage points at 0.5B, 1.5B, and 3B parameters, with both adjacent directional contrasts significant after family-wise correction. At 7B, Foundation accuracy rises to 33.25% while four-slot ASCENT answers every question, which caps the gain at 66.75 points; the 3B-to-7B accuracy increment is -15.88 points [-20.55, -11.20]. The accuracy-side claim is therefore established from 0.5B to 3B; the 7B conclusion rests on the non-saturated negative-log-likelihood factorial. A three-model by three-state factorial yields co-scaled negative log-likelihood gains of 0.388, 2.099, and 7.406 nats at 1.5B, 3B, and 7B; all interaction and gain contrasts retain positive simultaneous lower bounds. Five public 7B-8B readers obtain 66.75-85.63 point gains, and ASCENT exceeds task-trained retrieval checkpoints at the two larger shared scales. Controls, baselines, systems accounting, matched-hardware reproduction, and a cross-architecture sensitivity analysis accompany the experiments. The results establish scale-complementary external state under the evaluated benchmarks, metrics, and state schema.
  </details>

- **[Universal Byte-Level Encoding: UTF-8/UTF-16 Routing to Reduce Cross-Script Token-Budget Disparities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01984)**  `arXiv:2610.01984`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hyunsik Kim, Youngmoon Jung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Byte-level byte-pair encoding (BBPE) tokenizers are attractive for multilingual large language models because they cover all Unicode text. In UTF-8-based BBPE, however, many scripts start from a higher fallback cost than English: when no learned merges apply, a multibyte character requires multiple byte-derived symbols. We call this worst-case pre-merge cost the encoding floor. A higher floor can increase token counts and per-request cost while shrinking usable context. Changing the whole text encoding can reduce this gap but make already-efficient English spans more expensive in mixed-script text. Thus, we propose Universal Byte-Level Encoding (UBE), a dual-alphabet tokenizer that keeps 1-2-byte UTF-8 characters in UTF-8 and routes 3-4-byte characters through UTF-16. This lowers the encoding floor for 3-byte Basic Multilingual Plane characters, used by many scripts that need more tokens than English, without raising it for any character. UBE changes only the symbols presented to byte-pair encoding, and a Unicode 17 audit confirms exact round trips for every scalar value and every input in three official test suites. UBE also composes with other boundary policies and morphology-based encodings. Across intrinsic evaluations, UBE lowers dispersion in English-normalized token-count ratios (Gini 6-14% lower on FLORES-200 at 16K-64K), reducing cross-lingual token-budget disparity. In multilingual language models, UBE matches BBPE's quality. In the main multilingual settings, UBE reduces token counts most where they are highest and also lowers English counts, yielding more usable context and faster prompt processing in content-matched benchmarks. Byte-level models benefit directly: UBE shortens their inputs by 29-33% in all 39 of the FLORES-200 languages written mostly in 3-byte characters (1.4-1.5 times as much text per context window), lowers byte-level Gini by 28%, and lengthens no input.
  </details>

- **[The Imperfective Paradox Is Not Necessarily in Large Language Models: A Benchmark Failure Before a Model Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25005)**  `arXiv:2608.25005`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiqiao Han, Katarzyna Pruś, Mark Steedman, Yizhou Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work uses an NLI benchmark of the imperfective paradox to argue that large language models exhibit a Teleological Bias and Calibration Crisis. We identify three validity concerns in benchmark construction, including aspectual reduction, semantic bias, and relation bias. Under a strict NLI standard, 76% of the Group A instances do not explicitly rule out culmination; in native-speaker annotation, 38% of Group A and 29% of Group C examples permit an alternative interpretation. To better isolate the semantic contrast, we construct Lexically Matched Minimal Pairs. Moreover, by formulating event-semantic NLI as a multi-step reasoning problem, we evaluate intermediate semantic judgments and use oracle-guided settings. We find that incorrect NLI entailment does not fully support teleological bias. Prompting often changes final NLI decisions without reliably improving the underlying semantic judgments, offering a possible explanation for the Calibration Crisis.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Supervision Targets for Multimodal LLM Unlearning: Less Private Information, Reduced Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09939)**  `arXiv:2611.09939`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Leijiang Gu, Zhen Zeng, Feng Li, Le Zhang, Zenglin Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can memorize and reproduce sensitive information from their training data, posing significant privacy risks. Their ability to encode knowledge across both textual and visual modalities further complicates the selective removal of such private information. Many existing methods guide model unlearning by training the model toward constructed labels or output distributions, which we refer to as supervision targets. However, these methods often suffer from a trade-off between forgetting effectiveness and model utility. We identify supervision target limitations as a key factor in this trade-off and empirically show that targets containing more private information lead to insufficient forgetting, while larger distribution shifts from the original output distribution are associated with greater utility degradation. Motivated by these findings, we propose FFN-Perturbed Self-Distillation (FPD), which starts from the teacher's original output distribution as a zero-shift reference and selectively perturbs FFN activations associated with private information across textual and visual modalities. By precisely reducing private information from the teacher's original output distribution, FPD constructs supervision targets with less private information and reduced distribution shift. Extensive experiments on S-MLLMUn Bench demonstrate that FPD achieves a better forgetting-utility trade-off than existing methods.
  </details>

- **[SocialGym: Evaluating LLM Social Intelligence through Verifiable Multi-Agent Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09128)**  `arXiv:2608.09128`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Keyu He, Xuhui Zhou, Maarten Sap_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in multi-agent social settings where they must cooperate, negotiate, and adapt to other agents. Measuring these social skills is hard because most social interactions offer no objective ground truth. Evaluations often fall back on LLM judges, which are costly, subjective, and noisy. We introduce SocialGym, an environment of 22 multi-agent social games, from matrix games to hidden-role deduction, with verifiable outcomes determined by game rules. We benchmark ten LLMs across all games and roles using an Elo tournament. Our results show that no model is best in every game, and rankings shift across game families and sides. Secret keeping correlates more strongly with theory-of-mind and privacy benchmarks than with general knowledge benchmarks. Half of the models give away their side's secret in Chameleon and Spyfall, and the smallest models are not reliably better than chance at voting for hidden opponents in most hidden-role games. Finally, we examine whether models can improve their play by reflecting on self-play and writing strategy playbooks, finding mixed results across models and games. SocialGym provides verifiable measures for diagnosing LLM social capabilities across settings and roles.
  </details>

- **[Who Remembers What? Multi-Granularity Memory Extraction for Multi-User, Multi-Agent Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10439)**  `arXiv:2611.10439`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Yunzhe Wang, Mesut Erhan Unal, Sheng Zhang, Shabnam Ghadar, Peng Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving agents improve their own skills, memory, and harness code from experience without retraining the base LLM, and group-evolving agents go further by sharing these lessons across agents. Yet most such systems overlook that sharing across users and heterogeneous agents requires deciding who can benefit from each lesson and what information must remain private. We present MixUA, a memory system that distills independent user-agent trajectories into lessons and assigns each lesson to a user-agent pair, user, agent, or global scope according to whom it benefits and what it may disclose. For evaluation, we introduce CrossLearnBench, a mixed-user-agent benchmark that pairs established agent tasks with user preferences and privacy probes that test whether private facts leak into memory other users can read. With five users and seven agents, MixUA raises pass rates by up to 16 points over no memory and 5 over a single global memory, even on tasks previously tried only by other users on other agents. It retains up to 90% of previously stated preferences, and at most 0.02% of privacy probes find a planted private fact in a forbidden scope. We hope MixUA offers a scalable path toward a global system where users and agents co-evolve.
  </details>

- **[HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12872)**  `arXiv:2611.12872`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haozhen Zhang, Bohan Liu, Jianzhu Bao, Qingyuan Liu, Haodong Yue, Xiao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.
  </details>

- **[Phrase-Level Speculative Decoding on a Frozen Target](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12464)**  `arXiv:2611.12464`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Madhusudhana Naidu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoders raise throughput by banking more target tokens per forward pass, and Medusa, EAGLE-3, DFlash and DSpark all do it the same way: more candidates that survive verification. All of them draft in the target's own vocabulary, so one node of the candidate tree is one target token, and the tokenizer makes that unit look like a constant. It is not. BPE's pretokenizer splits on whitespace before merges are learned, so no multi-word string can ever become a single token: in Qwen3-4B, "United States of America" is four tokens and no adjacent pair of them exists as one entry, so a phrase is out of reach of any method drafting in the target's vocabulary.We change the unit. A small MLP head reads the frozen target's hidden state and predicts a one- to three-word phrase as one entry in a separate draft vocabulary; greedy longest-match expansion maps it back into the target's own tokens, and N candidates are verified in one forward pass behind a prefix-tree mask, so the change is orthogonal to tree depth and width. The target and its tokenizer stay frozen and a candidate is accepted only while it matches the target's own argmax, so the served output is identical to greedy decoding up to floating-point tie-breaking.On one A5000 at batch 1, against HuggingFace generate(), a 21.94M head (0.545% of Qwen3-4B) reaches 2.391x on GSM8K, 2.444x on MATH-500 and 1.544x on MT-Bench. Because the head's output layer is one row of 257 parameters per entry, the draft vocabulary size is the parameter budget: an 8,000-entry head is 3.434M parameters, 0.0854% of the target and 64x smaller than EAGLE-3's drafter, and keeps 92.3% of the larger head's GSM8K tokens per pass. With EAGLE-3 run on same prompts and baseline, our 21.94M head is ahead of tree-16 on both maths sets and behind tree-60 on every set.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Semantic Divergence for LLM Agent Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33012)**  `arXiv:2610.33012`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Harshavardhan Abichandani, Penny Chong, Atin Ghosh, Daniel Dahlmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool use can behave inconsistently across independent runs from identical states. They may call different tools, produce contradictory outputs, or follow divergentthis http URLvariability compounds over time and undermines deployment reliability. Existing evaluation metrics primarily measure task correctness via completion-based scores and do not capture consistency in agent behavior across executions. Separately, agent behavior is analyzed either at the token level using probability distributions or at the single-response level using semantic clustering, but neither captures how inconsistency propagates across future turns of a trajectory. We introduce Recursive Semantic Divergence (RSD), a trajectory-level consistency metric adapted from bisimulation metrics in reinforcement learning. RSD measures the expected cumulative semantic disagreement between two independent rollouts of the same agent from the same state, using natural language inference to detect logical inconsistency. It is defined recursively over future states and approximated by a neural network trained on independent rollouts. The learned metric estimates future divergence from the current state, providing a consistency score at each turn without requiring the full trajectory to complete. When used as a reward signal for fine-tuning, RSD reduces AUC coefficient of variation (CV) by up to 30.9% on tau2-bench and 50.5% on ToolSandbox. Across Qwen-2.5 and Qwen-3.5 models, RSD reduces AUC CV in seven out of eight model-benchmark settings, while improving the final task progress in all eight settings.
  </details>

- **[Unmasking Four Knapsack Problems in the Traveling Thief Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12597)**  `arXiv:2611.12597`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammed Mizan Alanazi, Mahdi Khemakhem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The traveling thief problem (TTP) asks a thief to choose a tour and a packing together, and the benchmark of 9,720 instances of Polyakovskiy et al. (2014) is the standard test set for comparing TTP heuristics. We show that four of these instances are not TTP instances at all. Their files give a renting rate of 0.00, so the tour has no effect on the objective and each instance reduces to a 0-1 knapsack problem. One of them belongs to the 60 categorized instances on which new methods are usually compared, and on it five of nine published methods report exactly the same value. The cause is the format of the files, which keep the renting rate with two decimals, while the rule of the generator gives a rate close to 0.003 on these instances. The same rounding may shift the rate of 949 other instances by more than 1%. Since the tour and the packing used by the generator were not published, the original rates cannot be recovered. We therefore define new rates with the same rule, applied to a fully specified reference solution that we publish, and we show with a published heuristic that the corrected instances behave as TTP instances again. We also give reference results on the corrected instances and recommendations for future studies. Finally, we present ttplib, a new open-source Python library that loads any of the 9,720 instances, including the corrected ones, without downloading the whole benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Lost in Rounding: bf16 Recurrent State Makes Hybrid Reasoning Models Think Longer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12127)**  `arXiv:2611.12127`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuchuan Weng, Xin Chen, Chi Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid reasoning models interleave attention with recurrent mixers such as Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet, whose state is cached between decoding steps. Prior work showed that storing this state in FP16 or INT8 with round-to-nearest (RTN) makes Mamba-2 and GDN hybrids more verbose, and that stochastic rounding (SR) fixes it. We study the other 16-bit format that serving engines expose, bf16, on six open hybrid reasoning models and 590 competition-math problems. With bf16 RTN state, five of six models reason longer (geometric-mean length 1.07x to 1.27x; also under greedy decoding in the two models tested) and hit the 32K token cap more often. On Qwen3.5-9B, cap hits rise from 118 to 223 and accuracy falls from 85.6% to 70.8%. On LiveCodeBench, both models tested also reason longer and lose 12.6 and 13.4 points of pass@1. Rerunning the capped items with a 128K budget halves the accuracy gap but does not close it (93.6% vs. 86.6%): bf16 runs that never finish are twice as common, and 85% of them are exact repetition loops. On Nemotron-Nano-9B-v2, by contrast, 30 of 31 extra caps finish at 128K, so the cost there is mostly extra tokens (exploratory). Same-width bf16 SR removes the length increase in four of five affected models, restores Qwen3.5-9B to 87.6% and brings code length and pass@1 near FP32 levels, so the failure is mostly rounding bias, not missing resolution. With an integer-hash random generator, SR on Qwen3.5-9B matches FP32 on cap hits and accuracy (113 vs. 118; 85.6% for both) at the memory and decoding speed of bf16 RTN. In the Nemotron-Nano-v2 lineage, SR on 25% of heads (an intermediate-decay band) removes the effect, while random heads and the top teacher-forced-KL octiles do not; in Nemotron-3 and Qwen3.5-9B the effect is spread across heads. We recommend that evaluations of hybrid models report the state dtype, cap rate and output length alongside accuracy.
  </details>

- **[BharatSynthQA: A Context-Grounded Question Answering Dataset on Indian Temples and Religious Heritage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11952)**  `arXiv:2611.11952`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Riya Naik, Shivam Sharma, Swati Agarwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  India's temples and other places of worship are among its richest repositories of cultural heritage, bringing together religious tradition, history, architecture, and art. Due to the widespread curiosity, people increasingly turn to Artificial Intelligence (AI) assistants for information about them. Current models are rarely trained or evaluated on this domain. We present BharatSynthQA, a passage grounded question answering (QA) dataset focused on Indian temples, which also covers other religious sites connected to them. Our pipeline crawls Wikipedia from topics seeded by authentic heritage sources, filters articles for topical relevance, and locations in India. Further it generates Boolean, factoid, and explanatory QA pairs with a fewshot prompted LLM. An LLM judge filters the pairs on answer correctness, retaining 98,677 of 131,494 generated pairs. Additionally, 300 human-annotated pairs are released separately as a reference set. Human evaluation of the subsample of this dataset showed 89.83% of the questions were formed correctly. Benchmarking five current LLMs shows that factoid token F1 is only 0.18-0.30 without the source passage but 0.73-0.77 with it, indicating that this domain is poorly captured in model knowledge. The dataset is available atthis https URLto support research on culturally grounded QA and retrieval-augmented generation for Indian heritage.
  </details>

- **[A Large-Scale Dataset for Cross-Lingual Multi-Jurisdiction Patent Drafting from Patent Cooperation Treaty Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11891)**  `arXiv:2611.11891`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Trung Phuong Le, Hirofumi Nonaka, Koji Marusaki, Katsuhito Sudoh, Seiya Kawano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Securing patent protection across countries requires drafting claims separately for each national patent office, in the relevant language and according to its legal and stylistic conventions. Patent claims can therefore differ across languages and jurisdictions because of translation, target-office drafting practices, and prosecution-stage changes. We formulate cross-lingual patent drafting as generating plausible candidate claim sets for a specified target language, jurisdiction, and prosecution stage from a source claim set in another language. The task models jurisdiction- and stage-specific transformations beyond content-preserving translation. We construct a large-scale Japanese-English dataset by linking Japan Patent Office (JPO) and United States Patent and Trademark Office (USPTO) records through 884,179 Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT)-derived patent families. Across six released configurations, these families yield 4,067,168 directional claim-set instances; four core configurations form the main benchmark. Commercial machine translation and zero- and few-shot prompting capture part of the task, while supervised adaptation provides the strongest overall baseline, showing that task-specific patterns can be learned from linked cross-jurisdictional claim sets.
  </details>

- **[HiddenPathQA: A Benchmark for Knowledge Graph Question Answering When Questions Hide Their Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11802)**  `arXiv:2611.11802`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sumin Jo, Edward Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) systems are typically evaluated on questions whose surface tokens lexically expose the gold KG path. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. We identify this collapse of path-question alignment as a blind spot in existing KGQA evaluation. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic. (iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.
  </details>

- **[ScrapeBench: Evaluating Legal Compliance of AI Agents in Website Scraping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11306)**  `arXiv:2611.11306`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Joseph Marvin Imperial, Daniel Slate, Noam Kolt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents deployed on the open web are becoming more capable of performing actions that may breach contractual provisions and violate computer security laws. In this paper, we introduce ScrapeBench, the first agentic benchmark for empirically evaluating the legal compliance of AI agents with website scraping policies, namely terms of service (ToS) andthis http URLdirectives. Across widely used closed-weight and open-weight AI agents, we find that most agents exhibit low compliance rates (< 5%) when instructed to scrape websites in our sample of 1,626 popular websites that explicitly prohibit scraping. In addition, we observe that even where AI agents actively check the applicable ToS andthis http URL, they continue to exhibit low compliance rates. Conversely, in our sample of 269 websites that do not prohibit scraping, we observe that agents sometimes refuse to scrape despite it being lawful, such as Claude Opus 4.6 exhibiting an over-refusal rate of 98.5%. Our human baseline experiments (n = 180 participants) suggest that humans exhibit compliance rates comparable to AI agents but over-refuse at much higher rates.
  </details>

- **[A Layer-wise Study of How Style Rewriting Breaks the Calibration of Zero-Shot LLM Fake-News Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11239)**  `arXiv:2611.11239`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Prabhjot Singh, Praney Goyal, Raksha Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly asked to judge whether a news article is true, and their stated confidence is often taken at face value. We test what happens to that confidence when an article's writing style, but not its facts, is manipulated, as in recent \emph{style attacks} on fake-news detectors. Tracing how the prediction and its confidence form layer by layer inside the network, we study three model families (LLaMA-3-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B) on three fake-news benchmarks, and find the same three patterns in every model--dataset pairing. First, on the original articles the models are competent and largely honest judges: they settle on an answer, briefly become overconfident, and then correct their confidence in the final layers. Second, style-rewritten articles break this self-correction: the models become far less accurate, under the strongest attack, worse than random guessing, yet remain just as confident, so they are \emph{confidently wrong} precisely when a warning is most needed. Third, nearly all of the damage comes from rewriting into sensational or emotionally charged styles, while sober, neutral rewrites leave the models' honesty largely intact. The practical implication is that an LLM's confidence about veracity cannot be trusted as a safety signal when an article's style may have been manipulated.
  </details>

- **[KeyASRBench: Measuring Whole-Phrase Keyword Preservation in Real-World ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10852)**  `arXiv:2611.10852`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Guanzheng Wei, Ziyi Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Public automatic speech recognition (ASR) benchmarks increasingly report low sequence error rates, yet production systems still use hotword lists, contextual biasing, retrieval, and post-correction. This motivates a complementary question: does a transcript preserve selected names, products, technical terms, versions, and relational phrases? We present KeyASRBench, a bilingual pilot that operationalizes occurrence-based Keyword Error Rate (KER) over frozen whole-phrase annotations. The 10-hour model-conditioned challenge set contains 1,501 Chinese and English clips with 4,723 keyword occurrences. Across five locally inferred checkpoints and two official API systems, language-specific MER/WER ranges from 5.31% to 12.91%, while KER ranges from 13.61% to 33.81%. A disjoint 30-minute model-blind control yields KER of 10.65%-27.31%. The control confirms that selected-phrase errors extend beyond challenge-set selection.
  </details>

- **[AFVE-Bench: Measuring Abandoned-Frame Verbalization Errors in Multi-Turn LLM Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10541)**  `arXiv:2611.10541`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaming Chu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn revision often asks a model to replace an earlier organizing frame while preserving still-valid facts and artifact requirements. Existing instruction-following evaluations mainly assess the latest target and may therefore score an artifact as successful even when it adopts the new frame but exposes the superseded one. We call this failure an abandoned-frame verbalization error (AFVE). AFVE-Bench evaluates silent frame replacement in English and Chinese using 192 matched four-turn dialogues across eight writing domains. Each seed has zero, one, or two pre-target user requests to generate under the source frame. AFVE eligibility depends on the user-request history and applies to the one- and two-request conditions; the zero-request condition serves as a frame-bearing baseline. Eleven model configurations yielded 4,224 expected post-target response units. Two annotators independently scored 4,204 scorable responses for target adoption, source-frame verbalization, and direct usability; model and condition metadata were hidden, and disagreements were adjudicated. AFVE occurs in 25.54% (715/2,799) of history-conditioned responses. Of 2,388 target-passing history-conditioned responses, 472 (19.77%) still expose the abandoned source frame, most often through contrastive framing (423/472). For these standalone revision tasks, target adoption does not ensure source-frame silence.
  </details>

- **[Coding or Cheating? An Investigation of Frontier Coding LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10404)**  `arXiv:2611.10404`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuntao Gui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier agentic LLMs are trained on verifiable rewards in reinforcement learning (RL) environments built from real software repositories. A passing test suite is treated as the reward for solving the task. We show that, on hard problems, frontier models often satisfy these rewards by obtaining the answer instead of deriving it, known as reward hacking. Prior reports document this behavior on public benchmarks. We test its persistence on unseen problems.In a hardened environment where the answer cannot leak, nearly every model we tested attempted to recover the answer, escalating through tiers of increasingly specialized exploitation: mining the repository history for the upstream fix, inspecting the git object store directly, and sweeping the filesystem for a possible newer copy of the source or hidden tests. These attempts were concentrated on the hardest problems, and the models differed in whether, how often, and how deeply they escalated. Our position is that this behavior is a learned shortcut, acquired in RL environments that leak the answer, not a sign of intelligence. We release agent traces for these models to support further study atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Jais 3: Introducing Arabic-centric Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10224)**  `arXiv:2611.10224`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Team IFM, Haonan Li, Hadi Abdine, Nikhil Ranjan, Mohamed Anwar, Ahmad Chamma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Jais 3, a fleet of six open, Arabic-centric agentic language models ranging from 0.9B to 375B parameters. Jais 3 is built to complete tasks for Arabic speakers, not only to answer questions in plain text: it is trained to plan multi-step tasks, call tools and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, operate a terminal, search the web, and read, edit, and produce PDF, spreadsheet, and presentation files, while communicating with the user in Arabic. Instead of pretraining from scratch, we adapt each model of the fully open K2 Horizon fleet to Arabic through continual pretraining, followed by supervised fine-tuning (SFT), final token preference optimization (FTPO), and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We combine natural Arabic text with translated, transliterated, and synthetic Arabic, and replay the base model's own training data to keep its reasoning, agentic, and English abilities. Jais 3 covers Modern Standard Arabic and the major regional dialects, follows instructions with Arabic-specific constraints, and inherits the 512K-token context window of K2 Horizon (all models except the 0.9B). To our knowledge, its 375B model is the largest open Arabic-centric language model to date. For evaluation, we revisit how Arabic models are assessed, moving to fully generative evaluation on audited Arabic benchmarks. Across model sizes, Jais 3 matches or outperforms open-weight models of comparable size on Arabic and agentic benchmarks, with particularly strong agentic capabilities and Arabic instruction following, and far fewer hallucinations. We release the weights and the checkpoints from every training stage of all six models, together with samples of the training data, the data recipes and training configurations, the training logs, the audited benchmarks, and the evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[Where Did the Evidence Go? Diagnosing Proof-Set Loss in Long-Term Conversational Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10170)**  `arXiv:2611.10170`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bogdan Roshchupkin, Maria Khodorchenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term conversational question answering can require several separated dialogue turns that support an answer only together. We introduce EviCover, a retrieval approach designed to retain this complementary evidence. It converts questions into observable evidence requirements, checks requirement coverage using retrieved source identifiers, and selects turns jointly. We track annotated support sets - the benchmark-labeled dialogue turns associated with each question - through retrieval, reranking, and final answer-generation context. We evaluate the baseline memory pipeline on generated, human-verified LoCoMo conversations and human-written REALTALK conversations, and examine EviCover on REALTALK's 108 multi-hop questions. Compared with the hybrid lexical/dense baseline, support-turn recall among 60 candidate turns rises from 0.526 to 0.575, and recall in the final ten-turn context rises from 0.349 to 0.379. The evidence funnel identifies separate discovery and selection failures, while paired answer evaluation shows that greater evidence recall does not yet establish a correctness gain. This analysis locates where complementary evidence is lost and distinguishes evidence retrieval from its use in answer generation.
  </details>

- **[CulturalCrosswords: A Multilingual Benchmark comprised of Answer Constraints and Grid Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10044)**  `arXiv:2611.10044`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Isaac Saxton-Knight, Gabriel Saxton-Knight, Diego Oliveira, Tidor-Vlad Pricope, Aston Garratt, Jocelyn D'Arcy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When several answers fit a riddle, exact-match scoring can penalise a valid response. CulturalCrosswords tests how answer constraints affect large language models (LLMs) solving cultural riddles, whose solutions draw on knowledge specific to a community. We constructed 53 crosswords from crowdsourced riddles in 42 languages and evaluated 11 LLM configurations on five versions of each clue set: plain batches, batches with lengths, batches with lengths and partial-answer hints, and crosswords requesting an answer list or a completed grid. On 962 clues with alternative answers, lengths improve agreement with the reference by 22.8 percentage points, compared with 8.4 points when accepted alternatives also receive credit. Part of the gain therefore appears to come from selecting a required answer form. Crossword input increases the share of all crossings with compatible answers by 9.7 points; requiring a completed grid reveals large differences between models. The benchmark distinguishes agreement with a reference from the ability to produce answers that fit together and occupy the correct cells.
  </details>

- **[MedToolFault and MedGuard: evaluating and improving clinical agent reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09942)**  `arXiv:2611.09942`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zaifu Zhan, Min Zeng, Sicheng Zhou, Xiaoyi Chen, Kai Yu, Meijia Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical agents must retrieve patient-specific evidence and correctly update records despite unreliable tool responses. We introduce MedToolFault, a fault-injection benchmark linking six tool-failure classes to answer correctness, required state changes and forbidden mutations in public clinical-agent tasks. We also introduce MedGuard, a tool-layer method that verifies read evidence and attributes ambiguous writes through public interfaces. Across 127,861 formal episode evaluations, we assess fault recovery, mechanism controls, task expansion, constrained tool budgets and external question answering. Across nine model configurations, unguarded agents fail in 32.2-67.8% of fault-injected evaluations on tasks each configuration solves without faults. MedGuard improves paired safe success by 13.7-46.6 percentage points on these tasks.
  </details>

- **[DramaGrow: A Regime-Aware Benchmark for Auditing LLM Judges of Multi-Episode Drama](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09938)**  `arXiv:2611.09938`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hui Zhang, Yibo Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs can now draft complete multi-episode dramas, and LLM judges are increasingly used to evaluate them. Assessing the reliability of these judges is difficult because drama quality encompasses specification-backed errors, reproducible preferences, and legitimate disagreement among human raters. Most existing evaluations reduce these cases to a single score from one judge call and leave tie calibration, call-to-call variation, and the vote margin underlying the human reference unreported. We introduce DramaGrow, a regime-aware benchmark and audit protocol for AI-generated Chinese five-episode drama. The benchmark assigns verifiable constraint violations, four-of-five preferences, and within-panel disagreements to separate denominators and evidence rules. It contains 800 episodes, 120 byte-preserving interventions, and 1,155 blinded five-rater records, including a frozen extension with 114 supermajority pairs and 21 disagreement pairs. On the frozen sets of 114 preference pairs and 35 constraint interventions, the repeated absolute-score rubrics yield overlapping performance, with mean accuracies of 53.8-55.8% and three-call agreement of 29.8-32.5%, and their accuracy falls from 71.6-71.9% to 34.8-37.1% as the tie band widens. DirectPair-GLM reaches 85.1% preference accuracy, 89.5% constraint F1, and 81.9% Detect+Locate on the same items, and exact subpanel enumeration identifies four-of-five as the smallest stable human vote margin. Evaluation protocol, tie calibration, judge-call variation, and panel margin therefore affect performance as much as rubric design. We will release the specification packages, scripts, interventions, anonymized human votes and rationales, frozen judge runs, and analysis code.
  </details>

- **[How Many Examples, and Which Ones? A Study of In-Context Learning for Natural Language Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09936)**  `arXiv:2611.09936`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Georgos Dikaios, Isidoros Perikos, Ioannis Hatzilygeroudis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language inference (NLI) is a fundamental task of natural language processing and crucial for evaluating the strengths and weaknesses of AI methods. Research has mostly focused on encoder or encoder-decoder architectures. However, the field of decoder-only Large Language Models (LLMs) remains underexplored. While prior work has mainly investigated complex sampling and prompting techniques, the effectiveness of a minimalistic prompting approach is yet to be examined in decoder-only LLMs. In this work, we examine and provide a comprehensive benchmark of LLMs (Llama, Mistral, Gemma, and Phi 4) in this domain, across the MNLI, QNLI and SciTail datasets. The performance of these models was evaluated using zero-shot and few-shot prompting strategies with simple and direct prompts. The results of the article indicate that LLMs generally perform exceptionally well when they are provided with a simple, straightforward prompt, challenging the notion that complex prompting techniques are always required for NLI tasks. Additionally, we find that the choice of demonstration label in one-shot prompting significantly affects performance for most models, with entailment-primed examples performing best. Our findings also show that the use of a few representative examples, in the case of few-shot, produced performance comparable or superior to prompts with higher example counts, although the efficacy of the prompting techniques is model and dataset-dependent. Moreover, our simple prompting approach consistently matched or outperformed more elaborate methods such as ICS and MCQ-based prompting.
  </details>

- **[One Assistant, Many Memories: Benchmarking Real-World Group Assistance When Recall Is Not Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04867)**  `arXiv:2611.04867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhanghao Hu, Linhai Zhang, Qian Zhao, Qi Zhu, Yuan Hua, Di Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The value of memory lies not only in recall but in using it to provide appropriate assistance. Recent benchmarks test memory use in personalisation and agentic tasks, but seldom evaluate assistance without fixed answers or integration across multiple participants. Group chats make both challenges more acute: an assistant must integrate information from multiple members, track revisions and conflicts over time, and produce one response for the group. Yet existing group memory benchmarks rely on synthetic conversations and inherit constrained evaluation from general memory benchmarks, focusing on recall against fixed answers or decisions over a closed set. We introduce GroupAssistBench, the first memory benchmark from real-world group conversations with a deployed group assistant, covering 80,299 messages in 50 groups and costing over $53K. Over the same histories, GroupAssistBench evaluates memory recall (L1) with reference answers and memory use (L2) with service requests without fixed answers across single, distributed, and conflicting memories (M1--M3). Requests are verified to require group history, memories are anchored to their sources, and L2 uses tailored rubrics with penalties derived from real user feedback. Across evaluations of frontier models with full context, retrieval methods, and memory systems, the strongest memory system rivals full context on recall but falls far behind on use, especially for distributed and conflicting memories. Together, two additions that relate retrieved memories and bind remembered facts as response constraints bring consistent gains, showing that group memory systems must improve use, not recall alone.
  </details>

- **[DynEdit: Dynamic Entropy-Guided Sequential Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34768)**  `arXiv:2610.34768`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinhu Fu, Yan Bai, Yihang Lou, Li Sun, Jiahong Wu, Xiangxiang Chu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge editing aims to update outdated or incorrect knowledge in large language models without full retraining. Existing parameter-editing methods remain limited for long-sequence knowledge: single-point editors provide insufficient coverage for extended targets, while fixed chunk-based editors distribute editing effort uniformly across rigid predefined windows, failing to prioritize difficult, information-dense tokens. In this work, we show that token entropy is closely associated with both editing difficulty and semantic importance: high-entropy regions are harder to edit and often contain key factual content. Motivated by this observation, we propose DynEdit, a dynamic framework for long-sequence knowledge editing. DynEdit uses token-level entropy to guide non-uniform sliding, increasing the coverage of difficult high-entropy regions, and further applies entropy-guided token-level optimization to strengthen updates on information-dense tokens within each editing window. Experiments on three benchmarks and two backbone models show that DynEdit consistently outperforms competitive baselines across diverse long-form editing settings, achieving gains of up to +14.0 BLEU points on challenging paraphrase queries and near-saturated semantic scores on several diverse knowledge domains.
  </details>

- **[Polarity Blindness in LLM Safety: A Systematic Study of Negation, Antonym Substitution, and Adversarial Framing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27908)**  `arXiv:2610.27908`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Diego Miguel Lozano, Erik Derner, Juan Antonio Pérez-Ortiz, Nuria Oliver_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Negation remains a persistent challenge for language models across NLP tasks and modalities. We show that this weakness can undermine LLM safety guardrails through a structural vulnerability we term polarity blindness: the failure to recognize harmful intent after polarity-altering linguistic transformations. We study two forms of polarity inversion: grammatical negation and antonym replacement, and use synonym replacement as a polarity-preserving control. We further test whether an adversarial textual wrapper amplifies these failures. Using a 260-prompt benchmark across 10 state-of-the-art LLMs and 3 languages (English, Spanish, Czech), stratified by harmfulness potential and evaluated on a validated 0-5 harm scale, we find that polarity blindness affects most evaluated models, with mean harm scores reaching 4.12 for the most vulnerable model. The effect persists across typologically differing negation systems, suggesting that polarity blindness reflects a broader limitation in semantic robustness rather than a language- or grammar-specific failure.
  </details>

- **[Staying In Character: Role-Playing Agents with Perspective-Bounded Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25632)**  `arXiv:2606.25632`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xushuo Tang, Junhe Zhang, Zihan Yang, Yifu Tang, Sichao Li, Longbin Lai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent LLM role-playing systems build character agents from novels by extracting characters, scenes, and relations. Yet long-narrative role playing suffers from two failures: Factual Overreach, where shared retrieval or parametric memory lets a character use facts outside its perspective, and Stylistic Monotony, where profile descriptions flatten a character into a fixed voice. To address these failures, we propose ReverieMem, a three-layer memory architecture for book-based character agents. The episodic layer stores first-person scene memories; the semantic layer stores visibility-tagged facts; and the personality layer stores situation-dependent speech and behaviour patterns. We evaluate ReverieMem and six reference methods on KBF-QA, a new benchmark of 4,386 questions across eight novels that assesses character knowledge boundaries. ReverieMem improves Knowledge Boundary Fidelity by 34.6 percentage points over the strongest reference method. On BookWorld's five-dimension pairwise narrative protocol, ReverieMem achieves a mean win rate of 78.86% against BookWorld across five generator LLMs and two generation modes, suggesting that perspective-bounded memory improves both boundary fidelity and character-grounded narrative generation.
  </details>

- **[Group of Skills: Group-Structured Skill Retrieval for Agent Skill Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06978)**  `arXiv:2605.06978`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Kun Zeng, Yu Huo, Siyu Zhang, Haoyue Liu, Yuecheng Zhuo, Zi Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill-augmented agents increasingly rely on large reusable skill libraries, but retrieving relevant skills is not the same as presenting usable context. Existing methods typically return atomic skills or dependency-aware bundles whose internal roles remain implicit, leaving the agent to infer the execution entry point, support skills, visible requirements, and failure-avoidance guidance. We introduce Group of Skills (GoSkills), an inference-time group-structured retrieval method that changes the agent-facing retrieval object from a flat skill list to a compact, role-labeled execution context. GoSkills builds anchor-centered skill groups from a typed skill graph, expands support groups through a group graph, bottlenecks the selected group plan into a bounded set of atomic skill payloads, and renders a fixed execution contract with Start, Support, Check, and Avoid fields, without changing the downstream agent, skill payloads, or execution environment. Across SkillsBench, ALFWorld, and Terminal-Bench 2 with six backbones, GoSkills attains the highest mean reward or accuracy among the evaluated methods in every benchmark-backbone setting, and matched controls show higher reward and fewer agent turns with group selection than with fixed-contract or post-hoc role-annotation alternatives.
  </details>

- **[ImplicitBBQ: Benchmarking Implicit Bias in Large Language Models through Characteristic Based Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01925)**  `arXiv:2604.01925`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bhaskara Hanuma Vedula, Darshan Anghan, Ishita Goyal, Ponnurangam Kumaraguru, Abhijnan Chakraborty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models increasingly suppress biased outputs when demographic identity is stated explicitly, yet may still exhibit implicit biases when identity is conveyed indirectly. Existing benchmarks use name based proxies to detect implicit biases, which carry weak associations with many social demographics and cannot extend to dimensions like age or socioeconomic status. We introduce ImplicitBBQ, a QA benchmark that evaluates implicit bias through characteristic based cues, demographically associated attributes that signal implicitly, across age, gender, region, religion, caste, and socioeconomic status. Evaluating 11 models, we find that implicit bias in ambiguous contexts is over six times higher than explicit bias in open weight models. Notably, this bias is distributed unevenly across demographics: caste emerges as the most severe while gender is the least affected. Safety prompting and chain-of-thought reasoning fail to substantially close this gap; even few-shot prompting, which reduces implicit bias by 79%, leaves caste bias at four times the level of any other dimension. These findings indicate that current alignment and prompting strategies address the surface of bias evaluation while leaving demographically associated stereotypic associations largely unresolved. We publicly release our code and dataset for model providers and researchers to benchmark potential mitigation techniques.
  </details>

- **[BFS-PO: Best-First Search for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14917)**  `arXiv:2602.14917`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Wenhui Tan, Enver Sangineto, Xiaoyi Yu, Ruihua Song, Rita Cucchiara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have shown excellent performance in reasoning tasks using long Chains of Thought (CoTs). However, this has also led to a significant increase of computational costs and the generation of verbose output, a phenomenon known as overthinking. The tendency to overthink is often exacerbated by Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms such as GRPO/DAPO. In this paper, we propose BFS-PO, an RL algorithm which alleviates this problem using a Best-First Search exploration strategy. Specifically, the reasoning chains generated at training time by BFS-PO are organized into a search tree, within which the shortest correct sequence is selected as the best and expanded using a backtracking mechanism based on maximum entropy nodes. In this way, we bias the exploration of the solution space towards the search for increasingly shorter solutions, training the LRM to generate progressively more concise answers. Using different benchmarks and base LRMs, we show that BFS-PO can simultaneously increase the LRM accuracy and shorten its reasoning chains.
  </details>

- **[DialectLLM: A Dialect-Aware Dialog[ue] Generation Framework Beyond Standard American English](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22888)**  `arXiv:2601.22888`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jio Oh, Paul Vicinanza, Thomas Butler, Steven Euijong Whang, Dezhi Hong, Amani Namboori_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  More than 80% of the 1.6B English speakers do not use Standard American English (SAE), yet LLMs often fail to correctly identify non-SAE dialects and generate stereotyped responses for their speakers. We introduce DialectLLM, the first large-scale framework for generating high-quality multi-dialectal conversational data encompassing the three pillars of written dialect---lexical (vocabulary), orthographic (spelling), and morphosyntactic (grammar) features. DialectLLM produces a dialect-parallel dialog dataset spanning nine English dialects. Partnering with native linguists, we design and validate SAE-to-dialect transformation rules, ensuring authenticity. Our approach challenges the prevailing practice of applying a single morphosyntactic feature set to both user utterances and model responses, showing that models should not reproduce up to 90% of the grammatical features of a dialect. Human evaluation confirms data quality, with annotators preferring DialectLLM over prior methods in 98.8% of pairwise comparisons for dialect naturalness. We then construct DialectLLM-Bench, a dialect-parallel benchmark with 50k+ dialogs, resulting in 97k+ QA pairs, and evaluate 17 LLMs on dialect identification and response generation tasks. Even frontier models achieve around 70% accuracy, fail to reach 40% for prominent dialects like Canadian English, and systematically misclassify non-SAE dialects as American or British. Beyond benchmarking, we show that DialectLLM data also serve as a scalable LLM post-training resource, suggesting a practical path toward dialect-aware conversational AI.
  </details>

- **[InFi-Check: Interpretable and Fine-Grained Fact-Checking of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06666)**  `arXiv:2601.06666`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuzhuo Bai, Shuzheng Si, Kangyang Luo, Qingyi Wang, Wenhao Li, Gang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often hallucinate, yet most existing fact-checking methods treat factuality evaluation as a binary classification problem, offering limited interpretability and failing to capture fine-grained error types. In this paper, we introduce InFi-Check, a framework for interpretable and fine-grained fact-checking of LLM outputs. Specifically, we first propose a controlled data synthesis pipeline that generates high-quality data featuring explicit evidence, fine-grained error type labels, justifications, and corrections. Based on this, we further construct large-scale training data and a manually verified benchmark InFi-Check-FG for fine-grained fact-checking of LLM outputs. Building on these high-quality training data, we further propose InFi-Checker, which can jointly provide supporting evidence, classify fine-grained error types, and produce justifications along with corrections. Experiments show that InFi-Checker achieves state-of-the-art performance on InFi-Check-FG and strong generalization across various downstream tasks, significantly improving the utility and trustworthiness of factuality evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Feedback Forensics: A Toolkit to Measure AI Personality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26305)**  `arXiv:2509.26305`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Arduin Findeis, Timo Kaufmann, Eyke Hüllermeier, Robert Mullins_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Some traits making a "good" AI model are hard to describe upfront. For example, should responses be more polite or more casual? Such traits are sometimes summarized as model character or personality. Without a clear objective, conventional benchmarks based on automatic checks struggle to measure such traits. Evaluation methods using human feedback such as Chatbot Arena have emerged as a popular alternative. These methods infer "better" personality and other desirable traits implicitly by ranking multiple model responses relative to each other. Recent issues with model releases highlight limitations of these existing opaque evaluation approaches: goblin-obsessed, sycophantic and generally over-the-top personalities have resulted in model rollbacks and extensive media coverage. Despite these known issues, limited public tooling exists to explicitly evaluate model personality. We introduce Feedback Forensics: an open-source toolkit to track AI personality and related behavioural traits, both those encouraged by human (or AI) feedback, and those exhibited across AI models trained and evaluated on such feedback. Feedback Forensics is adaptive and can automatically find new traits appearing in models. We demonstrate the toolkit's usefulness in two steps: (A) first we analyse the personality traits encouraged in popular human feedback datasets including MultiPref, PRISM and data from Chatbot Arena; and (B) then use our toolkit to analyse how much popular models exhibit such traits. We release (1) our Feedback Forensics toolkit alongside (2) a web app tracking AI personality in popular models and feedback datasets and (3) the underlying annotation data, seethis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sentinel: Decoding Context Utilization via Attention Probing for Efficient LLM Context Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23277)**  `arXiv:2505.23277`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yong Zhang, Heng Li, Yanwen Huang, Ning Cheng, Yang Guo, Yun Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieved contexts are often long, noisy, and redundant, motivating query-aware compression before answer generation. Native attention provides signals for context selection, but heterogeneous head behaviors complicate their direct aggregation into sentence-utility scores. We introduce Sentinel, a sentence-level compressor that learns to combine these signals through a signed linear readout across layers and heads. Only the readout is trained using existing QA evidence annotations, while the backbone remains frozen and sentence scoring requires no autoregressive generation. Across twelve LongBench tasks, Sentinel with a 0.5B backbone outperforms Full Context on multiple tasks under a 2,000-token budget with each of three readers: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. Despite English-only QA supervision, the same readout transfers to Chinese tasks without task-specific retraining. Length-stratified profiling further shows lower compression latency and GPU memory usage than LLMLingua-2 and CPC.
  </details>

- **[How Well Does AI Peer Review Work? A Benchmark Built from 100 Planted Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12820)**  `arXiv:2611.12820`  `cs.CL`  
  _Paul Litvak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One hundred known errors were planted into 10 open-access psychology papers, and the modified papers were then run through frontier models and two commercial AI review tools. The best single system caught 71 of the 100 errors, while the worst caught 30. Pooling every system's output caught 93. Models are only partly correlated in the errors they find, which makes ensembling a big lever for finding issues in papers. Seven errors could not be caught by any system, and all seven were omissions: information deleted from a paper rather than mistakes inserted into it.this http URLcontributes more unique catches than any other single system, though it is expensive on a per-error basis. False positives were not measured, and how this error distribution compares to the distribution of errors in real papers remains unknown. The papers, errors, model outputs, and full experiment log are public, so that others can build on this toward a comprehensive evaluation benchmark across disciplines.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models as a Universal Searcher for Open-World Automated Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12791)**  `arXiv:2611.12791`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuxuan Hu, Jihao Liu, Weikang Shi, Xijie Huang, Linda Wei, Mingjie Zhan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
