  Developing effective machine learning models requires complex manual tuning, while current Automated Machine Learning (AutoML) frameworks remain domain-specific and isolate model architecture, training hyperparameter, and data ratio optimization. Here, we bridge this gap by proposing a universal, large language model (LLM)-based AutoML framework that jointly optimizes these configurations for the first time without the need for task-specific fine-tuning. We propose NCode, a unified numerical encoding scheme that translates diverse AutoML design choices into universal discrete sequences and effectively reframes the optimization process as a unified ranking task. Trained solely on optimization trajectories derived from a limited, single-domain vision task, the proposed LLM-based searcher demonstrates impressive cross-task, cross-modality and joint search-space optimization generalizability, achieving state-of-the-art performance across image classification, image segmentation, speaker verification and text-to-image benchmarks. Crucially, it exhibits superior scalability in the large-scale training and joint optimization of a billion-parameter Diffusion Transformer, requiring 896 GPU-days and attaining a state-of-the-art GenEval score of 0.91. By leveraging reinforcement learning to instill Chain-of-Thought reasoning, we provide a systematic way to interpret the LLM-based searcher's internal decision-making mechanisms, revealing its optimization strategy shifted from the early-stage diversity-driven exploration to the late-stage anchor exploitation. Our proposed framework provides a scalable, generalizable, and interpretable pathway toward general-purpose open-world machine learning optimization.
  </details>

- **[A Lattice-Based HMM Approach to Aruz Scansion of Ottoman Poetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12691)**  `arXiv:2611.12691`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mehmet Emre Tiryaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ottoman Divan poetry is composed in aruz, a quantitative meter based on the weights of syllables. The meter is sensitive to specific characters recorded in the original writing in the Arabic script. This work is concerned with automatic meter finding and scansion alignment for Ottoman Divan poetry, especially on modern Turkish spelling without the special diacritics that scholarly transliterations of Ottoman preserve. We define two tasks on this input, first identifying the meter used in the poem, and second aligning the meter's weights with the syllabification of the poem, identifying the flaws and freedoms the poet has taken along the way. We build a corpus of 52,000 lines split by poet, with scansion annotations for 500 test lines, and a robustness benchmark generated at the deviation rates measured on real web and OCR copies. Our model is a hidden Markov model over the positions of each meter, decoded over a lattice of every candidate path, and trained by EM from the printed meter, without scansion labels. On a held-out set it scans 77.6% of the test lines fully correctly, against 26.8% for the available rule-based scanner, 55.0% for a neural tagger trained with the same supervision and 63.6% for Claude Opus 5.5 few-shot. The code is open-sourced and the tool is released on the web.
  </details>

- **[Path-Level Structural and Semantic Alignment for Hierarchical Multi-Label Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12420)**  `arXiv:2611.12420`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xindi Wang, Aifen Xiong, Baiyang Wang, Xuri Ge, Robert Mercer, Frank Rudzicz, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical multi-label text classification (HMTC) requires models to capture both structural dependencies among labels and the fine-grained semantics of labels at different hierarchy levels. Existing methods often model hierarchy through prediction constraints or representations of individual labels and local relations. Structural and semantic supervision, however, are usually formulated separately. We instead use complete root-to-node label paths as a shared unit for both. Our framework introduces two auxiliary training objectives while retaining a standard multi-label classifier at inference. PathSimNCE aligns document representations with hierarchical paths using hierarchy-aware contrastive targets. These targets provide graded supervision for structurally related paths while preserving explicit supervision for the ground-truth paths. The complementary semantic objective uses LLM-generated, document-conditioned descriptions of the same positive paths. It thereby provides context-specific label semantics beyond static label representations. Experiments on three benchmarks show consistent improvements: structural alignment is particularly beneficial in deeper hierarchies, while semantic alignment provides additional gains for fine-grained labels.
  </details>

- **[BDBBQ: A Human-AI Collaborative Benchmark for Bengali Social Bias in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12407)**  `arXiv:2611.12407`  `cs.CL`  
  _Provat Saha, Ashika Habib Khan, Tahseen Tanha, Hasin Ishrak, Muhammad Iqbal Hossain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite growing attention to social bias in large language models (LLMs), existing bias benchmarks provide limited coverage of culturally grounded evaluation in Bengali, particularly within Bangladesh's sociocultural context. To address this gap, we develop a bilingual extension of BanglaBBQ comprising 61,312 question-answering instances across 12 social categories in English and Bengali. The benchmark incorporates balanced answer permutations, contrasting question framings, and both ambiguous and disambiguated contexts to systematically examine model performance and stereotype-aligned bias across languages. We validate the dataset through structural checks, 220 statistical tests, and expert annotation of 150 instances by four annotators, achieving strong inter-annotator agreement (Fleiss' \k{appa}=0.8349). We evaluate eight LLMs across 490,496 instances and identify five key findings. First, disambiguated contexts yield substantially greater accuracy improvements in Bengali (+36.59 percentage points) than in English (+17.71). Second, all evaluated models exhibit lower accuracy in Bengali, although Mistral-Nemo shows the smallest cross-lingual accuracy gap (5.12 percentage points). Third, stereotype-aligned bias exhibits language and model dependent variation, increasing in Bengali for Gemma 2 9B but decreasing for Mistral-Nemo. Fourth, higher accuracy does not necessarily correspond to lower stereotype-aligned bias. Finally, bias varies considerably across social categories, with Qwen 2.5 7B exhibiting stronger stereotype alignment in Bengali for Politics and Physical Appearance. These findings demonstrate that cross-lingual performance and social bias are distinct dimensions of LLM behavior and underscore the importance of culturally grounded, language-sensitive benchmarks for evaluating fairness beyond English-centric settings.
  </details>

- **[More Than Just Style: Benchmarking Personality Influence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12392)**  `arXiv:2611.12392`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhao Liu, Mengyu Wang, Xiao Chen, Lu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In addition to the effects of explicit input prompts and instructions, large language model outputs are influenced by underlying behavioral preferences similar to human personality tendencies. These tendencies can affect how models interpret situations and make decisions. However, their influence is difficult to disentangle from the effects of model capabilities and instructions. Therefore, we introduce SPACE, a benchmark framework combining a controlled evaluation protocol with complementary datasets for personality verification, capability assessment, and hierarchical situational tasks. Controlled comparisons across guidance conditions help distinguish personality influence from measured capability changes and immediate instruction effects. Experimental results demonstrate that although prompting can shift generation style, models' internal personality tendencies are persistent and not readily overridden by opposing instructions. Furthermore, these underlying tendencies have an observable influence on LLM task performance, highlighting the need for greater attention to these characteristics and potential applications. Training code and SPACE benchmark specifications are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MNN-Pocket-0.3B Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12311)**  `arXiv:2611.12311`  `cs.CL`  
  _MNN Team_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This technical report presents MNN-Pocket-0.3B, a compact 300M-parameter dense language model designed for efficient on-device deployment and supporting context lengths of up to 32K tokens. With an INT4 deployment footprint of only 173 MB and decoding throughput of up to 466 tokens/s on iPad Pro M5 (Metal), it can be seamlessly integrated into applications on edge devices such as smartphones and smart glasses. MNN-Pocket-0.3B is developed through the co-design of its architecture, data, training pipeline, and inference system. Hardware-aware architecture selection and dependency-aware vocabulary pruning balance model capability with on-device memory and compute constraints. A four-stage pre-training pipeline covering approximately 4.1 trillion high-quality Chinese and English tokens equips the model with broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities. This is followed by a three-stage post-training pipeline comprising instruction fine-tuning, multi-teacher on-policy distillation, and quantization-aware training. Finally, hardware-specific optimizations in the MNN inference engine enable fast, memory-efficient inference and long-context processing. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that MNN-Pocket-0.3B achieves frontier-level performance among models of comparable size, with particularly strong results in Chinese and English knowledge and reasoning, mathematics, coding, and instruction following. It consistently outperforms similarly sized open-source models and even surpasses several 0.5B-parameter models on instruction-following benchmarks. We release MNN-Pocket-0.3B with open weights and ready-to-use MNN deployment packages. We hope it will provide a practical foundation for capable, responsive, and memory-efficient edge applications while advancing research and development in on-device language models.
  </details>

- **[MIRIX-2.0: Multi-Agent Memory System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12068)**  `arXiv:2611.12068`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wei-Chieh Huang, Weizhi Zhang, Jiayan Nan, Peng Xia, Yu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory lets LLM agents personalize, stay consistent, and reuse what worked before. Parametric memories need training, consolidation can lose details for good, and keeping every record leaves a growing store to search. We present MIRIX-2.0, a multi-agent memory system designed to be practical, accurate, and efficient at once. It is the most accurate of the compared systems on four of five QA benchmarks. It reaches 91.4% on LoCoMo and 72.6% on LongMemEval-S, where it leads the strongest baseline by 14.3 points, and with AutoDream disabled it gains 31.0 and 22.0 points over HyperMem on single-hop and multi-hop document QA. As procedural memory, it yields the highest mean success among the compared memory methods on ALFWorld and WebShop and reaches 49.2% on MetaClaw-Bench, against 44.0% for MetaClaw's skill library. Six typed memory agents keep complete records as the system of record, and a provenance hypergraph indexes episodic and semantic records with n-ary frames that cite their rows. Retrieval runs under explicit budgets, so the evidence a question inspects does not grow with the history. AutoDream merges duplicate anchors outside the write path without rewriting any record, and it distills task sessions into skills. With AutoDream disabled, MIRIX-2.0 answers faster than HyperMem on all five timed benchmarks. It needs no fine-tuning, runs with closed and open models, and its code is public.
  </details>

- **[SMO: Compiling Natural Language Skills into Graphs of Reusable State-Move-Outcome Bricks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12041)**  `arXiv:2611.12041`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chao Chen, Jiashuo Zhang, Yunhang Shen, Liangyu Lian, Rongrong Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly rely on reusable natural-language skills, yet growing skill collections remain accumulations of isolated text artifacts that are difficult to compare, deduplicate, and compose. We argue that the reusable content of a skill lies not in its surface wording but in its procedural transitions: which operation to execute under a given state and which observable outcome to produce. Based on this observation, we propose State-Move-Outcome (SMO), a compilation-like framework that decomposes raw skills into reusable procedural bricks and organizes admitted bricks as an externalized symbolic structure. When compiling a skill, SMO first retrieves and composes existing bricks, dynamically connects them through Outcome-to-State conditions, and creates a candidate brick only for an uncovered procedure; the candidate becomes reusable only after validation and admission. Experiments on SkillsBench v1.1 demonstrate that SMO represents 232 rawSkills from 87 underlying tasks with 95 shared bricks, reducing the corresponding skill representations from 366,472 to 149,154 tokens--a $\mathbf{59.3}\times$ reduction and $\mathbf{2.46}\times$ compression. Across 232 skill-conditioned evaluation instances, SMO raises the average task score from $\mathbf{0.6379}$ to $\mathbf{0.7478}$ and increases 5/5 Pass instances from $\mathbf{138}$ to $\mathbf{164}$, while matching or outperforming rawSkill on $\mathbf{208}$ of $\mathbf{232}$ instances. It also reduces average runtime token consumption by $\mathbf{38.38\%}$. These results show that compiling natural-language skills into shared procedural units can jointly improve skill reuse, representation efficiency, and downstream execution.
  </details>

- **[Robust Domain-Mixture Scheduling for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12012)**  `arXiv:2611.12012`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yankai Chen, Sida Lin, Yifan Li, Hanjun Luo, Hanrong Zhang, Zhuohan Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) merges the capabilities of multiple domain-expert teachers, such as mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction following, into a single compact student. Since all domains share a limited training budget, how to allocate it across domains directly determines the quality of distillation. Existing methods allocate the budget by each domain's learning progress, but offer no guarantee for lagging domains. We therefore introduce distributionally robust optimization (DRO) into MOPD to prioritize the worst-performing domain. However, the per-domain training signals are estimated from a few rollouts and are inherently noisy, so directly applying DRO mistakes noise for the worst case, lets occasional spikes hijack the budget, and protects inherently hard domains rather than truly regressing ones. To address these issues, we propose RDS-MOPD, which keeps the DRO objective and redesigns its three parts: it shifts the allocation only when differences clearly exceed the noise, bounds the influence of any single anomaly, and defines the worst case as the regression from each domain's own best level, all with theoretical guarantees. Experiments show that RDS-MOPD outperforms recent competing methods and ranks first on most mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Entropy-Sparing for Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11901)**  `arXiv:2611.11901`  `cs.CL`  
  _Cong Jiang, Xiaofeng Zhang, Tom Ko, Zheng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) produce explicit chains of thought to improve transparency, but they frequently overthink, generating long yet inefficient reasoning chains that inflate computational costs and risk introducing errors. While numerous reinforcement learning methods struggle to penalize verbosity to achieve conciseness, they typically apply the penalty uniformly across lengthy sequences, which leads to credit confounding, that is, the failure to distinguish essential reasoning steps from superfluous ones. In this paper, we study a measurable proxy for this confounding: high-entropy tokens that often coincide with surface transition points where reasoning re-enters verification. Based on this insight, we propose Adaptive Entropy-Sparing (AES), which selectively spares high-entropy tokens that occur before an efficient in-group reference, while penalizing overlength high-entropy suffix tokens associated with superfluous branching. Our AES thereby navigates the suppression-exploration trade-off, reducing overthinking while preserving average reasoning accuracy. We also present an entropy-length model that motivates why local uncertainty can serve as a proxy for future reasoning cost. Across eight reasoning benchmarks, AES reduces reasoning length by up to 52.9% while increasing mean accuracy by up to 3.8% over the base models, consistently outperforming existing efficiency-oriented methods. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CaseLaw: A Policy-Decoupled Framework for Training-Free Content Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11812)**  `arXiv:2611.11812`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qi Lu, Sen Li, Likun Shi, Xiaolong Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Content moderation is inherently policy-dependent: moderation boundaries are often subtle, subjective, and frequently updated, while conventional rule based or fine-tuning based systems require costly rule rewriting, model retraining, or system redeployment. We propose CaseLaw, a policy-decoupled and training-free framework for adaptive content moderation. CaseLaw reformulates moderation as precedent-based retrieval and voting, where multimodal content is transformed into evidence-preserved structured semantics, embedded into a stable semantic space, and classified by thresholded voting over retrieved precedent labels. We train NoteCap on a large-scale corpus of structurally annotated notes to ensure consistent semantic extraction and freeze it during subsequent policy adaptation, which requires no additional training. Policy adaptation is achieved by updating the semantic case library, including case labels and mappings, without retraining models or modifying prompts. To evaluate CaseLaw, we construct MFO-RedNotes, a benchmark for the male-female opposition risk domain. We additionally provide a five category CoPro comparison. Experiments show that CaseLaw consistently outperforms competing baselines in both precision and recall across policy-dependent risk levels and broader safety categories, with category-wise precision/recall reaching up to 86.8%/90.1% on MFO-RedNotes across both policy settings and 94.5%/92.3% on CoPro. Our work highlights the potential of precedent-based reasoning for policy adaptive content moderation and offers a practical direction toward more stable, transparent, and training-free moderation systems.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Transcript: Measuring and Improving the Use of Acoustic Information in Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11573)**  `arXiv:2611.11573`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linkai Peng, Baorian Nuchged, Yuyang Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio language model benchmarks provide comprehensive evaluation across diverse tasks. However, some tasks can be answered from the transcript or even the question alone, and benchmark scores can therefore confound acoustic understanding with language modeling ability. We distinguish lexical tasks, for which the transcript usually suffices, from global-attribute and within-recording-structure tasks, which require the model to analyse the recording beyond its words, either as a whole, for properties such as the speaker and emotion, or within it, for patterns such as stress and intonation. We evaluated 19 open models and Gemini 3.1 Pro on 92 tasks from 6 benchmarks and estimated the recording gain, the accuracy advantage of the original recording over references that retain its words, by comparing the recording with synthetic readings of its transcript and by adding it to the transcript. On attribute and structure tasks, the open models fall behind Gemini by 28.6 and 41.9 percentage points, and about half or more of each deficit lies in the recording gain. Nevertheless, linear probes in Gemma-4 12B and Qwen2.5-Omni-7B show that the acoustic information needed for answering is already readable in early language-model layers, while the readout of many cues declines in the layers that follow. We therefore constructed training data and trained LoRA adapters in these layers, with a content anchor, which raises attribute and structure accuracy by 12.8 to 44.4 points in the two models and keeps mean lexical accuracy within 1 point of the base model. Most of the improvement appears as larger recording gains. These results suggest that training improves the use of acoustic information that the models already encode.
  </details>

- **[THEATRON: A Benchmark for Evaluating Persona Persistence in Long-Horizon LLM Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11487)**  `arXiv:2611.11487`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuyan Wang, Yuechen Jiang, Zhiwei Liu, Jimin Huang, Sophia Ananiadou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing evaluations often test persona expression rather than whether persona information persists in extended decision-making. We introduce THEATRON, a controlled benchmark that uses financial replay to evaluate persona persistence in long-horizon sequential decision-making. In these environments, models conditioned on fixed behavioral-finance personas observe shared daily market evidence and trajectory-specific account-state feedback and make sequential decisions over multi-month trajectories. We evaluate Zero-Shot, One-Shot, and Chain-of-Thought persona injection across four LLMs, using cumulative-return and action trajectories to diagnose within-persona stability, cross-persona separability, and action collapse. Persona conditioning produces systematic behavioral shifts beyond a no-persona baseline, and persona-level separation generally exceeds the variation induced by semantically equivalent descriptions. Nevertheless, these shifts do not reliably form coherent persona-specific policies: apparently stable separation can arise from stereotyped action collapse, while diverse policies can exhibit noisy trajectory switching. These findings expose a gap between observable persona-induced behavior and persistent, context-sensitive persona-conditioned decision policies. Code and benchmark data are publicly available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[WarBias: A Bilingual LLM Benchmark of Stereotypes About Internally Displaced People and Veterans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11284)**  `arXiv:2611.11284`  `cs.CL`  
  _Viktoriia Makovska, Tetiana Zakharchenko, Oleksandra Ostafiichuk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper contains examples of stereotypes about veterans and displaced people that may be offensive or distressing. Social bias benchmarks for language models commonly examine categories such as gender, race, and religion, often drawn from lists of legally protected characteristics. Veterans and internally displaced people (IDPs) fall outside these lists, and stereotypes about them, which can undermine access to employment, housing, and social participation, remain largely absent from language model evaluation. We introduce WarBias, a Ukrainian--English benchmark of stereotypes about these two groups. Built from a taxonomy of 120 stereotype claims grounded in research and monitoring reports, WarBias contains 1,007 stereotype--counter-stereotype--unrelated sentence triplets and 240 multiple-choice questions per language. A Ukrainian language model selected in a blinded human pilot generated the Ukrainian triplets. At least two independent annotators reviewed each triplet in the annotation cohort. The authors revised items in response to the reviews, and the final release was machine-translated into English. Each claim carries a human severity rating. In a baseline evaluation of six models, four prefer stereotypical Ukrainian sentences about at least one group, most strongly about IDPs, while English scores are mostly balanced. WarBias supports complementary evaluations of stereotypical preferences and context-sensitive judgments, and provides paired forget/retain material for unlearning-based bias mitigation. We document the benchmark's construction, intended uses, and limitations, and offer its construction method as a template for analogous benchmarks in other conflict-affected societies.
  </details>

- **[Pole or Vector? Encoders Capture Bangla Compound Verbs, but LLMs Read Them Literally](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10909)**  `arXiv:2611.10909`  `cs.CL`  
  _Md. Asaduzzaman Shuvo, Md Mehrab Karim Antor, Md. Adnanul Islam, Shahanur Rahman Bappy, Sajid Ahamed, Mahfuz Hasan Reza, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Bangla, a main verb is often followed by one of a small set of vector verbs. These verbs lose their own meaning and add a shade of meaning instead, such as completion. For example, likhe phello means that someone finished writing, not that they wrote and threw something. The same two verbs can also describe two separate events, so only the context shows which meaning is meant. This makes compound verbs a hard test of how language models handle multiword expressions. We present BanglaCV-Probe, a benchmark of 17 vector verbs with 1,044 minimal pairs, 204 entailment items and 197 pairs of correct and incorrect combinations, all checked by native speakers. We test 26 models (encoders, LLMs and translation systems) on four skills: telling the two meanings apart internally, understanding what a vector adds, preferring correct combinations and keeping the vector's meaning in translation. Probes on five encoders tell the two meanings apart and still reach up to 93% accuracy when trained on pairs without surface cues. Prompted models rarely use this knowledge. Entailment accuracy stays between 59% and 81%. Several LLMs answer Yes or No to almost every item, and nine of ten models miss that someone who ate up the rice left none. Most instruction-tuned LLMs translate vectors literally in 15-20% of vector sentences and in up to 55% for mora (die). On correct combinations, a small Bangla-only model (88%) matches BLOOM-7B1 (87%) and both beat Qwen2.5-7B (74%). Data and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Cascading Circle Loss: Order-Preserving Contrastive Learning for Graded Legal Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10765)**  `arXiv:2611.10765`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rayane Kimouche, Ahmed Touila_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces Cascading Circle Loss (CasCL), a contrastive learning framework for legal retrieval tasks with graded relevance. CasCL combines an InfoNCE objective for positive-negative separation with a cascade of ranking boundaries that model ordered relevance levels between queries and legal clauses. The framework also includes a negative-aware self-paced optimization mechanism and a relevance gain weighting strategy to improve ranking quality. Experiments on legal passage retrieval benchmarks show that CasCL consistently outperforms existing methods, including InfoNCE, Circle Loss, MNRL and Margin MSE, across both binary retrieval and graded ranking metrics.
  </details>

- **[TransMem: Transition-Aware Retrieval for Evolving Personal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10683)**  `arXiv:2611.10683`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shigeng Chen, Linhao Luo, Changlong Shi, Zhangchi Qiu, Shirui Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term personalized agents are increasingly expected to answer queries over user histories in which preferences, goals, and constraints evolve across interleaved conversations. Existing retrieval-based and structured-memory systems can surface relevant facts, but often return isolated memory units that do not explain which earlier preference was refined, contradicted, or superseded by later evidence. We introduce TransMem, a transition-aware memory retrieval framework that stores preference updates as explicit retrieval units alongside ordinary factual memories. During memory construction, TransMem routes user interaction history into topic-structured memory graphs, detects shift events, and grounds them as transition records. At inference time, TransMem first retrieves factual anchors and then adaptively expands over transition records, producing a compact context of factual anchors and accepted transition records. Experiments on PersonaMem and PersonaBench across two Qwen backbones show that TransMem outperforms representative retrieval-based and structured-memory baselines. Controlled analyses further show that transition records and adaptive expansion contribute beyond factual anchoring, chronological expansion, flat transition retrieval, and fixed-hop traversal. These results suggest that preference-update records are useful retrieval units for long-term personalization.
  </details>

- **[Global Context Guidance for Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10577)**  `arXiv:2611.10577`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeimin Jeon, Bumsub Ham_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (LLMs) have recently emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive LLMs, enabling parallel generation through iterative denoising. Recent guidance methods improve diffusion LLMs by introducing a weak model to guide the prediction of the base model during denoising. However, the influence of the weak model during denoising remains less clear, and computing its prediction requires an additional forward pass at each denoising step. We present in this paper an in-depth analysis of how diffusion LLMs use contextual information during denoising. Our analysis reveals that diffusion LLMs rely more heavily on local context while often underutilizing global context. Based on this observation, we introduce Global Context Guidance (GCG), a feature-space guidance method that enhances global-context utilization without constructing an explicit weak model. Extensive experiments on LLaDA-8B, Dream-7B, and LLaDA-1.5 across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that GCG consistently outperforms existing guidance methods, while incurring much lower inference overhead.
  </details>

- **[YRC-Bench: Benchmarking Culturally Aware Machine Translation for Historical Heritage via Tri-Level Semantic Bridging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10555)**  `arXiv:2611.10555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Huang Yan, Aobo Li, Jiayi Wang, Huidong Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Translating historical cultural heritage poses profound challenges for machine translation systems due to the acute semantic asymmetry across eras and cultures, where terse classical syntax frequently obscures domain-specific cultural knowledge. While Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable prowess in generic translation benchmarks, they struggle to negotiate Culturally-Specific Items and diachronic shifts in domain-rich historical texts, such as Yellow River cultural records. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce YRC-Bench, the first human-curated, multi-parallel benchmark dedicated to historical heritage translation across Classical Chinese, Modern Chinese, and nine target foreign languages. YRC-Bench establishes a tri-level parallel corpus incorporating modern explanatory glosses as explicit semantic bridges and operationalizes translation strategies through paired conserved versus substituted CSI variants categorized by cultural translatability. Systematic evaluations of nine leading LLMs across bidirectional translation directions reveal that leveraging modern Chinese glosses - especially human-curated ones - dramatically elevates both lexical fidelity and semantic adequacy. Furthermore, multidimensional human preference audits by native experts unveil distinct cross-cultural preferences between Sinographic-tradition and non-Sinographic language groups.
  </details>

- **[LongCapHarness: Capability-Guided Auto-Harnessing for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10463)**  `arXiv:2611.10463`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tao Zhang, Yuhang He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing methods enhancing an LLM's long-context reasoning capability often rely on data synthesis and follow-up LLM post-training. However, the data synthesis strategy and the post-training recipes are empirically driven and computationally intensive. In this work, we propose a new framework, dubbed LongCapHarness, to instead harness the underlying capabilities for long-context reasoning. The harnessing process recursively revises and updates capability primitive implementation, capability state and control logic through recursive self-improvement on the development set. With neither data synthesis nor LLM post-training, LongCapHarness is applied to Llama3-8B and transferred to Qwen2.5-3B. LongCapHarness substantially outperforms recent data-synthesis-based baselines on HELMET and RULER benchmarks; its estimated development cost is less than 50% of the estimated training costs of NExtLong and PolicyLong. We hope these results motivate further research into harnessing the latent capabilities of LLMs to solve tasks beyond their explicit training.
  </details>

- **[Prove What You Can: Hybrid Neurosymbolic Reasoning for Logical Natural Language Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10319)**  `arXiv:2611.10319`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ada Canoğlu, Sadiq Qara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Logical reasoning over natural language requires both semantic interpretation and deductive search. Large language models (LLMs) can propose plausible intermediate derivations, but they often fail to compose them into a complete proof and overpredict uncertainty. Neurosymbolic methods improve search by translating an entire problem into first-order logic and handing it to a prover, but a single bad clause can make the whole translation nonexecutable. We present Hybrid Reasoning, a three-stage pipeline that decouples proposing a proof, validating its steps, and deciding a label. An LLM proposes a structured proof tree with a raw verdict; a theorem prover validates each derivation locally, keeping only the subset it can prove; and an uncertainty-gated repair attempts to re-derive the label from the premises plus the validated derivations, when the raw verdict is UNCERTAIN. Across four benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, ProverQA, PrOntoQA) and five contemporary LLMs, Hybrid Reasoning is the top-performing method on the two hardest datasets, improving on the strongest baseline by 5.3 points on FOLIO and 4.9 on ProverQA, and reaches ceiling-level accuracy on the easiest. The repair stage operates at 78-98% precision, producing 326 correct relabellings against 19 regressions.
  </details>

- **[A Model Organism for Studying Unverbalized Evaluation Awareness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09945)**  `arXiv:2611.09945`  `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Donnelly, Evan Chapple, Haritz Puerto, Sahar Abdelnabi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can recognize when they are being evaluated and change their behavior accordingly, undermining the reliability of safety benchmarks. Evaluation awareness (EA) is usually measured by having an LLM judge scan reasoning traces for explicit statements of recognition, but this signal is increasingly unreliable. Recent frontier-model system cards report a drop in verbalized rates between releases, which is indistinguishable from awareness that has gone unverbalized. Interpretability methods could detect such unverbalized evaluation awareness (UEA) in individual interactions, but lack instance-level ground truth. Asking the model alters the input, and dataset labels do not reveal whether it recognized a particular prompt as an evaluation. We address this gap with a model organism. Using synthetic document fine-tuning, SFT, and DPO, we train Qwen3.5-27B to emit a marker (a designated token) in its final response when it recognizes an evaluation, while suppressing the marker and verbalized awareness in its reasoning. The marker largely tracks the evaluation concept as it can be strongly modulated by evaluation framing in the system prompt. When not told whether it is being evaluated, the organism recognizes evaluations from test-question cues, mirroring what its base model often verbalizes. Using the marker as a label, we benchmark linear probes, the Jacobian Lens, and Activation Oracles, built on the base model and blind to the marker. None recovers the label reliably. These results suggest that current interpretability methods are not yet reliable UEA detectors, even when recognition relies on simple cues. Our model organism offers a testbed for improving them.
  </details>

- **[BIBIMBAP: A Benchmark for Instructional BIM-Based Automated Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09933)**  `arXiv:2611.09933`  `cs.CL`  
  _Clemens Kujat, Bharathi Kannan Nithyanantham, Stefan Telgmann, Tobias Sesterhenn, Joern Ploennigs, Stefan Lüdtke, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are emerging as natural-language interfaces for Building Information Modeling (BIM), successfully achieving information retrieval, but no benchmark exists to evaluate their ability to edit Industry Foundation Classes (IFC). We introduce BIBIMBAP, a Text-to-BIM benchmark with 100 curated tasks covering Create-Read-Update-Delete (CRUD) operations across spatial, geometric, topological, numeric, and conceptual categories. Each task includes a natural-language prompt, an IFC model, expected structured outputs, and test scripts for automated evaluation. Baseline results show limited performance (best: 50.2%) and frequent failures in constraint preservation and spatial reasoning, with some correct outputs achieved despite flawed reasoning.
  </details>

- **[When Does Better Confidence Improve a Knowledge-Graph Audit?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05877)**  `arXiv:2611.05877`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zehao Lin, Kai Chen, Xixuan Hao, Erenkai Zhu, Chunyu Li, Helan Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Confidence estimates guide human review in LLM-built knowledge graphs, but an erroneous shared entity need not be the only reason an incident fact is wrong. We study how this distinction affects budgeted audits, alongside selection headroom and label validity. Modelling non-adaptive auditing as budgeted covering, we characterise the minimal decision-relevant error statistics and bound the regret of scalar-preserving first-order planning by a structural mass $\Delta^{\mathrm{cl}}$. We simulate ideal repair against benchmark-aligned labels for five extraction configurations and two cross-document graphs on Re-DocRED, followed by frozen confirmation on test and replication on 500 hash-selected training documents. An error-rate $\times$ fan-out baseline leaves 3.98-4.74 percentage points more errors than joint planning with the same actions. Independent-block planning also avoids this disadvantage: its held-out point estimates improve on a fact-only scalar baseline by 1.22-1.79 points and are slightly better than the joint model, although the frozen equivalence criterion remains inconclusive. On 300 DWIE training documents, independent and joint plans also outperform fan-out in point estimates, with extractor-dependent gains over fact-only review. For verification, a generic support check improves three of four model-budget configurations on each held-out set. These comparisons motivate evaluating complete-repair probabilities and retaining both fact-only and factorised baselines when choosing an audit model.
  </details>

- **[XDecideBench: Evaluating Typed Decision Models and Confidence Policies Across Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01370)**  `arXiv:2611.01370`  `cs.CL`  
  _Marek Šuppa, Jaroslav Kopčan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed Decision Models such as Jev, Clef and pplx-decider return a probability for each answer an application provides. Applications use them in many languages, but are compared mostly in English. We introduce XDecideBench, a multilingual benchmark of nine suites in 122 languages. On our twelve-language suite, three open models fine-tuned for typed decisions beat their Qwen base models in every language. On new test data with local base models, their accuracy gain is larger in languages with less web text than in those with more. They fix a similar share of base-model errors in both, so the larger gain may reflect weaker base models. The best model on the twelve-language suite is best on only two other suites. The best English model on each suite, chosen in hindsight, is significantly beaten in about a quarter of suite and language combinations. On Belebele, Decision-1 is best in English but falls far behind Jev in Burmese. A confidence threshold fitted on English to allow 5% errors gives a higher error rate on XNLI, even in German and French. XDecideBench shows that results from one suite or from English are a poor guide to how a model and its threshold behave in other languages.
  </details>

- **[HEARSAYBENCH: Can AI Navigate from Abstract Human Rights to Lived Lives?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39062)**  `arXiv:2610.39062`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ava Iranmanesh, Sobhan Lotfi, Ali Iranmanesh, Liwei Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) reach users worldwide, personal counseling has emerged as one of their most consequential applications, sometimes in life-critical situations. Yet broader access does not mean equal support. The text these models learn from disproportionately reflects people with the freedom to act, embedding an implicit assumption that users can leave, report, seek help, or appeal. Existing benchmarks largely inherit this assumption, overlooking users whose agency is constrained by structural conditions such as war, statelessness, and displacement. Meanwhile, models are increasingly shaped by normative frameworks such as Constitutional AI, often grounded in ideals like human rights, even as many users live where those rights offer little protection or recourse.We introduce HEARSAYBENCH, a human-authored dataset of 400 test cases with in-region human verification grounded in scenarios where structural barriers stand between a person and the exercise of their rights. Built from United Nations and other human rights reports, the scenarios span over 80 regions and cover social, environmental, and personal constraints. Drawing on Nobel laureate Amartya Sen's Capabilities Approach, HEARSAYBENCH tests whether models can distinguish what a person is formally entitled to do from what they are actually able to do. Across 14 proprietary and open-weight models, every model infers a user's situation more accurately than it advises on it. Stronger models narrow this gap, but their recommendations remain unsafe to follow. Models may understand lives shaped by constraint, yet that knowledge remains hearsay if it cannot translate into advice people can act on. Those people are not alignment's long tail; they are among the users for whom good guidance matters most. HEARSAYBENCH makes this failure measurable, a step toward systems that serve the world as it is rather than as assumed.
  </details>

- **[Rewarding Novel Deductions: Solver-guided Process Supervision for Logical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34660)**  `arXiv:2609.34660`  `cs.CL`  
  _Muhammad Asif Ali, Wenqing Wang, Huan Wang, Mohammad Raza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Logical reasoning remains a major challenge for large language models (LLMs), particularly on structured problems that require precise constraint tracking, consistency preservation, and multi-step deduction. This challenge is especially acute for small-scale LLMs, which are more prone to producing inconsistent, redundant, or brittle reasoning trajectories. Existing approaches for improving logical reasoning largely optimize for final-answer correctness, providing only weak supervision over the intermediate reasoning process. In this work, we propose SPRING: (Solver-guided Process Rewards for Novel LogIcal ReasoNing Step Generation). SPRING uses an SMT solver as a training-time verifier of intermediate reasoning steps to provide process-level supervision. It introduces the notion of a novel reasoning step, namely, a step that is logically valid, consistent with the evolving reasoning state, and not already implied by previously accepted non-contradictory deductions. Based on this solver-based assessment, it designs process rewards that encourage novel inferential progress while penalizing contradictory and uninformative reasoning steps. Evaluation across three logical reasoning benchmarks: ZebraLogic, AR-LSAT, and Knights and Knaves, and four LLMs shows that SPRING consistently outperforms base LLMs, outcome-only reward baselines, and Logic-LM. On ZebraLogic, SPRING improves puzzle accuracy by up to 49.71 and 15.43 points over the base LLM and strongest outcome-only baseline, respectively. On AR-LSAT, it improves overall accuracy by up to 64.43 and 11.39 percentage points over the base LLM and strongest outcome-only baseline, respectively. On Knights and Knaves, SPRING achieves up to 93.14 puzzle accuracy and 96.05 person accuracy.
  </details>

- **[ToolSearcher: Optimizing Tool Selection at Scale via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30906)**  `arXiv:2609.30906`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenlong Dai, Xujie Song, Zitong Wang, Tong Niu, Jian liu, Weiqiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel at natural language processing but struggle to interact with external environments. Tool learning provides a promising way to extend LLMs into actionable agents, where tool selection is a critical prerequisite for successful tool use. Existing work often assumes a small or predefined set of tools, leaving large-scale tool selection underexplored. Real-world repositories contain a vast and diverse array of tools, making it difficult for LLMs to effectively search, distinguish, and compose tools under context-length constraints. We identify large-scale tool selection as a new challenge for agentic reinforcement learning, highlighting that existing RL methods for knowledge-based question answering are inadequate for selecting tools while considering compatibility. To address this challenge, we propose ToolSearcher, a novel RL framework for effective multi-turn search and fine-grained optimization in large-scale tool selection. Specifically, we introduce category-constrained tool discrimination to improve the model's ability to distinguish functionally similar tools, event-level search modeling to explicitly optimize the discovery of target tools during multi-turn search, and trajectory-aligned credit allocation to provide fine-grained reward signals for different stages of the search-selection process. Extensive experiments on large-scale tool selection benchmarks demonstrate that ToolSearcher consistently outperforms a set of strong baselines in challenging settings involving iterative search and complex tool composition.
  </details>

- **[PACE-dLLM: Elastic Block Decoding via Confidence Cliff Estimation for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26249)**  `arXiv:2609.26249`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaocheng Lu, Shuhan Guo, Ziyue Ma, Jie Zhang, Jian Liu, Jingcai Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (dLLMs), such as LLaDA and Dream, have become competitive with autoregressive (AR) LLMs in generation quality while supporting native parallel decoding. A standard acceleration strategy is block-wise decoding, where each forward pass predicts a block of length B and commits high-confidence tokens. Confidence-threshold commitment adapts which tokens are accepted, but a fixed B still constrains the prediction scope. Existing horizon policies use manually chosen sizes, volatility statistics, or candidate-boundary scores. We instead use the model's own per-step confidence: in-window confidence typically follows a context-dependent cliff, providing a direct signal for adapting the look-ahead horizon. In this work, we propose PACE-dLLM, which refits this parametric cliff periodically in closed form, sets the next horizon at a shared confidence anchor, and retains confidence-threshold token commitment. Under a saturated-yield abstraction with iid, policy-independent monotone profiles, the oracle horizon is the smallest attaining maximal per-pass yield and improves the asymptotic forward-pass rate when fixed blocks undershoot it with positive probability. On four reasoning and code benchmarks across two open-source dLLM backbones, PACE-dLLM achieves the best average accuracy among the evaluated decoders on both backbones, with average throughput speedups of 5.23x on LLaDA and 3.06x on Dream (up to 8.52x on LLaDA MATH) over the unaccelerated semi-AR baseline, advancing the quality-throughput Pareto frontier.
  </details>

- **[MJ: Multi-turn LLM Jailbreaking via Decomposed Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11070)**  `arXiv:2607.11070`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junyoung Park, Namgyu Park, Sechan Lee, Yoon-Chan Jhi, Jihoon Cho, Sangdon Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern large language models (LLMs) operate in interactive multi-turn settings, making multi-turn jailbreaking a realistic threat model and an important setting for automated red teaming. A core challenge in learning multi-turn jailbreak attackers is credit assignment: different turns contribute differently to the final outcome, yet existing learning signals are often too coarse to identify their individual contributions.We propose decomposed credit GRPO (DC-GRPO), a unified turn-level credit assignment framework for Group Relative Policy Optimization in multi-turn jailbreak learning. DC-GRPO assigns a separate group-relative learning signal to each turn by combining immediate and future credit, avoiding the credit misassignment induced by broadcasting a single trajectory-level score across the dialogue. We instantiate this framework with static and dynamic weighting rules that differ in how the two credit sources are balanced while sharing the same turn-level structure.Across four victim LLMs and three benchmarks, DC-GRPO achieves up to 98.26% average ASR_5@3, substantially outperforming the state-of-the-art methods, including SEMA (86.58%) and TROJail (86.23%), demonstrating the effectiveness of our turn-level group-relative credit assignment.Warning: This paper contains examples of harmful content.
  </details>

- **[Are Multilingual Models Actually Improving? Revisiting Cross-Lingual Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21954)**  `arXiv:2606.21954`  `cs.CL`  
  _Prasoon Bajpai, Eleftheria Briakou, Colin Cherry, Preethi Jyothi, Vihari Piratla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-lingual transfer is a model's ability to generalize capabilities from well-represented source languages to under-represented target languages. Existing measures of a model's transfer strength conflate improvements in transfer with general improvements to accuracy in the source language. We advocate for an alternate metric that reliably captures transfer strength called Hardness Adjusted Transfer (HAT) Score, and use it to derive multiple insights on factors influencing transfer strength. Our analysis across twenty diverse language models and three popular mainstream multilingual benchmarks argues that 1) transfer in small models is not broken, 2) we are making slower than expected progress in cross-lingual transfer with model size, and 3) we have made clear progress over time.
  </details>

- **[Geometric Latent Reasoning Induces Shorter Generations in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02248)**  `arXiv:2606.02248`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shashi Kumar, Yacouba Kaloga, Petr Motlicek, Ina Kodrasi, Andrea Cavallaro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models solve complex problems by generating lengthy chains of explicit reasoning tokens. While effective, this makes reasoning expensive, length-sensitive, and constrained to (discrete) natural language. While latent reasoning offers a continuous alternative, determining useful structures for intermediate latent states is an open challenge. In this paper, we formulate latent reasoning as a geometric path-approximation problem within the model's pretrained token-embedding space. We introduce Geometric Latent Reasoning (GLR), which uses a lightweight transition head to predict iterative direction updates in embedding space. Using textual chain-of-thought traces as anchors, GLR learns to approximate discrete reasoning trajectories while permitting continuous deviations from exact token embeddings. Evaluations on mathematical reasoning benchmarks using Qwen3 models reveal an emergent phenomenon: geometric latent reasoning induces substantially shorter generations without an explicit length objective. By replacing early explicit reasoning with continuous latent steps, models often reach correct answers using substantially fewer total generation steps. These findings suggest that continuous trajectories act as compact intermediate reasoning states, exposing a new tradeoff between latent computation budget, output length, and accuracy. Our code is publicly available at:this https URL. The project page is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[dMoE: dLLMs with Learnable Block Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30876)**  `arXiv:2605.30876`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sicheng Feng, Zigeng Chen, Gongfan Fang, Xinyin Ma, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) have recently emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive models, offering competitive performance while naturally supporting parallel decoding. However, as dLLMs are increasingly integrated with Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures to scale model capacity, a fundamental mismatch arises between block parallel decoding and token-level expert selection. Specifically, each dLLM forward pass processes multiple tokens with bidirectional dependencies, whereas conventional MoE layers route each token independently. This mismatch substantially increases the number of uniquely activated experts, making inference increasingly memory-bound. To address this, we propose dMoE, a simple yet effective block-level MoE framework. The central idea of dMoE is to aggregate token-level expert distributions within each block into a unified block-level expert distribution, which is then used to guide block expert routing in a coherent manner. In this way, dMoE substantially reduces the number of uniquely activated experts during inference without sacrificing performance, thereby mitigating the memory-bound bottleneck. Extensive experiments across a variety of benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of dMoE. On average, dMoE reduces the number of uniquely activated experts from 69.5 to 16.6 while retaining 99.55% of the original performance. Meanwhile, it reduces memory usage by 74.36% to 76.78% and achieves $1.36\times$ to $2.04\times$ end-to-end latency speedup. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[StateRAG: Typed State Contracts for Complex Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25379)**  `arXiv:2605.25379`  `cs.CL`  
  _Miaohe Niu, Pengxiang Li, Jingbo Zhu, Tong Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex retrieval-augmented generation requires evidence retrieval and control over what to retrieve next, which paths to explore, whether evidence is sufficient, and which intermediate results to retain. Existing RAG paradigms encode these decisions through method-specific model contexts, traversal procedures, verification signals, and memory. We introduce StateRAG, which represents retrieval control as a typed state external to the final reader. The state records the query plan, typed traversal path, candidate evidence, verification verdict, and reusable artifacts, with defined field semantics and designated update sources. Ordered role operators propose field values, and the controller validates and commits accepted proposals. A one-time compact-evidence check may select Bypass. Otherwise, the controller combines the committed verdict with the remaining budget to select Release, Revise, or Fallback. The final reader is invoked only after a terminal action and at most once per query. Among the evaluated methods, StateRAG achieves the best score on 10 of 11 reported quality metrics across LongBench, QASPER, and DocVQA. Its LongBench macro-average EM and F1 exceed ReAct by 6.3 and 6.1 percentage points, and its largest task-level gain is 9.8 F1 points on MuSiQue. Under normalized model-size weighting, StateRAG records lower mean LLM-token use per query than ReAct across all five workloads. In a matched LongBench control, the typed, role-owned carrier exceeds a free-form shared carrier by 1.3, 2.2, and 3.0 percentage points in macro EM, F1, and accuracy, with 6.2% lower mean RET. Removing TAM, MARS, or SMP lowers every reported LongBench and QASPER ablation metric.
  </details>

- **[LLM Agents Already Know When to Call Tools -- Even Without Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09252)**  `arXiv:2605.09252`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chung-En Sun, Linbo Liu, Ge Yan, Zimo Wang, Tsui-Wei Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-augmented LLM agents tend to call tools indiscriminately, even when the model can answer directly. Each unnecessary call wastes API fees and latency, yet no existing benchmark systematically studies when a tool call is actually needed. We propose When2Tool, a benchmark of 18 environments (15 single-hop, 3 multi-hop) spanning three categories of tool necessity -- computational scale, knowledge boundaries, and execution reliability -- each with controlled difficulty levels that create a clear decision boundary between tool-necessary and tool-unnecessary tasks. We evaluate two families of training-free baselines: Prompt-only (varying the prompt to discourage unnecessary calls) and Reason-then-Act (requiring the model to reason about tool necessity before acting). Both provide limited control: Prompt-only suppresses necessary calls alongside unnecessary ones, and Reason-then-Act still incurs a disproportionate accuracy cost on hard tasks. To understand why these baselines fail, we probe the models' hidden states and find that tool necessity is linearly decodable from the pre-generation representation with AUROC 0.89--0.96 across six models, substantially exceeding the model's own verbalized reasoning. This reveals that models already know when tools are needed, but fail to act on this knowledge during generation. Building on this finding, we propose Probe&Prefill, which uses a lightweight linear probe to read the hidden-state signal and prefills the model's response with a steering sentence. Across all models tested, Probe&Prefill reduces tool calls by 38\% with only 1.7\% accuracy loss, while the best baseline at comparable accuracy only reduces 6% of tool calls, or achieves a similar tool call reduction but incurs a 5$\times$ higher accuracy loss. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[LoopWeaver: Weaving Feedback Loops into Hierarchical Generation under Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04996)**  `arXiv:2603.04996`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yifan Zhu, Guanting Chen, Bing Wei, Haoran Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models face a multi-objective optimization problem in constrained long-text generation, balancing global structure, local coherence, and constraint satisfaction. We propose LoopWeaver, a hierarchical workflow with two sequential offline preference-optimization stages. Constraint-aware planning produces structured plans, feasibility filtering removes invalid plans, and a shared deterministic reward constructs preference pairs for plan-level and generation-level DPO. At inference time, LoopWeaver remains feed-forward: it generates and revises a complete plan, filters the revised plan, and then generates segments conditioned on the current sub-plan and running narrative. We evaluate the method on four LongGenBench-style structured scenarios across multiple backbones. Results show improvements in both constraint accuracy and text quality across all four open-source backbones. Our software and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HAPS: Hierarchical LLM Routing with Joint Architecture and Parameter Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05903)**  `arXiv:2601.05903`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihang Tian, Rui Li, Jingsen Zhang, Xiaohe Bo, Wei Huo, Xu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) routing aims to exploit the specialized strengths of different LLMs for diverse tasks. However, existing approaches typically focus only on selecting LLM architectures, treating candidate models as fixed while overlooking parameter adaptation, which can substantially affect task performance. In this paper, we introduce HAPS, a hierarchical LLM routing framework that jointly searches over model architectures and parameters. Specifically, a high-level router selects among candidate LLM architectures, while a low-level router generates input-conditioned LoRA parameters for the selected architecture, expanding each candidate from a single fixed behavior into a region of reachable behaviors. The low-level router is built on the backbone of the high-level router, so that architecture selection and parameter generation share a common representation of the prompt. We further optimize the whole framework with a reinforcement learning objective. Extensive experiments show that HAPS consistently outperforms strong routing baselines across reasoning- and knowledge-intensive benchmarks, with analyses of its components, design choices, scalability, cost, and mixed open- and closed-source deployment further demonstrating the robustness of these gains. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Guessing is Rewarded: Rethinking Language Model Evaluation with Distributional Uncertainty Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04302)**  `arXiv:2510.04302`  `cs.CL`  
  _Thomas F Burns_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard language model evaluation assigns scores to single predicted answers, rewarding high-confidence responses regardless of how residual probability mass is distributed over alternative options. This creates a systematic pressure toward overconfident guessing: under accuracy-based schemes, a model maximises its expected score by always committing to an answer rather than abstaining, even when its uncertainty is high. While penalty-based approaches partially address this by raising the confidence threshold for strategic guessing, they still treat all sub-threshold responses identically, ignoring a fundamental distinction in how models can express uncertainty - for example between hedging toward incorrect answers versus hedging toward "I don't know" responses. This paper introduces a novel evaluation metric to solve this problem of not considering a model's entire probability distribution over answer choices. The metric naturally distinguishes between harmful overconfidence in wrong answers and uncertainty expressed through abstention, providing scores in an interpretable default range. Through theoretical analysis and illustrative examples, the metric is shown to offer a more nuanced and aligned evaluation paradigm that incentivises models to express genuine uncertainty rather than guessing. Adapting 12 existing evaluation benchmarks to the metric's variants and measuring performance on six language models shows that for half of the tested benchmarks scores are negative across all tested models, indicating significant tendencies towards hallucination.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing Knowledge, Promoting Reasoning: Dual-Token Constraints for RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15778)**  `arXiv:2507.15778`  `cs.CL`  
  _Runze Liu, Jiakang Wang, Fuzheng Zhang, Xiu Li, Guorui Zhou, Kun Gai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become an effective post-training method for improving the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing methods mainly apply uniform optimization constraints across all tokens, ignoring their heterogeneous roles. Prior work shows that high-entropy tokens are closely tied to reasoning, while low-entropy tokens primarily encode factual knowledge, and recent approaches attempt to exploit this distinction by isolating token updates via masking or asynchronous training. We argue that retaining training signals across token types can lead to more effective learning. To address this, we propose Archer, an entropy-aware RLVR framework with dual-token constraints that preserves joint optimization while modulating update strength across token types. Our method introduces response-level entropy normalization for stable token classification and applies differentiated clipping ranges and KL regularization to encourage exploration on reasoning tokens while preserving knowledge tokens. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks show that Archer consistently outperforms strong baselines across multiple model scales, improving both pass@1 and pass@K performance. These results highlight the importance of respecting sequence-level dependencies when designing fine-grained RL optimization strategies for LLMs. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EMERGE: A Benchmark for Updating Knowledge Graphs with Emerging Textual Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.03617)**  `arXiv:2507.03617`  `cs.CL`  
  _Klim Zaporojets, Daniel Daza, Edoardo Barba, Ira Assent, Roberto Navigli, Paul Groth_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge Graphs (KGs) are structured knowledge repositories containing entities and relations between them. In this paper, we study the problem of automatically updating KGs over time in response to evolving knowledge in unstructured textual sources. Addressing this problem requires identifying a wide range of update operations based on the state of an existing KG at a given time and the information extracted from text. This contrasts with traditional information extraction pipelines, which extract knowledge from text independently of the current state of a KG. To address this challenge, we propose a generic and extensible pipeline that pairs textual passages with the KG edit operations they induce on a given KG snapshot. We instantiate this pipeline on the Wikidata knowledge graph and the English Wikipedia corpus to create EMERGE, a dataset of 233K Wikipedia passages associated with a total of 1.19 million KG edits across seven yearly Wikidata snapshots from 2019 to 2025. Our experimental results highlight key challenges in updating KG snapshots based on emerging textual knowledge, particularly in integrating knowledge expressed in text with the existing KG structure. We further show that KG access substantially improves executable updates and enables triple deprecation. These findings position EMERGE as a benchmark for developing methods that keep KGs up to date with emerging textual knowledge. The dataset (this https URL) and code (this https URL) are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Which Questions Improve Learning the Most? Utility Estimation of Questions with LM-based Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17383)**  `arXiv:2502.17383`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dong-Ho Lee, Hyundong Cho, Jonathan May, Jay Pujara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asking questions is fundamental for deeper understanding, yet evaluating and generating questions that improve downstream outcomes remains a challenging problem. To address this gap, we introduce QUEST (Question Utility Estimation with Simulated Tests), a framework that offers a new outcome-driven paradigm for question evaluation and generation, instead of indirect metrics that are based on the underlying content of the question and its answer. QUEST uses language models to simulate learners that ask questions while studying a textbook chapter and quantifies the utility of a question as its direct contribution to understanding as measured by the learner's exam performance. To analyze the effectiveness of QUEST, we curate TEXTBOOK-EXAM, a benchmark that aligns textbook sections with exam questions across five academic disciplines. Using QUEST, we filter for high-utility questions and fine-tune question generators via rejection sampling and show that questions generated by QUEST-trained models improve simulated exam scores by 18.5% compared to the next strongest baseline. Furthermore, we demonstrate the practical real-world value of QUEST by showing that simulation-computed utility of a question is moderately positively correlated with its usefulness as perceived by human evaluators.
  </details>

- **[Self-Supervised Keyframe Discovery for Horizon-Invariant Behavior Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10857)**  `arXiv:2610.10857`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Prabin Kumar Rath, Omkar Patil, Nakul Gopalan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Behavior cloning (BC) in non-Markovian environments is a challenging problem because policies have to reason over contextual information over long horizons. Existing policy architectures rely on recurrent or attention-based mechanisms to capture long-term dependencies. However, recurrent models suffer from hidden-state collapse and gradient instability under backpropagation through time while attention-based models are fundamentally limited by context length. To address these issues, we propose Keyframe Mnemonics, a novel self-supervised method that $\textit{discovers}$ a set of information-critical observations ($\textit{mnemonics}$) by learning an objective from randomly sampled past observations and using it as a reward for keyframe selection. We then train a BC policy that conditions on the discovered keyframes to model the action distribution. Under the simplicity bias assumption, our formulation provides context retention guarantees over an infinite horizon, while maintaining a small set of decision-relevant keyframes in the policy's working memory. We evaluate our method on synthetic memory domains, where mnemonic-conditioned BC policies achieve $100$% success rates (SR) and generalize to horizons orders of magnitude beyond training without performance degradation. Moreover, we evaluate on a memory-intensive robot manipulation benchmark, achieving a $13.9$% average absolute SR improvement over the strongest baseline across $23$ tasks. Finally, we demonstrate generalization with $80$% SR at $20\times$ longer time horizons on a real robot. Code and videos available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Gamified Framework for Conflict-Driven Design: Discovering Strategies for Collaborative Resilience with Multi-Agent Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10841)**  `arXiv:2611.10841`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Jin Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conflicting stakeholder objectives make it difficult to coordinate investments in urban resilience. We model conflict-driven urban design as a non-zero-sum digital board game in which stakeholder agents allocate scarce resources and balance individual payoffs against shared urban development and resilience objectives. We use multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) to train policies and examine their behavior across three settings. On a 4 x 4 board, learned policies reach about 84% of the total return found by a simulated annealing benchmark. On an 8 x 8 board with spatial and resource constraints, trained agents favor projects that improve resilience in self-play. In a 12 x 12 simulation initialized from historical maps of Tatsumi, Tokyo, trained agents achieve a mean total score of 452.1, compared with 357.3 for a random-policy baseline across 10,000 simulation runs. Gains in adaptability, sustainability, flood resistance, and greenery come with reductions in vitality and density. A human-in-the-loop interface supports replay and lets participants take over stakeholder decisions, providing a testbed for exploring these trade-offs in participatory urban planning.
  </details>

- **[MaxIM: Maximally Informative Incremental Summarization via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12825)**  `arXiv:2611.12825`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jihwan Jeong, Guy Tennenholtz, Yinlam Chow, Chih-Wei Hsu, Craig Boutilier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI systems must often maintain bounded memory of unbounded interaction histories. However, optimizing the memory representation, and its updates, for downstream task utility is difficult for standard reinforcement learning (RL) due to severe credit assignment bottlenecks induced by sparse, delayed rewards. We introduce MaxIM (Maximally Informative Incremental Memory), a framework that formulates incremental summarization as a capacity-constrained, information-theoretic reward-shaping problem. Grounded in value suboptimality bounds, we show that bounding sequential information loss motivates maximizing the predictive log-likelihood of future, task-relevant targets, a variational lower bound on predictive sufficiency. We introduce a recursive consistency condition to ensure summaries are grounded by maximizing mutual information (MI) with the observed history. We mitigate credit assignment issues with a parameterized asymmetric critic that computes step-wise variational MI bounds for dense reward shaping. Empirical evaluation on long-horizon scientific reading and multi-turn dialogue benchmarks shows that MaxIM outperforms RL, supervised fine-tuning, and prompt-focused baselines on factual grounding, comprehensive autorater metrics, and downstream task utility across strict token budgets and extended horizons, without the need for expensive human preference labels.
  </details>

- **[AutoBuddy: Evaluating Proactive, Always-On Agents on Task Discovery and Execution in a Simulated Workplace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12393)**  `arXiv:2611.12393`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shiqi Chen, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Wei-Ning Chen, Gbola Afonja, Fangkai Yang, Rujia Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents become more autonomous, they are increasingly expected to run as always-on assistants on a user's behalf, identifying work to be done and completing it without being asked. Yet existing benchmarks usually give the agent its task, whereas in knowledge work the requests sit in the user's workspace and the agent must first recognise which obligations exist. We study proactive task discovery and execution, in which an agent must infer a user's pending tasks from their workspace and complete them in the systems where the work lives. This setting poses two challenges. For the agent, the tasks are buried in a large volume of mail, chat, documents and records. For evaluation, a benchmark needs a simulated organisation whose people, events and documents are mutually consistent, so that tasks arise realistically and their correct outcomes are known. We introduce AutoBuddy-Bench, a simulator that addresses these challenges. It models an organisation's employees, projects, business systems and event history as a temporal graph, renders it into realistic workspace artefacts, derives ground-truth tasks from the events that create obligations, and computes each task's outcome from the world state; execution is judged by the agent's changes to the workspace. To evaluate current LLMs, we build AutoBuddy, a reference agent with interchangeable backbone LLMs: it identifies tasks from the workspace, grounding each in its source records, and executes each in a separate session with write access. Varying only the backbone, we find a systematic shift in where tasks are lost: weaker models fail mainly at discovery, intermediate models identify tasks but often stop short of carrying them out, and the strongest models fail mainly during execution, most often on business-system operations. Discovery precision is only weakly related to execution quality, suggesting the two are distinct capabilities.
  </details>

- **[CABrain: A Correlation-Aware Brain Foundation Model for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11782)**  `arXiv:2611.11782`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _SuGil Choi, Mooseop Kim, Chi Yoon Jeong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models have emerged as promising paradigms for decoding brain activity through large-scale self-supervised pretraining. Notably, most existing approaches employ masked patch reconstruction, in which randomly selected EEG patches are masked and recovered by the network. However, purely random masking may limit representation learning as informative patches from highly correlated channels often remain unmasked, causing the network to underutilize weakly correlated channels or serial dependencies. Furthermore, because these models are typically based on transformer architectures, their performance is influenced by the choice of positional encoding. Although positional encoding has been extensively explored in natural language processing and computer vision, few studies have adapted it to the unique characteristics of EEG signals. To address these limitations, this study introduces a conditional positional encoding method based on graph convolutional networks (GCNs) to explicitly model inter-channel correlations, along with an adaptive masking strategy that enhances representation learning by modulating the difficulty of masked patch reconstruction. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to integrate graph-based representations into conditional positional encoding and introduce adaptive masking within EEG foundation model pretraining. Experimental results on ten benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed GCN-based positional encoding and adaptive masking strategies, both individually and in combination, achieve state-of-the-art or competitive performance, highlighting their effectiveness in EEG foundation modeling.
  </details>

- **[The Delta Gap: Cellular World Models for Perturbation Response Prediction Are Far Harder Than Global Correlation Implies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10658)**  `arXiv:2611.10658`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Junhui Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cellular world models aim to predict how cells respond to genetic perturbations, analogous to world models in reinforcement learning. Perturbation response models are evaluated almost exclusively by global Pearson correlation between predicted and observed transcriptomes. We show this metric is dominated by thousands of non-responsive genes and severely inflates apparent performance. On the Replogle K562 screen (509 unseen perturbations, 5000 genes), GEARS achieves global Pearson 0.977, but its delta Pearson is only 0.076. A trivial baseline predicting mean training delta achieves 0.267, which is 3.5x better than GEARS.
