Our primary contribution is methodological: we identify and quantify this delta gap as a systematic evaluation artifact, and propose delta-space evaluation as the primary benchmark. We provide two post-hoc corrections (RWR on co-expression graphs and kNN matching) and show that 2.8x of the remaining gap lies in perturbation representation geometry, not decoder capacity. We further show the gap is partially addressable end-to-end: delta-aligned training (lambda = 3.0) raises delta Pearson to 0.179 on Replogle (2.35x over GEARS) and surpasses the trivial baseline on direction accuracy (0.680 vs 0.567) and gene recovery (1.52 vs 1.19); combined with kNN it reaches 0.217. An amplitude-calibration variant (eta = 0.3) further raises gene recovery to 2.16 while reaching 0.218. Critically, these representations transfer zero-shot across cell lines: from Replogle K562 to unseen Norman A549, the delta-aligned model reaches 0.328 on 741 shared genes, 4.7x a trivial baseline and 2.1x standard GEARS. This is the first systematic quantification of the delta gap, and the first diagnostic framework localizing the residual gap to perturbation representation geometry via an oracle upper bound.
  </details>

- **[LegoSR: A Unified Framework for Self-improving Agent-Guided Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33465)**  `arXiv:2610.33465`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hao Mao, Xu T. Liu, Hao Xu, Peng Zhao, Qian Li, Dongxiao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discovering equations from data provides governing laws, constitutive relations and closure models for scientific understanding and prediction. Yet a method that succeeds on one system may fail on another or on noisy data, so discovery depends on expert trial and error. Unsuccessful searches can still reveal useful parts of an equation, but these clues must be recognized and combined. We present LegoSR, a unified framework for agent-guided equation discovery built on composable discovery and cumulative experience. Seven complementary equation discovery methods and shared computational modules form reusable building blocks, connected through a common equation representation. Researchers can launch discovery with one command and guide it with partially known equations. LegoSR Agent selects methods, revises searches using feedback and combines partial findings into new hypotheses. Its Experience Library consolidates graded outcomes into lessons that guide later tasks across equation families. Evaluation covers 134 algebraic regression problems, 40 ordinary differential equation targets and 17 partial differential equation problems at several noise levels, together with three case studies. On noisy differential equation benchmarks, LegoSR Agent nearly matches the recovery coverage of seven expert-tuned methods combined and exceeds a generic agent with the same model, tools and budget by up to 29.4 percentage points, with 51-61% shorter median wall-clock time.
  </details>

- **[Same Beliefs, Different Policies: Learning Reusable Representations for Early Stopping in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30450)**  `arXiv:2610.30450`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingwei Ni, Dmitry Abulkhanov, Vladislav Smirnov, Markus Leippold, Elliott Ash, Timothy Baldwin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning LLMs often continue reasoning after reaching a correct answer, wasting computation and sometimes replacing it with an incorrect one. Deciding when to stop is a metareasoning problem: the model must weigh what further reasoning could achieve against its cost, given what it can already answer. Direct utility training learns a stopping representation at a chosen token price; supporting new prices can require additional controller training. We introduce BELT (BELief-based Termination), which separates outcome prediction from stopping control. It learns beliefs about current and future answers from a frozen LLM's hidden states, then fits a linear stopping policy on the frozen features. The prediction targets are independent of token price, so one probe supports different accuracy-cost trade-offs through CPU policy refitting or threshold adjustment. Averaged over five out-of-distribution (OOD) math benchmarks, BELT reduces generated tokens by 41% on Qwen3-1.7B and 51% on OLMo-3-7B-Think without loss of accuracy. The same math-trained BELT transfers to coding and agentic tasks without training on code or agentic data. In all three domains, its accuracy-cost trade-offs are competitive with or better than those of end-to-end controllers. On OLMo3, its probe has 21.2x fewer parameters than one checkpoint of the strongest baseline, OS-Pruner, and needs 55x fewer training GPU-hours than OS-Pruner's five price-specific checkpoints.
  </details>

- **[Cooperative Coevolution for Resource-Constrained Agentic LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02391)**  `arXiv:2608.02391`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiyuan Wang, Shengcai Liu, Jiahao Wu, Ning Lu, Hui Ouyang, Shaofeng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents produce long, multi-turn trajectories, making gradient-based post-training memory-intensive. Evolution strategies (ES) enable memory-efficient full-parameter post-training without backpropagation and can eventually match the performance of gradient-based reinforcement learning (RL). However, resource-constrained settings typically offer only a few GPUs, so the high GPU-hour requirements of ES translate into prohibitively long training times. To address this, we introduce Cooperative Parameter-subspace Evolution Strategy (CoPES), a cooperative coevolutionary method that decomposes the full parameter space into lower-dimensional subspaces and searches over them cooperatively to improve optimization efficiency. On Qwen3.5-4B math, CoPES recovers 92% of full-parameter GRPO's validation-accuracy gain within the GPU-hour budget of GRPO's best validation checkpoint, versus 67% for standard ES. Its theoretical GPU memory requirement is less than one-eighth that of full-parameter GRPO. It consistently outperforms standard ES and LoRA-based GRPO on all evaluated pass@k metrics across five math benchmarks. CoPES's gains extend to Qwen3.5-9B with unchanged optimization hyperparameters and to multi-hop QA. These results demonstrate an improved trade-off between memory requirements and training time for agentic LLM post-training under resource constraints. The code is open-sourced inthis https URL
  </details>

- **[PEARL: Solver-in-the-Loop Interactive Optimization Modeling from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18256)**  `arXiv:2607.18256`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hongliang Lu, Zhong Li, Yuxuan Chen, Yuan Lan, Fan Zhang, Zaiwen Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimization modeling is the process of translating real-world decision problems, often described in natural language, into formal mathematical formulations and executable solver code. While recent advances in large language models have shown promise in automating this process, many approaches focus on one-shot generation or rely on predefined execution-and-repair workflows, without explicitly learning a policy for tool use and revision. This stands in sharp contrast to real-world optimization modeling, which is inherently interactive and proceeds through repeated solve-debug-revise cycles. We introduce PEARL, a system for multi-turn solver-interactive optimization modeling that uses Python execution and mathematical programming solvers inside this loop. Rather than relying on a fixed repair workflow, PEARL learns when to test partial models, how to revise from solver diagnostics, and when to stop. It operates in a multi-turn tool-integrated setting where intermediate execution results, feasibility signals, and solution checks are used to improve both formulations and solver code before finalization. Across diverse optimization benchmarks, PEARL substantially improves executable objective agreement over the same-scale base model and is competitive with strong one-shot and tool-augmented baselines. Notably, PEARL-Qwen3-4B outperforms the much larger DeepSeek-V3.2-685B baseline under direct prompting on both macro- and micro-averaged Pass@1. Code and datasets are provided atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Isn't Enough: Unlocking Visual Reasoning by Bridging the Reasoning-Access Gap with TIGER](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10552)**  `arXiv:2611.10552`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Minh Huynh Nguyen, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inherit strong textual reasoning capabilities from their language backbones and text pretraining, but often fail to apply the same level of competency to visual inputs. We show that this degradation cannot be attributed to only perception failures, by evaluating models on semantically-equivalent text and rendered-text image inputs: models can accurately read text in the images and recover near-text performance when provided their own transcriptions, but just cannot reason well on the images directly. We characterize this discrepancy as a modality reasoning-access gap, where models fail to route perceived visual content into existing reasoning machinery accessible with text. To bridge this, we propose TIGER (Text-to-Image Gap-targeted training for Enhanced Reasoning), which first mines synthetic modality counterfactuals where a model succeeds on a text problem but fails when provided the corresponding rendered image, and post-trains the model on the image input via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), along with SFT and DPO variants. Importantly, although trained exclusively on synthetic rendered-text images with no multimodal annotations, TIGER results in model performance improvements across diverse real-world visual benchmarks including geometric diagrams (MathVerse), multi-disciplinary sciences (EMMA, MMMU), complex charts (ChartQAPro), and embodied spatial reasoning (RoboSpatial-Home). TIGER also complements existing state-of-the-art RLVR visual reasoning methods, yielding further performance gains. Analysis involving reasoning-subspace activation and activation patching provide support indicating that TIGER helps to align visual-to-reasoning pathways toward text reasoning states. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Your Agents Are Aging Too: Agent Lifespan Engineering for Deployed Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26302)**  `arXiv:2605.26302`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Jianing Zhu, Yeonju Ro, John Robertson, Kevin Wang, Junbo Li, Haris Vikalo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-lived AI agents are increasingly deployed as persistent operational systems, yet they are still mostly evaluated like freshly initialized models. Day-one benchmarks miss a basic systems question: how does an agent's reliability change after deployment? Even when model weights are frozen, an agent's effective state keeps changing as it compresses interaction history, retrieves from a growing memory store, revises facts after updates, and undergoes routine maintenance. Reliability therefore becomes a lifespan property of the full agent harness, not only a snapshot property of the base model. We introduce AgingBench, a longitudinal reliability benchmark for agent lifespan engineering: measuring not only whether deployed agents degrade, but what form the degradation takes and where repair should target. AgingBench organizes agent aging into four mechanisms: compression aging, where writing and rewriting drops future-relevant details; interference aging, where accumulated similar memories crowd out the target fact; revision aging, where changed or derived state is not updated correctly; and maintenance aging, where lifecycle events such as model swapping trigger regressions. To diagnose these failures, AgingBench uses temporal dependency graphs and counterfactual probes that produce diagnostic profiles for the write, retrieval, and utilization stages of the memory pipeline. Across 6 scenarios, 10 memory systems and 6 models from two families, we find that agent aging is not one-dimensional over 400 runs spanning 50-200 sessions: a well-formed reply can hide a value the memory no longer supplies; derived state collapses as updates accumulate; and the same wrong answer can require different repairs depending on what the diagnostic profile points to.
  </details>

- **[ODSA: Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11621)**  `arXiv:2611.11621`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Macardle Meng, Haomin Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling improves the accuracy of a language model's answers by spending more computation at inference time. Established methods do so either sequentially, revising a single solution over several passes, or in parallel, generating several independent solutions and selecting among them. We present ODSA (Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation), an LLM-directed scaling framework in which an orchestrator LLM is given existing test-time scaling methods as actions --- discovery of a new independent solution, comparison of existing solutions to build a better one, and acceptance of a proposed answer, which triggers its verification --- and directs stateless workers to carry them out. The orchestrator does not solve the problem itself: it allocates computation, feeds the results of earlier actions into later ones, and takes every action from a single context, so that each decision is informed by everything tried before. On Humanity's Last Exam, ODSA improves accuracy by 4.7 to 18.0 points across three models over a single direct call to the model with no test-time scaling. On LiveCodeBench, where a single direct call already solves most problems, it improves accuracy by 0.6 to 14.0 points.
  </details>

- **[PolicyGuide: From Guarding One Action to Guiding the Whole Workflow for Policy-Compliant LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19861)**  `arXiv:2608.19861`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Seongjae Kang, Taehyung Yu, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Customer-service LLM agents must follow organizational policy when acting on a user's behalf. Compliance failures arise from either forbidden actions, such as granting an ineligible change, or omitted procedural requirements, such as identification or confirmation. Runtime safeguards can intervene on risky actions, but action-local checks do not guide an agent through a multi-step procedure. Workflow-following systems support prescribed process execution, but primarily target workflow completion rather than safeguarding agent behavior. PolicyGuide instead compiles each domain policy into a workflow graph and invokes a proactive verifier at user-turn boundaries. From persisted graph state, the verifier reconciles open requests and returns step-specific remediation along a policy-compliant path. Across the $\tau^2$-bench airline, retail, and telecom domains with a GPT 5.4 agent and verifier, PolicyGuide raises mean $\mathrm{Pass}^4$ from $0.42$ to $0.62$, with the largest gain on telecom ($0.19$ to $0.61$), the most workflow-structured domain. The same workflows transfer to Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Gemini 2.5 Pro agents. Complementary evaluations find the lowest observed attack-success rate under adversarial users and the strongest procedural compliance in an independently validated workflow-level audit.
  </details>

- **[Weakly Supervised Distillation of Hallucination Signals into Transformer Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06277)**  `arXiv:2604.06277`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shoaib Sadiq Salehmohamed, Jinal Prashant Thakkar, Hansika Aredla, Shaik Mohammed Omar, Naveen Bera, Pulijala Sai Nikhila, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing hallucination detection methods for large language models (LLMs) rely on external verification at inference time, requiring gold answers, retrieval systems, or auxiliary judge models. We ask whether this external supervision can instead be distilled into the model's own representations during training, enabling hallucination detection from internal activations alone at inference time.We introduce a weak supervision framework that combines three complementary grounding signals: substring matching, sentence embedding similarity, and an LLM as a judge verdict to label generated responses as grounded or hallucinated without human annotation. Using this framework, we construct a 15000-sample dataset from SQuAD v2 (10500 train/development samples and a separate 5000-sample test set), where each example pairs a LLaMA-2-7B generated answer with its full per-layer hidden states and structured hallucination labels.We then train five probing classifiers: ProbeMLP (M0), LayerWiseMLP (M1), CrossLayerTransformer (M2), HierarchicalTransformer (M3), and CrossLayerAttentionTransformerV2 (M4), directly on these hidden states, treating external grounding signals as training-time supervision only. Our central hypothesis is that hallucination detection signals can be distilled into transformer representations, enabling internal detection without any external verification at inference time.Results support this hypothesis. Transformer-based probes achieve the strongest discrimination, with M2 performing best on 5-fold average AUC/F1, and M3 performing best on both single-fold validation and held-out test evaluation. We also benchmark inference efficiency: probe latency ranges from 0.15 to 5.62 ms (batched) and 1.55 to 6.66 ms (single sample), while end-to-end generation plus probe throughput remains approximately 0.231 queries per second, indicating negligible practical overhead.
  </details>

- **[OGDA: Ontology-Grounded Data Agents for Reliable Analysis over Heterogeneous Data Lakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12114)**  `arXiv:2611.12114`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Boyi Zhang, Hong Ting Tsang, Yisen Gao, Qiao Xiao, LI Yufei, Haoyu Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-pipeline data analysis over real-world data lakes requires LLM agents to locate and integrate information across large collections of tables with different schemas. Existing data agents follow task-specific exploration, which exhausts the context window in massive lakes. As the context fills, the agent's reasoning degrades, leading it to locate and process incorrect data files. These errors propagate into the final answer. To overcome these limitations, we introduce Ontology-Grounded Data Agent (OGDA), which utilizes a compact ontology to reduce exploration overhead and constrain data processing. OGDA constructs the ontology through agentic hypothesis testing. At each step, a proposer agent induces schemas, concepts, and relations from raw sources, and a verifier checks each proposal against the underlying data. During task execution, the ontology serves as a representation of the data lake for the agent to explore. Every data-processing action is constrained by a task graph, which the agent can extend only after observing the semantic meaning of the data. Across KramaBench and DiscoveryBench with three LLM backbones, OGDA outperforms the strongest baselines by up to 10.82 points. These results suggest that explicitly modeling the data lake is a key step toward reliable end-to-end data agents.
  </details>

- **[Infrathon: Benchmarking Language Agents for Real-World, Long-Horizon AI Infra Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11995)**  `arXiv:2611.11995`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhengyu Hu, Zheyuan Xiao, Yike Wang, Hamish Ivison, Haoyu Wang, Yige Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we study whether frontier agent can continually improve real-world AI infrastructure over long horizons. We introduce Infrathon, a benchmark of diverse, long-horizon AI infrastructure optimization tasks across different levels. We evaluate six models across 150 runs with 24-hour budgets, totaling over 10,000 GPU-hours. Our results show that model rankings vary with the evaluation horizon, while 135 of the 150 runs achieve further improvements during the second half of their budgets. These findings indicate that shorter evaluations can overlook both optimization gains and changes in relative model performance. We also find agents can discover solutions that outperform human-designed baselines: Claude Opus 5.5 increases the mean KV-cache compression ratio from 6.82% with the human-designed Quant-VideoGen codec to 19.86%. Analysis of agent trajectories identifies task violations, unreliable testing, and premature completion as barriers to sustained improvement. Together, Our Infrathon framework provide resources for constructing long-horizon tasks, evaluating frontier agents, and discovering good infrastructure solutions.
  </details>

- **[FinCutBench: A Continually Updated Benchmark for Measuring the Temporal Boundaries of LLM Financial Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11588)**  `arXiv:2611.11588`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Siyuan Li, Ke Wan, Boyong Hou, Yujie Deng, Zixuan Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In financial backtesting and other point-in-time tasks, external inputs can be restricted by date, but model parameters may retain financial facts disclosed later, introducing parametric look-ahead bias. We introduce FinCutBench, a continually updated monthly financial knowledge benchmark for measuring how far a fixed model's financial knowledge extends over calendar time. The current dataset contains 9,000 Chinese and English four-option multiple-choice questions from January 2023 to September 2026, covering Chinese and U.S. markets and four categories of financial information. We construct monthly knowledge profiles using the approximate months in which the tested facts became public. After a profile passes the decline test and knot checks, we define the FinCut Boundary as the first month when the fitted profile reaches its lower level, and report the stability of the estimated date under resampling. Boundary dates and localization precision vary across models. Using the independently measured Transition Start and Boundary as fixed references for downstream sample grouping, we find clear relative declines after the Boundary in U.S. asset ranking and earnings recall for some models, while Chinese-market ranking estimates vary across models and all eight disclosure-period-paired forecast-error comparisons have unadjusted $p$ values above 0.10. The FinCut Boundary thus provides an empirical reference for the temporal scope of parametric knowledge in point-in-time financial evaluation, supporting evaluation-window selection and result interpretation.
  </details>

- **[CoRA-Bench: Evaluating Evidence Preservation by Context Selectors Under Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10238)**  `arXiv:2611.10238`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Huzaifa Pardawala, Siddhant Sukhani, Sudheer Chava, Agam Shah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Context selection can reduce input length during long-input inference but can remove evidence required to answer a query. We introduce CoRA-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating evidence preservation under shared token budgets. We compare head and tail truncation, BM25, dense retrieval, dense retrieval with reranking, LLMLingua-2, and Provence across BABILong, LOONG Financial Spotlight, a custom NoLiMa diagnostic, and LongMemEval. Evidence-preservation results reveal task-dependent tradeoffs: BM25 achieves 0.921 supporting-fact recall on BABILong at 128K, dense retrieval and dense reranking each achieve 0.908 needle recall on NoLiMa at 64K, and dense retrieval achieves 0.993 answer-bearing-turn recall on LongMemEval at 64K. Evidence, token retention, and complementary reader evaluations use the same selected contexts. Reader evaluations use dataset-native answer metrics and three runs on the same questions, with paired bootstrap confidence intervals. At a 32K budget, BM25 achieves 46.20% BABILong answer accuracy and dense retrieval achieves 71.13% LongMemEval accuracy. The results show that method rankings vary across evidence types and between evidence recall and downstream accuracy.
  </details>

- **[DeepResearch RewardBench: Evaluating Reward Models for Deep Research Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10156)**  `arXiv:2611.10156`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shudong Liu, Yuting Zhong, Guanhua Chen, Xinyi Yang, Junnan Liu, Lidia S. Chao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Empowered by Large Language Models (LLMs), deep research systems integrate online information into research reports through multi-step web searching and information synthesis. Evaluating these reports presents a significant challenge due to their long context, high information density, and domain specificity, necessitating the use of AI evaluation as a substitute for prohibitively expensive human assessment. However, few studies have examined the effectiveness of existing reward models or LLMs in understanding and evaluating such research reports. To bridge this gap, we introduce DeepResearch RewardBench, the first benchmark dedicated to evaluating reward modeling for research reports. We collect diverse report candidates from multiple agents or tool-augmented LLMs. These candidates are scored using query-specific golden rubrics, and we ensure data quality and difficulty through a combination of multi-round filtering and human verification. Our benchmark comprises preference datasets formatted as pairs, triplets, and quadruplets to test ranking capabilities across varying levels of difficulty. Extensive experiments demonstrate that while current reward models handle general coherence effectively, their performance significantly degrades when evaluating research reports. Generative LLMs acting as judges demonstrate comprehensive superiority in this domain, with comparative evaluation proving more effective than direct scoring methods. We anticipate that this benchmark will serve as a vital testbed for the evaluation and advancement of research agents.
  </details>

- **[DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03601)**  `arXiv:2611.03601`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Chengxi Li, Shaoxuan Li, Deyao Hong, Qingle Liu, Yi Huang, Puheng Bian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.
  </details>

- **[Marginal utility, matrix factorization, and the Key-Value (KV) cache: a unified information-economic framework for sovereign geo-mining inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20068)**  `arXiv:2609.20068`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Caroline Gans Combe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper builds a theoretical bridge between the economic notion of marginal utility and two machine-learning constructs, matrix factorization and the Key--Value cache of transformer language models. The singular value spectrum of a rating matrix is shown to be a diminishing marginal utility schedule for latent factors, the eigenvalue spectrum of the projected covariance operator to be the marginal utility schedule of a model's learned representation, and cache eviction and low-rank cache compression to be instances of constrained utility maximization under a memory budget. The three collapse into a single allocation rule: retain the top dimensions whose eigenvalue exceeds the shadow price of the binding constraint. The framework is applied to the automated extraction of structured information from geo-mining documents, where it motivates a multi-pass inference protocol, a layer-wise TIES model merging procedure, and a selection policy combining extraction quality, localization drift and energy, scalarized with a Conditional Value-at-Risk term on drift. Two empirical contributions are reported. An 11.2-million-parameter hierarchical classifier, trained in about five minutes on a single GPU, reaches 90.0 per cent level-1 accuracy on a held-out test set from a 973-document uranium-exploration corpus, against 92.0 per cent for a proprietary model on a fifty-document human audit of the same corpus, at a latency of 2.62 ms per card against approximately 2,000 ms for the API and at negligible cost. A diagnostic of uniform-density TIES merging exposes a reproducible degenerate mode in which the merged model returns token-identical outputs across five geographically distinct districts while declaring high confidence; re-executing the merge under layer-wise calibrated densities removes that signature on the diagnostic sample. The full-scale extraction benchmark, including LoRA fine-tuning, is reported as projected rather than measured and remains an empirical extension of this work.
  </details>

- **[PolicyGuard: A Dialogue-Grounded Sub-Agent Verifier for Policy Adherence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29225)**  `arXiv:2606.29225`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Seongjae Kang, Taehyung Yu, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents handle user requests on behalf of organizations through tool calls and must follow the company policies stated in their system prompts. Prior work approaches this as a safeguarding problem -- external checks that block non-compliant agent actions. We argue that policy adherence is broader: real workflows unfold across many turns, require explicit user confirmation and prerequisite reads, and hinge on dialogue rather than on any single argument value. We introduce PolicyGuard, a sub-agent verifier that shares the agent's view of the dialogue, reasons over the policy in context, and returns actionable remediation. On $\tau^2$-bench airline across GPT 5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Gemini 2.5 Pro, PolicyGuard improves $\mathrm{Pass}^4$, the fraction of tasks successful in all four trials, by +12.0 / +6.0 / +12.0 pp; pooled paired tests support an overall cross-vendor effect. We further evaluate PolicyGuard on the full retail and telecom base splits, covering all three $\tau^2$-bench customer-service domains. Further analyses show that PolicyGuard improves reliability beyond agent self-checking, catches more policy violations with fewer blocks than argument-level guards, and preserves its refusal accuracy under three adversarial probes.
  </details>

- **[PersonaDrive: Human-Style Retrieval-Augmented VLA Agents for Closed-Loop Driving Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12616)**  `arXiv:2606.12616`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Mahmoud Srewa, Praneetsai Vasu Iddamsetty, Mohammad Abdullah Al Faruque, Salma Elmalaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-loop driving simulators mostly fill their environments with traffic agents that behave alike, whether they are rule-based or learned. Recent methods that control driving style learn it from labels inferred from existing driving recordings, from synthetic data generated to reflect each style, or from reward weights estimated by a large language model (LLM). All of these are indirect estimates of a style, not recordings of people who were instructed to drive in it. We introduce PersonaDrive, a vision-language-action (VLA) driving agent that takes its style from retrieved human demonstrations. The demonstrations come from a new dataset in which 15 participants drove CARLA routes on a driving simulator under conservative, neutral, and aggressive instructions. PersonaDrive trains a lightweight retrieval head on features from a frozen image encoder and on control signals over per-style databases, and fine-tunes one VLA backbone over the retrieved demonstrations. Switching style only means searching a different FAISS index, with no retraining. On the Bench2Drive benchmark without style conditioning, PersonaDrive improves the Driving Score by 4.6% over SimLingo and by 2.5% over HiP-AD, a gain that fine-tuning without retrieval does not reproduce. With style conditioning, PersonaDrive outperforms the strongest baseline, Drive My Way, in every style, with a Driving Score 6.7% to 11.6% higher. Its driving also follows the instruction, since average speed rises by 19.8% from the conservative to the aggressive style. Each traffic agent is evaluated individually as a driving policy. The dataset and model weights are available on the project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sage-Taiyi: A Metacognitive Architecture for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12762)**  `arXiv:2611.12762`  `cs.AI`  
  _Weida Wang, Benteng Chen, Yigeng Jiang, Shuchen Pu, Weiping Shen, Zichao Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Sage-Taiyi, a metacognition-inspired architecture that uses accumulated scientific understanding to guide what to investigate next and how to investigate it. Through explicit monitoring and control, the system revises its understanding of evidence, assumptions, and unresolved questions, then uses these assessments to adapt research targets and select reusable reasoning methods. On Taiyi-Bench, an AI co-scientist ideation benchmark comprising 265 tasks from 50 papers across ten disciplines, Sage-Taiyi ranks first on all eight metrics of historical rediscovery and open-ended ideation, achieving an Overall score of 54.0 compared with 47.9 for the strongest baseline, AI Scientist-v2. It also performs well on two external scientific ideation benchmarks. Five expert-guided collaborations further connect its proposals to experimental and computational validation across chemistry, materials science, and engineering. In chemistry, reaction-condition optimization raises the yield of cobalt-catalyzed carbonylation from trace amounts to 84% and photocatalytic allylic amination from 13% to 69%. These results support metacognitive regulation as an approach to scientific ideation and its progression toward practical validation.
  </details>

- **[SciMARS: Multi-Agent Recursive Self-Improvement for Closed-Loop Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12552)**  `arXiv:2611.12552`  `cs.AI`  
  _Weimin Wu, Han Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose SciMARS, a framework for recursive task-local adaptation of multi-agent harnesses in closed-loop scientific discovery. Multi-agent systems coordinate analysis, experimental design, and verification as scientific evidence evolves. However, two problems limit their effectiveness: (i) without shared memory, agents lose observations and repeat work; (ii) fixed role procedures, handoffs, and memory policies perpetuate mistakes despite retained evidence. We address these problems with a shared evidence graph and separate component editors. SciMARS adapts roles and coordination; SciMARS + Memory adds a third editor for how agents record, retrieve, and receive evidence. Candidate probes guide composition and selection of the next harness. We evaluate scientific discovery through experimental-data replay and a digital twin. SciMARS + Memory resolves 12/18 GPT-6-Astra and 2/18 GLM-5.3 attempts on Autonomous Science Bench, compared with 8/18 and 1/18 for the base multi-agent team. On ScienceAgentBench and AppWorld, it solves 14/38 and 11/20 tasks, compared with 11/38 and 5/20 for the base team. These results support combining shared memory with harness adaptation across scientific and interactive tasks.
  </details>

- **[AuDiR: Diagnosing Audio Reasoning with Rule and Percept Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12478)**  `arXiv:2611.12478`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chengwei Wei, Yi Ma, Nancy F. Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answer accuracy alone does not distinguish failures in acoustic perception, rule knowledge, and rule application. AuDiR (Audio Diagnostic Reasoning) examines these distinctions through controlled input interventions. Each item contains an audio clip, a multiple-choice question, a task-general rule, and a textual description of the acoustic observations needed to answer. Three conditions progressively supply the rule and observations; a fourth removes the audio while retaining the question and rule. The first correct diagnostic condition assigns an operational response label, without identifying a unique internal cause. The current benchmark contains 201 items in 18 families drawn from 9 sources, with answer references documented separately from percept extraction. In a preliminary evaluation of Gemini-3.8-flash, accuracy is 29.3% without supplied information, 31.3% with the rule, and 98.5% with the observations also supplied. No-audio accuracy is 23.9%, with a confidence interval containing the 25.0% chance rate. A family-level knowledge probe associates incomplete rule recall with larger gains from rule provision. These results show the benefit of supplying acoustic observations as text, while incomplete validation, possible recognition shortcuts, and non-monotonic responses limit stronger failure-attribution claims.
  </details>

- **[DForkTree: Accelerating Speculative Decoding with Fork-Aware Block Diffusion Draft Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12356)**  `arXiv:2611.12356`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhicheng Zheng, Cheng Zhang, Chenkai Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding speeds up language models by letting a lightweight drafter propose several future tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Block diffusion drafters such as DFlash draft a whole block in one forward pass, and DDTree builds a draft tree from their per-position distributions. Each of these distributions assumes that the drafter's own most likely continuation is correct. A tree, however, gains over a chain only on branches that leave this continuation, where the drafter's top-1 accuracy drops from 89% to 18% and its candidate lists miss the right token far more often. When re-run from the fork token, the same drafter is usually correct, so the errors come from the prefix that the distributions condition on rather than from what the drafter knows. We introduce DForkTree, a training-free method that re-anchors each branch on its own tokens. DForkTree adds n-gram statistics from the target model's own outputs to the drafter's scores as pointwise mutual information, weights them more after the fork, and builds the optimal tree under these scores exactly on the GPU. On Qwen3.5-4B and 9B, with all tree methods sharing one drafter, tree pipeline and verifier, DForkTree is the fastest tree method on every benchmark. It is up to 41.5% faster than DFlash and 15.1% faster than DDTree in the same setting, with average speedups of up to 4.75x over autoregressive decoding.
  </details>

- **[AMSchem: Automated Schematic Visualization of AMS Circuits via Verifiable Agentic Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12333)**  `arXiv:2611.12333`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yichen Shi, Zhiyuan Wang, Jianing Cui, Yao Lai, Jinting Zhang, Zhuofu Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For decades, the netlist-to-schematic gap has remained a persistent challenge in design automation, especially for analog and mixed-signal (AMS) circuits. Netlists encode connectivity, not visual presentation, making their manual conversion into readable schematics time-consuming and dependent on expert conventions. The same gap leaves LLM-based design tools without structured visual feedback, forcing them to iterate on circuits they cannot see. We present AMSchem, a training-free, verifier-guided agentic framework that automatically generates human-readable schematics from AMS netlists. AMSchem separates circuit-aware visual planning (DrawingIntent) from executable schematic geometry (SchematicIR), and applies localized visual feedback with bounded, revalidated repair patches. Deterministic validators enforce device preservation, pin-net consistency, and geometric correctness throughout. For evaluation, we construct AMSchem-Bench comprising 64 circuits of 12 types. On the 40-circuit schematic subset, AMSchem with gpt-5.5 achieves 85.0\% validity (vs. 32.5\% direct generation). When integrated as a visual feedback module in LLM-based analog design, AMSchem resolves all 13 previously failed cases from AnalogCoder benchmark across five models, substantially improving the success rate. Our dataset and benchmark are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Toward Sustainable Agentic AI: Carbon Tradeoffs Across Iterative Self-Refinement and Tool-Augmented Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12128)**  `arXiv:2611.12128`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boris Ruf, Adrien de Mailly Nesle, Marcin Detyniecki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic language model systems can improve performance either by iterating on their own outputs or by using external tools in grounded environments. While these strategies can reduce error rates and substitute for larger models in some settings, their environmental cost remains relatively underexplored. We present two complementary empirical studies that estimate the energy and carbon footprint of agentic AI across settings of increasing complexity. Study~1 evaluates iterative verbal self-refinement: GPT-4.1-mini at $k = 1$--$5$ steps versus a GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline on two MATH benchmark splits. Study~2 evaluates tool-augmented agentic coding: GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Opus~4.6, and GPT-5.3-codex on SWE-bench Lite, where agents operate inside Docker-isolated environments with access to code execution and file editing tools. In Study~1, $k=3$ refinements close a 16-percentage-point accuracy gap to within 1~point, but nearly triple the carbon footprint relative to the cheap single-pass baseline while remaining below the GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline under the current token-based carbon scenario. In Study~2, GPT-5.3-codex achieves the highest resolution rate in this sample (81.2\%), ahead of Claude Opus~4.6 (68.9\%) and GPT-4.1-mini (25.0\%), but resolved-task-normalized efficiency remains mixed: GPT-4.1-mini is lowest on estimated carbon and cost per resolved task, while GPT-5.3-codex delivers substantially higher task success. All carbon estimates cover output-token inference only; prompt tokens, which exceed 99\% of tokens in Study~2, and tool execution are not charged, so the figures are lower bounds on the full agentic footprint.
  </details>

- **[System Optimizations for Cost-Efficient Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12122)**  `arXiv:2611.12122`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jongseok Park, Jiaxiang Yu, Eric Zhou, Tony Hong, Sunga Kim, Yusuf Afifi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agent harnesses play the critical role of providing LLMs with an interface to a computing environment. Recent studies show that AI agents' task-execution accuracy depends mostly on the LLM used, while harness-side variances such as system prompts or tools have minimal impact. Rather, such harness-side variances show a larger impact on the cost of agent executions, achieving cost reductions up to 2.6 times on the same model and task. To further explore these harness-side cost optimization opportunities, we analyze the cost accumulation patterns across various agent traces and identify system opportunities to reduce token use and LLM calls without modifying the model or affecting task accuracy. Based on this analysis, we design Agency, a harness interposition platform that provides a unified abstraction and allows easy and portable implementation of harness-side system optimizations on existing off-the-shelf harnesses such as Claude Code or OpenAI Codex. Using Agency, we implement proof-of-concept optimization techniques based on our analysis and achieve cost reductions up to 35% across various real-world benchmarks on existing harnesses without compromising accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Joi-Bench-Task: Benchmarking Task Coordination in Full-duplex Voice Agents through Interactive Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12118)**  `arXiv:2611.12118`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiangheng He, Mingjun Zhao, Zhehao Fan, Zhengjia Li, Xiaopeng Sun, Qingqing Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-duplex voice agents enable natural, hands-free interaction, allowing users to refine requests, redirect ongoing work, and seek clarification while the agent is speaking. However, existing voice-agent benchmarks insufficiently isolate coordination between task execution and conversation. We introduce a benchmark that assesses this coordination by examining whether agents track what is being executed, what has been completed, what has been communicated, and what remains to be reported as the conversation evolves. We propose an interactive user simulator that introduces configurable interventions at designated timing in tool execution and spoken result delivery, supporting controllable and reproducible evaluation conditions. We evaluate five leading commercial voice agents along three dimensions: execution responsiveness, coordination under request changes, and coordination under interruptions. Agent evaluations reveal persistent coordination failures. Under request changes, the highest result completion rate is only 55%, indicating difficulty for current voice agents adapting to new requests while preserving earlier obligations. After user interruption, four of the five agents exhibit average information omission rates of at least 23%. These findings highlight task coordination as a central challenge for current voice agents in complex, evolving conversations.
  </details>

- **[COMPASS: Composable Policy-Amortized Structured Search for LLM-Based Optimization Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12060)**  `arXiv:2611.12060`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nguyen Le Minh Hoang, Van Duc Cuong, Bui Trong Duc, Huynh Thi Thanh Binh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models offer a promising technique for translating natural-language optimization problems into solver code, but their reliability remains constrained by the quality of template retrieval. While recent structured methods improve over flat prompting through hierarchical taxonomy search, their traversal rules remain fixed or LLM-driven at inference time, leaving retrieval unable to improve from past successes and failures. This motivates treating template retrieval as a policy that can be learned from modeling experience, rather than as a static inference-time rule. We propose COMPASS, a policy-amortized framework for retrieving optimization templates in LLM-based solver modeling, which represents the template library as a directed acyclic graph and formulates retrieval as a sequential decision process over template nodes. A deep Q-network learns a reusable traversal policy from template-level judgments and execution feedback, reducing reliance on fixed heuristic traversal through feedback-driven graph navigation. To support extensible retrieval, COMPASS embeds problem descriptions and template nodes in a shared semantic space, allowing newly inserted templates to be scored from their text descriptions without redefining the action space. Experiments across multiple LLM backbones and optimization-modeling benchmarks demonstrate that COMPASS consistently improves over heuristic structured retrieval, with the largest gains on complex and mixed-category problems.
  </details>

- **[ChainCheck: Near-Perfect Evidence-Sufficiency Scores Can Conceal Sensitivity to Construction Artifacts -- Paired Conditional Contrasts that Expose an Evaluation Confound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11965)**  `arXiv:2611.11965`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hyojung Han, Ryangkyung Kang, Youngeun Kim, Seung-Hun Jeon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evidence-sufficiency benchmarks ask whether a model can tell a complete passage set from one whose reasoning chain is broken. When broken sets are made by editing text, near-perfect sufficiency scores--and even positive crossed-control scores--need not demonstrate multi-hop reasoning. We present ChainCheck, an evaluation-validity audit that pairs validator-certified counterfactual cells with artifact-only baselines. In an exploratory crossed control on 2,214 HotpotQA pairs, a language-model scorer that never reads the chain reaches a nominal AUC of 0.997, and a one-string match reaches the maximum of the crossed chain-sensitivity statistic CS4 (0.500), while the 11 evaluated systems score from 0.308 to 0.484; the two statistics are different metrics. We therefore read positive CS4 as operational bridge-consistency sensitivity, not as semantic multi-hop reasoning. Every system is also sensitive to one extra edit, and the edits are detectable according to model-based naturalness ratings. On an independent HotpotQA set of 2,276 pairs, patterns pre-specified on MuSiQue and 2WikiMultiHopQA did not replicate: 5 of 6 trained readouts were edit-dominated, and the reference judge's selectivity interval [-0.007, +0.042] includes zero. Judge scores also depended on batch layout. The MuSiQue and 2Wiki records are public; the HotpotQA records and the fine-tuned checkpoints are not, so external reproduction is partial.
  </details>

- **[CHORD: Multi-resolution Compression for Multimodal Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11913)**  `arXiv:2611.11913`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bibhus Luitel, Payal Mohapatra, Subrata Biswas, Ekata Poudel, Sunwoong Kim, Qi Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearables, mobile devices, and IoT platforms generate multimodal time-series data continuously and at scale, making efficient storage and transfer a primary concern. The same recordings are reused after collection by many downstream models, often at different temporal resolutions, so a compressed representation should be small, useful across resolutions, and produced at a bit rate known in advance. Prior work meets parts of this: symbolic and error-bounded compressors reconstruct accurately but fix resolution at compression time, learned codecs target one signal domain, and discrete tokenizers reconstruct without accounting for rate. We introduce CHORD, a coarse-to-fine compressor for heterogeneous multimodal time series. A residual cascade orders each payload from broad to fine temporal scales and synthesizes corrections through learned shapelet-inspired atoms. A shared pathway supplies a reusable synthesis basis across modalities, while private pathways retain capacity for sensor-specific morphology. Finite scalar quantization fixes the bit width of every modality and scale, so payload size is known before encoding, and scale dropout trains each coarse-to-fine prefix to be a valid reconstruction operating point. Across seven wearable-sensing and forecasting benchmarks, CHORD stays within 0.62 points of uncompressed classification accuracy on average at the 8x target, the smallest gap of any compressed method, with all resolutions decoded from one payload.
  </details>

- **[Layer Roles Matter: Mitigating Modality Imbalance in Multimodal Emotion Reasoning via Adaptive Layer Profiling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11907)**  `arXiv:2611.11907`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengyu Huang, Peipei Song, Beier Zhu, Zhiyuan Han, Jinpeng Hu, Xun Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Emotion Reasoning (MER) requires perceiving and integrating emotion cues from multiple modalities. Recent advances increasingly leverage Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to enhance reasoning capacity. However, due to the inherent heterogeneity in conveying emotions across modalities, these models often suffer from modality imbalance and confront flawed emotion understanding. In this paper, we conduct an in-depth analysis on MER models and uncover two key empirical insights. (i) In MER, modality imbalance manifests two distinct failure modes: unimodal over-reliance and insufficient cross-modal utilization, which call for different intervention strategies. (ii) MLLM layers exhibit implicit functional hierarchies in multimodal emotion processing, playing separable roles in stabilizing unimodal representations and integrating cross-modal information. Motivated by these observations, we propose a Layer-Aware Modality Rebalancing (LAMR) framework by explicitly aligning intervention strategies with layer roles. Specifically, we first introduce two quantized metrics for adaptive layer role profiling. In each layer, Modality Entropy (ME) measures the dispersion of modality contributions, and Cross-modal Intensity (CMI) captures the strength of cross-modal interactions. Then, we respectively enhance modality equilibrium in stabilization layers and cross-modal propagation in integration layers. Extensive experiments on two comprehensive MER benchmarks demonstrate that LAMR effectively mitigates modality imbalance and improves emotion reasoning across diverse MLLM backbones, without additional training.
  </details>

- **[LEGION: Benchmarking Language Agents on Sparse-Signal Investigation Under Adversarial Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11896)**  `arXiv:2611.11896`  `cs.AI`  
  _Samuel Joseph Amouyal, Ofek Glick, Yaniv Menasherov, Eyal Fisher, Ohav Barbi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous language-model agents are increasingly deployed on discovery-driven tasks where signal is sparse and most retrieved information is irrelevant. Current benchmarks fail to evaluate this regime due to dense signals, cooperative environments, and ground truth typically being only a few tool calls away. We introduce Legion, a benchmark framework coupling a stochastic enterprise-telemetry simulator (Legion-Sim) with a tiered library of 74 expertly-crafted adversarial scenarios (Legion-Bench) and an evaluation protocol that scores investigation efficiency alongside detection accuracy. Scenarios are injected into freshly simulated companies, allowing independent control over confounder density and event volume. We test five agent architectures on two simulated companies and find that while recall is high (0.82-0.88) precision lags behind (0.61--0.72). They fail to find all of the indicators of compromise of the attack or mistake genuine indicators with attack related ones, which is critical in the context of cybersecurity investigation. Failures are consistent across reasoning tiers and persist across confounder density and event volume. We release Legion, the scenario library, performance on common agent architectures, and pipeline for reproducibility and extension.
  </details>

- **[MOTIF: LLM-Defined Material Taxonomies for OOD Property Prediction via Invariant Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11846)**  `arXiv:2611.11846`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gwangseon Jang, Sieun Yun, Dong Ho Lee, Kyong-Ha Lee, Chanuk Lim, Jin Woo Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization remains a central challenge in materials property prediction, where models must extrapolate to novel chemistries beyond the training distribution. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong scientific reasoning, their direct use for quantitative prediction remains limited. To address this, we systematically evaluate multiple roles for LLM assistance, finding that only taxonomy-based grouping consistently improves OOD generalization over a strong kNN+GBT baseline. Building on this, we here propose MOTIF, a framework that leverages LLMs not as predictors, but as generators of candidate taxonomies that partition materials into chemically meaningful environments. These taxonomies are evaluated via training-only pseudo-OOD validation, and only those that improve generalization are retained. Invariant feature selection is then applied within each accepted taxonomy, followed by gated aggregation across taxonomies. We evaluate on 2D halide perovskites, where bandgap prediction must generalize across distinct organic spacer families. On this benchmark, MOTIF reduces mean OOD MAE from 0.122 to 0.111 eV. Under a frozen dev/test protocol, the deployable configuration further improves errors from 0.123 to 0.117 eV (electronic bandgap) and from 0.181 to 0.173 eV (optical bandgap). Beyond this primary task, we validate transferability across additional materials datasets, including 3D perovskite solar cell efficiencies and Matbench benchmarks with structure-based splits. Across domains, MOTIF consistently improves robustness under distribution shift, outperforming expert-defined groupings, rule-based heuristics, and clustering baselines, while remaining competitive with strong classical selection methods. These results demonstrate that LLMs are most effective not as predictors, but as tools for discovering structure in data.
  </details>

- **[RADE: Risk-Adjusted Deployment Efficiency, A Framework for Routing, Memory, and Escalation in Safe LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11659)**  `arXiv:2611.11659`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chaymaa Abbas, Kareem Hassani, Mariette Awad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A deployed language model does not merely answer questions. It spends computation, consumes time, consults evidence, exposes memory, invites or avoids human review, and sometimes makes mistakes whose consequences are radically unequal. Yet LLM systems are still commonly compared as if the relevant object were a single model and the relevant outcome were a single benchmark score. This paper argues that the unit of analysis should instead be the deployment policy: the mechanism that decides when to retrieve, when to remember, when to call a larger model, when to verify, and when not to answer at all. We introduce risk-adjusted deployment efficiency (RADE), a system-level objective that scores the utility a deployment delivers net of inference, retry, oversight, and priced expected failure cost, while treating harms that cannot be priced as hard budgets alongside constraints on latency, privacy, and resources. The framework brings selective routing, retrieval and governed memory, efficient specialization, uncertainty estimation, abstention, and human escalation into a common account. We then examine five deployment regimes in which the same nominal LLM capability leads to different rational architectures, and propose a reporting protocol based on risk-coverage behavior, end-to-end cost, update drift, memory exposure, evidence quality, and escalation load. The central claim is modest but consequential: frontier capability should be treated as a resource to allocate, not a default state to maintain. A safe and sustainable system is not the one that always uses the strongest model; it is the one that spends capability where the reduction in risk or increase in utility justifies the cost.
  </details>

- **[Optimizing Multi-Tier Intermediation in Programmatic Advertising: A DAG-Based Mechanism for Supply Path Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11651)**  `arXiv:2611.11651`  `cs.AI`  
  _Amelia Bădică, Costin Bădică_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Digital programmatic advertising operates through complex, multi-hop supply chains. The opacity of these intermediary networks leads to significant efficiency losses, often referred to as the ``ad-tech tax.'' While current industry practices utilize heuristic Supply Path Optimization (SPO) to trim inefficient routes, they fail to optimize the equilibrium of the entire network. This paper extends previous work on Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) intermediation models by applying them to traceable programmatic supply chains mapped via the OpenRTB SupplyChain object. We introduce multi-attribute utility functions and Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) to account for substitutable routing (OR nodes) and percentage-based fee structures. Furthermore, we outline a computational methodology and experimental framework using public \texttt{schain} and \texttt{this http URL} data to benchmark optimal Nash social welfare against current uncoordinated market outcomes.
  </details>

- **[LiveAsset: Learning Intent Assets from Corrective Interactions for Agent Runtime Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11560)**  `arXiv:2611.11560`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guanming Liu, Weihang Wang, Peng Zhang, Meng Wu, Kainan Tu, Jiahao Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents acting across web applications and online services often need multi-turn corrections to align their work with users' underspecified goals and preferences. In practice, users may identify problems in the current outcome without specifying the desired result, so learning user intentions requires interpreting each correction against the agent's preceding and revised outcomes. These expectations are often connected to a particular context, making it hard to know when they apply and how to ensure the agent later acts as the user expects. To this end, we propose LiveAsset, an intent harness that learns reusable intent assets from corrective interactions and uses them for runtime control of agent execution. LiveAsset jointly interprets each correction with the agent's original and revised results to infer user intent, incrementally updating the assets while preserving earlier requirements that remain applicable. The learned requirements guide agent execution from task entry and provide criteria for runtime verification, which uses the explanations to assess applicability before each reply is delivered and directs the agent to revise the result to address unmet requirements while preserving those already satisfied. Following a learning phase in which simulated users progressively reveal requirements through multi-turn corrections, we evaluate LiveAsset using same-task replay of the original requests on {\pi}-Bench, PERMA, and Vita and cross-task transfer on PAST-Bench and SkillEvolBench. Experiments show that LiveAsset better fulfills requirements learned from corrective interactions and improves performance on these benchmarks compared with memory, skill, and rule-based harness baselines. Code is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11470)**  `arXiv:2611.11470`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitian Pang, Yifan Li, Xiaowei Yue, Li Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.
  </details>

- **[BiVerify: Closing the Loop Between Backward and Forward Verification for Mathematical Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11368)**  `arXiv:2611.11368`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Xin Zhao, Mengqi Wei, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning but still produce incorrect solutions to complex problems, indicating the need for more reliable self-correction mechanisms. Mathematical self-correction typically identifies errors through verification, which can proceed backward or forward. The two directions provide complementary verification capabilities. Backward verification checks a candidate answer against the original constraints, bypassing complex intermediate reasoning, but cannot ensure correct reasoning or objective completeness. Forward verification follows the problem conditions and current solution, enabling it to locate reasoning errors and assess objective completeness, but may overlook key constraints in complex solution chains. We propose BiVerify, a dynamically scheduled framework for bidirectional mathematical verification. BiVerify begins with backward verification to screen the answer against the original constraints, followed by forward verification of the reasoning process; subsequent direction switching, state resetting, and re-verification are governed by verification outcomes. Upon failure, the system revises the candidate or verification flow until the same candidate passes both directions. We also introduce shared mathematical tools that separate logical reasoning from precise computation. We systematically evaluate BiVerify on five challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks using multiple mainstream DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini models. BiVerify exceeds the strongest representative public baseline evaluated in this work by 13.5 percentage points in average accuracy, with the margin reaching 31.2 percentage points on GPT. Further analysis shows that BiVerify's overall performance exceeds that of either verification direction alone.
  </details>

- **[When Self-Consistency Hurts: The Hidden Cost of Majority Voting in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11201)**  `arXiv:2611.11201`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanrong Yang, Lingfang Li, Jun Zhang, Dominik Wojtczak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study prompted chain-of-thought (CoT) with self-consistency (SC), i.e. sampling N traces and plurality-voting, on small language models, a regime where per-query compute budgets are tight yet SC is commonly applied with a fixed sample budget inherited from large-model practice, regardless of instance difficulty. Across two model families (Qwen-2.5-0.5B, Gemma-3-1B) at FP16 and MLX-Q4 (4-bit quantisation in Apple's MLX framework), on GSM8K and MATH-500, we run 8 per-task and 4 task-pooled experiments at n=200-400 with 1,000x paired-bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs). Our first result is an upper bound: an oracle gate deciding per prompt whether to invoke SC reaches 109-174% of always-SC's accuracy at 23-30% of its compute in all 12/12 cases, so at N=5 always-SC is dominated everywhere on our grid. That margin is exactly the rate at which SC flips correct greedy answers to wrong, here 3-10.5% of prompts, which on Qwen-Q4 x MATH-500 exceeds the wrong-to-right rate and leaves always-SC 3 pt worse than always-greedy. Our second result is a deployable approximation: a gate over greedy-pass signals skips 45-99% of SC calls, with bootstrap CI half-widths under 5 pt, removing 36-79% of always-SC compute. Accuracy retention at those operating points has point estimates of 98.1-98.8%, which we report as point estimates because their CIs are wide (lower bounds 76-86%) over an SC accuracy of only 15-36%. The evidence covers N=5 on two <=1B families and two math benchmarks, which is the scope we claim. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Keeping LLM Agents on Procedure: Executable Finite-State Control for Long-Horizon Instruction Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10884)**  `arXiv:2611.10884`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yutao Sun, Haokun Li, Mingshuai Chen, Guanyan Li, Zhongyi Wang, Shengchao Qin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Much of the automation handed to language-model agents is procedural: each step needs little intelligence, but a long unattended run must follow a skill or SOP stated once at the start. We show that agents drift from such procedures over long runs, and that restating the instructions or resuming a stopped agent only moves the drift to another shortcut. We study an executable finite-state machine (FSM) that keeps the workflow state outside the model's context and enforces it at three points: tool calls, state transitions and the end of the agent's turn. In a pre-registered experiment on 200-account SOP-Bench batches, Claude Haiku 4.5 with the FSM decided on average 139 accounts correctly and by the procedure, against 48 for the strongest baseline (95% CI of the difference [46, 130]), and kept this rate to the end; across three models, the gain tracked how much the plain agent drifted. Because the runtime, not the model, supplies this long-horizon reliability, the FSM can let small models carry procedural work: Haiku 4.5 with it averaged more such decisions than plain Claude Sonnet 4.6 at 38% of the cost. Analyses on SWE-bench Verified, $\tau^2$-bench, and staged and cross-context workflows further suggest that reliable long-horizon automation can come from stronger execution control, not only stronger models.
  </details>

- **[Inference for Reasoning Models: Practical Lessons from Evaluation and Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10845)**  `arXiv:2611.10845`  `cs.AI`  
  _Luv Verma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Running a language model involves more than its trained weights. The same model can receive very different benchmark scores, produce different answers, or cost more to serve depending on how it is evaluated and run. In one experiment in this book, changing only the maximum response length moved accuracy on a math benchmark from 0.08% to 93.18%.This book explains how inference settings shape the results we report and the systems we deploy. We begin with evaluation, including generation limits, answer extraction, scoring methods, and quantization. We then examine why outputs can change across batches, attention backends, and caching, even when sampling is disabled. The next chapters cover serving performance, including batching, memory capacity, latency, throughput, and cost. We also study structured outputs, tool calls, reasoning budgets, and long-context tasks, showing what these controls and benchmarks do and do not establish.The book concludes with practical guidance for recording inference runs, checking evaluations, estimating capacity, and comparing systems fairly. Written for researchers and engineers working with language models, it aims to make inference results easier to understand, reproduce, and trust.
  </details>

- **[WARP-JEPA: World-Model-in-the-Loop Amortized Rollout Planning for Frozen Hierarchical Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10806)**  `arXiv:2611.10806`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junjie Li, Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Kaixiang Wang, Zihan Wang, QingSong Zhao, Jiong Lou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (H-JEPA) enable long-horizon visual control but rely on costly test-time gradient descent for planning. Our analysis reveals that iterative optimization consumes up to 97.7% of episode runtime and that low predicted rollout costs do not necessarily translate into successful control. We further identify a planning-horizon asymmetry between long-horizon top-level composition and short two-step subordinate planning. To address these challenges, we propose WARP-JEPA, a hierarchical amortized planning framework that replaces online gradient optimization with learned multi-candidate trajectory proposers while keeping the world models frozen. WARP-JEPA combines a lightweight world-model-in-the-loop causal Transformer at the top level with parallel residual MLPs at subordinate levels. The proposers are trained using model-based rollout costs and best-of-K behavior cloning, enabling efficient candidate selection without test-time gradient optimization. Across 30 frozen H-JEPA models on four benchmarks, WARP-JEPA achieves 9.6-18.8x faster episode inference on long-horizon tasks (2.9-3.0x on Cube) and improves task success by up to 33.3 percentage points. These results demonstrate that amortized planning can substantially improve both inference efficiency and control performance without retraining the world models. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BlenderFORGE: Framework for Optimizing Reactive 3D-Graphics Editing Ability of MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10768)**  `arXiv:2611.10768`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zilin Guo, Tianrui Zhang, Yi Rong, Yichen Liu, Yuxin Guo, Jingcheng Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown promise as interactive agents, yet precise, programmatic 3D graphics editing remains difficult to train because existing systems largely rely on closed-source model APIs and lack deterministic, state-verifiable execution environments. We present BlenderFORGE, a trainable data-environment-optimization framework for open-weight MLLM agents that edit explicit Blender scenes through Blender Python (bpy) code generation. BlenderFORGE targets four fundamental state-verifiable editing task families: object placement, shape-key editing, lighting adjustment, and material modification. It integrates three components: (1) a scalable perturbation-based data generation pipeline built from curated assets and large-scale 3D scene repositories; (2) an isolated Blender Sandbox for deterministic code execution, structured state export, and closed-loop reward computation; and (3) a two-stage training pipeline that uses offline teacher trajectories for cold-start Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and sandbox-computed rewards for multi-turn tool-agentic Reinforcement Learning (AgentLoop-RL). Experiments across the four task families show that the resulting open-weight Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B BlenderFORGE agents substantially improve over their base models and achieve competitive performance with several zero-shot proprietary MLLM baselines on selected metrics. Additional cross-benchmark evaluations and camera-reframing experiments provide evidence of transfer beyond the original training setting.
  </details>

- **[No Second Run: Does RSI Stop at Frontier Pre-training?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10719)**  `arXiv:2611.10719`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaojie Cai, Jiayi Chen, Junwei Chen, Yu Chen, Sikai Cheng, Jinquan Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-training is where every foundation model begins; bringing recursive self-improvement (RSI) there would let AI improve the process that creates AI. Yet public RSI work has so far targeted only post-training, which starts from an existing base model, runs trials costing GPU-hours, and returns verifiable scores. Pre-training breaks all three conditions at orders-of-magnitude higher cost: a run consumes hundreds of thousands of GPU-hours over months and cannot be repeated; its objective, post-training headroom, cannot be read off any single score; and what must improve is not a model but the whole production system, from data pipelines and training infrastructure to the teams that run them. To our knowledge, we are the first to apply RSI to an entire frontier pre-training run from scratch and to report it in detail. RSI does not stop at frontier pre-training, but it changes form: since no end-to-end loop can close over a single unrepeatable run, we decompose it into smaller loops that can, each repairing a step the full run breaks. The result is 801-30B-A3B-Base, a 30B-A3B MoE trained from scratch on 11.8T tokens in three months by a newly formed team. We release it as a base model; the lightweight SFT reported here (6B tokens, no RL) only probes post-training headroom. As a base model, 801-30B-A3B outperforms Qwen3-30B-A3B and Nemotron-3.5-Lightning-30B-A3B, trailing only the larger Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, and keeps this position under identical SFT; with lightweight SFT alone, it achieves the highest average on four agentic benchmarks against fully post-trained models, including Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Since post-training RSI presupposes a strong base model, bringing RSI into pre-training acts on the foundation every later stage builds on. We open-source 801-30B-A3B-Base and the lightweight SFT data atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RankShift: Evaluating Ranking Instability Across Benchmark Dimensions in Document Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10679)**  `arXiv:2611.10679`  `cs.AI`  
  _Halimat Afolabi, Daeyoung Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document parsing benchmarks are often treated as stable measures of model quality, yet rankings may depend substantially on benchmark design choices. In this work, we present RankShift, a systematic study comparing OCR and vision-language models (VLMs) under controlled variation in dataset composition, matching strategy, and scoring methodology. Using OmniDocBench as a case study, we evaluate the ranking stability of 16 models across 3 dataset configurations, 6 matching strategies, and 3 scoring metrics. We quantify rank stability using pairwise rank correlation, top-k churn, and pairwise reversal rate. Our results show that the interaction between matching strategy and ground truth format is a substantial but understudied driver of instability: scoring against OmniDocBench's JSON annotations versus its Markdown rendering yields a mean Kendall's tau of approximately 0.33 under region-level matching strategies, with individual pairs as low as tau = 0.23, compared with tau = 0.77 when each page is scored as a single concatenated string. Dataset composition produces comparable instability across page-type strata (mean tau = 0.48) and layout-type strata (mean tau = 0.49), while the cross-dataset shift from OmniDocBench to Real5's Scanning reconstruction of the same pages is more stable (tau = 0.80). Metric choice is the most stable dimension, with mean tau of approximately 0.78 across NED, BLEU, and METEOR. These findings indicate that leaderboard position is not solely a property of model capability, motivating leaderboards that report not only headline scores but also the robustness of rankings across reasonable design choices.
  </details>
