- **[Agentic discrete-event simulation for manufacturing system design: an open 3D simulator, a benchmark and an industrial case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10602)**  `arXiv:2611.10602`  `cs.AI`  
  _Javier Pernas-Álvarez, Diego Crespo-Pereira_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The design of manufacturing systems relies on discrete-event simulation (DES) models that experts still build in tool-specific interfaces. AI agents can now write simulation code, but every model they write from scratch is new, unverified software. We present SimuLean, an open-source 3D DES simulator on the Unity engine designed to be programmed by AI agents: its modelling semantics are verified once, in the platform, and the agent builds and studies one model through domain-level commands, in the 3D scene or headless; physically simulated durations are calibrated for fast headless studies. We evaluated it on 18 manufacturing modelling tasks, from single machines to published production lines. A frontier agent built valid models in 93 of 108 attempts (86%), in about two minutes each; on the benchmark's interface version, two frontier agents built 105 of 108 models through domain commands alone, so that each comes with a record of how it was built. Every residual failure was silent - a model that builds and runs but answers wrongly - and only comparison with reference models revealed them all. In an industrial case, one agent working alone rebuilt, verified and studied a robotised welding workshop from a published study, in SimuLean and in SimPy. Comparing them with the published, expert-built model exposed an error in it; against the corrected model, the agent-built models agree within 1%. One agent can already build complete, realistic and traceable models for the design of manufacturing systems; independent reference models are what make them trustworthy.
  </details>

- **[InfoOrgBench: Can Agents Turn Scattered Information into Coherent Hierarchies?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10600)**  `arXiv:2611.10600`  `cs.AI`  
  _Justice Ou, Lanming Ma, Zijian Zhang, Yilun Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When agents organize records into folders, they may correctly group the excerpts of a design proposal yet place the proposal under the wrong owning group. Existing agent benchmarks do not directly measure such higher-level errors. We introduce InfoOrgBench, a benchmark of 150 tasks in which agents organize scattered records from meeting recaps, issue threads and design proposals into hierarchies. Integrity checks verify that every record is preserved, and a Structure Score measures agreement with the source hierarchy at every level, independent of folder names and order. We evaluate 45 systems that pair nine models with five agent harnesses. The best system scores 83.2 out of 100, but no system exceeds 78 on meeting recaps or issue threads. Model choice affects scores far more than harness choice: model means span about 27 points, whereas the average effect of each harness is below one point. More importantly, parent-level relations have higher error rates than leaf-level relations in all three domains. In design proposals, nearly half of the outputs analyzed by level make exactly this kind of error. These results show that evaluating information organization requires measuring higher-level relations separately from record preservation and local grouping.
  </details>

- **[D-REAL: Acceptance-Length Optimization with Unbiased Gradients for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10432)**  `arXiv:2611.10432`  `cs.AI`  
  _Meng Wei, Yefei Chen, Antong Li, Yanan Gao, Xingliang Shi, Xin Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding efficiency depends on the number of consecutively accepted draft tokens, yet token-level training objectives do not directly optimize this quantity. We introduce D-REAL (Drafter Reinforcement of Expected Acceptance Length), a post-training method that directly optimizes expected acceptance length for a fixed decoding context. D-REAL weights position-wise draft-target distributional overlap by prefix survival probabilities along a draft-sampled prefix, faithfully capturing the sequential dependence of verification on earlier proposals. By accounting for the parameter dependence of the proposal distribution, we derive an unbiased gradient estimator for this single-round objective under stochastic verification. We evaluate D-REAL on DSpark and DFlash2 with Qwen3-8B as the target model across ten benchmarks. Starting from baselines whose mean acceptance length has plateaued, one epoch of D-REAL post-training consistently improves the benchmark-averaged mean acceptance length by up to 0.23 tokens across both architectures under greedy (T = 0.0) and stochastic (T = 1.0) sampling, translating into up to a 9.4% end-to-end serving throughput improvement.
  </details>

- **[TurnFlow: Benchmarking User-State Reasoning in Multi-Turn LLM Agents via Scene-Guided User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10412)**  `arXiv:2611.10412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhixun Chen, Ming Li, Anjie Liu, Xiaoyuan Zhang, Quanyu Dai, Huangxun Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed through conversational interfaces, yet strong single-turn performance does not imply reliability in multi-turn settings. Multi-turn assistance requires a model to maintain an evolving representation of user requirements and to reason over it before complying with later requests. Failures can therefore be subtle: models may fail to recognize a changed constraint or hidden conflict, or recognize it without enforcing it in the final response. Existing benchmarks primarily test fully specified prompts, long-context recall, or controlled domain environments, leaving open-domain requirement reconciliation undermeasured. We introduce TurnFlow, an interactive benchmark for evaluating whether models preserve, revise, and act on user requirements across turns. TurnFlow combines adaptive user simulation with verifiable judging, allowing conversations to unfold naturally while keeping hidden requirements and pass/fail criteria controlled. In our evaluation, nine LLMs achieve overall pass rates from 42.86% to 59.59% on 490 scenarios. Performance varies substantially across challenge types: models perform well on Stateful Multi-Constraint Following but remain considerably less reliable on Scope Creep and Goal Shift. These results reveal persistent limitations in reasoning over evolving user requirements across turns.
  </details>

- **[Tail-risk-aware Agent Safety: Constraining Tail Risks Across Multi-step LLM Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10411)**  `arXiv:2611.10411`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shilong Pan, Zhiliang Tian, Zihan Chen, Wentao Zou, Weiguo Chen, DongSheng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents extend LLMs with tool calls, memory, planning, and retrieval to act in external environments, but they are vulnerable to attacks at different workflow steps. Existing defenses, including prompt engineering and post-training alignment, primarily improve LLM-only safety and often overlook risks that arise from multi-step execution. External verification adds guardrails to check each step separately, but it typically ignores how risks can propagate across steps. We argue that we should consider risk transitions across dependent steps through the agent workflow, rather than separate verification on a single step. In this paper, we propose \textbf{T}ail-risk-aware \textbf{A}gent \textbf{S}afety (TAS), a tail-risk-aware safety framework that models agent workflow safety as a sequence of dependent safety states and constrains the rare but extreme risks (i.e., tail risk) with a shared budget. TAS first models safety-state transitions sequentially along the workflow as an MDP to trace risk propagation, then measures a tail-sensitive risk to target rare but high-cost risks (i.e., tail risk), and constrains the risk with a shared budget to derive a step-wise safety strategy. By constraining tail risk rather than the overall average, TAS suppresses rare high-cost failures while preserving utility in normal cases. Experiments show that TAS achieves better performance compared with eleven baselines across three benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[TripleGrader: A Web-Based Platform Supporting Collaborative Triple Annotation with Quality Grading and Inter-Rater Agreement Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10317)**  `arXiv:2611.10317`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rajan Lamichhane, Songhui Yue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs need validated relational triples, and gold benchmarks of such triples are often built by several annotators. Most text annotation platforms focus on span tagging and compute agreement on categorical labels. They are not designed for ordinal quality grading of triples that have already been extracted. We present TripleGrader, an open-source web platform for grading subject-predicate-object triples on a 0 to 10 ordinal scale. In TripleGrader, each triple is scored independently, and peer scores stay hidden until the grader has rated it. Graders then discuss entries in threaded comments and propose revisions, which the session owner approves and publishes as new versions. For agreement, the platform computes quadratic-weighted Cohen's kappa for two raters and a quadratic-weighted Fleiss-type kappa for three or more. Graded sessions and the final benchmark are exported in common formats. We evaluated the platform in three ways. We ran 72 automated tests, including 18 workflow scenarios through the REST API. We also injected 10 error types to check whether prescribed corrections could be applied while preserving unaffected triples. On two small corpora with existing gold benchmarks, we then used the platform to curate the triples extracted by four large language models into each benchmark. Only 10% to 32% of the candidate triples could be kept unchanged, and 53% to 71% needed corrections, partly to match the benchmarks' naming conventions. Per model, 11 to 29 missing triples had to be added. On these corpora, the extracted triples therefore needed substantial human review. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Ensemble Routing for Prompt-Adaptive Cost-Aware LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10193)**  `arXiv:2611.10193`  `cs.AI`  
  _Saul Urso, Emanuele Carlini, Patrizio Dazzi, Matteo Mordacchini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly support Web-based services that must balance response quality and inference cost across heterogeneous requests. Conventional routing selects one model per prompt, while fixed ensembles cannot adapt their composition and cost to individual requests. We introduce PISA, a prompt-adaptive, cost-aware single-model router, and PISA-E, its extension to ensemble routing. At the core of both methods, PISA combines soft cluster memberships with model-specific predictors to estimate individual model capabilities. Building on these estimates, PISA-E jointly selects ensemble composition and cardinality under an explicit cost--quality preference that can be adjusted at inference time without retraining. A tractable ensemble-success surrogate and efficient candidate selection enable this extension without ensemble-specific training. Across four LLM routing benchmarks under in-distribution (ID) and out-of-distribution (OOD) conditions, PISA achieves the highest macro-average cost--performance hypervolume among the evaluated single-model routers in both settings. Meanwhile, PISA-E achieves the highest macro-average task performance among the evaluated ensemble approaches when prioritizing response quality, surpassing the strongest competitors by 2.09 and 2.87 percentage points under ID and OOD conditions, respectively. At selected lower-cost operating points, it also exceeds the strongest ensemble competitors' macro-average task performance while reducing average inference cost by approximately 36% and 25%, respectively. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of prompt-adaptive capability estimation for both single-model routing and selective ensembling.
  </details>

- **[AutoEMatic: Runtime-Adaptive Mixed-Precision LLM Inference via Automatic Exponent-Mantissa Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10165)**  `arXiv:2611.10165`  `cs.AI`  
  _Akshat Ramachandran, Reena Elangovan, Rangharajan Venkatesan, Charbel Sakr, Tijmen Blankevoort, Brucek Khailany, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing LLM post-training quantization techniques determine precision before inference begins, yet the precision a model requires varies dynamically across prompts, layers, and inference phases. We present AutoEMatic, a full-stack co-design that adapts the precision of weights, activations, and the KV cache at runtime. A lightweight policy selects each layer's precision at every prefill and decode step from two runtime signals: the entropy of the next-token distribution and the magnitude of each layer's update to its hidden states. This runtime adaptation is made practical by AutoFP, a multi-scale floating-point format that derives 2-, 4-, and 6-bit precisions from a single NVFP4 base format. We realize AutoEMatic on two targets: GPU kernels that execute AutoFP on NVFP4 Tensor Cores, and an accelerator whose vector MAC datapath executes all AutoFP formats natively. Across reasoning and agentic benchmarks, AutoEMatic improves accuracy by up to 21.7% over NVFP4 and matches group-scaled FP6 accuracy on most benchmarks, using 4.9 bits per weight versus 4.5 for NVFP4. On GB300 GPUs, AutoEMatic retains approximately 90-98% of NVFP4 decode throughput across the evaluated configurations. Furthermore, the AutoEMatic accelerator reduces latency by 1.6-2.3x and energy by 7-23% compared to an accelerator with NVFP4-only datapath at iso-accuracy.
  </details>

- **[WorldMind: Test-Time Scaling for Visual Spatial Reasoning through Latent World Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10151)**  `arXiv:2611.10151`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoming Wang, Yongjian Fu, Ju Ren, Wei Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have continuous deficiency in spatial reasoning tasks that require \emph{mental spatial transformation}, such as reasoning under hypothetical changes in viewpoint or position, known as perspective-taking tasks. Existing approaches either scale linguistic reasoning or augment VLMs with static geometric representations, but neither directly enables active transformation of the represented scene. World models offer this capability, yet existing systems typically communicate through synthesized RGB images, introducing a latent-to-pixel-to-latent bottleneck. We introduce \emph{WorldMind}, a framework for test-time scaling of spatial reasoning through latent world simulation. Given a query and observation, the VLM selects useful spatial transformations, while a world model executes them and exposes aligned intermediate latent features directly to the VLM. The resulting latent evidence is incorporated into subsequent reasoning steps, enabling iterative spatial simulation without repeatedly decoding imagined states into pixels. To optimize this interaction, WorldMind further employs process-verifiable reinforcement learning that rewards reasoning progress only when the associated action and latent transition are valid. WorldMind thus scales spatial reasoning by allocating additional inference-time computation to active world simulation rather than generating more language or pixels. Across VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench, and MindCube-Tiny, WorldMind achieves an average improvement of 16.0% over the base VLM and 8.3% over the strongest RGB-based world simulation.
  </details>

- **[Don't Blame The Preferences: Examining The Emergence of Sycophancy in LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10045)**  `arXiv:2611.10045`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Peter Yang, Catherine Zehr, Pranav Bijith, Brandon Fain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI sycophancy, the tendency of large language models (LLMs) to flatter users and affirm their beliefs, can undermine accuracy and contribute to misinformation, dependence, and overconfidence. Although prior work has benchmarked sycophancy in released models and proposed mitigations, its origins during training remain poorly understood. Preference alignment methods such as RLHF and DPO are often blamed, but their effects are rarely compared with earlier training stages. We investigate sycophancy across the training pipelines of three model families and three benchmarks. We find that sycophancy is largely established by the end of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), rather than introduced by preference alignment. Across all nine model benchmark combinations, SFT checkpoints attain at least 92% of preference aligned models' sycophancy scores and at least 85% of final models' scores; on average, they are about as sycophantic as final models. Preference alignment increases sycophancy in only four of nine cases, by an average of 4.2 percentage points when it does. To probe the underlying causes, we isolate model representations associated with sycophancy using mechanistic interpretability and measure their activation on training datasets from each stage. SFT datasets show roughly the same average activation as other training data but contribute substantially more examples, and the highest-scoring individual datasets all come from SFT. Our findings suggest that addressing sycophancy requires interventions throughout the training pipeline, particularly in data selection and augmentation for pre-training and SFT.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Agent Trajectory Compression: A Workflow-Aware Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08865)**  `arXiv:2611.08865`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Rui Liu, Wenqi Hu, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As long-horizon agents increasingly rely on multi-step tool interactions to accomplish complex tasks, their execution trajectories continuously accumulate structured outputs and intermediate artifacts, leading to growing context overhead. Existing compression methods primarily rely on text importance or fixed truncation rules, often overlooking cross-step dependencies within workflows and potentially discarding critical information required for subsequent task execution. To address this challenge, we propose WAAC, a workflow-aware framework for agent trajectory compression. WAAC structurally models successful historical trajectories to extract reusable workflow compression experience and incorporates online backward dependency analysis to dynamically identify information retention requirements. By combining current dependency constraints with historical experience, WAAC selects appropriate compression strategies for different types of historical information. Experiments on PinchBench and Odysseys demonstrate that WAAC achieves an overall score of 87.6 and a perfect completion rate of 19.0%, respectively, while reducing execution costs by 19.7% and 29.1% compared with the strongest baseline methods. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of workflow-aware trajectory compression in improving task performance and cost efficiency for long-horizon agents. Code and resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PCBRouteAgent: How Close Are LLM Agents to Becoming PCB Routing Engineers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08175)**  `arXiv:2611.08175`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Zhang, Jilei Sun, Liqiang Jing, Yijie Wei, Ruohan Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automating printed circuit board (PCB) routing requires coordinating board-level connectivity decisions with geometric execution under strict design rules. Although recent agents can interact with native routing tools, reliable routing on complex boards remains challenging. We present PCBRouteAgent, a multimodal PCB routing agent inspired by how human engineers coordinate global strategy with local execution. A Global Routing Planner combines board images and structured electrical and geometric information to specify connection topology, routing order, corridors, and layer transitions. A Detailed Routing Executor realizes these guides through KiCad's native routing engine and performs bounded local recovery. Unresolved failures are returned as structured feedback, enabling targeted guide revisions while preserving accepted routes. We evaluate PCBRouteAgent on 247 synthetic and real-world boards from four PCBWorld-Bench test splits. It achieves clean-pass rates of 100%, 94.9%, 100%, and 40% on D2, D3-A, D3-B, and D3-C, respectively. On the three real-world subsets, PCBRouteAgent outperforms the best evaluated baseline in clean-pass rate by 13.9, 36, and 10 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[SAVeR$^2$: Reasoning-based Safety Alignment for Large Reasoning Models via Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07482)**  `arXiv:2611.07482`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yichen Sun, Qirui Liu, Yan Wang, Jianan Lin, Linbo Jiang, Shiyu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have achieved impressive performance via explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, yet this process introduces critical safety risks. We formalize LRM safety objectives as requiring non-malicious reasoning and answers, safety consistency, and explicit intent identification. We argue that existing alignment methods suffer from poor adversarial generalization, a significant ``safety tax'' on reasoning, and unfaithful coarse-grained rewards, primarily due to their neglect of the LRM's intrinsic reasoning capabilities for safety. To address these, we propose \textbf{SAVeR$^2$}, a novel \textbf{S}afety \textbf{A}lignment method via \textbf{R}ewards with \textbf{R}easoning capability. SAVeR$^2$ decomposes the safety objective into four fine-grained verifiable reward signals and utilizes a difficulty-aware data selection strategy to stabilize training. Crucially, we introduce a reasoning-preserving gradient projection mechanism that analytically resolves conflicts between safety and reasoning gradients, effectively eliminating the safety tax. Extensive experiments on models ranging from 1.5B to 671B demonstrate that SAVeR$^2$ achieves superior safety performance and significantly lower over-refusal rates across multiple benchmarks without compromising general reasoning capabilities.
  </details>

- **[EnzymeRSI: A dual-loop recursive self-improvement system for enzyme design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06321)**  `arXiv:2611.06321`  `cs.AI`  
  _Genoria AI Team, Yuejie Hou, Lixiang Yang, Yisi Liu, Weiting Tang, Yueyuxiao Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enzyme engineering usually optimises which candidates to assay within a fixed model and workflow; when the bottleneck is a mismatched method or a failing workflow, that route is hard to escape. We present EnzymeRSI, a dual-loop system for recursive self-improvement (RSI). The outer loop evolves harness assets without changing model weights; the inner loop develops prediction and selection algorithms under controlled conditions; a cloud laboratory system is specified for closing the physical loop with quality-controlled feedback. The outer loop raised the mean development-set Spearman correlation on ProteinGym by 0.044 across five assays, reaching 0.335 on CD19 and 0.683 on OPSD (rhodopsin); of the two first places, only CD19 was produced by the outer loop itself. In ThermoMPNN self-improvement the inner loop reached a PCC of 0.849, and on TadA-Bench a Spearman correlation of 0.307 in 1.13 h. We examined the system's experimental decision-making in three enzyme systems. For the luciferase RLuc, 10 of the 32 nominated substitutions not pre-specified reached at least twice wild-type activity in a single round. For the cytidine deaminase A3A, a variant selected by a rule that caps loss of unmodified C raised 5mC conversion in the weaker CT context from 11.4% to 46.5% and reversed the substrate preference in both contexts. For the DNA polymerase KOD-E, 189 single-substitution variants defined a landscape on which different objective functions selected divergent next steps from the same parent; one assayed combination reached 31.1 times the parent on a modified substrate. By treating the organisation of research and the development of algorithms as separately scored objects of evolution, and linking a frozen decision record to a physical measurement, this work offers an evolutionary paradigm for moving beyond the blind spots of a fixed method and for autonomous enzyme engineering.
  </details>

- **[CogniLife: Continuous Cognitive Distillation for Deployed Multi-Expert Agent Marketplaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04483)**  `arXiv:2611.04483`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuwei Tan, Jingchun Huang, Ming Cai, Shuangquan Lyu, Tiange Wang, Yinzhi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-expert agent marketplaces -- where creators publish domain-specific AI experts for end users to consult -- are an emerging deployment pattern for language technology. A published expert can receive many requests yet little evidence about its creator, who remains active elsewhere: the cold-expert problem. We frame continuous cognitive distillation as converting attributed creator observations into revisable decision rules, communication patterns, and domain procedures. CogniLife proposes a cross-expert attribution design for this problem, with Creator Profiles for transferable creator patterns and Expert Journals for listing-specific knowledge; this design is not yet implemented or evaluated. A deployed platform partially realizes the surrounding lifecycle through creator-authorized authoring, capability-owned proposal filtering, and reversible consolidation of eligible owner-scoped memory. On ProfyBench-v1, an 85-task offline benchmark built from replay seeds and controlled internal tests, the Profy Full configuration as a whole achieves a mean overall score of 4.65/5 from the primary LLM judge, compared with 4.01 for Raw LLM and 4.28 for Expert Prompt, with the largest gains in completeness, specificity, and missing-information tasks. These configuration-level scores do not test cold-expert mitigation, cross-expert transfer, or longitudinal distillation.
  </details>

- **[Training LLM Agents to Automate LLM Post-Training with RSI-Dojo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03698)**  `arXiv:2611.03698`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuzheng Gao, Zhaojian Yu, Penghao Yin, Yuling Shi, Kai Cai, Shilin He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) aims to enable AI systems to autonomously build and improve AI systems themselves, including models, harnesses, and underlying infrastructure. Within this broader agenda, a central capability is enabling agents to train and improve base models. However, existing studies on automated model training primarily focused on workflow- or harness-level optimizations, rather than internalizing the ability to conduct post-training into the model itself. Developing this capability requires executable model training environments that can produce high-quality trajectories demonstrating how to select data, train models, diagnose failures, and iteratively improve results. In this paper, we introduce RSI-Dojo, a collection of executable model training environments designed to teach LLM agents to conduct post-training. To construct RSI-Dojo, we develop RSI-Craft, an automatic pipeline that converts human research artifacts into executable model training environments. RSI-Craft automatically collects research papers from top venues, instantiates datasets and metrics into a unified interface, and reproduces evaluation dependencies. It then filters tasks by data size, execution reliability, and discriminability, yielding RSI-Dojo with 281 seed model training tasks. Building on RSI-Dojo, we employ a trajectory collection method with an improved harness to collect high-quality model-training trajectories from frontier models, then retain examples through rubric-based and outcome-based filtering. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B on these trajectories. The resulting RSI-Dojo-9B and RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B improve over their base models by 8.74 and 16.87 PostTrainBench points, respectively. RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B reaches 29.95 on PostTrainBench and 53.29 on PostTrainBench-Lite, matching the performance of frontier models such as GLM-5.2 and GPT 5.5.
  </details>

- **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412)**  `arXiv:2611.03412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yumou Liu, Mingju Chen, Zhoufutu Wen, Qianyu Yang, Zijie Wang, Sihan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds together, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Reconstructed from real materials with 13 domain experts, its 11 projects span settings such as financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting. They comprise 150 subtasks, with 2-15 subsequent releases of new materials and client feedback per project. Later subtasks build on the agent's own earlier deliverables, so unresolved errors can carry into downstream work. Expert rubrics assess each subtask submission and the final delivery package. Across seven model-harness systems, the best scores 65.0/100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery; delivery also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.
  </details>

- **[CHATDIFY: Automating End-to-End Dify Workflow Synthesis from Natural Language via Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01563)**  `arXiv:2611.01563`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tao Fan, Guoqiang Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The proliferation of Large Language Models has catalyzed powerful application development platforms like Dify, which enable users to build sophisticated LLM-powered workflows through modular node orchestration. However, manually configuring these workflows requires deep platform expertise and remains time-consuming and error-prone, creating a significant barrier for non-expert users. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. We evaluate \textsc{ChatDify} on a curated benchmark of 100 production-inspired tasks across three state-of-the-art LLM backends, achieving 0.96, 0.91, and 0.75 success rates with Qwen-3.7, GLM-5.1, and DeepSeek-v4, respectively. These results demonstrate its robustness and practical applicability for lowering the technical barrier to creating sophisticated LLM applications.
  </details>

- **[P-ContextBench: From Personal Fact Recall to Personalized Agent Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40959)**  `arXiv:2610.40959`  `cs.AI`  
  _Grant Nasir Brown, Abdul Basir Bari, Anmol Tadikonda, Isabel Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for personal tasks, yet evaluating their safe use of personal context remains difficult because such contexts are private, fragmented, dynamic, and socially situated. We introduce P-ContextBench, a realistic, verifiable, and scalable benchmark for evaluating personal-context understanding. P-ContextBench constructs population-structured, life-story-grounded digital humans from demographic seeds reflecting the U.S. population. Rather than using explicit, static profiles, P-ContextBench embeds traits, routines, goals, and constraints as implicit evidence across fragmented, evolving synthetic mobile-app records. It evaluates five capabilities: single-source fact recovery, multi-source fact recovery, temporal state tracking, inference of higher-level personal patterns from routine behavior, and safe handling of requests conflicting with persona-specific constraints through compliance, warning, clarification, or refusal. A multi-stage validation pipeline checks persona plausibility, context recoverability, information leakage, evidence preservation, test-case grounding, and human-judge agreement. We instantiate P-ContextBench with 100 personas, 10,088 hidden facts, 18,261 app-trace fragments, and 4,810 verified test cases. The pipeline supports configurable generation across scales and demographic groups. Cost-effective state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs relevant to personal agents achieve below 60% accuracy on temporal tracking, higher-level pattern inference, and constraint-aware action-boundary decisions, with fewer than 10% of test cases answered correctly across all three repeated runs. Even a high-performing SOTA model remains inconsistent on these capabilities. P-ContextBench provides measurable targets for improving the accuracy, reliability, and safety of LLM-based personal agents.
  </details>

- **[LiveSearch: Questions as Executable Verifier Code for a Live Benchmark of Professional-Source Financial Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37166)**  `arXiv:2610.37166`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sinuo Wang, Wentao Zhao, Jiacheng Lu, Rui Sun, Beidi Luan, Qiming Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In finance, the answer that matters is usually the current one: prices, rates, and disclosures hold only as of a given day, so retrieving up-to-date facts is a core capability of a financial agent. Yet most financial benchmarks overlook timeliness. They fix each answer at annotation time, so the gold goes stale as markets move and, once leaked into training data, rewards recall rather than search and analysis. We introduce LiveSearch, a live benchmark that stores each question as executable verifier code: a typed program of calls to a professional financial data service, followed by a declarative extractor. The question is frozen, while its gold answer is determined by executing the program against professional financial data at evaluation time, and a daily job re-executes it with no human or language model in the loop. A memorized answer is thus likely outdated, and a model must reach the current one through tools and analysis. Construction is equally automatic: a language model only verbalizes programs enumerated from real tool responses and never chooses an answer. Under general web search, six contemporary tool-calling models reach 70.9% to 76.7% answer accuracy. Over three consecutive days, 70% of served questions change their gold value, and even under tolerance-aware matching, a literally reused earlier snapshot fails on a majority of questions within a single day. Per-question tracking uncovers dynamic shifts in correctness masked by aggregate accuracy.
  </details>

- **[WorkFactory: Turning Professional Intents into Executable Work for Scalable Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37161)**  `arXiv:2610.37161`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Zeng, Tingyang Chen, Jia Deng, Qiyao Wang, Shuo Tang, Yike Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling supervised fine-tuning for working agents requires not only broad coverage of professional tasks, but also expert demonstrations that faithfully carry out professional workflows to completion. However, missing or inconsistent task information can disrupt expert demonstrations, weakening the resulting supervision. We introduce WorkFactory, a scalable task-construction framework that turns professional intents into executable task environments through hybrid intent sourcing, semantic workflow planning, and execution-based repair. Starting from these intents, WorkFactory constructs a Semantic Workflow Graph that explicitly represents information requirements, dependencies, and locations within the task environment. The graph guides the instantiation of task environments, while an adversarial execution-refinement loop exposes missing or inconsistent information and steers targeted repairs that preserve the original intent. Using WorkFactory, we construct 112,686 tasks spanning 612 occupations and train the WorkPilot family at 4B, 27B, and 35B-A3B scales on the resulting teacher trajectories. All three models improve over their respective base models across four professional-work benchmarks, with WorkPilot-35B-A3B rising from 879 to 1565 Elo on GDPval-AA v2.1. Scaling experiments with up to 110k tasks further show that larger models benefit more from additional task data but exhibit diminishing returns at smaller data scales, while WorkPilot's long-horizon execution evolves from verbose trajectories toward more efficient task completion. Controlled component studies quantify the negative effects of task-environment defects on downstream performance and show how semantic workflow planning and execution-based repair yield downstream gains through improved task-environment quality. The subset of data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DriveMind: Mind-Evolving Belief Tracking for Closed-Loop Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32069)**  `arXiv:2610.32069`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yan Shi, Kehua Chen, Jiarui Qi, Yihong Tang, Haicheng Liao, Yinhai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can perceive a traffic scene, reason about it, and propose a driving action within a single model. However, existing MLLM-based drivers reason from scratch at each frame and commit to a single explanation, discarding earlier hypotheses about the scene. When the scene is ambiguous, the agent locks in on the wrong interpretation and acts on it before evidence accumulates. In this paper, we propose DriveMind, an MLLM-based driving framework that keeps several hypotheses about the scene alive at the same time and updates their weights as new frames arrive. Inspired by particle filtering, DriveMind propagates each hypothesis through a VLM, scores it against the current observation, and resamples to maintain diversity. On Bench2Drive, adding belief tracking to the same driving pipeline improves the Driving Score (DS) from 68.50 to 81.37 and the Success Rate (SR) from 41.36% to 54.09%. The largest gains occur on ambiguous scenarios such as unsignalized intersections (+20.0 DS) and oncoming-traffic interactions (+20.5 DS). Beyond the overall metrics, DriveMind also improves fine-grained interactive driving abilities such as merging, emergency braking, and give-way.
  </details>

- **[LG-Bench: A Graph-Structured Evaluation Benchmark for Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26787)**  `arXiv:2610.26787`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lu Sun, Xiangyi Zhang, Xiangyang Zhu, Zijian Chen, Qiang Hu, Yuan Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional evaluation benchmarks reduce inherently interconnected scientific knowledge in life sciences into flat lists of questions, disregarding the underlying topological structure of the knowledge. We introduce LG-Bench, the first graph-structured benchmark for life sciences, featuring over 10,000 high-quality multiple-choice questions across medicine, biology, and chemistry. Our approach constructs a weighted evaluation graph using bidirectional matching and semantic similarity algorithms, where nodes represent questions and edge weights capture their semantic relationships. Leveraging this graph topology, we design two novel evaluation metrics. The Global Coherence Score (GCS) measures a model's consistency within semantically related neighborhoods, while Knowledge Balance Score (KBS) analyzes how model errors are distributed across the graph to reveal conceptual blind spots. LG-Bench facilitates fine-grained comparison of LLMs by surfacing differences in conceptual coherence and patterns of knowledge organization across models. Our framework shifts the evaluation paradigm from flat accuracy metrics to structure-aware analysis, offering a new lens for diagnosing and improving LLM performance in the life sciences domain.
  </details>

- **[ACED-Bench: Evaluating How LLM Agents Acquire and Act on Causal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14381)**  `arXiv:2610.14381`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwei Chen, Amila Weerasinghe, Siddharth Biswal, Utkrisht Rajkumar, Olcay Boz, Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used as scientific assistants, yet we still lack reliable ways to evaluate whether they can investigate a system rather than merely answer a prompt. A useful evaluation must let agents choose experiments, interpret finite evidence, and make causal decisions, while preserving objective ground truth. We introduce ACED (Active Causal Evidence and Decision Benchmark), a neuro-symbolic benchmark that separates the readable scientific surface from the formal causal world: natural-language scenarios describe the system, while a hidden causal model defines the data, the effect of every intervention, and the oracle answers. In ACED-Repair, agents diagnose a failing production system: within 15 experiments they must decide which controls to test, read confounded and filtered data, and recommend a fix, which we re-simulate on a hidden true objective. In ACED-Query, agents answer causal questions and make constrained causal decisions in worlds backed by categorical probabilistic graphical models. Acceptance on ACED-Repair ranges from 43% to 0% across 12 LLMs, while strong agents approach ceiling on ACED-Query's qualification questions. Tracing every experiment shows where investigations stop: most open-weight models test a true lever on its own in fewer than half of the worlds, the strongest models test the right levers but often miss the working dose or combination, and agents that do run a working fix almost always recommend it. No model reliably repairs systems with two independent causes or systems that need a subgroup-specific policy. These results suggest that reliable AI co-scientists need explicit support for choosing experiments, keeping track of evidence, and composing interventions. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CaLR: Causal Latent Revision for Robust Diffusion Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20981)**  `arXiv:2609.20981`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Cai, Jian Zhao, Yuchen Yuan, Xuelong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) models suffer from local greediness, while diffusion language models (DLMs) often lack the strict causal structure required for reasoning. To combine the advantages and overcome the drawbacks of the dual, we propose Causal Latent Revision (CaLR), a framework that reformulates reasoning as constrained latent optimization. By adopting a causal topology matrix (CTM) from an expert model and implicit differentiation, CaLR performs gradient-guided ``thought revision" to enforce logical consistency, enabling dynamic self-correction of intermediate steps during parallel generation. Empirically, CaLR achieves SOTA DLM performance on complex benchmarks, surpassing strong AR baselines and demonstrating superior robustness in constrained tasks like Sudoku.
  </details>

- **[AdaPath: Query-Adaptive Path-Finding via Path-Bank for Multi-Hop Implicit Biomedical KGQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30556)**  `arXiv:2608.30556`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jun Hyeong Kim, Dongki Kim, Yinhua Piao, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Path-finding over knowledge graphs has become an effective way to ground LLM reasoning on multi-hop questions. However, biomedical QA introduces two distinct challenges that general-domain methods are not designed for: (i) queries do not expose intermediate reasoning and can be answered through multiple valid pathways, and (ii) biomedical knowledge graphs are densely connected, so path-finding methods easily take wrong turns. To address these challenges, we propose AdaPath, a path-finding framework that retrieves query-adaptive meta-paths from Path-Bank, which captures both query semantics and biomedical knowledge graph structure. AdaPath provides the missing cues in biomedical queries while effectively pruning dense knowledge graph neighborhoods during multi-hop reasoning. We further release BioStrat-QA, a biomedical KGQA benchmark that stratifies multi-hop queries by how much intermediate reasoning they expose. Across biomedical KGQA benchmarks, AdaPath consistently outperforms baselines, sustaining meaningful path-finding even when multi-hop queries expose less surface information. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Science Edge Evaluation: SEE the Missing Step Toward Real Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06931)**  `arXiv:2608.06931`  `cs.AI`  
  _Taolin Han, Yuchen Zhang, Jinghang Wang, Yun Wu, Wai Yuet Chiu, Zhaohai Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly involved in scientific discovery, yet it remains unclear whether they can support complex real laboratory science. Here we introduce Science Edge Evaluation (SEE), a multimodal benchmark of expert-curated questions grounded in peer-reviewed literature and experimental practice in chemistry, biology, and materials science. Evaluation of 28 multimodal large language models (MLLMs) shows that even the best-performing model reaches only 54.9% accuracy. Moreover, general-purpose models outperform science-specialized models on average. In the visual-agent evaluation, the use of tools increases the best accuracy to 56.1%. Tool use can expand the information available to models, but more information does not necessarily lead to reliable scientific reasoning. The key challenge is whether models can manage tool-derived information within the boundaries of the original experimental evidence. Together, these findings reveal that current MLLMs still cannot reliably make justified and evidence-bounded inferences from experimental results, which is an essential capability in real scientific discovery. Bridging this gap requires MLLMs to transition from explaining established scientific concepts to deriving novel and evidence-based insights from experimental data.
  </details>

- **[Contextualized Evaluation of Vision Language Models through Dynamic, Multi-turn Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14499)**  `arXiv:2607.14499`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijiang Li, Huiqi Zou, Bingyang Wang, Ziang Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial advances on benchmarks, yet their real-world effectiveness remains uncertain. This gap stems from the fundamental misalignment between benchmarks in controlled, static settings and the dynamic, interactive, and contextualized nature of real-world applications. To bridge this gap, we propose CEDI (Contextualized Evaluation of MLLMs through Dynamic, multi-round Interactions), a framework that recasts evaluation as a three-party interaction between an evaluatee model, an automated examiner, and a grader. The examiner conducts multi-turn, semi-structured conversations guided by a graph-based representation of the task. By navigating state-space transitions, CEDI deploys diverse strategies, from clarification requests to adversarial probes, to elicit performance evidence. We apply CEDI to visual hallucinations. Empirical results across multiple models, diverse settings, datasets, and domains show that contextualized, interactive evaluations reveal not only significantly more hallucinations than conventional static evaluation but also ones that more closely resemble those arising in practical use cases. We further show that hallucinations often accumulate over long contexts, through self-reinforcing dialogue history, and models are particularly vulnerable to questions requiring premise rejection or refusal. Together, these findings highlight CEDI as a step toward realistic, systematic, and ecologically valid assessments of MLLMs' capabilities. Code is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Turn Agent Self-Distillation with Compressed Hindsight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11559)**  `arXiv:2606.11559`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Liu, Yuwei Zhang, Xiyao Li, Bohan Lyu, Jingbo Shang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In reinforcement learning for multi-turn agents, a sparse outcome reward makes credit assignment across intermediate tool calls difficult. On-policy self-distillation eases credit assignment by having the model teach itself with dense supervision: the model with privileged information (the teacher) scores each token generated by the model without it (the student). However, it remains unclear what forms of feedback are most effective in multi-turn agentic settings. We find that the natural multi-turn variant fails: when the teacher is given a complete successful demonstration at every turn, training makes the agent worse than the untrained model. The teacher ends up rewarding the student for copying the demonstration, even when a copied action is not right in the student's context. We propose HINT, in which a reflector, by default the policy itself, reads the completed trajectory and writes short turn-level feedback. The teacher then sees what went wrong at each turn, not the actions that followed. On Tau-Bench and WebShop, HINT matches GRPO with one-eighth as many rollouts.
  </details>

- **[Synthesizing End-to-End Human Trajectory Anomalies: An LLM-Driven Framework with Map-Constrained Routing and Sensor Noise Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10314)**  `arXiv:2606.10314`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yueyang Liu, Joon-Seok Kim, Andreas Züfle_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although the study of human trajectory anomalies is critical for advancing spatial data mining, empirical research remains severely hindered by a pervasive lack of ground-truth datasets. Despite the availability of several real-world and simulated human trajectory collections, these datasets exclusively capture normal mobility patterns and lack annotated anomalies. This specific scarcity is fundamentally driven by the inherent statistical rarity of anomalous events, precluding the feasibility of conventional observational methods. Compounding this challenge, the systematic acquisition of large-scale mobility data is strictly bottlenecked by prohibitive costs and stringent privacy regulations. To overcome these fundamental limitations and establish a reliable human trajectory anomalies dataset with annotated ground truth, we introduce an end-to-end generative framework designed to synthesize context-aware trajectory anomalies at scale. Our architecture bridges the gap between purely synthetic mobility data and complex real-world physical constraints by operating directly on baseline simulated trajectories. We employ Large Language Model (LLM) agents to systematically inject semantically meaningful behavioral anomalies such as irregular out-of-distribution check-ins and skipped routine visits. To ensure rigorous spatial validity, the system leverages map-constrained routing reconstruction to recalculate the physical transitions between these LLM agent-modified staypoints. Moreover, to narrow the simulation-to-reality gap, we augment the resulting trajectories with a context-aware spatial noise model, parameterized by environmental and location-specific variables, to emulate heterogeneous GPS sensor degradation. Through this pipeline, the system generates systematically annotated mobility datasets that provide a standardized benchmark for evaluating downstream spatial data mining models.
  </details>

- **[SkillDAG: Self-Evolving Typed Skill Graphs for LLM Skill Selection at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03056)**  `arXiv:2606.03056`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tong Bai, Zhenglin Wan, Pengfei Zhou, Xingrui Yu, Yang You, Ivor W. Tsang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large skill libraries are now standard equipment for LLM agents, and which subset reaches the prompt decides what the agent can do. Enumeration and top-$K$ embedding retrieval both treat skills as independent documents, assuming relevance is a property of one skill at a time. It is not: skills depend on, conflict with, specialize, and duplicate one another, so the closest match to a task can name it while omitting a prerequisite it needs. Graph retrieval encodes these relations but spends them in a fixed pipeline, handing the agent an opaque bundle. We present SkillDAG, which exposes a typed skill graph to the LLM as an agent-callable interface rather than consuming it for the agent: search returns vector matches, typed-edge neighbors, and conflict signals, and a propose-then-commit protocol lets it register execution-backed edges as it works. Across two benchmarks and three model families, SkillDAG outperforms full-library prompting, vector retrieval, and Graph-of-Skills, gaining +14.3 points of ALFWorld success and tying only at ceiling; its ranking holds flat across a $10\times$ pool expansion that degrades the fixed pipeline. Inter-skill structure, these results suggest, is worth more to an agent that can query and revise it than to a retriever that consumes it.
  </details>

- **[Tiny but Trusted: Efficient Vision-Language Reasoning for Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30344)**  `arXiv:2605.30344`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaona Zhou, Muntasir Wahed, Tianjiao Yu, Constantin Brif, Ismini Lourentzou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in Vision--Language Models (VLMs) have achieved impressive performance across many tasks, yet prior studies report unsatisfactory performance when applying large language or multimodal models to finding abnormal patterns in sequential data. Public anomaly detection benchmarks typically provide interval annotations but not natural-language rationales, making it difficult to fine-tune VLMs to produce grounded, interpretable decisions. To address this gap, we construct VisAnomBench, a curated benchmark built from public time-series datasets and augmented with high-quality anomaly explanations selected from multiple large VLMs using fine-grained, task-specific rewards. Through fine-tuning on this benchmark, we develop VisAnomReasoner, a parameter-efficient VLM for time-series anomaly detection. Results on VisAnomBench show that VisAnomReasoner achieves more accurate anomaly localization and consistently outperforms all baselines, with improvements of at least 21.23 and 23.87 percentage points in precision and F1, respectively. Additional experiments on the TSB-AD-U benchmark demonstrate strong cross-benchmark generalization, with VisAnomReasoner improving precision and F1 by 9.57 and 13.39 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[OpenClawBench: Benchmarking Process-side Anomalies in Real-world Agent Execution Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29253)**  `arXiv:2605.29253`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yibing Liu, Yangze Liu, Xiaolong Yin, Bin Wang, Chong Zhang, Hao Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task success can hide process anomalies in real-world agent executions. An agent may pass the final task oracle while still accumulating unresolved ambiguity, unsafe external writes, ignored errors, weakly grounded commitments, or capability-boundary overcommitment. We study this mismatch as the Outcome-Process Gap and introduce OpenClawBench, a large-scale dataset for measuring and supervising process-side anomalies in real agent execution processes. OpenClawBench is built from BFCL-driven OpenClaw sessions produced by 6 source models and contains 31,264 annotated trajectories. FullTax converts the aligned trajectories into structured anomaly supervision: binary labels, supporting evidence, onset/span localization, severity, recoverability, and a 5-class anomaly taxonomy. Using OpenClawBench, we make the Outcome-Process Gap measurable. Among 31,135 oracle-passing executions, 2,904 (9.33%) are still labeled process-anomalous under FullTax. Within oracle-passing executions that contain high-risk process evidence, 1,765 of 1,904 (92.70%) are labeled anomalous. On Gold-300, a test subset labeled independently by two human annotators, silver labels match the final human binary label on 93.3% of trajectories. A LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma 3 12B detector trained on the high-confidence FullTax supervised pool reaches binary F1=0.729 on the cleaner-labels silver test split (n=2,646). It outperforms the GPT-5.4 frontier reference by +0.302 absolute, the no-fine-tuning base by +0.357, and wins on all six source-model agent slices. The gain comes from calibration rather than higher recall: zero-shot detectors over-flag at 42-50% against a 14.8% label rate, while the fine-tuned detector predicts anomalies 18.2% of the time. Together, OpenClawBench turns real agent execution logs into auditable and reusable supervision for studying, diagnosing, and operationally monitoring runtime agent reliability.
  </details>

- **[Reward Bias Substitution: Single-Axis Bias Mitigations Redirect Optimization Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27996)**  `arXiv:2605.27996`  `cs.AI`  
  _Max Lamparth, Daniel Fein, Andreas Haupt, Marcel Hussing, Mykel J. Kochenderfer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-axis mitigations of reward-model biases, such as reducing proxy reliance on length, sycophancy, or style, can redirect optimization pressure onto correlated proxies rather than eliminate it, a new type of reward hacking we call reward bias substitution. The failure is enabled by a measurement-versus-optimization gap between mitigation evaluation on the audit distribution and policy training on the policy-induced distribution. Prior reward-hacking analyses assume a fixed reward proxy, whereas we study the mitigation operator that removes one bias from it, the basic step of iterative reward refinement. We formalize mitigation outcomes into a regime taxonomy and prove that successful mitigation, bias substitution, and overcorrection can produce identical observables under every audit-distribution score, including ranking accuracy and win-rate, even when granted oracle access to the true reward. Across 30 published preference-learning mitigation works, no method we survey reports the evidence needed to certify successful mitigation. Augmenting evaluation with policy-induced distributions while tracking multiple biases provably resolves this distributional gap, and we translate this into actionable prescriptions for mitigation methods and benchmarks. We demonstrate bias substitution in RLHF, where a length penalty in GRPO shortens responses as intended yet makes the policy overconfident and less accurate on open-ended questions, show that the same distributional gap hides failures of published mitigations, and find the necessary preconditions for reward bias substitution documented in published results and in our study of length-sycophancy coupling in LLM responses.
  </details>

- **[Boosting Inference with Guided Reasoning: Stochastic Exploration for Recursive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25230)**  `arXiv:2605.25230`  `cs.AI`  
  _Andrew Corbett, Archit Sood, Anna Tzatzopoulou, Sai-Aakash Ramesh, Tim Dodwell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work on recursive architectures has shown that tiny neural networks can be surprisingly powerful on structured reasoning tasks. The trick is to model reasoning trajectories with a latent dynamical system. We argue that the inference-time behaviour of these architectures is best understood as approximate inference over latent reasoning trajectories, with deterministic recursion as the one-particle, zero-noise limit. We make this view operational through guided reasoning: stochastic perturbations of the reasoning dynamics propose neighbouring trajectories, and the model's existing early-stopping head reweights them online. The framework yields three diagnostics computable from inference traces: local stability, guide alignment, with a label-free necessary condition, and cloud-token entropy. These predict whether the procedure will help and which of its outputs to trust. On Sudoku-Extreme it lifts exact-solve accuracy from $85.9\%$ to $98.0\%$ without retraining, and on PPBench, a four-type pencil-puzzle benchmark, from $62.5\%$ to $77.9\%$ with no tuning; on Maze-Hard the diagnostics flag a misaligned guide, as validation performance later confirms. The same machinery thus characterises both when recursive reasoning has room to improve at the trajectory level and when the model's internal guide can recover said improvements. Code is available at `this https URL`.
  </details>

- **[Probabilistic Tiny Recursive Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19943)**  `arXiv:2605.19943`  `cs.AI`  
  _Amin Sghaier, Ali Parviz, Alexia Jolicoeur-Martineau_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tiny Recursive Models (TRM) solve complex reasoning tasks with a fraction of the parameters of modern large language models (LLMs) by iteratively refining a latent state and final answer. While powerful, their deterministic recursion can lead to convergence at suboptimal solutions, without escape mechanism. A common workaround relies on task-specific input perturbations at test time combined with answer aggregation via voting. We introduce Probabilistic TRM (PTRM), a task-agnostic framework for test-time compute scaling that addresses this limitation through stochastic exploration. PTRM injects Gaussian noise at each deep recursion step, enabling parallel trajectories to explore diverse solution basins, and selects among them using the model's existing Q head (used for early stopping in the original TRM). Without requiring retraining or task-specific augmentations, PTRM enables substantial accuracy gains across benchmarks, including Sudoku-Extreme (87.4% to 98.75%) and on various puzzles from Pencil Puzzle Bench (62.6% to 91.2%). On the latter, PTRM achieves nearly double the accuracy of frontier LLMs (91.2% vs. 55.1%) at less than 0.0001x the cost, using only 7M parameters.
  </details>

- **[Do Language Models Share Your Doctor's Priorities? Measuring Values from Clinical Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18738)**  `arXiv:2605.18738`  `cs.AI`  
  _Payal Chandak, Victoria Alkin, David Wu, Maya Dagan, Taposh Dutta Roy, Maria Clara Saad Menezes, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical decisions often require weighing autonomy, beneficence, nonmaleficence, and justice. Reasonable physicians may prioritize these values differently, yet the values that language models bring to medical advice remain poorly characterized. We introduce ValuePrism, an instrument for measuring value priorities from clinical decisions. ValuePrism combines a benchmark of 50 clinical dilemmas, each reviewed by two physicians, with an attribution method that estimates relative value priorities from choices between medically reasonable recommendations. Each case presents a clinical vignette and two mutually exclusive recommendations designed so that choosing one necessarily promotes certain values at the expense of others. Applying the instrument to frontier language models and a US-based physician panel, we find that models frequently acknowledge values favoring both recommendations but make highly consistent decisions across repeated queries, even on cases that sharply divide physicians. The ValuePrism attribution method shows that both physicians and language models express differentiated value priorities. Collectively, model values exhibit substantial diversity and most tend to fall within the physician-panel reference range. Nevertheless, a single model deployed unchecked would amplify its particular values to patients at scale. ValuePrism provides a common basis for measuring and comparing the clinical value priorities expressed by language models and physicians. Data and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Are Agents Ready to Teach? A Multi-Stage Benchmark for Real-World Teaching Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14322)**  `arXiv:2605.14322`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zixin Chen, Peng Liu, Rui Sheng, Haobo Li, Jianhong Tu, Xiaodong Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language agents are increasingly deployed in professional workflows, yet tutoring remains a high-stakes capability that existing evaluations only partially capture. Effective tutor agents require more than producing correct answers or executing accurate tool calls: they must infer a warranted teaching decision from evidence, adapt support as learner state changes, and carry an instructor's request through a learning-management system (LMS) to a completed, verified intervention. We introduce TeachArena, a source-grounded benchmark that jointly evaluates three complementary surfaces of teaching work: professional pedagogical judgment, situated multi-turn tutoring, and end-to-end LMS teaching workflows. Its 354 tasks are each built around a pedagogical insight, grounded in evidence, and evaluated with matched verifiers over observable turn-level responses, tutoring trajectories, and persistent artifacts or environment states. Across a comprehensive evaluation of frontier models, our findings reveal that current models are generally capable of bounded pedagogical judgment, but still fall short of professional teaching standards in situated tutoring and end-to-end teaching-workflow execution. By unifying teacher judgment, adaptive tutoring, and institutional action in one benchmark, TeachArena provides a measurement foundation for developing tutor agents that can support realistic teaching work.
  </details>

- **[AgentRobustBench: A Sim-to-Real Benchmark and Domain-Randomized RL Recipe for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11928)**  `arXiv:2605.11928`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaolin Zhou, Aojie Yuan, Zheng Luo, Zipeng Ling, Xixiao Pan, Yicheng Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-use language agents are evaluated on benchmarks that assume clean inputs, unambiguous tool registries, and reliable APIs. Real deployments violate all these assumptions: user typos propagate into hallucinated tool names, a misconfigured request timeout can stall an agent indefinitely, and duplicate tool names across servers can freeze an SDK. We study these failures as a sim-to-real gap in the tool-use partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP), where deployment noise enters through the observation, action space, reward-relevant metadata, or transition dynamics. We introduce AgentRobustBench, a benchmark with 22 perturbation types organized by these four POMDP components, each grounded in a verified GitHub issue or documented tool-calling failure. Across 21 models from 1.5B to 32B parameters (including the closed-source o4-mini), the robustness profile is sharply uneven: observation perturbations reduce accuracy by less than 5%, while reward-relevant and transition perturbations reduce accuracy by roughly 40% and 30%, respectively; scale alone does not close these gaps. We then propose ToolRL-DR, a domain-randomization reinforcement learning (RL) recipe that trains a tool-use agent on perturbation-augmented trajectories spanning the three statically encodable POMDP components. On a 3B backbone, ToolRL-DR-Full retains roughly three-quarters of clean accuracy and reaches an aggregate perturbed accuracy comparable to open-source 14B function-calling baselines while substantially narrowing the gap to o4-mini. It closes approximately 27% of the Transition gap despite never seeing transition perturbations in training, suggesting that RL on adversarial static tool-use inputs induces a more persistent retry policy that transfers to unseen runtime failures. Code, dataset, checkpoints, and leaderboard are accessible through our GitHub repository.
  </details>

- **[Consistency as a Testable Property: Statistical Methods to Evaluate AI Agent Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10516)**  `arXiv:2605.10516`  `cs.AI`  
  _Harsh Raj, Niranjan Orkat, Suvrorup Mukherjee, Aritra Guha, Cheryl Flynn, Subhabrata Majumdar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper establishes a rigorous measurement science for AI agent reliability, providing a foundational framework for quantifying consistency under semantically preserving perturbations. By leveraging $U$-statistics for output-level reliability and kernel-based metrics for trajectory-level stability, we offer a principled approach to evaluating agents across diverse operating conditions. Our proposal highlights the important distinction between the core capability and execution robustness of an agent, showing that minor task-level variations can induce complete strategy breakdowns despite the agent possessing the requisite knowledge for the task. We validate our framework through extensive experiments on three agentic benchmarks, demonstrating that trajectory-level consistency metrics provide far greater diagnostic sensitivity than traditional pass@1 rates. By providing the mathematical tools to isolate where and why agents deviate, we enable the identification and rectification of architectural concerns that hinder the deployment of agents in high-stakes, real-world environments.
  </details>

- **[Auto-Rubric as Reward: From Implicit Preferences to Explicit Multimodal Generative Criteria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08354)**  `arXiv:2605.08354`  `cs.AI`  
  _Juanxi Tian, Fengyuan Liu, Jiaming Han, Yilei Jiang, Yongliang Wu, Yesheng Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning multimodal generative models with human preferences demands reward signals that respect the compositional, multi-dimensional structure of human judgment. Prevailing RLHF approaches reduce this structure to scalar or pairwise labels, collapsing nuanced preferences into opaque parametric proxies and exposing vulnerabilities to reward hacking. While recent Rubrics-as-Reward (RaR) methods attempt to recover this structure through explicit criteria, generating rubrics that are simultaneously reliable, scalable, and data-efficient remains an open problem. We introduce Auto-Rubric as Reward (ARR), a framework that reframes reward modeling from implicit weight optimization to explicit, criteria-based decomposition. Before any pairwise comparison, ARR externalizes a VLM's internalized preference knowledge as prompt-specific rubrics, translating holistic intent into independently verifiable quality dimensions. This conversion of implicit preference structure into inspectable, interpretable constraints substantially suppresses evaluation biases including positional bias, enabling both zero-shot deployment and few-shot conditioning on minimal supervision. To extend these gains into generative training, we propose Rubric Policy Optimization (RPO), which distills ARR's structured multi-dimensional evaluation into a robust binary reward, replacing opaque scalar regression with rubric-conditioned preference decisions that stabilize policy gradients. On text-to-image generation and image editing benchmarks, ARR-RPO outperforms pairwise reward models and VLM judges, demonstrating that explicitly externalizing implicit preference knowledge into structured rubrics achieves more reliable, data-efficient multimodal alignment, revealing that the bottleneck is the absence of a factorized interface, not a deficit of knowledge.
  </details>

- **[TACT: Mitigating Overthinking and Overacting in Coding Agents via Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05980)**  `arXiv:2605.05980`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuan Sui, Yulin Chen, Yibo Li, Xue Jiang, Yufei He, Yihong Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When language model agents tackle complex software engineering tasks, they often degrade over long trajectories, which we define as *agent drift*. We focus on two recurring failure modes *overthinking* and *overacting*, i.e., where the agent repeatedly reasons over information it already has, and where it issues tool calls without integrating recent observations or acquiring new evidence. In this paper, we introduce TACT (Think-Act Calibration via activation Steering), to detect and mitigate agent drift in the residual stream before it surfaces as a behavioral failure. In specific, we label trajectory steps as overthinking, overacting, or calibrated, and find that their hidden states can separate linearly along two *drift axes*, pointing from calibrated behavior toward each failure mode (AUC $\approx$ 0.9). To mitigate agent drift, we project each step's activation onto these axes at test time and pull drifted ones back toward the calibrated region. Experiments show that TACT outperforms unsteered baselines across SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.0, and CLAW-Eval, lifting average resolve rate by $+5.8$ pp on Qwen3.5-27B and $+4.8$ pp on Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it while cutting steps-to-resolve by up to $26\%$. These gains frame agent drift as a steerable direction in the residual stream, and position TACT as a viable handle for reliable long-horizon agents.
  </details>

- **[SAVOIR: Learning Social Savoir-Faire via Shapley-based Reward Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18982)**  `arXiv:2604.18982`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiachong Feng, Yi Jiang, Xiaocheng Feng, Deyi Yin, Libo Qin, Yangfan Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social intelligence, the ability to navigate complex interpersonal interactions, presents a fundamental challenge for language agents. Training such agents via reinforcement learning requires solving the credit assignment problem: determining how individual utterances contribute to multi-turn dialogue outcomes. Existing approaches directly employ language models to distribute episode-level rewards, yielding attributions that are retrospective and lack theoretical grounding. We propose SAVOIR (ShApley Value fOr SocIal RL), a novel principled framework grounded in cooperative game theory. Our approach combines two complementary principles: expected utility shifts evaluation from retrospective attribution to prospective valuation, capturing an utterance's strategic potential for enabling favorable future trajectories; Shapley values ensure fair credit distribution with axiomatic guarantees of efficiency, symmetry, and marginality. Experiments on the SOTOPIA benchmark demonstrate that SAVOIR achieves new state-of-the-art performance across all evaluation settings, with our 7B model matching or exceeding proprietary models including GPT-4o and Claude-3.5-Sonnet. Notably, even large reasoning models consistently underperform, suggesting social intelligence requires qualitatively different capabilities than analytical reasoning.
  </details>

- **[A Self-Evolving Defect Detection Framework for Industrial Photovoltaic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14869)**  `arXiv:2603.14869`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyu He, Yu Duan, Wenzhen Liu, Hanyuan Hang, Boyu Qin, Qiantu Tuo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable photovoltaic (PV) power generation requires timely detection of module defects that may reduce energy yield, accelerate degradation, and increase lifecycle operation and maintenance costs during field operation. Electroluminescence imaging is therefore widely adopted for PV module inspection; however, automated defect detection in real operational environments remains challenging due to heterogeneous module geometries, low-resolution imaging conditions, subtle defect morphology, long-tailed defect distributions, and persistent data and label-space shifts from evolving inspection and labeling processes. These factors limit the robustness and long-term maintainability of conventional deep-learning inspection pipelines. To address these challenges, this paper proposes SEPDD, a Self-Evolving Photovoltaic Defect Detection framework designed for evolving industrial PV inspection scenarios. SEPDD integrates automated pipeline optimization with a trigger-driven evolution mechanism that re-optimizes the whole training/evaluation pipeline when deployment indicators (retraining failure, label evolution, environmental change, periodic maintenance) fire, enabling deployment-time adaptation to distribution shifts and newly emerging defect patterns. Experiments on a public PV defect benchmark and a private industrial dataset, both exhibiting severe class imbalance and significant domain shift, demonstrate SEPDD's effectiveness: it attains mAP50 of 91.5% on the public dataset and 49.5% on the private dataset, surpassing the autonomous baseline by 14.9% and human experts by 4.8% on the public dataset, and by 4.9% and 2.5%, respectively, on the private dataset; comparisons are limited to the two evaluated datasets and the studied baselines. By improving the robustness and adaptability of automated PV inspection, the proposed framework supports more reliable maintenance of distributed PV systems.
  </details>

- **[Benchmark Health Index: A Systematic Framework for Benchmarking the Benchmarks of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11674)**  `arXiv:2602.11674`  `cs.AI`  
  _Longyuan Zhu, Hairan Hua, Linlin Miao, Wei Wang, Bing Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are advancing rapidly, yet the benchmarks used to measure this progress are becoming increasingly unreliable. Score inflation and selective reporting have eroded the authority of standard benchmarks, leaving the community uncertain about which evaluation results remain trustworthy. We introduce the Benchmark Health Index (BHI), a pure data-driven framework for auditing evaluation sets along three orthogonal and complementary axes: (1) Capability Discrimination, measuring how sharply a benchmark separates model performance beyond noise; (2) Anti-Saturation, estimating remaining headroom before ceiling effects erode resolution and thus the benchmark's expected longevity; and (3) Impact, quantifying influence across academic and industrial ecosystems via adoption breadth and practice-shaping power. By distilling 106 validated benchmarks from the technical reports of 91 representative models in 2025, we systematically characterize the evaluation landscape. BHI is the first framework to quantify benchmark health at a macro level, providing a principled basis for benchmark selection and enabling dynamic lifecycle management for next-generation evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[Batch-of-Thought: Cross-Instance Learning for Enhanced LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02950)**  `arXiv:2601.02950`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuan Yang, Furong Jia, Roy Xie, Xi Xiong, Hengwei Bian, Jian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current Large Language Model reasoning systems process queries independently, discarding valuable cross-instance signals such as shared reasoning patterns and consistency constraints.
