We introduce Batch-of-Thought (BoT), a training-free method that processes related queries jointly to enable cross-instance learning. By performing comparative analysis across queries, BoT identifies high-quality reasoning templates, detects errors through consistency checks, and amortizes computational costs. We instantiate BoT within a multi-agent reflection architecture (BoT-R), where a Reflector performs joint evaluation to unlock mutual information gain unavailable in isolated processing. Experiments across three model families and six benchmarks demonstrate that BoT-R consistently improves accuracy and confidence calibration while reducing inference costs by up to 61%. Our theoretical and experimental analysis reveals when and why batch-aware reasoning benefits LLM systems. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge and Reasoning Augmented Learning for Language Model Assisted Antimicrobial Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.15974)**  `arXiv:2511.15974`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhe Li, Yehan Qiu, Yuntian Huang, Xin Ding, Yujie Chen, Jing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Antimicrobial decision support requires both clinical knowledge and patient-specific reasoning. We developed Knowledge and Reasoning Augmented Learning (KRAL) to improve language model performance on antimicrobial decision-making tasks. Methods: In this retrospective technical study, a 27-billion-parameter student model underwent guideline-grounded supervised fine-tuning followed by Multi-turn Agentic GRPO using clinical reasoning trajectories. Six configurations were evaluated on 123 clinician-validated clinical antimicrobial treatment cases from Peking Union Medical College Hospital (PUMCH) and 100 JAMA Clinical Challenge cases. A language model judge scored responses, and paired tests compared configurations. Component ablations assessed benchmark performance and training stability. Results: On the PUMCH benchmark, KRAL achieved the highest composite score (0.895 on a 0-1 scale), outperforming all five controls (all P<0.001). On JAMA, KRAL scored 0.600 and exceeded the base model with and without retrieval (P = 0.0048 and 0.0499, respectively). Reinforcement learning ablation experiments demonstrate that the reward smoothing can significantly improve the performance and training stability of vanilla GRPO. Conclusions: KRAL improved performance on a single-center clinical antimicrobial benchmark and publicly available JAMA clinical challenge cases by combining guideline-grounded learning with Multi-turn Agentic GRPO. Further external clinical validation is needed to evaluate the practical utility and effectiveness of KRAL.
  </details>

- **[Retrieval as Exploration: Retriever-Free Retrieval via Corpus-Traversing MCTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16409)**  `arXiv:2505.16409`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chaeeun Kim, Seungone Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in multi-step reasoning and calling search tools at appropriate steps. However, existing retrieval-augmented reasoning approaches still rely on separate retrieval models, limiting LRMs to deciding when to retrieve and how to formulate queries rather than leveraging their pretrained knowledge and reasoning capabilities for retrieval itself. This structural separation introduces a representation bottleneck, as retrieval relies on separately learned representations that may obscure fine-grained distinctions needed for reasoning. Moreover, these approaches commonly employ fixed top-k retrieval, which can introduce superfluous documents and increase retrieval noise and context overhead. To address this, we reformulate retrieval from query-document relevance matching into the exploration of answer-containing paths within the corpus, and propose FREESON (Retriever-FREE Retrieval-Augmented ReaSONing), a framework that enables LRMs to directly navigate the original corpus text to locate information that satisfies their specific needs, serving as both the reasoner and the retriever. Specifically, we introduce CT-MCTS (Corpus-Traversing Monte Carlo Tree Search), a retrieval-specialized variant of Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). CT-MCTS iteratively expands and evaluates exploration paths, starting from multiple promising entry points across the corpus. This allows LRMs to selectively reference specific segments identified during traversal, instead of fetching a fixed top-k set. Across nine benchmarks, FREESON improves average EM and F1 by 15 points over four retriever-based baselines on five general-domain QA tasks, matching the strongest baseline. It also gains 12 points on fact-checking and 13 points on medical QA under zero-shot transfer, performing on par with the much larger MedGemma-27B.
  </details>
